"The burden of the desert of the sea. As whirlwinds in the south
pass through, so it cometh from the desert, from a terrible land.
... For it is a day of trouble, and of treading down, and of per-
plexity by the Lord God of hosts in the valley of vision, breaking
down the walls, and of crying to the mountains.”—Isaiah.

"The biblical Jews were a rough, warlike nation of
herdsmen who needed and created [borrowed] a strong
willed, confident god of battle as an ego ideal. If one under-
stands Jahwe' as the personification of the hard and tena-
cious individual will of the wild herdsmen constantly fight-
ing against enemies, then one recognizes in the Bible the
first noble attempt of a victorious people arrived at stability
and prosperity to dethrone their old god of war and to per-
ceive themselves in his strong will. But after that, all that
is accomplished and attained is ascribed to a creative god
who earlier had been only a destructive one, and to whom
the chosen people which feels itself as completely heroic
voluntarily submits"-OTTO RANK
1
BECAUSE IT WAS RAINING the girl stood sheltered by the
doorway, looking down across the hills, her ears straining
to the sound of approaching riders; but she could see no-
body in the wet morning nor any sign of life anywhere.
Without turning she spoke, and her voice rose in the scale
of impatience.
"Father, did you really see someone?"
Her father made an anxious sound but he did not an-
swer. Her mother said: "He saw men who looked like
King David's men."
When the girl left the doorway and came back into
the dim windowless room, small bells tinkled at her
ankles, for she had come to the age of mating, though
she was barely twelve. The bells were a sign that her
father had placed her in the market. Lest her token of
virginity be destroyed, without which her bride-price
would have been small indeed, she wore a cord under her
coarse sleeved garment, tied around each leg above the
knee. This hobble forced her to take short mincing steps.
She crossed the room looking at her father who sat
1
hunched over on a piece of cedar bole. Turning, she lis-
tened again for the sound of the King's men. Had evil
spirits appeared to her father in his dreams?
"I saw no one," she said, looking at her mother.
"Your father saw men and they carried spears and ban-
ners and they were riding on asses."
"Why are they coming here?"
"Don't ask," said her father.
Confused and frightened, Abishag went to a dim cor-
ner and sat, and though she continued to listen she could
hear only the sounds of water. A daughter's life, she was
thinking, was not her own. Like his goats or orchard or
corn she belonged to her father, who had the right to sell
her to the highest bidder, as beulah or concubine or slave.
He had no right to sell his sons, though they could be
seized by creditors as payment on debt. All this she was
thinking with resentment and fright in her eyes. Was a
man coming to buy her? In the anxious face of her father
she read the signs of some impending unhappiness or dan-
ger, but in what way it was related to men carrying a
banner in a storm she could not imagine.
Her father now arose and went to the door to look
out. Abishag stared at his bent form and the black un-
combed mass of his hair, and then at his frail lower limbs,
which were not hidden by the garment that fell only to
his knees. His feet were bare.
After a few moments he said: "They're coming here."
Abishag joined him and looked out; and in the drip-
ping world under a gray sky she saw three men astride
asses, riding up the hill to her father's door. Her father
went out to meet them. She saw the men dismount. She
saw the four men talking. Once her father gestured
toward the house and seemed to shrink and fall back; and
then the big man, who seemed to be the leader, came
quickly toward her, several strides ahead of her father.
Abishag moved back into the room and in a voice choked
with fright said to her mother:
2
"Please don't let him buy me!"
The big man came boldly in and he looked like the
worst rogue in all of Syria. He was tall and huge, with
a broad back, a short neck, long heavy arms, and a point-
ed black beard that flicked up and down as he wagged
his head, peering into the gloom. He grunted and ex-
claimed while staring at Abishag. One of his companions
had followed him in. Turning to this man he said:
"Is this the girl you saw?"
His companion advanced and peered at Abishag.
"Yes," he said.
The big man, whose name was Zikla, turned to Abi-
shag's father. In a voice that roared and shook the walls
he cried: "You dog, why don't you have some light in
here? Can I stand in the dark and see if this girl is beauti-
ful?" He looked round for oil and wicks, for a window,
for a skylight, and finding none he summoned the girl
with a snap of his fingers, saying: "Come here!"
Almost fainting with fright Abishag went over to him
by the doorway and with rough hands he framed her in
the gray light. He did not look at her face at once; his
black eyes moved over her body and his hands assisted
his vision by pressing and stroking her to make out her
shape. When he looked at her face he sucked at his breath
and his tongue came out and lay flat and red on his beard.
Abishag's father had told friends that she was the
loveliest girl in all the world roundabout. This opinion.
Zikla would have accepted. He was looking into the large
lustrous wells of her eyes, fathomless as water in moon-
less night, gentle as the gazelle's, and embosomed with
the heaviest lashes he had ever seen. He looked into her
eyes
until the emotion in him had risen to his throat. Then
his gaze fell to her mouth and saw lips as ripe and red as
pomegranates. Her throat, her cheeks, her forehead, her
hair, her chin and even her ears he looked at in turn and
found all of them perfect. Here was indeed a girl to fan
3
the dying embers in his King. Turning to her father he
said in a loud voice:
"What is her name?"
"Abishag."
"Is she a virgin?"
In a voice shaken by fear the father said: "Yes, my
lord!"
"Has she stood under the mistletoe in the house of the
gods?"
"Ah, no!"
"Has she," asked Zikla maliciously, "perfumed her
bed with myrrh?" Sensing the father's fear he became
subtler in his cruelty. "It is said of the Persians that they
take no account of these things. Can a woman be raped?
They say no. Plainly no woman can be raped if she does
not herself wish it." He laughed loudly at his jest and
pinched Abishag's chin. "Where is this girl's mother?"
"Here," said a weak voice in the gloom.
"Woman, come here so that I can see the lies in your
face." Barely supported by her trembling legs the mother
came forward. Zikla grasped her and turned her toward
the light. "Don't lie to me," he said, "or I'll yank your
tongue out. You've been this girl's guardian. Is she a vir-
gin or has she lain in the love-booths?"
"She has known no man."
"Has she danced for Ishtar?"
"No, my lord."
Thrusting his beard close to the mother's face he de-
manded: "Is she in her courses?"
"No, my lord."
"Stupid woman, I'll take this girl to the King. If she's
in her courses he will sicken and die and you'll be buried
alive in a lime-pit !"
"Isn't it true?" asked the mother, appealing to her
daughter.
"Yes," said Abishag.
The father came forward and in a voice so shaken that
4
he could barely speak he asked: "If you take my daughter
to the King who will pay me?"
With insolence twitching his beard and shining in his
cruel black eyes Zikla stared at the father; and at last,
dropping his voice to a sinister whisper and making a
hideous face, he said: "Has some fool told you that the
King buys his women?" The thought made him burst
with laughter. But almost at once he sobered and leaned
down and forward, his beard thrust out, his brows low-
ered, his wicked eyes glowing. "Did the King pay for
Bathsheba?" Blowing heavy breath from his nostrils he
asked: "Did he pay for Ahinoam?" Then, changing sud-
denly, his lips curling and his eyes flashing, he thundered:
"You evil pig, isn't the King the Anointed? Do the gods
buy their women?"
The father shuddered. To oppose David's will was to
oppose the will of Yah. Nevertheless he said sadly: "She
is all I have. She is very beautiful."
Zikla looked again at Abishag and his dark Syrian
face opened in a lustful grin.
Drawing on all her strength the mother now spoke,
softly but firmly, saying: "Is there no payment for us
from her baal?"
Zikla was outraged. He grasped Abishag's arm to drag
her after him. Forgetting his humble position the father
moved forward to restrain him and received a brutal
blow on his mouth. Then Zikla shoved him and sent the
old man reeling to a wall and in the next moment swept
the girl up to his arms and left the house, with Abishag
screaming...
It was true that David, King of the Hebrew tribes and
of all the people he had conquered and enslaved, was sick
unto death. He had had in his young years a frame as
tough as a hornbeam chariot but he had lived self-indul-
gently, even for his time, when few restraints were laid
on the appetites of those in positions of power. Now in
old age he lay on his bed, and though his mind was still
5
clear his will was feeble, like a flame with no oil in its
wick. Among those who hovered over him were some
who waited impatiently for him to die.
Out of fear for the mysterious and dreadful world of
the shades, rather than out of mercy or love, everything
had been done that wise men could do to restore him to
vigor. No menstrual woman had been allowed in his pres-
ence. The most famous sorcerers and magicians had been
brought to his bedside. They had tried to draw demons
through his nostrils or ears; they had fed him calamus,
colchicum, acacia juice and various salts; they had bathed
and massaged him with the spittle of priests and the love-
oil of virgins. They had rubbed him from head to feet
with cattle dung. They had anointed him with holy
waters, with the juice of the colocasium plant, with fenu-
greek, with rancid kidney fat. They had chanted magical
words to allay fevers and ague. When his old eyes
watered constantly they asked: had he been looked on by
the Evil Eye?-for it was an envious thing and like the
sun had power to destroy. To neutralize its power you
looked with a kindly expression; and so it was that the
persons around him tried to gaze at one another pleasant-
ly, though in all of them there was wicked impatience
for his death.
But all their devotion had been of no use.
Having heard that sailors used a caul to protect them
from shipwreck Bathsheba had sent men to find one; but
this likewise had been powerless for good. Some of his
wives, driven to desperation, had called on alien gods, on
Melech, on Shamash, on Milcom and Bel. It was Abia-
thar, sole survivor of the family of Ahimelech, whom
Saul in a fit of lunacy had slain, who bethought himself
one day of virgins, recalling that their effect was said to
be divine on the waning powers of men. This notion he
confided to Bathsheba.
In her deep throaty voice Bathsheba said: "Are there
wiser men in Egypt?"
6
"No!" cried the priest, shocked.
"Well, he does not walk as of old, or play the chalil
or eat the figcake." She was saying discreetly that her
baal was ill. Looking into Abiathar's sunken eyes she
said slowly: "All that you have done is wind on the
sands."
"May Yah forbid!" he cried.
"You're the thorny burnet on which goats feed."
He looked away, appalled by the expression in her
eyes, and there came to him a thought. But he said only:
"I'm sorry I have displeased you."
Gazing at him, Bathsheba was thinking that the He-
brews looked down on her people, who were the Hittites.
Most of them were slaves or menials, and she herself
might have been if the lustful David had not seen her
unclothed and called her to his bed.
Abiathar now told her that the love of a beautiful vir-
gin could restore the strength of an ailing man, having
a miraculous effect on his creative powers. With a straight
face Bathsheba listened to him, studying his mouth and
eyes.
"Very well, then," she said, and turned away.
And so it was that messengers had been dispatched
north, south and west to find the loveliest virgin between
the waters of Lebanon and the springs of Kadesh. If
David could arouse himself to cover her, thereby ful-
filling, as was his divine right, one of the functions of
godhood, he might be invigorated with youthful heat;
and Yah would then withdraw his anger. Failing in that,
David could in any case look upon her house of life.
Two weeks later, several persons stood in the King's
chamber, all of them silent and waiting. Bathsheba was
there, standing by David's bedside. Nathan, the elderly
seer, was there, a man who for many years had been Da-
vid's tutor and friend. It was Nathan who, with the par-
able of a sheep, had convinced David of his sin in seizing
Bathsheba and slaying her husband; and who in more
7
recent years, conspiring with Bathsheba, had prevailed
on the King to look on Solomon as his successor. As a
token of atonement for David's sin, Solomon had been
given the title of Jedediah, meaning the beloved of Yah,
the Hebrew god.
Abiathar was there and standing with him was Adoni-
jah, David's oldest living son and the only one of all the
sons whom the common people expected to see next on
the throne. Tall and haughty, intelligent and proud, he
had the firm chin of his mother Haggith, who was a Phili-
stine, and the piercing eyes of his father. Adonijah—his
name meant Yah is the Lord-stood back in a dim cor-
ner, his veiled eyes studying Bathsheba and her son.
That son was Solomon. He was shorter than his half-
brother, and by comparison fair of complexion, with the
color of sunlight in his hair. The Hebrews had straight
noses but the Hittite nose was crooked and sometimes
bulbous. Solomon, half-Hittite, had the nose of his
mother. The most striking thing about him was his large
luminous eyes, and next to that his hair. The Hebrews
admired coal-black hair. Solomon was unhappily con-
scious of the reddish luster of his curls.
Rain was falling outside. This was the wet season, and
the chamber in which David lay was gloomy with damp-
ness and chill. Besides gloom there was the presence of
evil, of sinister and cynical treacheries; because five per-
sons were waiting for a king to die and in the minds of
all of them the thoughts were of the same dark color.
Bathsheba had schemed for years to make her son king
and seemed likely to succeed, though he had no right at
all to the throne. As for Adonijah, who knew of the plot,
he was wondering if he should seize the throne at once
and have Solomon slain. . . .
In preparing Abishag for the King, skillful hands had
enhanced the girl's natural beauty with powders and
paints. Though she was hysterical and fainting she was
bathed with scented waters and then thoroughly drench-
8
ed with sweet essences. Persians, it was said, had a per-
fume blended of twenty-five odors; the Hebrews had
nothing like that but they did successfully mix crocus
and myrrh and rare musk with various flowers. Between
the girl's breasts the attendants rubbed spikenard; on
the soles of her feet, opobalsam; on her belly, lilies; on
her thighs, lotus; on her lower legs, mint and thyme.
The attendants walked round and round her sniffing or
leaning forward to breathe from her hair, her throat,
her loins.
They dressed her in a simple white garment and put
on her feet sandals with gay rosettes. Her hair, drenched
with perfumes, was arranged in coiled masses against
her neck, contained with a white cap, and adorned with
red tassels and a brazen serpent. The gown was confined
at her waist with a long girdle which was wrapped sev-
eral times round her and secured with a jeweled pin.
To add to the luster of her eyes the long heavy lashes
were saturated with a mixture of egg-white and pow-
dered gum-ammoniac; and an ivory probe, dipped into
scented water and touched to powdered kohl, was drawn
between her closed lids.. . .
"Stop your weeping!" the woman cried. "Hold up
your hands!"
Her fingernails and even her palms were dyed red.
Her cheeks and throat were brushed with a perfumed
powder. A sign like an Egyptian phallic cross was faintly
drawn on her forehead.
This, then, was the girl who was to employ the magic
of her virginal youth on a sick king. When she was thrust
in, gasping and weeping and terrified, it would be im-
possible to say which of those who beheld her was most
impressed. The door was slammed shut and there was
silence, broken only by her sobs. Then David rose slowly
to an elbow in an effort to see her, and there was the
sound of his hoarse breathing. His face was ashen, his
lower lids fell away from his eyes, his upraised hand,
9
mottled with age and swollen at the knuckles, beckoned
to the girl to draw near. But she was too terrified to
move. She stared at the shrunken spectral form and then
for a startled moment looked at the cool deep eyes of
Bathsheba. She looked next at Abiathar and shuddered.
Then she saw the meaning in Adonijah's eyes and gasped
as though drenched with frost and looked round her for
a way to escape.
In a voice that was hardly more than heavy labored
breath David asked: "What is your name?"
Abishag tried to speak and failed and then looked
appealingly to Bathsheba.
"His Majesty the King," said Bathsheba, "asked you
a question.'
"Yes!"
"Answer him."
"Never mind," said David.
Though his sins had been many, though at times he
had been as cruel as any other despot, King David had
in him a vein of kindliness-almost, it had sometimes
seemed, of compassion. There was compassion in the old
man now. With an effort that was painfully visible he
was resting on an elbow and looking at the girl. He
wanted her to come to him. He wanted to put his dying
arms around her and lie close to her youngness, drawing
into himself, if he could, the godlike powers of her
youth. In Adonijah's face the lusting was plain. It was
plain but more subtle in the face of Solomon. But in
David, no matter how wanton and self-indulgent he had
been, the hunger was spiritual. His need came from the
loneliness in those who are dying among those waiting
for their death.
Bathsheba came over to the girl and pushed her, reel-
ing and shaken, toward the bed and then shoved her over
to the King. With a great sigh David reached out for
her-and it was less a shrunken and repulsive old man,
laying palsied hands on a virgin, than it was age, with
10
its bitter cold, trying to warm itself at the heart of
youth. The scene on the bed was more than those stand-
ing wished to look at. Bathsheba went to the door and
turned there and looked back at her son, and then she
went out and Solomon quickly followed her. Adonijah
looked at the priest.
"We'd better go," he said.
"Yes, let's go."
Softly they went outside. As Adonijah closed the door
behind him he could hear the hoarse breathing of an old
man and the terrified weeping of a girl who was only a
child.
2
ADONIJAH HAD BEEN THINKING: "If I don't have Solo-
mon slain at once he will have me killed." Should he
seize the throne now, even though the season was not
favorable to ceremonies out-of-doors? In the Lebanon
by early autumn and in southern Israel by mid-autumn
the rains began to fall; and for the Hebrews there were
only two seasons, the hot and the cold, the wet and the
dry. Sometimes for weeks the rain fell continuously and
was then known as The Pourer. The moisture-laden
winds from the west and southwest drove in from the
sea, occasionally dropping hail on the high hills or cloth-
ing Jerusalem with melting white flakes. The rainfall,
heavy in the winter months, began to abate by early
spring, and ceased entirely during the time of flowers.
After the rains ceased, fountains gushed forth in many
places, as at the feet of Hermon, of Gilboa, and the
Samaritan hills; and for a little while the eroded and
desolate land of Judea was lovely under its flowers.
11
It was raining when Adonijah left his father's cham-
ber. The whole sky was leaden and dark when he walked
through his small private garden near the well of Rogel,
with a goathair cape flung over his shoulders. Looking at
nothing but the muddy path under his feet he went with
Abiathar to the priest's tent and there they paused to
shake moisture from their garments and hair. Then they
went in and to a small back chamber and sat by a char-
coal brasier.
"They watched us," said Adonijah.
"I don't doubt it."
"My father is completely in the power of this woman
Bathsheba. Did you notice how she looked at Solomon
when she went out?"
"Yes."
"Do I have the support of the people?"
"I think so."
"How about Joab and the host?"
"I think Joab is on your side."
"Does Yah favor me?"
"I am sure of it."
"Have you asked the oracles ?"
"Yes."
For a few moments Adonijah was silent, staring at his
hands. Then suddenly he raised his head and met the
priest's eyes. "Should I seize the throne now?"
"I-I don't know."
"If I wait-”
"Yes, I know. What is Solomon planning?"
"Who can say but Solomon and his mother? If I seize
the throne can I count on you?"
Abiathar's lean face opened with astonishment. "Let's
make the covenant," he said. Gravely Adonijah raised
his right hand and Abiathar smote his palm sharply, for
this was one way to seal a compact. Then the right hand
of each grasped the other's beard and they swore an oath
of loyalty.
12
Now the priest arose and entered the other room, re-
turning with a small basin of water; and this water he
poured over Adonijah's hands, thus making it plain, in
a manner sacred to them, that the priest would be the
king's servant. Feeling secure in the friendship, Adonijah
relaxed.
"Should I wait till morning?"
"I think it would be safe enough."
"If we could find out what Solomon is plotting. You
know, I think his plans are all laid and that Nathan is
privy to them and that my father has approved."
"Maybe your father knows nothing about it."
"But if I wait long this impostor will destroy me. He
has his assassins-"
"Hark!" said Abiathar, and listened. He went to a
wall and put his ear to it. The room in which they sat had
no windows and no outside door but its walls were thin,
being only heavy tent-cloth. "I heard someone," he said,
whispering. "It was a spy."
Adonijah arose and went over to him. "Bathsheba,"
he said, whispering, "has many spies."
The two men went back and sat on stools and both
were wondering what they should do. Adonijah did not
know how to have himself proclaimed king. Kingship was
a new privilege for these children of the desert. There
had been Saul, an uncouth man given to lunacies, who had
lived in a tent, ignorant of the splendid kingdoms around
him. There was David, who had never quite abandoned
his simple ancestral way of life. Adonijah knew, and Solo-
mon knew even more than Adonijah, that by comparison
with the great civilizations on the Nile and the Euphrates
the Hebrew kingdom was a poor thing indeed. Their
people had come out of the desert to seize the fertile
lands of another people and for a long while had strug-
gled with only the crudest weapons to make their hold se-
cure. And so, having no art, no culture and no splendors
13
of any kind, it was a painful sense of his ignorance that
troubled Adonijah.
"How should I go about it?" he asked at last. "Should
I just have Joab kill my brother and proclaim me king?"
"Not quite," said Abiathar, who as priest was guided
by instructions from Yah. "You will give a feast in your
private garden and invite all your brothers but Solomon.
You will need much wine-fat for the joy of the people
and after you have all eaten and drunk there will be salt
between you and them and they will bless you."
"Then?"
"I will anoint you."
And so, to disguise his motives, Adonijah let it be
known that he was going to make offerings to Yah; and
to make this plausible he announced that he had had a
dream. He had been in the topmost branches of a tall tree
and clung to twigs that barely supported him. He had
known that he could climb no farther without Yah's aid;
but Yah was angry with him and had cast him down. He
had fallen head foremost and around him had been the
sound of angry winds and of a voice in the winds rebuk-
ing him.
When word of this dream came to Nathan, the old
seer, he knew the meaning at once, as Abiathar had sus-
pected that he would. Nathan could interpret dreams. He
knew that for his people a valley was a depth and the
judgments of Yah were depths; their word for abyss
meant terror and destruction; their word for salvation
meant a wide open space. Their god was one who dwelt
on and marched across the high places. Their name for
the great Jordan river meant the down-falling one.
Knowing the meaning of the dream, Nathan hastened
to tell Bathsheba. He told her that Adonijah coveted the
throne and had climbed to talk to Yah, but Yah, angered
by his presumption, had cast him down. Yah had de-
nounced him in the winds. Now Adonijah had to make
peace with his god with an offering.
14
Bathsheba was a very shrewd woman. "Are you sure
this isn't a trick?"
"I think," said Nathan, "that I have read his heart."
He used the word heart because for the Hebrews the
heart was the seat of intelligence. The head was the seat
of life, and so it was that a hand on the head was a sign
of mourning and a laying of hands on the head was a
blessing of life.
"He's an evil man," said Bathsheba. "It'll take a
strong hand to push his head backward for he's like a ram
caught by his horns. Watch him. I don't trust him."
Nathan watched Adonijah. He saw him put on a rag-
ged garment and walk like one borne down by the weight
of sin. He saw him praying by the ancient stone of Zohe-
leth, facing the east and making great lamentations. He
prayed by the stone because the spirit of a deity lived in
a stone which was a house for him.
Two mornings later the rain fell away and just at dawn
the city was awakened by the sound of music. Unknown
to Nathan or to any of Bathsheba's spies Adonijah had
gathered a score of musicians, some with the toph, a small
timbrel, some with the shofar, some with a long tube or
which terrifying blasts could be blown, and still others
with the nebel, a kind of harp, or the chalil, a kind of
oboe. Like all people of their time the Hebrews had no
knowledge of harmony; their music was simple, coarse
and ear-splitting, each musician doing his best to make
the loudest and shrillest sounds. Some sang while they
played, their song being a cantillation as shrill as their
music.
Spring up, O well! Leap, O fountain of life!
Come to the desert as gifts to us!....
Sing to Yah, for victorious is he!
The horse and its rider he has hurled into the sea!....
And suddenly a man gave a great shout: "May Yah
save King Adonijah!"
15
On the altar-stone a small fire was burning and by it
was a pile of acacia, a sacred plant in the desert, the com-
mon name of which was shittimwood. When the noisy
musicians approached the altar Adonijah was not yet
there, but Abiathar was there, slaughtering a fatted beast
and laying its organs and fat on the fire. Adonijah was in
the back room of his tent putting on such royal robes as
he had and presently he came forth. Over his loincloth
hung a robe with a hole in the center through which he
had thrust his head; and though its color was royal purple
and its edge fringed, it was only of cheap wool, stained
with a cheap dye. Round his waist he had wrapped a long
girdle, with the ends hanging down in front; and into this
girdle, which he used as a sword-belt, he had thrust a
dagger. On his head he wore a mitre-for at this time the
king was also chief priest-which was a long strip of
cloth wound round and round his head and shaped in a
conical apex, resembling the tiara of Babylon's kings. At-
tached to it behind and falling to his shoulders was
fil-
let of purple ribbon. Within doors the Hebrews wore
nothing on their feet but Adonijah had put on sandals
which were simple leather soles held by a leather latchet.
When he went forth the multitude greeted him with a
tremendous shout: "Hail, King Adonijah!"
Kicking his sandals off before treading holy ground,
Adonijah went to the altar and Abiathar came to him
with the anointing oil. This, mixed according to divine
prescription, was scented with myrrh, cinnamon, calamus
and cassia. When Adonijah knelt there was silence. Upon
his head Abiathar poured the warmed oil in the holy rite
of consecration and it ran over Adonijah's mitre and hair
and down over his robe. Anointing him with the oil in-
vested him with a part of Yah's holiness. When he arose
he was King of all the tribes of Israel and another great
shout acclaimed him. His eyes swept the multitude, now
prostrate in homage before him, but the words which he
had intended to say were never uttered. The multitude
16
was beside itself with joy and soon arose to its feet, shout-
ing and acknowledging him, some addressing him as mes-
siah and lord, and some, whose knowledge was greater,
as Adoni-ham-melech.
"Adoni-ham-melech !" some were shouting; and oth-
ers:
"The Anointed, the King of Israel!"
"Our Lord, Yah's holy one!"
"Praise ye Yah!"
"Halleluyah!"
They closed in then and hoisted him to their shoulders
and bore him triumphantly. The mob headed for the cen-
ter of the city, with the musicians making their shrill
sounds, with King Adonijah riding on the shoulders of
his people, while runners went ahead crying with a loud
voice that the King was coming. Joab and a part of
his armed host closed in behind.
"May Yah save King Adonijah !"
"Adonijah-Adoni-King!"
Nathan had come out and seeing the multitude had
been appalled; and at once he set off as fast as his weary
old legs would carry him to find Bathsheba; but she had
already heard the shouting and divined the treachery and
was hastening from her tent to David's chambers. With-
out pausing to announce her presence she burst in upon
him. "Go!" she cried to the frightened Abishag, and the
girl fled, leaving the door ajar. Bathsheba closed it and
barred it and went over and sat on the bed and looked
at David's face.
"David!" she said softly, and when he did not open his
eyes she spoke sharply: "David, look at me!"
"What's the trouble?" his lips asked, but his eyes re-
mained closed.
"You know what the trouble is! Have you betrayed
us ?"
When his eyelids fluttered she leaned forward to look
17
at them, thinking that possibly the stuff running from
them had cemented the lashes. With a finger she turned
a lid upward and back until she could see the pupil; and
she knew then by the way the eye looked at her that Da-
vid was deliberately keeping his eyes shut. It was a way he
had when he chose not to look at things that annoyed or
perplexed him.
"David, open your eyes!"
"Why?" He opened one eye and looked at her. She
probed at the other lid and forced it up. Staring at her
with both eyes, faded and sunken and with moisture well-
ing at the lower lids, he asked:
"What was the noise?"
'Adonijah has had himself proclaimed king, that's
what it is! Did you encourage him? Did you?"
"No."
"Are you going to do nothing about it?"
Slowly a lid sank, covering the right eye, which was
farthest from her; but the other, which seemed to be
brighter when shining alone, fixed her with calm and in-
quiring interest. Wearily he spoke, again reminding her
of an obvious truth.
"He is my oldest son."
In such a moment, some women might have accused
him of baseness, some might have wept; but this Hittite,
with her face showing no emotion, merely looked at him
while her mind searched out the words with which to
move him, the ways by which she might proceed.
"David, do you remember your promise?"
Slowly, feebly he answered her, saying: "I've made
many promises-and some-may Yah forgive me-I
have broken."
Bathsheba looked at the one eye, filmed with sickness,
and considered her next words. "You promised me," she
said at last, "that Solomon would be the next king. Have
you forgotten that?"
18
"No," he said.
"You're the Anointed, the son of Yah, the Lord-"
Something in his face told her that these titles did not
please him. She tried again: "Yah, you have told me, is
the greatest of all the gods. His spirit is in you, his will
is your will, your words are his words for when you speak
it is Yah who speaks. When you made the promise it was
Yah who spoke. Isn't that true? Isn't it true that Yah
chose Solomon, whose name means peace, to rule after
you, who have spent your life in war?"
"Yah commanded me to make war."
"Yes. But answer me: did you speak for Yah when
you promised?"
Upon the answer to that question hung the fate of a
kingdom; and David, sensing this, took his time, trying
to foresee the results of Yes and the results of No. He
had wanted his son Absalom to succeed him but Absalom
had betrayed him. Among his sons was there any one who
could hold the hotheaded tribes together and consolidate
the kingdom which he had built? If Adonijah were king
he might wage war on his neighbors for he was warlike
in his moods. Solomon on the other hand would try to
build splendors equal to those in other kingdoms, never
realizing that Israel was too poor to compete with the
Euphrates or the Nile. It is impossible to know what
David's decision might have been if there had not been
a knock on the door. Bathsheba went over and admitted
Nathan and her son.
"Nathan," she said, "tell David whether Yah is
pleased with Adonijah.”
Nathan came forward and stood by the bed. He was
an old man with a gray beard and a heavy mane of dark
hair streaked with white.
"Adonijah has angered Yah," he said, "because he had
himself proclaimed when he was unclean."
Watching David closely Bathsheba saw him wince.
19
"What shall I do?" asked David, who was weary of
war.
"Call Joab and have him take Solomon to the Hill of
Sion, there to be anointed."
"Nathan, does Yah command this?"
"Yes, David."
"Do the stones say so?"
"Yes, David."
"Do all the signs favor Solomon?"
99
"The signs say he will reign in peace for forty years.'
David's lips were murmuring: "Yah is my Rock, he is
my shield, he is my tower and refuge. Unto you now I
cry, O Yah, my Rock, for your laws are in my bones, what
you command I will do."
David was lying on his back. He now strove to turn and
face the east. Perceiving that he was too weak to move
Nathan placed hands under his back and shoulders; and
when David had turned to his right side he said:
"Open the door."
When the door was opened he looked through and be-
yond to the sky above Moab. For a little while he seemed
too exhausted to speak, but at last he said:
"Call Joab."
The fanatical Joab, nephew of David, and for many
years his ablest general, was at this moment a very un-
happy man. Like most of the people he had supposed
that Adonijah, the eldest surviving son, would be the
next king, and he had therefore taken a part in the anoint-
ing. On hearing from Nathan that Solomon was to be the
king, Joab wondered what mischief and treachery was
afoot, and recalled with a shudder of dismay those times
when he had offended David. For Absalom's treachery
Joab had slain him and David had then denounced him.
Ruthless, sometimes vindictive, but always loyal to his
King, Joab believed that killing was a religious duty and
he took his religion seriously.
20
When he entered the chamber he wondered if he had
been summoned to his death but he strode boldly, as was
his way, going to the bed and looking straight at David.
With both eyes open David looked up at his old general.
"David, have I offended you?”
"You have angered Yah."
Joab looked at Bathsheba and then at Nathan. Of the
ways of the gods he pretended to no knowledge; fighting
had been his work.
"You are to take Solomon," said David, speaking slow-
ly and with obvious distaste, "to the Hill of Sion and
there proclaim him King."
"But Adonijah-David, he has been anointed."
David closed both his eyes and with an effort he spoke
again. "Joab," he asked, his eyes shut, "can you hear
me?"
"Yes, David."
"Then why do you stand there?"
"Adonijah-"
"-is not the King," said David.
Joab looked again at Bathsheba. More than bugles
might have done the half-lidded light in her eyes told him
that he was in the presence of danger. In his impetuous
way of old he turned again to his King.
"Do you want me to kill Adonijah?"
"No," said Bathsheba quickly.
"Obey David's orders," said Nathan. "Take many
men and conduct Solomon to the fountain of Gihon.
Take musicians. I'll follow and bring the royal ass."
Joab looked at David and waited. When the King
did not speak or look at him he asked: "David, do you
command me to do these things?"
"Yah commands you."
"Shall I bring Adonijah here?"
"No."
"David, are we to have two kings?"
21
"You heard your King's command," said Bathsheba.
"Go."
Joab left the chamber, followed by Nathan, who was
determined that the anointing of Solomon would be,
unlike that of Adonijah, in accordance with divine rules.
For one thing there must be water. Moses had stood by
the spring of Kadesh, the Fountain of Judgment, when
he instructed his people in the wisdom of Yah. Nathan
knew that the waters in this old Jebusite city of Salem
were not so holy as the fountain of Dan or the seven
wells of Beersheba or even the waters of Hebron; but
time pressed and they would have to do. The fountain of
Gihon was muddy and ill-smelling, and while proceeding
to it Nathan thought of a question which Solomon had
asked him again and again. If Yah was the most power-
ful of all the gods, and if water was his principal treas-
ure, as it was of deities everywhere, why did Judea have
so little of it, and the lands of other gods so much? If
the gods were born from rivers, Solomon asked, as a
babe was born from the waters that burst in its mother,
if water was the life-principle of staff and womb, if
long ago the ocean deeps had been the womb of the un-
created over which had hovered the divine fertilizing
spirit, and if the measure of a god's power and holiness
was the sweetness of his waters, what was one to think
of Yah when smelling the waters in his cisterns? In this,
as in so many other matters, Nathan had not been able
to satisfy Solomon's eager and inquiring mind. . . .
Joab had gathered soldiers and musicians, and with
royal pomp conducted Solomon to the Hill of Sion and
the waters of Gihon. There Nathan purified Solomon
with baptism by sprinkling water over him, and Solomon,
making a face at him, whispered: "Yah's waters still
stink!" After he had been anointed he said:
"Does my dog of a brother know that I am now
King?"
22
"Your mother will tell him."
"Let us hasten. He will not sit still."
Musicians were making shrill music and voices were

Blessed be our Anointed
who rides on the tawny ass
who stands on the rich carpet
and walks by the way of the waters! . . .
They were singing:
How beautiful are your tents, O Jacob
and your tabernacles, O Israel!
Like valleys they are spread forth
like gardens by the flowing waters
like the cedars which Yah stood
beside the waters! . . .
There was neither wine nor feasting; Nathan and his
anxious King had no time for these. Even when Nathan

the kidney-fat, the kidney-fat of oil, of the vintage, of
the corn-even when he took this he moved with such

found you!" said Solomon. "Now I'll be only half a

"Look!" said Nathan. "Here comes one of your

It was Naamah, daughter of Nahun, a petty king of
the Ammonites, a crafty and marauding people on
whom David had made war. Naamah looked like one
whose deity was Melech, the dreadful god who wished to
savor above all things the burning flesh of infants: she

23
"Why in the name of Melech," she demanded angrily,

"Is that," asked Solomon, "the way you address his
Majesty, the King of Israel?"
"Don't jest now!" cried Naamah angrily. "Your

"With what?" asked Solomon, giving her a wide-

"Oh, in the name of the gods! Can you understand

"Maybe we'd better go," Solomon said to Nathan.
He ran over to the royal ass and mounted, robed in
a purple cloak and with a staff in his right hand; and
forthwith the new King set off to claim his city. Criers
ran before him to announce his coming. Musicians, blow-

Naamah walked with Nathan. Her cunning mind
stripped all problems to the bare essentials of her
woman's world. Now as she walked over stones and
mud, feeling the chill on her feet and legs, she wondered
why Solomon did not send Joab at once to slay Adonijah.
Such, after all, was the custom among people—among
all the people at least whom she had known or had heard
of the new king always moved with vigor to destroy
his enemies, including all pretenders to the throne, who
were principally his brothers. Looking at Nathan she
asked:
"Why don't you tell Joab to kill Adonijah?"
"I'm not the King," said Nathan.
"You could ask David to command him."
"David," said Nathan gently, "is-I say this but do
not say it, for it is of the wind on the sands-David is
weak. Be patient."
"I will not!" she cried, looking at him wickedly. Then
24
she left him and ran forward, and when she came to the

"What do you want?" asked Bathsheba, rising angrily.
"I want to see you."
"Not now. Go."
""
Naamah slipped outside and then stood close by the

3
WHEN ADONIJAH SUSPECTED that his father had be-
trayed him he turned cold with fury and ran to the
building, intending to confront David, but guards there
hurled him back. Sickened and appalled, his face gray
with fear, he hastened to his own tent and sent a mes-
senger for Abiathar; and he was pacing back and forth,
gulping a strong liquor made of wine lees, dates and
honey when the priest came in.
"My father has betrayed me!" Adonijah cried.
"I know it."
"Joab has deserted me!"
"I know that also."
"Yah has tricked me!" What Adonijah had in mind
was the common belief that the gods themselves, like
their people, were fickle, treacherous, cruel and unpre-
dictable. Sometimes Yah had dreadful tantrums and
tried to destroy his people with plagues and pestilence
and famine or with thunderous storms. Not even a priest
25
could foretell his moods or explain the capricious changes
of his will.
Abiathar admitted that Yah appeared to have changed
his mind.
"What can I do? Am I to stay here like a beast in a
bramble thicket waiting for the jackals? You may as
well arouse yourself, for if I die you will die!"
"Solomon may get drunk. Then he will sleep."
"Yes!-with an armed force around him!"
"There is Abishag."
"Oh? Abishag!"
"There may be a way for you. It is the law among
the gods of all people that the new king inherits the
women of the old king, his mother only excepted. You
see," Abiathar went on, his voice sinking to a sibilant
whisper that deeply affected his listener, "if you can
get one of your father's women and this becomes known
to the people, they will regard Solomon as an impostor
and they will acclaim you. They will think that Yah has
changed his mind again. Once acclaimed you will move
swiftly to have Solomon killed."
"To think that I didn't when I had the power!"
"It would be folly to ask David for this girl; but
Bathsheba, who is ignorant, and in fact an accursed
woman-" A smile touched his face like a light. "You
are very handsome. Bathsheba thinks she is now the
Queen Mother. She is covetous of honors. She can be
flattered. You will pretend-and Yah will not punish
you, for even the gods deceive-that your midriff aches
intolerably for this girl-and that she will understand,
for she knows how David's ached when he committed
the sin on the housetop. I think," said Abiathar, and
again a thin smile gave sudden bright intelligence to
his features, "I think Bathsheba will be inclined to in-
dulge your passion for reasons of her own. Be humble.
Be willing to barter your rights for a trifle. If you can
act your part well-Do you feel humble?"
26
"Very humble," said Adonijah gravely.
"Go, then. Our lives depend on you.'
Bathsheba received Adonijah in her tent. He thought
her mood was indulgent. Adonijah knelt before her,
a sad suppliant, his face and garments stained, his eyes
downcast.
"Arise," she said. "What is it you want?"
"I've been a wicked man," he said. "The powers of
evil deceived me."
Her face impassive, her eyes inscrutable, Bathsheba
said: "That is true."
"I have come to ask a small favor of you."
"What?"
"The girl Abishag. I have four wives," he said hastily,
"but they are unattractive. I took them, as you know,
because my father asked me to, to promote peace among
the people." He almost said our people and then remem-
bered that she was a Hittite. He looked at her hooked
and bulbous nose and hoped his candor and his humble
mien would overthrow her suspicions. "Abishag affects
me very deeply. I would like to have her. Then I would
go away."
"Where?"
"To the north. I would become a son of the soil. When
I first saw her," he went on, shrugging for his lecheries,
"a great passion smote my bowels." He sighed, like one
resigned to his follies. "Only the strong," he said, sweep-
ing her face with a glance, "can be aloof to the passions
which torment them. I am not one of the strong.'
99
In a voice that told him nothing at all Bathsheba
asked: "When would you go?"
At this moment Naamah burst into the tent. Adonijah
arose and bowed, observing those amenities which were
the Queen Mother's due. Bathsheba said she would

27
Naamah hastening away from the tent. He would have
been more startled if he could have seen Bathsheba's
face pressed to a hole in the tent and her steady eyes

She sent for her son, and when he came, still in his
royal robes and stained with the anointing oil and smell-

request and concluded: "He has some plot in mind but

"You don't know ?" asked Solomon, lifting his brows.
"Do you?"
"Yes. But I'll soon take care of him. Do you have
any wine here?" Bathsheba brought forth a clay pitcher

"See that?" he said, indicating the pitcher. "What a
vessel with which to serve a king! I'm going to have all
the best things in the world, the choicest wines and

splendors and beautiful women and gardens and foun-

envied among the nations."
"If you can, my son."
"What will stop me?"
"You'll have many enemies, even among your own
people. Your people don't want you to have treasures
and splendors and palaces; and the prophets-"
"What prophets?"
"Well, there is Ahijah."
For a little while Solomon thought of Israel's proph-
ets, of whom there were many, nearly all of them naked
and mad and sometimes outrageously funny. With a
few musicians blowing music around them they would
stride down from the high places, their black eyes flash-
ing, their mouths covered with slobber, which was called
the froth of ecstasy, their lips denouncing everything
under the sky including priests and kings. Ahijah was of
a different sort.
28
"Should I have Ahijah killed?" Solomon asked at last.
"I've wondered," said Bathsheba. "They say he's
very popular with the people. Anyway, Adonijah is the
first one to take care of. My son, I wouldn't wait. He
probably has gone to the altar." Bathsheba had in mind.
the holy custom which protected those who stood by

"I'll have him slain upon the altar."
"Oh no, I wouldn't do that. Your people would be
offended."
"Then what am I to do?-leave him there forever?"
"Why not ask your father? And go now, son. Shall
I go with you?"
"No."
Solomon headed for David's chamber, and the guards

was there. She was on the bed, revealing her youth to

closed, David stared up at his son.
"May Yah protect you," Solomon said. With him
the words were hardly more than an empty formula.
"Yah is our Rock," David murmured.
"I am now the King of Israel."
"Yes," said David.
"Adonijah is plotting against me." He waited for
David to speak, but David only looked at him like
a corpse with one eye open. "Adonijah told my mother
that he wanted Abishag, this girl who was here. He said
he would go away with her and be a son of the soil and
never trouble me. He said this girl fills him with divine
fires."
"She is like the gods," David murmured.
'Adonijah thought that if he could get her, the people
would proclaim him king."
29
David opened the other eye and looked full on his son.
"I must kill Adonijah and do it at once. Do you
agree?"
"Maybe he wants to be a son of the soil. I was one."
"He lied. He is conspiring against me. Shall I have
all my brothers slain ?"
"In the name of Yah-" David began, protesting, but
Solomon interrupted him.
"You hung up the seven sons of Saul."
"That was to please Yah."
"Did Yah command it?"
"Yes."
"All right, Yah commands me to slay my brother."
"Has Nathan said so?"
Solomon went to the door and told a guard to bring
Nathan; and when the old seer came, Solomon said
to him:
"Does Yah command me to slay my brother Adoni-
jah?"
Nathan hesitated a moment but not out of compas-
sion. His god was, after all, a war-god, who commanded
his people to take the cities of the enemy and destroy
them, every man, woman and child. Yah commanded
that certain of the choice captives be offered to him upon
the altars.
"Yes," said Nathan, "Yah commands it."
"Does he command me to kill all my brothers ?"
"I haven't asked the stones." Nathan meant the urim
and thummin, two oracle-lots which he cast to obtain
Yah's will.
"Go ask them." After Nathan had gone Solomon was
about to speak when he observed one of his father's
hands. It was lying on the cover. The forefinger was
tapping up and down but only this one thing revealed
the agitation and anxiety in David. Solomon watched
the finger for several moments and then looked at
David's face.
30
"If I don't kill my brothers they will cause me strife
all the years of my reign. It will take a thousand spies
to watch them."
"You could banish them," said David gently.
"Oh yes, I could-so they could raise armies among
my enemies and march against me! And am I to spend
a part of my treasure supporting them in idleness all
their long years? You killed those who stood in your
way.'
"Yah commanded it."
"If Yah commands this?"
"If he does-"
The shutters drew across David's eyes and both eyes
were closed. Going to the door, Solomon told a guard
to fetch Benaiah. Benaiah, one of the captains of the
host, was a cruel and ruthless warrior who had cast his
lot with Solomon. For years he had been captain of
David's personal bodyguard, and, like Joab, a faithful
servant, obeying the King's orders as he would have
obeyed Yah's. Placing Moabite captives in a line on the
earth, David had measured off two-thirds of the men,
and these he had tortured and killed; and Benaiah had
leapt up and down with joy when he saw the slaughter.
His name meant that he was a son of Yah, and he served
Yah with all the weapons at his command.
When he appeared at the door, a thick rugged man
with the eyes of the professional killer, Solomon ad-
mitted him; and while the two men stood just inside
the door and talked, David cocked both ears to listen.
"Do you know where Adonijah is?"
"He's by the altar."
"Do any of the people hail him as king?"
"No man." This statement was false, but Benaiah
always put the best face on everything for his King.
"Is anybody with him at the altar?"
"No, my Lord."
"Where is Abiathar?"
81
E
"I don't know."
"I want you to watch him."
"Yes, my Lord."
"Assign him to one of your best men and tell him to
keep him in sight."
"Yes, my Lord."
"Do you have your dagger with you?"
"Here," said Benaiah and drew it from his girdle.
Solomon took the weapon and looked at it, testing
the blade on his thumb. The blade was about seven inches
long, highly polished and very sharp.
"You're to go now and kill Adonijah."
There was no change of expression in Benaiah's face
or eyes. Killing was his work and he liked his work. In a
matter-of-fact voice he asked:
"Shall I kill him on the altar?"
"Try to lure him away from it."
"If he won't come-?"
"Smite him where he stands. And listen, I don't want
any fumbles."
"When has Benaiah ever fumbled?"
"Drive the knife into him under his fifth rib."
"Fifth," said Benaiah thoughtfully and felt of his ribs.
He counted them off: "One-two-three-four-five-
Right here," he said, thrusting a finger at his side.
Solomon counted his own ribs. "Yes. If you do it right
the blade will reach his heart."
"Trust Benaiah, my Lord."
Looking into the cold eyes Solomon said: "And be
sure he's dead before you leave him."
"No man has ever walked again after Benaiah stabbed
him."
"All right, go now."
Benaiah went out, and after Solomon had closed the
door behind him he turned to his father. He caught
David staring at him but almost at once both eyes closed.
32
Solomon went over and looked down at him and asked
with grim humor:
"Why does a king ever have more than one son?"
Many years later he would ask that question again.

WHEN AHBAN AWOKE IT WAS RAINING. All night rain
had fallen in his dreams and his wandering soul had been
happy because rain was the most precious of all the
gifts from the gods. Ahban was a small landholder, with
a vineyard, a few olive trees and a few sheep. He was
not a land-owner: he knew well that the gods owned
the lands upon which they poured their living waters and
that the people who tilled the lands were only the ten-
ants. Here in Bethlehem it was Yah who gave grass to
the fields and drink to the roots; or who, when his anger
was hot against his people, shut up the skies and with-
held the fertility-because all fertility was in him and
came to the earth in water and sunlight.
Ahban lived in a time when his people were confused
but he knew that Yah was the most powerful of all the
gods, because Ahijah, the prophet, who was his friend,
had told him so. But some, like a surly old man who
lived in a thistle-patch in the Vale of Elah, said that Yah
was only one baal among many. "Does he own the land,"
this man had asked, "in Esdraelon up north, where
corn is as tall as a man and wheat is like a king's gold on
the hills?" Did he own the vast oak forests lying down-
ward from Carmel? No, good heavens, he owned only
these barren and terrible hills of Judea, where goats
searched over the naked stones and a man did well if he
ate enough to lift one foot behind the other.
33
Rain! What a blessing it was! Everything that grew
in this area was dependent on rain. Without it, every-
thing died. Some spots the gods chose as specially their
own, and blessed, and in these by pool and fountain life
was sweet; but other spots they abandoned to the de-
mons, the fields of dead stone, the hot naked mountains,
the volcanic wilderness of arid sands. Still, Ahijah said
that Yah had wandered for generations with his people
in the deserts. That was a strange thing indeed and Ah-
ban was never done with marveling about it.
Like his simple neighbors Ahban was perplexed by
many riddles and sought answers from the lips of Ahijah
or in vain. When winnowing against the afternoon breeze
from the northwest he thanked Yah for it and rejoiced-
and thought of the great wheatfields owned by Bel.
When gathering his stunted olives he uttered thanks-
giving and thought of the luscious fullgrown fruits
around Jericho. When leading his scrawny beasts from
shittimwood to thistle-patch or to stagnant pools, he
blessed his god for his life and his joy, but his thoughts
turned to the cool gushing waters in other lands. If
Yah was so powerful why didn't he bless his land with
greater abundance? Ahijah said it was because of
David's sins.
Turning over on his bed, which was only a sheepskin
on the earth floor, he prepared to rise, for daylight had
come and birds were singing. On another skin close by
lay his wife Mara, and across the small room were their
daughter Zeruah, and their son-in-law Nebat and their
son Jeroboam. Zeruah was blessed with heavenly beauty;
when Solomon passed through Bethlehem in the pre-
vious summer he had stared at her until Zeruah's face
shone like ripened wheat. Ahban had said to his wife:
"If I can judge a man's heart by his face Nebat will
be another Uriah."
"May Yah forbid!"
"The Prince-"
34
"No, ah no!"
When he now sat up and rubbed his eyes, remember-
ing how rains had fallen like blessings on his dreams, he
had no need to dress, for he slept in the coarse sacklike
garment which he wore. This had a hole for his head
and holes for his arms, and fell about to his knees. He
had no sandals, being too poor to afford them, but went
with naked feet over the brambles and thistles, the mud
or stones or hot sands. The soles of his feet were as thick
and tough as wild boarhide. Before leaving the hut he
prostrated himself in prayer, which was thanksgiving
really, facing the east; and when at last, creaking with
arthritis, he struggled to his feet, his glance fell first on
his household god. This was an image of Yah which
stood in a recession in the clay wall. While looking at it
in the dim windowless room, festooned with spidersilk
and carpeted with soft dust, he thought of Ahijah who
had told him that the spirit of Yah resided in the bull.
A small homely bull was his god-image.
Then he spoke to Mara. "Vineyard-" this was a
common term of endearment "I am going to David's
city."
Mara sat up, middleaged and workworn like her mate,
with the record of time and toil and sorrow graven deep
in her features. Her hair like his was streaked with gray.
Zeruah said: "There's trouble in David's city."
"Yes," said her father, "there is always trouble for
sinful people."
"His sons are fighting one another."
"Sons of rebellion !" cried Ahban. "Only the poor are
blessed."
He went outside. Adjoining his house was a small
yard, surrounded with a bramble fence, in which he kept
his few beasts; and when he looked in at them and noted
their expression of alarm Ahban felt that new and
greater misfortunes would soon overwhelm him. Pos-
sibly some of his neighbors had been regarding him with
35
the evil eye of covetousness. More prosperous than they,
he had a clay-hut, whereas most of them lived in tents
and caves. Going sunwise he walked round his house and
yard looking for footprints, for he suspected that demons
had frightened his beasts. Ahban did not know a great
deal about the spirits of evil and doom but he knew that
a jinni was not a phantom; if slain, its carcass was there
for anyone to see, a monstrous and ill-shaped thing cov-
ered with feathers or hair. It had power to change its
shape, to disappear at will, to assume human form, to
employ supernatural forces, and to afflict people with
lunacy, disease and the blind staggers. Ahban had never
seen jinn but he had talked to men who had seen men
who had seen them. . . .
Mara came outside, staring anxiously at her baal. In
the pale morning light she looked more like a jinni than
a woman. Among her people girls were sold when they
were hardly more than children; they were grand-
mothers before they were thirty; they were old, as Mara
was, in middle-age. In their desire to please Yah women
had all the children they could bear and prayed for more.
"Have the demons been here?" asked Mara, peering
at the earth.
"I can see no footprints."
Because this was washday Mara entered the house.
and came out with a few ragged garments and some
wood ashes, which she would use to cleanse them, being
unable to afford "soap," a vegetable alkali mixed with oil.
"If you're going to the city," she said, "you ought to
eat."
Ahban considered her statement while looking round
him at the earth. It was not the custom with his people
to eat a morning meal but Ahban was hungry and his
journey would cover many miles. But when he thought
of the tasteless food in his storage bin, the carobs and
sycomore figs and barley meal enveloped by mold, he
36
decided to go without eating. Mara meanwhile was
shaking garments to dislodge spiders.
"Will Prince Adonijah be the king?"
Ahban was a man with a dull mind, largely because so
many disasters and sorrows had befallen him; and before
answering a question he turned words over and over,
trying to choose those which, when uttered, would offend
neither Yah nor the powers of evil. Mara had forgotten
her question when he came to her and said softly:
"Yah will choose the next king."
"Ahijah says Yah did not choose David."
"Hush," said Ahban. “And while I'm gone, watch
Zeruah."
She knew what he had in mind; several of the princes
had been looking on Zeruah with lustful eyes. "I'll watch
her," she said.
Ahban went out to the wet road leading to David's
city, which was five miles north, and trudged along with
naked feet, his toes rubbing back and forth over the sand
that pushed up between them. He was unhappy and ap-
prehensive. He knew that Yah was angry because of the
sins of David. Such men as Ahijah and Ahban, who were
peasants, and whose knowledge was only the lore of their
people, looked back to the wandering in the desert,
when there had been no arrogant kings and princes and
no rich upon the shoulders of the poor. Then they had
been blood-brothers who shared with one another and
manifested their bond of fellowship when they all sat
to their simple meals. Then, if a brother was slain, they
did not say that his blood was spilled but that our blood
was spilled. But the ancient customs, which Ahijah and
Ahban loved, were being destroyed, or in any case adul-
terated, by abominable ways in Canaan.
As Ahban walked he kept looking round him or
glancing up at the sky and now and then for his pro-
tection he uttered a holy word. Like all the simple peas-
ants he was constantly harassed by evil spirits. He knew,
37
of course, that an evil spirit could be frightened, pro-
pitiated, bribed or deceived-but Ahban was too clumsy
to deceive, too poor to propitiate or bribe and too power-
less to frighten. His only protection against things of
evil lay in a pure life; Ahijah had said that wickedness
could not penetrate the armor of a pure man. "Be pure
in heart," Ahijah had told him, "and at twilight blow
the ram's horn." The ram's horn was blown at this hour
because it was then that the creatures of mischief
swarmed out of hiding, to spend the hours of darkness
in malice and evil. Besides the horn, Ahban's only weap-
ons against them were fire and holiness.
"I must be back before dark," he was thinking, and
tried to quicken his pace, though his legs were stiff and
aching. To be out of his house after dark almost par-
alyzed Ahban with fear. He could wear amulets or recite
magical numbers or invoke holy names or carry a torch,
or, when fasting, he could spit three times before him. But
he thought it safer never to venture out. Mara was
bolder. Sometimes she neglected to strike a vessel before
drinking out of it, or a door before opening it, though
she knew well enough that if she did not knock on things
she would not frighten lurking evil away. Ahijah had
told Ahban that when he felt in danger a very simple
way to protect himself was to clasp the left thumb with
the fingers of the right hand: all forms of evil quailed
before the emblems of fertility. Some of Ahban's neigh-
bors had tried to bribe the evil powers, as when, in
erecting a building, they buried something, usually an
infant alive or dead, in its walls. Ahban preferred the
magic in words; and while limping toward the city he
kept murmuring, "Yah who is shaddai-shaddai-
shaddai...
"9
Entering the city from the south he looked down on

large building which David had taken as his own. Under
38
the leaden sky and the persistent rain it was not a pleasant

"What is the trouble?"

"Hah! Do we have two kings?"

Ahban was looking round him at his tribal brothers.

mon!" but there was no echo. King Solomon had begun
his reign by hurling his brother from the holy place, and
there were some in the horrified multitude who saw in
the bloody deed a portent of things to come.
As for Ahban, he was sick in his bowels and deeply
shaken by violent tremors. He hastened away, with his
naked feet making no sound on the wet earth. Unwilling
to meet the gaze of any of his brothers he went up the
hill and in him was a wild voice crying over and over,

wicked king. Israel had a king so abominably vile and
wicked that he not only slew his brother, an act that called
for blood vengeance under the tribal laws of his people,
but he committed the awful crime upon the holiest spot
of soil in all of Israel.
"Ahijah, come!" he whispered. "Come, please come!"

THE NEXT MORNING King Solomon, robed in Egyptian
linen and drinking a sweet spiced wine, was feeling de-
pressed and anxious. He was a man of moods and his
moods faithfully followed the moon's cycle: when the
moon was old and full he felt low in spirit, but was
aroused to more than human vigor when the crescent an-
nounced the new month. While sipping his wine and wait-
ing for his mother he heard a guard saying that Queen
Naamah begged to see him.
43
"Send her in."
Naamah, mother of Rehoboam, entered and made a
little gesture of respect but she was not a woman to bow
before her baal, with forehead on the earth, as was the
way with some wives. Her gesture like her spirit was cold,
incisive and opaque; she made it and sank to a pile of
cushions and fixed her hard black eyes on Solomon's face.
"My Lord, why haven't you killed Joab? Will you
wait until he rouses the host against you? And there is
Abiathar."
Solomon was looking at her, despising her, wondering
if he ought to have her strangled before she gave him
trouble. She was the oldest of his wives, older than he,
who was in his middle twenties; and because her teeth
were rotting her breath was foul and because she already
had several children she was painfully unattractive. He
hated the sight of her.
Naamah looked at him and waited, in her mind only
one thought, hard and cold like the woman herself: if she
were to be as successful with intrigue as Bathsheba had
been, her oldest son would succeed his father and she
would be queen mother. Possibly Solomon would not live
very long. He might have an accident or a servant might
poison him or one of his brothers might drive a dagger
into his side. Did Solomon see these thoughts in her face?
"Go on," he said. "I don't want to be bothered with
you now."
"If you will only destroy your enemies-"
"If I destroyed all my enemies I would hang you. Now
go!"
He did not see the light in her eyes when she left and
if he had seen it he would not have cared. He intended
to make himself secure from poison and dagger. When
Bathsheba came he said:
"There is Joab. I guess I'd better have him slain."
"And Abiathar."
44
"I'm going to make Zadok the high priest," he said.
"He says the god Shamash gave a code of laws to some
ancient king and that Moses gave a code to Israel. He
wants to gather all of them and have them recorded."
When Bathsheba talked alone with her son she did not
fall into those evasions and subterfuges which were be-
lieved to protect persons from evil powers. She said:
"Your father is very ill."
"I know it."
"He wants us to bring the trees of life and set them
around him in his chamber."
Solomon looked at his mother across his wine-cup.
"What if he got well?"
"I was thinking that."
"I'll have some trees brought in. Abishag hasn't done
him any good."
"No, none at all." She gave her son a strange bitter
smile. "You didn't think she would, did you?"
"We didn't. Well, have your spies told you anything?"
"Nothing new. But I wouldn't wait about Joab. I'd go
see your father now."
When Solomon entered his father's chamber he found
several persons there and ordered them to leave. Then
he sat by David and said:
"I must talk to you alone. I need your advice."
The last statement was pure hypocrisy and David knew
it. He looked at his son from under his lids and in a voice
that seemed to have gained in strength he said: "You're a
wise king. What troubles can you have?"
Solomon ignored the malice. "Joab. He must die."
"Ah, Joab!" David shuddered. "He has been a thorn
in my foot all these years. How many times has his girdle
been wet? Absalom, your brother, Absalom, my son,
would now be king if Joab had not slain him. It was
Joab-" He paused, exhausted by his memories.
Solomon said again: "Joab must die."
45
"Is he conspiring against you?"
"Shall I wait till conspirators show their daggers?
Didn't he pledge himself to Adonijah ?"
"Then go, bring him down to his grave with blood on
his white hair."
Solomon went to the door and told a guard to summon

my brothers or banish them ?"
"Brothers strive against brothers, sons against fath-
ers."
Said Solomon drily: "And gods against gods."
"So it is written. Well, as is the way with all things of
the earth, I'll soon go down into darkness. I
pray Yah
that your reign will be one of peace and that you will
leave a strong kingdom to the son who inherits you. Our
people are proud and wilful, their blood is hot. You must
bring them together. You must unite them in a kingdom
that can stand against the enemies on the south, on the

"You spent your life in war. Why imagine that peace

"I fought only when Yah commanded me to fight."
"Did he command you to fight against Saul? No, you
were ambitious."
David was looking at his son. "The evil I have done
will live after me if you're not a wise king. Our people
must have peace. They hate, they are bitter; you must

sheaf, for Yah is a mighty one and our people will be
great in the earth."
"You've been listening to Ahijah."
"Ahijah is a seer. He speaks for Yah."
"I feel in my bones," said Solomon, "that Ahijah will
cause me trouble. Should I have him slain ?"
"Yah's mouthpiece!" David was aghast. "My son, you
speak like a Philistine!"
46
"And you, my father, speak like a goatherd from the

"My Lord, I will."

Returning to his father Solomon asked: "What were

suppose also you would have me spare the life of the trai-
tor Abiathar. Is that so?"
The gentleness left David's face. He closed his eyes
tight and seemed to be suffering intense pain. A far more
devout man than his son, he had always stood in awe be-
fore the seers and priests, and that reverence had deep-
ened in his later years. In the days of his power he might
have deposed a priest but he would never have slain him.
As for Solomon, his mind was looking out to other king-
doms and other peoples and customs, and to other gods.
Privately, he doubted that Yah was the mightiest of all
the gods; he could see no evidence to support that belief.
He could see a great deal of evidence against it. Though
only a young man, he was far more sophisticated than his
father, more ironic, more imaginative, and far subtler.
The Hittites had once been a great and civilized nation
in this part of the world and had even disputed with the
I
47
mighty Egypt. Solomon's mother was a Hittite and he
never forgot it.
With a grave face masking his inner mirth, Solomon

Hebron. He has never forgiven you and he has worked
evil against you. But why did Yah let him overcome
you ?"
David, sensing the mischief and malice, was staring at
his son between half-closed lids.
"You spurned the Baal's city when you moved to this

"Don't worry about the Baal of Hebron. Our Yah is

"Saul didn't think so."
"Saul was a son of folly."
"Saul named a son Ishbaal and another Meribaal. They

"Saul was wicked."
"If Yah is mightier than all the gods and loves Israel,

"What?" asked David, opening his eyes.

"It is known all over Israel. Furthermore," said Solo-

"Ahijah-whom you call Yah's spokesman-says that
for your wickedness I'll be cast down, for I am the fruit
of your sin." He saw the old man wince. "Is that true?"
"I have sinned," said David humbly.
"Well, tell me this: am I, the fruit of your sin, and now
48
King of Israel, to allow this Ahijah to rouse the people
against me?"
"What he does he does for Yah."
"You think Yah is so mighty that he gave false gods
to all other people, to punish them for their wickedness.
You think he is mightier than the great gods of Egypt or
Babylon. You think it because Ahijah says it."
"It is Yah who says it."
"If I say I'm the mightiest among kings, does that
make it so? My father, you have been a mighty warrior,
but as a king you've always been a goatherd. I intend to
be much more. This will shock you, but I intend to learn
to speak the Pharaoh's language. This will outrage you,
but I intend to bring to Israel the learning and art and
splendors of the nations around us. You would counsel
me always to live in a tent, as Saul did, as you have done;
but I intend to live in a great palace. You would counsel
me to eat goatcurd and barley broth but I intend to dine
on the choicest foods in the world."
Solomon's gaze swept the barren chamber. He went
on: "I don't understand how you can believe as you do.
You say that Yah is the mightiest, but not only that! You
say he is the only true god. Yet look at you. You've lived
like a shepherd in a tent, sucking the juice from figs and
the milk from goats and the honey from rocks, while
other kings live in magnificence. You have only coarse
blankets to lie on; the Pharaoh sleeps on the finest linen.
You eat goatcurd but he feasts on fatted fowl. While our
people eat locust beans and juniper roots, on the Eu-
phrates even the poorest have milk and honey. Why is all
that? Tell me. Is our god so powerful only to make us
the world's beggars? Are we the children of the Almighty
only to be cast down and trod upon?"
"If you will listen-"
"Why should I listen? I know what you would say. It
is what the lunatic Ahijah says. It is what all our people
say who still look back to our wanderings in the desert as
49
a blessed time, and hope that we'll always keep our cus-
toms of the shepherd. Well, not King Solomon. Let those
live in tents who like them. If our god is the mightiest of
the gods, I'll live in splendor as befits his rank. If he lets
me be cast down, then your words are a lie in your mouth.
If our family is greater than Egypt's or Babylon's or
Tyre's, it's time that we took our place in magnificence
above them. But look at you, drinking your cheap wine.
from a clay jug, eating your cheap food from an earthen
vessel, clothing your body with goathair, riding on an
ass instead of in a great chariot emblazoned with gold
and rubies-"
"My son, listen to me."
"No. You listen to me. I want you to know the kind of
king I'm going to be. I want you to know the kind of king-
dom I'm going to build. Don't you rejoice in my plans?"
"No, my son. We're a people-"
"Never mind that. I know what kind of people we've
been-wanderers in a wilderness, our eyes and hair filled
with sand, our tents pitched by the stagnant and stinking
pools. Look at this cheap pile of mudbricks you are living
in! Oh, I don't wish to be ungrateful. I know that all your
life you have been fighting to wrest this land from our
enemies, and still haven't entirely done so, for Gezer
stands there and defies us. Look at this city which you
would tell me is Yah's city-a place of mud and mudhuts,
tents, broken walls, filthy pathways, and roofs through
which Yah pours his rain on floors that need no rain! If
when the northwest wind blows you go to the southeast,
almost to the end of the wilderness you can still smell
this place. The smell of it is deep in the cisterns. Does it
have any pools, gardens, fountains and arbors? Do these
people of the false gods wear rags or fine linen, drink
from mud-jugs or from golden goblets, wear goatskin
or robes of gold edged with scarlet? Answer me, my
father!"
"If you will listen-"
50
"My father! My father, a son of valor, but still a goat-
herd! Once your nostrils burned, but now your heart
sinks to the ashes. Once you bent the great bow, but now
you can't rise to an elbow or turn over. Why has Yah al-

so little, you would say we have what is more precious

sparkling wine and the golden goblet and Osiris. We may
fool ourselves with such childishness as yours and Ahi-

have the gall to rise up in your cheap garments and stand

there praying among the lizards, might say it."
"Where," asked David, aghast, "have you picked up
this abominable wickedness?"
There was a knock on the door and Solomon went over
and opened it. Benaiah was there. Solomon stepped out-
side and waved the guards back.
"Well ?"
"My Lord, he is no more. The dog is dead."
"Have him buried outside the city. Where's Abia-

"Clinging to the altar."
"Wait here."
Solomon entered and closed the door and went over
to his father. "Now for Abiathar-"
"My son, he's the highest priest."
"I'm the highest priest. Zadok is next to me."
"Don't kill him," said David, but his voice was weak.
His son's heresies had exhausted his strength.
"What'll I do with him?"
51
"Banish him." David was fond of this man. When he

had faced the dangers with him. Even more, when Saul

The slaying of Joab had poured David's gall on the

ing that in a prone position he commanded no respect.
When he failed he made a strangled sound of protest.
Solomon had watched the futile efforts to rise and saw
David's haggard face burst with the sweat of exhaustion.
He went to the door and said to Benaiah:
"Bring him here."
"My Lord, he won't come."
David wore a ring, the signet of which he used as the
state seal. Solomon took the ring from his father's hand
and gave it to Benaiah, saying: "Show him this and he
will come."
Wet with the moisture of terror Abiathar came in,

"My old friend, why did you go with Adonijah?"
"David-"
"Didn't you
know Solomon was to be king?"

As though fixed between his old loyalty to David and

ments, yet did not lift his eyes, but shuffled about blindly

"Have you a house in Anathoth?"
"Yes, my Lord."
"And friends there?"
"All my friends are gone but one."
"And that one," said David, "is now enfolded by the

After Benaiah and the priest had left the room Solo-

my Rock and my high place above the wilderness, my
