HIS LAST STORMY SCENE with Solomon had brought Da-
vid close to death but for nothing in the world would those
permitted to look at him utter the word death or imply
with word or gesture that death was near. Sickness was
the work of evil supernatural powers and death was their
triumph. The earth and the sun renewed themselves, the
gods lived forever, but for some mysterious reason man
was struck down. Only his tragic shade survived him and
this wandered desolately in the underworld.
Remembering that David had expressed a wish for
the trees of life Bathsheba had his chamber filled with
willow, cedar, olive and grapevine; but one morning,
when the season of cold was drawing to its end and the
rains were abating she entered her husband's room and
knew that he was dead. She went to the bedside and for
a little while looked at him. He had placed his hands on
his chest with the left fingers clasping the right thumb.
His eyes were closed. There was no sign that he had died
in pain; his wasted features were calm and a strange deep
peace filled the place, as though evil, having accomplished
its purpose, had withdrawn, taking with it its offensive
odors. The cedar poles stood firm, with phallic amulets
hanging from their branches. The lamps still burned.
Looking round her quietly, Bathsheba observed these
things. She was moved almost to tears, and with a ges-
ture of infinite tenderness she bowed low to David and
whispered his name. She did not touch him, for then she
54
would have been unclean. He now belonged to Yah and
was therefore sacred. And all the while she was thinking
of her duties. Waiting until a sense of these filled her
with suffocation she screamed, and ran screaming from
the building, her hands rending her clothes and flesh.
Other women heard her cries and began to wail. The
etiquette of mourning and burial had been established by
ancient rules which were faithfully observed. Like all
Orientals, these people took fullest advantage of oppor-
tunities for self-display. A few minutes after her wild
cries had announced David's departure the city was a
bedlam, for it was believed that the emotions of sorrow
were completely beyond human control. Men who had
been laboring to repair the walls now came forward to
show their grief, smiting their thighs and exclaiming; and
women everywhere burst forth with shrill weeping. But
it was the wives who were expected to show the most in-
consolable sorrow.
At once they had the forepart of their heads shaved
because hair like blood was vital with the life-principle.
Bathsheba came before the house with a part of her hair
shorn and the remainder sprinkled with wood ashes. She
had put a haircloth girdle next to her skin and over it a
black mourning garment which would hide her from Da-
vid's ghost. Other wives hastened to do likewise. All the
male relatives who wore beards shaved them off. Women
who were not wives or daughters did not shear their hair
but loosed it in tumbling masses, making it look wildly
disordered. These women marched round and round with
one hand on their head and they kept up a continuous
wailing that was so unearthly in its sadness that nobody
who heard it could remain unmoved. Some of the men
were chanting:
"Alas, my brother! Alas, he has gone away into the
west!"
There was one, outwardly unmoved, who stood back
in shadow, unobserved but alertly observant. This was
55
King Solomon. He had withdrawn to watch his people
and think about them. For no reason that he could have
imagined he was thinking of a story Nathan had told
him about a Syrian woman who, in the tenth month of her
pregnancy, had burst open and delivered a god-child. He
wondered if all the gods had been born that way. He
knew of course that there were many gods and that each
owned a part of the earth, which was his house. Assyria
was the house of Asshur, Tyre of Melkarth, Egypt of
Osiris. But did Israel really belong to Yah?....
When Bathsheba sought him,out he saw the ashes in
her hair and a few drops of blood on her face. He said,
speaking in a low voice:
"Most of the people loved David. You should show
more grief."
"How? What shall I do?"
"Can't you get some blood somewhere?-and then
smear yourself. They'll think you have gouged yourself
and made many wounds."
"Yes," she said, considering the matter. "Yes, I'll do
that."
Though David had sinned it was true that he had been
popular with his people. He had been a brave and re-
sourceful leader. Solomon hoped that he could be even
half as popular as his father had been. While thinking of
this matter he saw one of David's oldest wives wildly
gouging her flesh and bleeding from many wounds. That
was good. A little later Bathsheba appeared and she was
a gory sight from head to feet. She had thrown blood
over her hair and garment and over her arms. Her hands
were red.
While looking out at the women Solomon wondered.
with what stratagem he could circumvent Ahijah and then
it occurred to him that he might do it by building for Yah
a handsome house, a temple, in the capital city. All the
more powerful gods, he had heard, had magnificent
houses. Possibly Yah was angry for having been shifted
56
like a vagrant from Hebron to Jebus, or for being car-
ried around in an ark. Solomon would build a great house
for him of aromatic cedar and costly stones and bronze
and around it there would be lovely gardens and foun-
tains and flowers, so that the savor of sweet things would
be with the deity constantly. There would be a huge and
splendid altar. There would be many offerings by fire, be-
cause Yah was a celestial god and enjoyed not only flames
but the odors of burning flesh. What would Ahijah say to
that? What would he say when Jerusalem, instead of be-
ing an ill-smelling sheepcote on desolate hills, was a lush
and fragrant woodland in the midst of the living waters?
When Bathsheba came by him, wailing and rending,
he beckoned to her and she moved over and hid with him
in shadow.
"I think I've figured out how to handle Ahijah. I'll
build a great house for Yah and put fountains and gar-
dens around it."
Bathsheba looked as though she had been rubbed in a
fresh carcass. "Of course," she said, after considering.
"Then he could not call you wicked. The people would
be proud to see their god in a splendid house."
"So it seems to me. Maybe I can give him a house as
fine as Bel's."
"I see no reason why you cannot."
"Nathan says Yah is a god of the desert and the arid
places, but he also said that Yah likes my city. I will
please him with many splendors."
"Of course," said Bathsheba, and returned to her
mourning.
It was Solomon whose tastes ran to riches and splen-
dors. He liked northern Israel where the hills and valleys
blossomed under abundant waters. Was Yah a sky-baal,
a lord of rains? Or was he like the solitary tree that grew
by the lonely watercourse? Nathan seemed to think-and
Ahijah even more-that he was an ascetic and austere
deity. Upon this question Solomon's people were divided;
57
to this question he knew that he must find an answer. For
centuries Yah had lived under the hot and angry desert
sky but Solomon believed that he would prefer soft splen-
dors and voluptuous delights. He himself intended to be
a worldly king, or as worldly in any case as he could be.
He saw Zadok, the highest priest, go by, a tall dark
man whose mouth was completely hidden by a black
beard. Zadok had many duties in a time like this. The
site of the grave had to be wisely chosen; the good were
not buried with the wicked; ghosts liked congenial com-
pany after death, preferably those who, in life, had had
about the same rank, tastes and interests. Quarrels were
not extinguished at the grave; to have buried two feudists.
side by side would have been to perpetuate their struggle
to the end of time. It would have been madness to bury
David close by Saul, or even close by his son Absalom.
The corpse had to be carefully prepared or its ghost
would not live in peace. It was bathed in salt water and
smeared with salt and in still other ways made immune
to the attacks of evil spirits. The fingers of the hands
were closed in fists to frighten demons away. Of all
forms of magic which promoted the welfare of the
living and the comfort of the dead none was more power-
ful than words; and so it was that Zadok brought the
wives to the body and told them to chant the formulas
that drove away evil and placated the ghost. A ghost,
as David had learned from Saul's, was lonely and dis-
consolate, malicious and vindictive, if his sins were not
forgotten and his virtues extolled.
Zadok had to be sure that the corpse left the build-
ing ahead of the mourners; that men and women on the
way to the grave walked apart, because evil was partial
to women and their presence was dangerous. For those
who were not wildly lamented the priest had to hire pro-
fessional mourners. There was no need in David's case
to do that. When his body was brought outside and borne
toward the grave the sounds of grief were ear-splitting,
58
and the behavior of some of the persons approached close
to madness. Rushing up to the corpse some of the wives
leaned over it, wringing their hands and screaming, and
asking in choked sobbing whispers why he had left his
people and what man anywhere was able enough to dis-
charge his great duties. They begged forgiveness for
having been imperfect and selfish wives. They were so
clamorous and frantic that those bearing the body were
almost thrown down. The wives tore their hair and
clothes, and some of them tried to rend their breasts;
and the people, seeing their frenzies, redoubled their
outcry. Because the paroxysms of the wives could not
be long sustained, other mourners screamed with re-
newed furies when the wailing of the women abated.
During such lulls in their frenzy the wives chanted
among themselves, reciting the virtues of their departed
baal and beseeching him to forgive them.
Never had Solomon seen emotions so wild and violent.
He still stood back in shadow, thinking about his people.
As the moment of interment drew nearer the lamenta-
tions became tumultuous and defiant. Now a few of the
wives, who were really beside themselves in the fury of
their passions, tried to prevent the placing of the body
in its tomb. They flung themselves on the men, clutch-
ing and screaming and rending; and in the awful din the
voice of Zadok crying for help was heard by none.
Women had attacked him with fingernails and probes
and his face was bleeding. Nathan, haggard and spent
and barely able to totter, was also smeared with his own.
blood. The men by the tomb hurled the women back,
pushing and shoving, and sometimes striking them; but
the women gathered themselves and charged again, their
mouths babbling insanely while their hands tried to de-
stroy. Never had Nathan seen anything like this, though
he had stood by hundreds of graves. The people from
the desert, proud of their better way of life and eager
for a glorious future, were burying their mighty mon-
59
arch, and they behaved as though all their hot passions
had been poured into the heat of this hour. For it was
true that men as well as women now lost control over
their emotions, and shrieked out of such dreadful insani-
ties that Zadok broke. away from the fighting mass and
ran to find his King.
When Solomon saw him coming he looked over where
Benaiah stood and beckoned; and when Benaiah came
he said:
"Take some of your men and go carry the wildest ones
away."
A little later there was a strange expression on Solo-
mon's face when a big man passed him, carrying one
of the old wives, with scarcely a rag on her, her face un-
sightly with wounds and her body red with gore. The
demented were borne away and those by the tomb took
up an antidemonic song:
Our Rock is in the walls around the high places!
We shall not be afraid of the night's terrors
nor the day's demons
nor of the pestilence that comes out of the swamps
nor of the famine in the rainless places
nor of the desolate lairs of evil!
When the singing ceased, some came forward to drop
a lock of their hair into the grave, and there were a few
who had a lock of David's hair, a priceless treasure, for
when the hair of the living and the dead mingled there
was established between them a permanent bond. As
Bathsheba laid a lock of David's hair upon a lock of her
own she felt that his ghost would always be her friend.
As soon as Zadok had the burial under control Solo-
mon went to his chambers and sent for Ahiah, his scribe.
Ahiah, and his brother Elihoreph, sons of Shisha, had
studied in Egypt and were learned men. Ahiah was an
ill-favored creature, with malformed feet, shoulders
round and stooped, a misshapen head and a sparse
60
stubble of coarse black beard. But this ugly gnomelike
man had a brilliant mind. He spoke Egyptian perfectly
and a smattering of various dialects.
When he came in, looking more like a monster than
a man, Solomon regarded him for a few moments with
undisguised amazement; and Ahiah stood like a wretch
summoned to his death, his gaze cast down, his hands
trembling at the end of grotesquely long arms.
"Ahiah, I have many things to do and not much time
in which to do them. I must destroy Gezer. I must fortify
my frontiers-Hazor, Tamar, Baalath, the Beth-
horons. I must station my military forces so that they
can put down internal revolts quickly. I'm going to learn
the Pharaoh's language and I want you to teach me, for
we shall be going to Egypt. Did you bring your writing
materials?"
"No, my Lord."
"Go get them. Hasten."
Gezer was an ancient city on the edge of the Philis-
tine Plain and was still in Philistine hands. David had
never been able to capture and subdue it, and Solomon
knew that to destroy it he would need the help of
Egyptian engineers.
When Ahiah returned his King said: "In comparison
to the Egyptians we must be pretty crude. Isn't it so?"
"Oh, my Lord-"
"Don't lie to me. We'll not always be so crude. I'm
going to have many splendors."
"Yes, my Lord."
"As my scribe you can make a record of all the mag-
nificent things I do. Will you like that?"
"Yes, my Lord and King."
"Ahiah, look at me." When with an effort Ahiah
raised his head and looked on his King, Solomon grinned
broadly and said: "You may look at your King, Ahiah.
I'll not eat you. Well, I want to send a message to that
lecherous old goat, the Pharaoh. Can he read?"
61
"My Lord, I don't know." Ahiah was sitting with his
writing materials before him. Some people wrote by
perforating leaves or other soft materials with symbols
but Ahiah had learned a better way; with an iron pencil
he scratched the characters on wood or stone or hard
clay, and then, to improve the appearance and make his
script more readable and permanent, he filled the de-
pressions with melted lead. He now had before him his
pencil and two small limestone slabs.
Solomon began to dictate: "To the Lord of Lords,
King of Kings, Sun of Suns-" He broke off, saying:
"No, I don't like that."
"Your salutation, my Lord, was very flattering," said
Ahiah quietly.
"I felt that it was too flattering," said Solomon, who
had never written a letter. How much deference ought
the King of Israel show to Egypt's Pharaoh? The Phar-
aoh was, of course, a mightier monarch by far-but
how much mightier? Even more, what was his own opin-
ion of himself?
"To the King of Egypt, my brother, I write as fol-
lows: I am well, all the signs favor me, the gods are
pleased-" He broke off, observing that Ahiah was not
scratching on the stone. "Why don't you record what I
say?"
"My Lord, I do not presume to advise my King but
are you seeking a favor?"
"Well, yes, I suppose. I'm making a request."
"If you seek a favor would it be well to address him
in more flattering terms? He is very vain, being unlike
our great and humble King."
Solomon put a hand to his mouth to hide a smile but
the scribe was not looking at him. He liked Ahiah. He
perceived that the man had quality. "Should I salute him
as the Sun of Suns?"
"That would please him," said Ahiah in his quiet way.
62
"The Pharaoh bows to the sun. Indeed, as I understand
these people, it is believed that he is the sun."
"How could that be?" asked Solomon, furrowing his
brow. "Well, is he the sun?"
"I think he is only a king, my Lord, and when I saw
him last he was pretty pompous. I would not call myself
his brother. Our god is Yah, not the sun nor the moon,
and the King of Egypt is not our brother."
"I perceive that I'm not so sure of some matters as
you seem to be. Very well, will this please him? At the
feet of the King, the Sun of Suns, the Baal of the Sky,
seven and seven times I prostrate myself on my back and
belly-"
"My Lord, I would not advise you, who are the
greatest of all the kings; but if you are seeking a favor
I think you should flatter him, indicating his rank and
powers, and even exaggerating these a little. But you
should not prostrate yourself, for that would manifest
the position of an inferior."
"Don't you think I'm inferior?"
"No, my Lord. Even if it were so you should not
admit it. Most kings, being unlike our Lord, are quick
to take advantage of one another."
Yes indeed, Solomon liked this queer little man. He
understood that Ahiah would be of much use to him.
"How would you say it?"
"I would address him this way. To the King of Egypt,
the Lord of Lords and Sun of Suns, I send greetings:
I am well, with my wives, children, houses, chariots, and
may it be well with you, and your wives, children, houses,
chariots. Would my Lord accept that?" asked Ahiah,
looking up shyly.
"Excellent, Ahiah! Proceed."
"Can you send him a present that would please him?
I believe it is customary for kings to exchange gifts."
"Hah, a gift!" Solomon thought of his meager pos-
sessions: where among them was an object priceless
63
enough to please the Pharaoh, who had so much of his
own? "What do I have?" he asked, and shrugged. And
with cruel irony he added: "Shall I send him a goat-
hair tent or one of my father's old women?"
Pretending to have missed the bitterness, Ahiah said.
quietly: "We must bear in mind what he has much of,
and what you have that he hasn't, yet may covet. It
would not be fruits," Ahiah went on, who for his time
was a learned man, "for though Yah blesses you with
plenty of plenties, the Sun of Suns has excellent fruits
and fine wines and raiment, many horses and chariots
and much gold. He has costly vessels and furnishings and
precious stones."
Feeling envious and poor Solomon cried: "By all the
gods what do I have? Nothing that he would covet!"
And with that cruel humor which was one of his most
striking traits, he asked: "Shall I send him Ahijah, the
seer? Possibly he needs a prophet. Or shall I send him
Abishag?"
"My Lord, if I may say so, he has his choice of all the
beautiful women in his provinces-"
"Of course he has, the old goat. Well—”
"If I am not too bold-"
"Ahiah, you humorless owl, do you know what I have
in mind? to ask him for a daughter or sister. I have
one of King Hiram's daughters and my relations with
Tyre are blessed. Are the women of Egypt beautiful?"
"They are not like Israel's women."
"Well, no matter. I have heard that he needs stallions.
His horses are poor and feeble beasts, hardly able to
carry a child. I could send him a fine stallion from the
north."
"Or three perhaps," said Ahiah.
"Of course, three. Say, then, I will send the Sun of
Suns as a token of my esteem three fine stallions from
the north country. Or," said Solomon, waving a hand,
"you put it in words that will please him."
64
For a little while Ahiah was busy scratching. Then
gravely he said: "You have a request to make."
"Tell my brother-but you say he isn't my brother?"
"We've been chosen to be Yah's people and he is
the mightiest of all the gods."
"Who thinks that besides you?"
"David, your illustrious father, said so, and Ahijah,
the holy seer from the Mount, does say so, my Lord."
"I've thought sometimes that all holy men are im-
postors. That is a thought which I'd like you to con-
sider, for you are a learned man. I've heard that the
Egyptians say that people are heavily laden asses and
the scribe is the driver. He sits at the King's table and
his belly is as filled with food as his heart with wisdom.
Is that true?"
Ahiah looked up with gentle amusement in his large
bright eyes. "It is our Lord the King whose heart is
filled with wisdom."
"Let us try to believe it," said Solomon, and made
a face.
"Has our Lord heard what are the four most intoler-
able things in the world?"
"A stupid woman must be one."
"That is true. And the other three are a servant who
reigns as a king, a fool whose belly is filled, and a female
servant who inherits the property of her mistress."
"Fine!" cried Solomon, delighted. "Is your brother
as wise as you?"
"My brother is handsome, as I am not, and his live-
liest interest, I am grieved to say, is women, who seem
to be wise, yet are not, for Yah has never blessed a
woman with wisdom."
"I wonder why. Ahiah, do you know?"
"Women, my Lord, were created inferior to men."
"Do the Egyptians think so? Those people are much
wiser than our people. For ages they had splendors and
triumphs when our people were herding their goats.
65
Well, tell this Sun of Suns that a place called Gezer is a
thorn in my foot-an abominable and impious and loath-
some people who must be destroyed. Tell him I need a
few engineers. Aren't Egyptian engineers the best in
the world?"
"Yes, my Lord. Their buildings are magnificent."
"Then tell him I'll defend his northern frontier against
invasion, even against the Assyrians. Tell him-but you
say it."
of
"Yes, my Lord," said Ahiah, busily scratching.
Dreaming of sumptuous banquets and vintaged wines,
power and glory, Solomon left the tent and almost at
once his gaze fell on a beautiful woman. He thought he
had seen her before. She was going down a crooked path
and at the end of it she paused and looked back. Solomon
summoned a guard and said:
"Go to that woman and learn her name and where
she lives."
Then, accompanied by a bodyguard, he climbed to an
eminence to view his capital city. He did not like what
he saw. He gazed down on an aggregation of small,
wretched, ill-smelling huts, separated by crooked lanes
and interspersed with tents. Each house had an opening
in its roof for light and air, and a crude door that swung
outward, with a bar to make it fast. The roof was of
beams laid from wall to wall, covered with branches,
twigs, chaff, straw and clay, the whole of it leveled and
hardened with a roller. There were a few houses with up-
per chambers but they were so offensive in their odors.
that most of the royal women preferred to live in tents.
The Jebusites had chosen to build their city on two hills
that could be defended and David had had a dreadful
time defeating them. These hills had a deep natural bar-
rier on the east and another on the west. Though not, like
his father, a man of military genius, Solomon understood
that the site had many advantages: invasion from the
north would be difficult, from the east impossible; the
66
city could be approached by an armed force only from the
west or southwest.
Solomon looked into the north, wondering where in
the blue haze Tyre stood, where Damascus. The dreaded
Assyrian power had paused on the threshold of its
neighbors, and was now held at bay chiefly by the Phoe-
nicians. With the Assyrians Solomon wanted no trouble.
They were cruel and bloodthirsty and utterly merciless;
they delighted in immense slaughters; and so proud were
they of their butcheries that they made records of them
and of their methods of torture, cut in hard stone. Their
god Asshur had as his symbol a tree of life which Assyri-
ans decorated and worshiped at the spring equinox; and
this god, and his equally cruel consort, Ishtar, loved to
take their people on campaigns of the most barbarous
destruction and enslavement, bearing their trees of life
in triumph before them. They had terrible weapons of
iron. Wherever they had gone they had left desolation
and ruin; and around the smoking heaps of towns tall
stakes on which had been impaled alive the flayed bodies
of captured princes and kings. The butchered captives
they piled up like cordwood.
Engraved on their statues, Solomon had heard, were
their boasts and one he remembered: "I have crushed
man and beast, and built my monument of human
corpses. Of my captives, I cut off the hands, I skinned
them alive, I tore out their tongues, I gouged out their
eyes while I held them in place with a chain passed
through their lips."
Solomon shuddered merely to think of these people-
and other kings were shuddering. After impaling and
torturing and butchering, these Assyrians razed the cities
and covered the ruins with salt. "I then flayed the chief
men and covered the posts with their skins; I suspended
their dead bodies from these same pillars; I impaled
others on the summit of the pillars; and I ranged others
on stakes around the pillars. ..."
67
Such were their records. The Persians in the far north
he was also afraid of; he had heard that no other people
were so ingenious in combining luxury and licentiousness
with appalling forms of torture. The Babylonians on the
other hand were quiet and unwarlike; and from the
Egyptians he would expect no trouble if the Pharaoh
would give him a daughter. As for the small feuding
tribes on his borders, he did not intend to fight them in-
cessantly, as David had done, but to marry a princess of
each and cultivate their good will.
He came down from the eminence and the guard ap-
proached him.
"Yes? Did you learn her name?"
"Zeruah, my Lord, the daughter of Ahban. She lives
in Bethlehem."
"Does she have a baal?"
"Yes, my Lord. His name is Nebat."
"Go."
Solomon went to his tents, reflecting that he must
move into the building which David had occupied. He
called for spiced wine, and before drinking poured out
a little and bade the servant drink. After waiting to see
if the servant would die from poison he filled his cup
and tinkled a bell, and another servant appeared, who
served as his valet. He told him to prepare his bed, by
which he meant to drench it with sweet essences and
pile it deep with cushions. Then he summoned a female
servant and told her to prepare Abishag to come to him.
Bathsheba had laden a table with such delicacies as
the land afforded. These included almonds and pistachio
nuts, cheese, honeycake, figs and dates and raisins, and
various fruit juices, inspissated by boiling, which his
people called honey. Of wines there was a sweet one
from Lebanon, famous for its fragrance; a sour wine
that foamed when stirred; a kind of cider made of
quinces and sharpened with lupine; another wine made
of bee-honey, grapes, skirret and pepper; and an insipid
68
beer. Thinking that these might have been poisoned,
Solomon had them taken away.
He did not bathe, though the city's cisterns and pools
had been filled with rain. He did rub sweet odors over
his face and body before going to his bed. When Abishag
appeared, thrust forward by someone behind her, Solo-
mon saw that she was gasping with fright. He arose
and went toward her, softly, his face smiling.
"My dear girl, are you afraid of me?"
"Yes!" she gasped.
When she seemed to be on the point of swooning he
took both her arms and led her to the bed. A voluptuary,
he took his time with food, drink and women, savoring
them fully, exploring their essences and subtleties and
moods. He would take his time with this frightened
child who had collapsed in his arms. Drawing her to a
sitting posture on the bed he offered her wine and sweet-
cakes; he was gentle and patient with her, coaxing her
to eat and drink and at last to smile. He would spend
most of the night with her, or possibly all of it, feasting
on her loveliness and spending from his own ardent body
his hunger and a part of his loneliness.
Toward morning he sent the girl from him and lay
on his soft cushions, with a linen sheet drawn carelessly
over him. He was staring at the darkness of the goat-
hair ceiling. When voluptuously exhausted and relaxed,
yet unable to put away his problems and dreams, he
liked to lie in thought in a darkened place. He could
then more fully sense the immanence of himself as a
firm and explicit meaning among the multiple meanings
of the world that pressed in with terrifying urgency in
full sunlight. His thoughts turned to his people, about
whom he knew only the lore and legend and myth of
Nathan's teaching. He knew that they were intense
and sensual and ardent and violently impetuous, allowing
their emotions to sweep them into the hazards. If he
69
could have read what was to be written about them a
long time afterward, that in their register of vision
there were no halftones, that they were a people of
primary colors, of black and white; that they knew only
truth and untruth, belief and unbelief, without delicate
shadings of thought and feeling-with all of that he
might have agreed.
He did not know that his people had taken their god
from the Kenites, or that they had borrowed legends,
ideas and laws from Babylonians and Egyptians, and
more recently from the Phoenicians, Midians, Hurrians,
Hittites, Canaanites and Mycenaeans. He would have
been surprised, but not at all pained, to learn that they
had taken their holy of holies, their ephod, their tera-
phim, their concept of divine kingship from neighbors.
He knew only that for ages they had wandered from
oasis to oasis across the deserts. He had little notion-
and this was to be one of his fatal mistakes-of their
strong sense of group and tribal solidarity, their fierce
pride, their distrust of splendors, their longing for their
desert heritage and its simple ways. He did not fully
grasp their qualities of insularity and pride and aloof-
ness which were already marking them as a people apart.
If Solomon had divined this he would have moved with
vigor to correct it, for he was neither by habit nor taste
a provincial man. He was never to know that his people.
had based a part of their philosophy and many of their
most telling metaphors on the habits of sheep. He was
never to suspect that they wanted to look upon him as
their shepherd-or that it was the shepherd, keen of
vision, lonely, weather-beaten and proud who was the
king-symbol for a man like Ahijah. Ahijah himself was
a product of Jeshimon, which meant devastation; of
the wilderness, a dreadful ruin, lying across crags and
precipices and eroded backs, across limestone and flint
and marl, clear to the shimmering and deadly deep of
the Ghôr and the cobalt blue of a salt sea.
70
Solomon wanted lightness and light in his kingdom
and people. He wanted ways that were gayer and bright-
er. He wanted a god of agriculture and soft loveliness
in meadow and vale rather than a god of war. He
wanted to build a splendid kingdom that would be rich
and colorful and noble with great buildings and lordly
furnishings, a kingdom with libraries and learning and
art, a capital city whose hospitality the elite of the earth
would be glad to accept. He would make Israel great
and wise and respected among the nations. Toward that
end he would learn to speak other languages, he would
visit other lands, he would acquaint himself with the
culture and wisdom of other peoples.
That was his dream as he looked at the goathair ceil-
ing and knew that dawn was breaking. But out in the
desert was a man with another kind of dream, and the
fight between king and prophet was to be the history
of Israel.
7
AHBAN WAS AWAKENED by the call of partridges which
in this season rang from cliff to cliff all over Judea; but
when he sat up he found that the house was still dark
with night. He had an old clay lamp with a flax wick,
but he had no oil for it; lamplight was a luxury which
only the very rich could afford. He also had a brazier
or firepan to warm his house in cold weather and
a big earthen cruse for the storing of oil. These
marked him as a man of means. So indeed did his wife's
churn, a whole goatskin with the hair turned out and
the neck closed, which could be slung on a tripod and
suspended with thongs, so that it could be rocked back
71
and forth. But Mara rarely had fuel for the fire, oil for
the lamp, or cream for the churn, even though her hus-
band was so prosperous that he had a lime-plastered bin
for storage, a mat of palm fibers with the ends curved
up and sharpened to keep scorpions away from sleepers,
a simlah that could serve as cloak or rug, and five clay
pots.
Sitting in a pair of goatskin drawers he looked round
him in the dusk, meanwhile scratching over his hairy
and unclean body and pausing at last to stick a finger
under his armpit and then smell of his finger. Then he
stuck a finger into his ear and smelled of it. Then he
looked down at his gaunt wife who was lying on her
back and breathing heavily through her open mouth.
He was fond of Mara, being in his affection unlike
most men of his time, for whom women were merely
vessels that housed sons. A husband could divorce his
wife by saying, "You're not my wife-get out!"; but if
a wife were rash enough to say, "You're not my baal,"
she was taken to deep water and drowned. An adul-
terous wife was driven into the wilderness, wearing only
a loincloth. Among Ahban's people there was no divorce
for women, no adultery for men, and no purpose in
marriage but procreation. If a wife proved to be sterile
her covenant was voided and she was cast out.
But Ahban and Mara loved and respected one another
with emotion deep enough to lie safely beyond life's
vicissitudes and to need no exchange of endearments.
Looking at her haggard face he touched her softly and
arose. Without making a sound he slipped outside to
look at the morning and listen to the music of the birds.
A little later, when he observed that the sun was pushing
his yellow warmth above Moab, he called to his family
and they came out and faced the east in an attitude of
prayer. Then Zeruah took a jug and went to the well
that stood by Bethlehem's gate.
72
Ahban spoke to his wife. "King Solomon is rebuilding
the walls of David's city. They say he will consecrate
the walls to Astarte."
"Ah no!" cried Mara, aghast.
"Or Ishtar," said Ahban, who liked to astound her.
"May Yah forbid!"
"Or Anatha."
"No, no, it can never be!"
Ahban took an old rag and wrapped it round his head
to confine his long hair, allowing three corners to fall
to his shoulders; and when this was done he looked slyly
at his wife. Like all people whose lives were empty and
undramatic, Ahban and Mara loved to discuss, over and
over endlessly, any exciting news that came to them.
Zeruah had told them in detail of the guard who ran up
to ask her name. When she returned from the well,
bearing the jug on her shoulder, Ahban spoke as though
Mara and Nebat had never heard the story.
"Zeruah was in David's city yesterday."
"No?" said Mara. "How came this to happen?"
"The King's guard ran up and spoke to her."
"Ah, I can't believe it! Ahban, this is some jest!"
"By my beard he did. Zeruah, isn't it so?"
"Mother, Father is speaking the truth."
Nebat was looking in turn at the three playing the
drama and digging wax out of his ears.
"Zeruah, you were walking down a path and you
passed the door to the King's tent."
"Yes, Father. Then King Solomon came out and he
was clothed in purple and looked royal and splendid like
the sun, or like a high place when Yah walks upon it.
I recognized him at once and I blushed."
"Hah!" said Ahban. When she told the story there
had been nothing said about blushing.
"He was looking at me, and his look was very bold
and eager."
73
"Hah!" said Ahban again, and glanced at his wife
to see what she made of that.
Mara found it easy to pretend that she had not heard

"You mean you turned red?" asked Nebat, who had

"I was frightened. I ran down the path and tried to

"He came running," said Ahban, prompting.

"Was he a tall man with a dagger who looked like a

"Father, how do you divine these things?"
Ahban clapped his hands in glee. "Was he wearing a

"This is amazing!" cried Zeruah. "How does Father

"And when he spoke his voice was loud like thunder

"I said, 'I am going to my father's tent.'"

"How," asked Mara anxiously, "could he know all

Nebat had dug a small bug out of his ear and was

"In the man's eyes I could read what was in his heart."
"The man was wicked," Ahban explained to Mara.
74
"May Yah punish him!"
"The guard asked my name."
"Did you lie to him?"
"Our daughter does not lie," said Mara.
"Oh, I know one time she lied," said Nebat, still ex-
ploring in his ears.
"I told him my name was Zeruah, the daughter of
Ahban, who lives in the city of bread."
"Did he mention the King?"
"I said, 'Who seeks to know my name, what person

whose name is great in all lands of earth, for no king
is so great as he.'"
Ahban made a sound of despair. In telling this story
he had hoped for a miracle, for the intervention of Yah
to spare his daughter; for it was plain to him, as it would
have been plain to any man less dull than Nebat, why
the King had sent a guard to ask Zeruah's name. Mara
turned tragic eyes on her husband.
"The King covets our daughter."
"Didn't King David covet Bathsheba ?"
"King David summoned her naked."
"He had Uriah killed," said Nebat calmly.
Ahban would never have dared to say that. Words
were deadly things, swollen with magic. If you filled
them with evil promise or portent or threat, they would
bear the evil out. To protect yourself you fought words
with words. And so it was that Ahban said at last:
"King Solomon does not covet my daughter."
Zeruah's next remark might have seemed innocent but
it was not. She said quietly: "Bathsheba is now the
Queen Mother. It is her son who rules today in David's
city."
Ahban groaned. He wondered what things were hid-
den in his daughter's heart.
"My daughter," he said sharply, "have you told us
all that happened?"
75
"Father, I would not lie."
"I know once you lied," said Nebat.
Ahban turned away, feeling unhappy and defenseless.
What could he do? Among the common people there
was great tolerance of cruelty, rapacity and arrogance
in their leaders. This was so because kings ruled by
divine right. If not gods themselves, they were hardly
distinguishable from gods. Ahban knew very well that
Solomon had fixed his lustful gaze on Zeruah, and that
no power but Yah could save her. Should he go into the
desert and try to find Ahijah and beg him to save his
daughter? Or might it be true-Ahban groaned again-
that he had committed some offense against his god?
Was Zeruah to be taken because he had used some wool
-only a very little that he had gathered from the acacia
-that belonged to Yah?
Ahban went away alone and sat and now he thought
of another matter. If a man married, built a house, and
sired a son, and his fortune on those occasions was good,
the remainder of his life would be prosperous; but if
his fortune was bad on those days, he would be cast
down. Judging his life by this simple law of his people,
Ahban had reason to be anxious: his sons had all been
slain in David's wars and the site of his house had been
ill-chosen. On the day he took a wife a storm had burst
over him, and on the day his firstborn had come he had
seen the face of a demon in Bethlehem's well.
He called to Mara and said: "Have you looked at
our house? The moles have tunneled all around the
rockwork of the bin."
Mara's thin sad face turned a shade paler. Burrowing
by moles was an unfailing sign that a member of the house
would commit adultery.
"Ahban, are you sure?"
"Yes, you can see for yourself. Come walk with me,"
he said.
76
He went over and looked at his few olive trees, which
were safe enough in this season. When in early summer
they put forth their white flowers even a slight wind
could scatter the blossoms and make the trees barren.
From his olives he turned to his grapevines, a trembling
and frightened man who sensed that disaster would
overwhelm him. Had he cut a tree or shrub that belonged
to the baal of this region, or killed a bird in a holy place?
He thought and thought but could find nothing in which
he had offended. If he needed stakes to support his vines
he went far into the wilderness to get them. If he snared
a partridge it was in an arid place where no god had
manifested his presence. He grazed his sheep around
Bethlehem but he understood that the local gods allowed
men to pasture in their domain if he gave up his tithes.
After a while he sent Mara away, wishing to be alone
with his problem; and at noon Mara called him to his
meal. In the house he sat with his family on the fiber
mat, their legs crossed, a large clay bowl at their feet.
After washing, they all dipped into this bowl with their
hands and ate. The food in the bowl was chiefly the
locust bean, the common food of the poor when locusts
themselves were not in season. Also in the bowl were
the coarse tasteless figs of the sycomore, which were
abundant in the western foothills. As seasoning, there
was a tiny piece of onion and a few drops of oil. This
was the kind of dinner Ahban ate week after week, year
after year. Once in a while he had barley and lentils and
black bread, and he had grapes in season. On those rare
occasions when he offered to Yah he had meat. For those
who could afford them, bread, wine and oil were the
chief foods in the whole Mediterranean area, but Ahban
tasted wine only in the vintage season, which was a time
of feasting everywhere in Israel.
Fixing her bleak sunken eyes on her baal, Mara said:
"Nebat has heard that the King will now increase the
taxes."
77
"But not in Judah," said Nebat.
Ahban thrust his hand into the pot and considered the
matter. A considerable part of what the poor people
produced was taken to feed the King's parasites. Would
Yah allow him to take more?
"Why not in Judah?" he asked, looking at Nebat.
"I don't know," said Nebat. "A man by the well just
told me." With a big hairy hand he was exploring in
the bowl to his wrist, trying to scoop up the locust beans.
"If Ahijah would come," said Mara.
Zeruah bent forward to look into the bowl and no
one observed that blood had dyed her face.
"Nobody knows," said Ahban, "where Ahijah is. Is
he over at Shiloh or on the Mount? Nobody knows,"
he said, answering his question.
Well, if what Nebat said was true, Ahban would be
favored, for he lived in Judah. But he knew of the in-
tense rivalries and hatreds and jealousies among the
tribes beyond Dan and he was tempted to speak his
heart. He was tempted but he did not dare to speak.
He wanted to rise and curse the King with all the passion
and fury of his soul; but he well knew that evil, like
holiness, was propagated by physical contact and words.
If he were carelessly to utter an unkind word about the
King, he could never neutralize its power, not in all the
days of his life. Indeed, the dangers in speech had forced
Ahban into long periods of silence. He knew that
Solomon was a wicked king; it was enough to remember
that he had spilled the blood of Adonijah and Joab on
holy ground. He knew-and his efforts to thrust the
knowledge away so tortured him that he groaned. His
thoughts of Solomon's wickedness trooped through his
consciousness like gay and dancing demons, and he made
movements with his body, struggling to reject them,
trying not to think them. Then he ceased chewing, and
sat like a man who expected imminent and horrible dis-
aster.
78
He arose suddenly, trying to think kind and flattering
things about his King. He tried to think, "He is a noble
king, a splendid king, a wise king...." But mixed up
with these thoughts, and warring against them, were
the imps of evil: "He has desecrated the holy place; he
has slain his brother; he lusts after my daughter..
"He is Yah's Anointed and a holy king."
But then: "He lusts after my daughter!"
"He is a wise and noble shepherd of his people."
And then: "He slew his own blood-brother!"

At last, with his emotions and fears and anxieties

"Father, why do you weep?"
"You-" he said, and broke off.
Too stricken to stand, he sank to the earth and bowed
to his knees. Tears as hot as anger ran down his cheeks.
Zeruah knelt by him, wondering why her father wept.
In a few moments Mara, Nebat and the child Jeroboam
came out of the house.
"What's his grief?" asked Nebat, picking at his teeth
and gazing placidly at Ahban.
"Keep your lip quiet!" said Zeruah.
"You talking to me? I guess you've forgotten you're
mine."
"Oh, Nebat, in the name of Yah, shut up!"
"I can't figure it out," said Nebat.
Jeroboam stepped forward, a strong and aggressive
boy, eight years old, and wise for his years. He looked
with astonishment at his grandfather. Ahban was no

his gaunt frame shook with his inner agony, and tears
fell in scalding drops to his arms.
"He wishes to be alone," said Mara. "All of you go
away.

79
Zeruah took her son's hand and went away, and Nebat
followed her. Mara looked round her for signs of evil,
scanning the earth and the sky. She did not speak to
Ahban until his grief had wasted itself and he was able
to rise. Then she took his arm and led him past the
grape arbor and over to a hill; and they sat there on a
stone, Ahban gazing dully at the earth, Mara looking
eastward to the opalescent haze above Moab.
She knew her husband well. She knew that only deep
terror or a deep sense of his own wickedness could move
him to open grief. She had not seen him weep since his
last son died. Unable to think of any sin in him, she
looked toward Jerusalem where at the moment King
Solomon was busy with his plans. Did Ahban think that
the King would seize Zeruah and force her into his bed?
Even if this was so, Mara could not feel deeply about
it. On her side was custom, centuries old, under which
women had been and still were chattels. In her heart she
knew that the concubine of a king was far more enviable
and likely to be much happier than the beulah of Nebat,
a dull fellow who could never hope to have a house of
his own or a piece of land, but must always be his
fatherinlaw's vassal. Mara did not hope that her daugh-
ter would be seized; she was simply too old and broken
and weary to care. But on Ahban's side, unknown to her,
was a deep passion for his daughter, now that he had
lost his sons.
These two sat, husband and wife, baal and beulah,
fond of one another, yet strangers to one another's
loneliness. Mara put an arm across his shoulder. Ahban
had buried his grief, and now in a gentle voice he said:
"The demons have been howling." He meant the
jackals, into which demons had entered.
"I know," said Mara.
"All last night they were howling."
"I heard them."
80
"I saw one and it came toward me, dragging its
rump."
There was no need to tell her the meaning of that.
When a jackal came whimpering toward you, dragging
its hind quarters, it meant your death or the death of one
close to you.
"Did it run away? ?"
"No."
"Did you utter holy words against it?"
"Mara, of course."
"Then what did it do?"
"It fawned and whined and crawled toward me."
"Did it touch you?"
"May Yah forbid!"
"Then what?"
"Up in the sky I saw a bird that flew round and round.
It fell."
"Did it strike the earth?"
"It spun round and round in falling and then struck."
Mara put a thumb and finger to her throat and
stroked it, moving downward. She looked away in the
direction of the holy mount and sighed.
"Didn't Ahijah ever say when he would be back?"
"No."
"He will be strong and holy when he comes. He will
denounce the wicked King."
"Hush! The King is great and noble and wise."
"Ahban, the King is wicked."
"No, no! The King is not wicked-not wicked-not
wicked!"
81
8
SOLOMON'S ENERGY WANED with each waning moon but
with each new moon he moved with such speed and vigor
that those were amazed who watched him. He divided
his kingdom into provinces and placed over each a de-
pendable tax collector; he dispatched troops to key spots
to repel invasion and put down insurrections; he wrote
to King Hiram of Tyre saying that he wished to employ
hundreds or possibly thousands of his most skilled work-
men; he sent great labor forces to the Lebanon to make
cedar slabs and beams and pillars, and bring them to his
city; he put a thousand slaves to work in stone quarries;
he wrote the Pharaoh and told him that he hoped soon
to visit him; he married two minor princesses and took
a half-dozen concubines-all these things he did and
many more as the first months passed. He felt that he
had a firm grasp on his royal duties and powers and
could proceed to a consummation of his ambitions. He
took his mother into his confidence on most matters,
but there was one which he cherished in private and
turned over and over searching it for its dangers and
promises; and as soon as the next spring equinox came
around he was ready to go to Megiddo. He would
travel, not in the way of his father, who was content
with a small bodyguard, but in style as befitted a king
of kings, with fifty runners going ahead with trumpets
to announce his coming, and with a guard of five hundred
picked men. He decided to take Zadok, the priest, and
Ahiah, his scribe, as well as Zikla, who was now captain.
of his guard. He would take many servants, and asses
laden with food and furnishings and tents, the finest
raiment and wines he had, and a few musicians.
It was a morning of deep quiet when he left Jerusalem
82
and headed north. Before him went his heralds, naked
but for loincloths. Robed in linen, Solomon was mounted
on the royal ass, whose color was milkwhite; and on
his left, when the path allowed it, rode Ahiah, and on his
right in his priestly robes Zadok. At Gibeon, Solomon
offered gifts to the sacred well, including wine and sweet-
cakes and a substance blended of oil and fragrant gums.
A little later he looked at Zadok and said:
"Does the well there belong to the Baal of Gibeon
or to Yah?"
Zadok did not know but he looked away to the soft
mists above Carmel and said: "To the Baal of Gibeon,
my Lord."
"Yah doesn't seem to own many waters, does he?"
It was believed that somewhere in the blue sky were
vast reservoirs of water from which the gods took their
rains. "Well, can you answer this?-to which god does
yonder river belong?" He indicated the Jordan.
"My Lord, I don't know. I think those waters be-
long to Yah, yet people do still pray to the river-god
before crossing."
"Is this river as mighty as the Nile or Euphrates?"
"I have never seen those, my Lord."
"Where is the northern boundary of Yah's land?"
Zadok looked round him but did not answer. Indeed,
he did not know the answer for he had no clear notion
of the lands which David, with Yah's help, had con-
quered.
"Did you ever know a woman," Solomon asked next,
"who was fertilized by swallowing a fish or inserting a
fish?"
"No," said Zadok, solemnly shaking his head.
"Did you?" he asked Ahiah.
"No, my Lord, but many women have been fertilized
with sacred fish from the holy waters. All the people in
the world know that a woman can be fertilized by a
serpent, though why the gods resort to such evasive
83
ways, when they have the power to assume the form of
men and mate with women, the wise men seem not to
know."
"Do the gods of Egypt take the form of men and
embrace mortal women?"
"Oh yes, my Lord. There are many scenes depicted
on the walls of their great buildings, showing the gods
copulating with women."
"Zadok, did you hear that?" When Zadok did not
reply, Solomon peered at him and said: "Zadok, do you
ride along sound asleep?—or are you awake?"
"My Lord, I heard what the scribe said. These are
wicked people. Yah, who is great and holy, does not
copulate with women."
Solomon turned to Ahiah. "Do all gods but Yah em-
brace women?"
"My Lord, I believe they do, though I am not familiar
with the gods in the far west."
"Why doesn't Yah have a wife?"
"I have thought often about that," said Ahiah. "I
do not know."
"Don't all other gods have wives?"
"Yes, my Lord, so far as I know. Osiris has Isis, Bel
has Ishtar, Shamash has Anatha-or is it Anatha?
Melkarth has Astarte-"
"I know all that," said Solomon. "Zadok? . . . Za-
dok, did I wake you up?"
"My Lord," said Zadok stiffly, "I am always listening
for your voice."
Solomon looked at Ahiah and winked. "Tell me, then,
are we Yah's children?"
"Yah is our holy Father and we are his children."
"But how can we be if he has no wife?" He looked
at Ahiah and winked again. "Answer me, Zadok. You
are the highest priest, a great and learned man. Can
84
children be begotten without a mother?... Zadok, what
do you say?"
"My Lord, we are Yah's children but he has no
wife."
"You ridiculous fellow! He must have had a wife.
Where is she? Did he drown her?"
"My Lord, what do you mean?"
"If a wife is accused by her husband of adultery and
is cast into waters, they will reveal her guilt or inno-
cence."
"If she is guilty," said Zadok, "the waters will reject
her."
"Hah! Ahiah, is that right?"
"Yes, my Lord."
"Zadok, I've had a thought. Yah is so incessantly
bent on war and destruction and has such awful rages
because he has lost his wife."
Zadok was not at all shocked. There was nothing
blasphemous in the words. In this time people talked
about their gods as they talked about their family and
friends.
"My Lord, I don't know. It is true that Yah is often
angry_"
"Nearly all the time, Zadok. As far back as I can
remember I've heard stories about his awful tantrums.
Now I've observed," said Solomon, who grew up under
the wings of a devoted mother, "that a man has a more
cheerful view of life when he has a woman to embrace.
I'd like to have you consider this matter-Yah's contin-
ual rages and his lack of a wife. He must have had a
wife some time-even a thousand wives. I want to know
what has happened to them.
"And that reminds me," he said, turning to Ahiah.
"If kings have so many wives, why do the gods have
only one? Or do they have many, whose names are not
known to us?"
85
Ahiah was delighted with a king whose mind was so
restless and inquiring. "My Lord, it may be that their
names are not known; or it may be that they prefer only
one wife, since they may descend to copulate with mor-
tal women whenever they please.'
"
"Yes," said Solomon, stroking his short beard. “That
may be the answer."
On a rocky hill a dozen miles north of Jerusalem
they came to the hamlet of Beth-el, meaning the house
of god, formerly a royal Canaanite city, but now a holy
place in Israel, enfolded by legends. There were four
springs here and a small reservoir but Solomon did not
stop. His heralds dashed into the town, blowing their
horns and crying that the King of Kings was coming;
and the people prostrated themselves before him as he
passed.
Late in the second day he left the desolate hills of
Judah and came to areas more lush, where the local gods
displayed their fertility and power; and he questioned
again many of the traditional virtues of Yah. Was he
merely another baal, or was he the god of gods, as Na-
than believed? This question more than any other was
to trouble Solomon throughout the whole period of his
reign. A baal was not migratory, but stayed close to his
home. Yah on the other hand journeyed in an ark, went
with his people to battle, and even now, Ahijah would
have said, had left Israel and was again on the Holy
Mount.
"Zadok, when I go to Egypt, should I take Yah with
me?" Solomon meant an image.
"Yes, my Lord."
"But what would the gods of Egypt make of that?
They might resent his presence. There might be war.
Osiris might drop a thunderbolt on me. Tell me, does
Yah like to be spoken of as the baal of his people?"
86
"Some say yes and some say no.'
"Who says no?"
"Ahijah, the seer. I say that he is our ha-el-haggadol,
the god of gods who divides the flames of the fire and
breaks the cedars of Lebanon. He is the Awe of our
fathers."
"What business has he with the cedars of Lebanon ?"
When Zadok did not answer, Solomon winked at his
owlish scribe and persisted: "Yah reveals himself in
storms and thunder and a breath of consuming fire and
a cloak of many colors. Do you know what Ahiah has
told me?"
"No, my Lord."
"Ahiah says a cloak of many colors is merely the
colors in the sky when the sun goes down, and that any
god who wears such a cloak is the sun. He says every
sun-god has twelve disciples who are the points in Baby-
lon's zodiac and that these stand for the twelve periods
of the moon. So the wise men told him in Egypt. Is that
so, Ahiah?"
"Yes, my Lord. When I asked a wise man in Egypt
why they have so many gods, yet Israel has only one,
he answered, saying: 'People who have been used to a
monotonous and unvarying landscape, such as a desert,
have only one god, but people who live with hills and
valleys and fountains and rivers and lakes have many
gods.'
9.99
"That is a wise answer," said Solomon. "Another
thing, Zadok, about which I have been thinking is this.
Our people are not a gay people. The Egyptians have
a word religion for which we have nothing but the
fear of Yah. Ahiah tells me that the Egyptians do not
live in constant dread of their gods. Between them and
their gods there is a joyous fellowship-and such fel-
lowship we shall find in Megiddo between Astarte and
87
her children. But Yah-Yah is like an arrogant and
wrathful and sullen father who is everlastingly looking
about him for ways to chastise his children. That may
be because he has lost his beulah. Or it may be because
he has depleted his reservoirs of rain. No god could be
very happy looking down on Judah, for in the whole
ghastly stretch of it there is not a decent fountain or
garden or forest. Even Melech or Shamash or Ammon
does better. Even Dagon of Philistia does better, or
Hadad-Rimmon-or any god, Zadok, you can think of.
"Is there any way to persuade Astarte to become the
goddess of Israel and fertilize and bless it?"
When Zadok did not answer, Solomon fell to dream-
ing while his nostrils sniffed the pleasant life-giving
odors about him. He did not see himself as an instru-
ment of destiny or fate, nor even, for that matter, of
Yah. He never suspected for a moment that the fight
between him and Ahijah, between king and prophet,
was a fight between change and changelessness, between
dogma and nature, that would in time convulse the
western world. He merely looked round him at the soft
loveliness and sensuous delights and wondered why
some gods blessed their lands with such abundance, and
others with almost nothing at all. He judged them by
what they gave to their people; and from the gods of
these northern lands, it seemed to him, Yah might well
take some lessons. He remembered having heard, as
a child, a Syrian priest say in tones of deep music: "Bel,
the great king, the king of the world. . . Many times
he had thought of those words. If Bel was king of the
whole world, then obviously all other gods were his
stewards, or they were impostors.
"Zadok!"
"Yes, my Lord."
99
"Did I wake you up? Look," said Solomon, and swept
88
an arm toward the blue dome. "Up there must be huge
reservoirs and cisterns, for look at the country around
us. If Yah had such vast waters he could make Judah's
desert blossom as the rose."
"Yah has such waters," said Zadok. "He withholds
them because his people have sinned."
"Ah yes, I have heard that before! You tell me, then,
that the people here haven't sinned-for see what bless-
ings they have!"
"Yah's people," said Zodak imperturbably, "are a
special people, appointed to a special purpose."
"What is that?"
"To be the priests and exemplars in righteousness to
the whole world."
Solomon turned to his scribe. "Some more of Ahi-
jah's dreadful lunacies," he said.
They were now passing through villages in which the
local lord of the sanctuary was represented by a masse-
bah-a stone pillar, a staff of life-erected near the
altar. This stone column was the god's dwelling-place,
his beth-el. Everywhere around him now Solomon saw the
holy places of Astarte-the groves and wooded hills,
with tassels and ornaments, hair and feathers, hanging
from trees. Above all, as he rode into the north, shifting
from ham to ham to ease his aching rump, the thing
that impressed him most was the difference between
Judah and these lands. Back there were stone and
thistle and dead dust. Here were magnificent trees-
walnut, terebinth, sycomore, the plane-tree by streams,
the almond and pistachio, date-palm and apricot, and
many species of oak. Here was the thick high bush of
olive and arbutus and myrtle, juniper and willow and
wild vine. Here the ravines were sweet with their masses
of flowers. Here in season were such luscious fruits as
citron, pomegranate, mulberry and almond and walnut,
89
date, fig, apricot, quince and olive. It was indeed a
blessed land. On the plain of Esdraelon were the per-
fumed and rampant excesses of convolvulus, honey-
suckle, hollyhock, mallow and golden broom. There
would be banks of red poppies against the vivid green
of the prickly-pear hedges. There were or would be
whole vast landscapes of pinks, narcissus, tulip, iris, jes-
samine. In the hills of Ephraim he had seen splendid
vales of fig and olive, the white stones of the terraces
mantled with ferns and white cyclamen and crocus. He
had come through the deep and odorous and alluring
forests of lower hills and plains. He had seen, in earlier
years, the spacious and fragrant and breezy plain north
of the Yarmuk, with its orchards and vineyards, its
fields of golden wheat, its moors rich with scented herbs.
There was balm in Hauran, the granary of the East.
There was balm in Gilead lying between the Yarmuk
and the Jabbok across the Jordan. Nowhere could he
now see any hint of arid wastes and empty places. The
gods had walked here. Trees were divine; where you
saw a tree growing, there a god had stood.
"You know," said Solomon, ignoring his austere
priest and turning to his scribe, "it is women who have
life. They are its house. It is a goddess, not a god, who
gives to this land such an overflowing of good things.
Of course Yah has no wife. All his country around my
city is goddess-forsaken."
He rode in thought for a few minutes and then said
with profound insight: "Our people had no Mother
in the wilderness. There was no need of a Mother where
there was no life. Here-" his arm swept to indicate
the wealth of the blessed earth "-here there is life
because the Mother is here. A god without a beulah
whose land is a wilderness, a waste, an emptiness on
the high places, and men like Ahijah crying to the moun-
90
tains! But here are the valleys and in the valleys there
is vision !"
"Some of our people," said Ahiah quietly, "think As-
tarte is Yah's wife."
"Many more will think it if I have my way. Look!"
he said, his eyes feasting upon the beauty.
He had seen signs of welcome and as he proceeded
these multiplied. The world everywhere declared the
glory and the goodness of its Mother; from all direc-
tions came the sweet breath of her nostrils; the earth
was lovely from the rising of the sun unto his going
down, and there was none to lift up his heel against
him. On the approach to Endor Solomon saw trees of
such majesty that he dismounted and stood under their
branches or turned to embrace them, pressing his cheeks
to their bark. Some trees were said to have the power
of speech. Solomon had never heard one utter words,
but he had heard from Nathan that a tree told David
when to attack the Philistines. He did not doubt that
trees were divine. Many people addressed prayers to
trees or in time of sickness slept under them or had their
branches brought to their bedside.
Resuming his journey he came to the altar at Endor,
beside which a tree stood, with human hair and pieces
of garments hanging from it. The stones of the altar
had been anointed with oil and blood, and upon them
was a loaf of bread. A man was lying prostrate before
the altar.
As Solomon followed the road beyond Endor he
wished it were possible to move his capital city to this
northern land-to Shechem with its great fountains or
to the shore of Kinnereth or to the slopes of Hermon
where the hills were like pregnant women. On glancing
down he saw that the path was strewn with flowers and
a few minutes later he came to the walls of Megiddo.
91
After choosing a site for his camp he went up a hill
for a better view; and while standing there and looking
round him, he recalled one of Nathan's stories. An
Egyptian king had once captured this place and carried
away with him a thousand chariots, hundreds of suits
of splendid armor, and enough gold and jewels to bur-
den a score of asses. Megiddo he intended to fortify be-
cause it stood in a strategic place where a main route
came over from the plain of Sharon to its pass. An army
invading from the north would have to confront it or
ford the Jordan. Just south of this hill were large foun-
tains and near them was flat earth upon which he in-
tended to erect magnificent stables to hold many horses
and chariots.
But he had come here not to look again at the site
but to participate in the thanksgiving to the goddess.
Because he had a taste for quixotic as well as erotic ad-
venture, he went to his tent and had his servants dis-
guise him as a Phoenician sailor. His face was painted
red and wood ashes were sprinkled over his hair. He
was drenched with rose water and smeared with fenu-
greek, crocus and musk. He smelled like the juices of
Astarte's own gardens when he left his tent, stepping
proudly in buckled sandals adorned with huge rosettes
and looking in his red dyes more like a murderer than
a lover.
When, discreetly shadowed by Zikla, he came to a
huge image of the goddess he paused to look at her.
Who was she? He could not know, of course, that she
had been Narda and would be the many-breasted Ar-
temis, the fertile Cybele, Hera and Miriam and Mary
and the Magna Mater. She was the great Mother;
that much he knew. Her commonest altar-in this land
-was the white obelisk. Sometimes she was represented
as a woman with the tail of a fish, thereby honoring the
92
fertilizing powers of water; sometimes she was crowned
with the head of a bull. She was called Astarte-the-cow
and Astarte-of-the-sheep, for she was the goddess of
flocks and herds. Some statues showed her as a sphinx
with a woman's head, or, more commonly, as the naked
Mother standing on a lion, her hands filled with flowers
and serpents. Sometimes she was warlike and held club,
lance and shield, and was shown mounted on a horse
rushing across space after her prey. Sometimes gentle,
sometimes cruel, shy and chaste in one mood, wanton
in the next, she was the moon-woman and her home
was the moon and the stars were her genii. But her
earthly home was the fragrant groves in which she hid.
As the morning star she was the goddess of battle; as
the evening star the goddess of love. She was worshiped
on the housetops; children gathered fuel for her fires;
women baked cakes in her honor. And in her honor
women gave themselves to love.
That much Solomon knew-and a little more. Some
said her husband was the sun, who sickened and died
when the earth chilled and the leaves fell. After the
sun-god had gone down into the regions of cold and
death Astarte went to search for him. The earth be-
came barren when she left it, and burst with green life
when she returned. Her most glorious time was now,
the spring of the year, after the resurrection of her
mate and her own return from the gloomy horrors of
the underworld. Looking round him, Solomon saw
people rejoicing everywhere, many of them dead drunk.
This small city seemed to be made of flowers. Women
had gone to the plains and hills and gathered thousands
of the lilies of Sharon-anemone and pimperel and a
dozen other kinds; and these they had hung in wreaths
from poles and trees, from buildings, from Astarte's
house and from all sacred things and places. Enormous
93
masses of them were strewn upon the paths and lay in
crushed carpets where the people walked. Their fra-
grance rose in great invisible clouds of incense. Children,
naked but for their garlands, ran and shrieked and rolled
like little drunken revelers, with boys chasing girls
everywhere. Women were everywhere, some veiled and
some with open faces, and some with so many flowers
in their hair and bouquets in their arms that they looked
like walking garlands.
For several days many of the people had been lost
in the ecstasies of wine. Under trees at a little distance
from the temple, men lay, dead to the world, their
mouths loose and open and their faces and garments
stained with Astarte's juices. There were women under
the trees and by their appearance it was plain to Solo-
mon that men and women before sinking into languorous
stupor had mated with terrific ardor. On some of them
there was blood, and the marks of nails and teeth. All
these things Solomon noted, his emotions suffused with
joy, his eyes shining like the backs of serpents. He would
put Astarte's women in Jerusalem's temple to give their
love to men.
It was known, of course, that Solomon, King of Is-
rael and Sun of Suns, had come to Megiddo; and after
Zikla for a bribe betrayed his disguise, women followed
Solomon, some rendering homage to him by falling
drunkenly on their faces and trying to clutch his legs,
while others with wine and worship danced round and
round him, tossing flowers. They challenged him with
their beauty-filled eyes. They ran before him, weaving
their handsome bodies with tantalizing grace and sing-
ing their songs of fertility and love. Ravished by their
caprices and mockery Solomon went to a court before
the temple where great bowls of wine stood and filled
a cup brimming full and drank. Looking round him he
94
saw the austere and melancholy priest. "What a dread-
ful fellow!" Solomon thought. "There is no joy in him."
For Solomon there was no evil here. He looked at
the women dancing before him, robed in purest white,
with red garlands in their hair and over their loins and
with tambourines in their hands. He listened to their
music and their singing:
Give praise to the glorious Astarte
the goddess above all others
the one whose thighs are love
the holy mother of men! .
•
Solomon looked round him at beautiful figures under
the semitransparent gowns. He quaffed another brim-
ming cup. He glanced at the altar, foursquare, huge
and ornate, with the figure of a woman at each corner,
and on each side a man with the body of a lion and the
wings of a hawk. He glanced at the temple, a rugged
structure of baked bricks, set on stone slabs and inter-
lined with mortar. Everywhere around him in this court
were figures of the goddess, most of them with tiny
breasts and a distended abdomen. An exaggerated
mount of love was intended to suggest the descent of
a child to the vulva. In other figures her head and
breasts were shown but the lower part of her body and
her legs were confined within a simple stone column that
spread at the base. In nearly all the figures her hands
were under her breasts in the gesture of offering them
to a nursing child. In the largest of the figures she
stood with lotus flowers in her upraised hands; a ser-
pent was crawling up her right thigh with its head
pointed to the door of life ...
While Solomon was drinking wine and gazing round
him he saw women passing with live serpents in their
hands. They were crying with delight and touching and
95
stroking and kissing the serpents. They took these to

came disguised as a Syrian peddler; and the King said

"Are you rejoicing?"
"Yes, my Lord."
"Great gods, if I only had my capital city in a place
like this! Ahiah?"
"Yes, my Lord?"
"After they are done with their ceremonials we'll
have a little fun. Don't run away."
The holy rites represented the ceaseless struggle be-
tween summer and winter, the god of light and the satan
of darkness, life and death. A grotesquely hideous figure
in a black robe, bearing in his hands mutilated flowers
and plants, rushed in like the winds and bore down upon
a group of men who pretended to be laboring in a
vineyard. Howling with scorn this monstrous figure of
Death tore his plants in two and spit on them, and with
imprecations and blasphemies hurled them to the earth
and ground them under his heels. He dashed at the men,
trying to interrupt their labors. Then the men rushed
upon him, flinging stones and flogging him with vines,
and drove him, howling and cursing and defeated into
an underground chamber where in his own black hell
he made a fearful clamor. When he vanished from the
scene of Life the multitude went wild with joy and
surged forward, waving their symbols of life, of which
many were small figurines of the goddess. Then a
priestly figure came forward and began to sing; and the
people, swaying from side to side and striving to sus-
pend their senses in contemplation of the divine, sang
with him:

and the pruners will train their vines

Death shall not prevail!
Death, go down!
Death shall be driven under the earth

Death, go down!
Death shall be driven where no flowers bloom

Death, go down!
The priest intoned:
I will summon the goddess gracious and beautiful

Three times they danced round the altar, waving
their garlands and singing their song of love. Then, at
the signal from their leader, they broke into this song:
We have decked our beds with coverings of tapestry
with the finest cloths of Egypt!
We have perfumed our beds with aloes and cinnamon
with myrrh and the flowering thyme
with saffron and mint and the waters of the rose!
O, glorious Goddess,
come, let us obey her,
flinging ourselves into the arms of love
to lie all night until the morning shines
for our house of life is empty and open
and hungers for the staff!...
While they danced round and round, these women
sacred to Astarte, their eyes mocked the men above
their veils, challenging, luring, dancing with mischief
and glowing with passion; and Solomon standing on an
inner circle, looked at them one by one and tried to de-
cide which one he would mate with. In his eyes ardor
was as naked as flame. There was one who took his
fancy above the others—a very young and dainty miss,
but fully matured, with breasts so firm that they did
not leap in the dance and with eyes so drenched with
passion and glory that they illumined her whole face.
A mass of loosened hair streamed like Astarte's foli-
age behind her. When this girl came abreast, Solomon
danced along at her side and whispered:
"You are mine tonight!"
98
"Yes!"
The holy women, the nuns of Astarte, danced away,
going to the inner chapels of the temple, where some
would stand demurely under mistletoe as a sign that
they waited to be embraced; and some, who had ap-
pointments, would withdraw to small love-chambers
banked deep with flowers. When the women disappeared
the men surged forward, eager to embrace them, for
they, the men, were the sacred male principle, awak-
ened in springtime to be Astarte's lovers, to multiply
her children as she multiplied the flowers on Sharon's
hills. Other women, too old or too homely to serve
as temple votaries, cut off a part of their hair, which
was still instinct with life, even after their breasts had
withered, and put it in tiny caskets, which the priests
would lay away in Astarte's house.
Solomon was thinking of the holy house in Baby-
lon, the Bit-Shaggathu, or temple of copulation. There,
every woman, rich or poor, princess or peasant, had once
in her lifetime to submit to the embrace of a stranger
in the holy place, and give her earnings to its goddess.
The holy place was usually crowded with women, wait-
ing to be covered. Some were so homely or sickly that
they had to wait for years, or in vain till their death.
Solomon knew also that in a northern land the noblest
families dedicated their daughters to the service of An-
aitis; the fabulous union of god and goddess was stimu-
lated and made more fruitful by copulations in holy
places of their children on earth. This, Ahiah had told
him, was also true in Egypt. In Babylon the man acting
for the god in sexual union had to be a stranger but
this was not true in Syria, nor generally in other sanc-
tuaries. The important thing, after all, was to worship
the holy act of creation and to use the earnings of the
love-nuns for the maintenance of the temple.
99
All this Solomon warmly approved. "I'm going to
have the most beautiful women I can find for Yah's
temple," he said. "Will he like it?"
"My Lord, I'm afraid he won't. Ahijah—”
"I've a mind to bring Ahijah down here to one of
these rejoicings. I'd like to see forty of these beautiful
women sit on him."
"If they did," said Ahiah, "he'd flee to the wilder-
ness and never return."
"If I could get rid of him that easily I'd do it. Ahiah,
do you have a love-woman for tonight?"
"No my Lord." He shrugged and turned away with
feverish haste but Solomon grasped his arm and faced
him.
"Ahiah, you're too sensitive. A misshapen back
doesn't mean a misshapen staff. Come, you will mate
with one of the loveliest."
Dragging his protesting and humiliated scribe after
him Solomon crossed the court and entered the inner
sanctum; and though it was dimly lighted with oil lamps
and faintly luminous from great banks of flowers he
searched in vain for the lady of his choice. When he
returned to find Ahiah, whom he had left by the door,
the scribe had flown. "The fool!" said the King, and
returned to the search. Like a boy-thief prowling in a
vineyard he slipped up and down the halls, peeping
into chambers and smelling the ravishing odors of each;
or with his sandaled feet he kicked into the masses
that had been strewn to walk on and sucked the fra-
grance into his being; or with his palms felt curiously
over the walls to learn what they were made of. While
he was thinking half of love and half of the temple
which he would build, a child came shyly to him and
took his hand and led him away. Her hand was so soft
and delicate that he raised it to his lips, and like a
100
little lady, demure and gracious, she offered her other
hand. Then she stopped before a closed door and told
him to enter. He opened the door and was amazed by
the flood of warm sweet scents that blew against him.
and over him, drowning his senses and filling his breath.
with the heavy saturation of a herb-garden. He stepped
inside and could see nothing but flowers. But then an-
other hand touched him and the door behind him closed.
