SOLOMON RETURNED to Jerusalem with two fresh
wives, Etillit, a Syrian princess, daughter of the king
of a petty northern province; and Havveh, a Hebrew
girl who caught his eye in Jericho. But the moment
he came in sight of the empty hills of Judea he felt ter-
ribly dejected and spiritless. "My city," he thought,
"is the world's rectum." Having contrived that extra-
ordinary metaphor he searched his knowledge for other
apt symbols. For him as for all people the man was
the measure of the world. The Assyrians spoke of the
nose of a tree, the mouth of a door, the tongue of a
fire, the rib of a ship, the arm of a river and the head
of any stream, meaning its source. The Hebrews like-
wise thought of the world in terms of the human body
and its parts. A common expression for fright was to
say a person was weak in the knees, and for pride that
he turned up his nose. For Solomon the lush northern
country was the heart, the liver, the lips and speech
and eyes and hair; but his ugly city with its offensive
odors and desolate environs . .
101
"Ah, by the gods," he said to Ahiah, "how the place
stinks! I don't think I'll ever be able to stand it here."
The very next morning he mounted the royal ass
and with his bodyguard set off across the wilderness to
the salt sea, hoping to find pure fountains. What he
found was a wasteland of eroded hills, covered with
broom and thistle; and then brown and yellow sands,
disintegrating limestone cliffs, fields of scattered stone,
with stiff thorn as the only vegetation. What a god-
forsaken region it was! There was not a tree in sight,
nor a shrub, nor even a blade of grass. There was only
the hot and burning earth, furrowed by winter tor-
rents, scorched and browned and baked by the awful
sun. From a distance the hills looked like great piles
of dust in a windless afternoon; at a closer range it
seemed that the angers and agonies of innumerable de-
mons had left their records here.
This wilderness of Jeshimon made him feel still
more hopeless about his capital city. So, for that matter,
did the city itself, with its tents and huts and dust, its
sprawling piles of building materials, its heat, its un-
clean and ill-smelling cisterns, its crooked paths, its
want of trees and shrubs and grass, the out-places where
his thousands of slaves voided. .
"I can never make anything of this city," he said to
his mother. "Where'll I get the water for fountains and
gardens? Out of cisterns? Why, great Bel, all the cis-
terns I have wouldn't nourish a hundred trees! The
gods must hate this place."
"It's Yah's land, isn't it?" asked his mother, who
didn't pretend to knowledge of these things. "Does Yah
hate water?"
"Well, being a god of the desert, I suppose he does.
This land belongs to him, no doubt, because no other
102
god wants it. That much, at least, I can understand at
last. Should I move north?"
"I can see that you've fallen in love with the north
country. It would be much easier to build a beautiful
city there such a city as Tyre is said to be; but could
you defend it?"
"No-no, I couldn't. I guess I'll have to stay here
and see what I can do. I'm going to Egypt soon. I
want to see a real kingdom-magnificent buildings and
furnishings-fountains, gardens, scented and flowering
shrubs and vines-I want to see all of it," he said, and
opened his arms wide to embrace the world.
But first he had to learn what his overseers were do-
ing and give instructions for the building of granaries,
warehouses, vintage-chambers, ponds and pools and
cisterns, armories and stables and chariot-sheds, foun-
tain-sites and wine-coolers. And he had to explore the
essences of his new wives.
Havveh, whom he summoned first, was a large
woman; she stood erect and proud and was stately when
she walked or moved. Solomon liked women with chic
and spirit and bright intuitions. He was to learn that
Havveh was not intuitive at all. She was deeply reli-
gious, which means that she observed all the rules; she
was so consumed by anxiety that she wore her soul in
a locket, haunted by the fear that it would be stolen.
This amused Solomon. Persons chronically apprehen-
sive abstracted their souls and wore them suspended
from their necks or tied in a fillet to their sleeves; or
they hid them away in jars. Havveh knew that mali-
cious persons, employing sorcery, could steal a soul,
or induce it to become truant. Havveh's mother had
lost her soul and had become nervous and thin and hys-
terical.
This chronic fear had developed in Havveh certain
103
mannerisms of speech and movement. She walked with
slow deliberate strides, as though measuring off distance.
She carefully considered her words before speaking, and
when at a loss for an answer she said, "Ohh!" and let
it go at that. But she was a strikingly beautiful woman,
with an enormous sheaf of lustrous hair, burnished and
golden; with large deep eyes whose lights could be en-
thralling behind their heavy curved lashes; with perfect
teeth and ripe red lips. Solomon, who unerringly sensed
a woman's depths, was not disappointed in her. Indeed,
in love she was so wanton that during their first em-
brace she sank her teeth into his neck and made him
cry out with astonishment. When sitting, she was so
tranquil and poised that few would have suspected what
wild and uprushing emotions could come out of her
quiet depths. Solomon liked her abandon and for sev
eral nights nourished his ardor at her breast.
"I'm a queen now," she would say, staring at him
with the naive wonder of a child.
"A lot of women," he said, "are queens."
"But not a lot of women from Jericho."
"You are sweet and ripe," he said, "like figs, or the
vineyards of Engedi."
When Havveh could not tell whether a person meant
what he said she sat very erect and still, with only her
handsome eyes expressing her doubts. In such times she
would glance at the person's eyes and then down, and
back again and down, her lids rising and falling with her
glance. She was naive, forthright, literal and indiscreet,
as her next question revealed.
"Do I please you as much as your other wives?"
"Vanity!" he said. "There is only vanity in women."
Etillit was a very different sort of woman, with a
subtle and refined cruelty. She was small, with exqui-
site features and a trim and shapely body, with slender
104
ankles, which few women had, and the poise which
came from walking on high heels. Only in her eyes,
which were as hard and cold as polished diorite, was
there any hint of the designs and treacheries which her
mind harbored. Solomon could look into Havveh's eyes
as into a night and see depths and sense the illimitable.
There was nothing to see in Etillit's eyes beyond the
black opaque surface. That surface shone with spite or
cunning, mockery, contempt or hate, but never with
humor or kindness. When she first saw Solomon's city
she had shrugged her tiny shoulders and turned disdain-
fully away on the high heel-caps which she wore to in-
crease her height and give a wanton boldness to her
flanks. But she dissembled well in the embrace, so well
indeed that Solomon was deceived. While the King
fondled her, her mind was busy taking a kind of inven-
tory of her opportunities here. She hoped to have a
son and to make him king.
"What is the sweet scent on your breasts?" asked
Solomon, lying on soft cushions and looking at her.
"Does my Lord like it?"
My Lord liked her musical and modulated voice.
"What is it?"
"Would you have me tell my secrets so that women
can steal them ?"
"Do you have a right to secrets?"
The coldness in her chilled. "I have so little," she
said, with an adorable shrug of her small shoulders,
"that is my own. Would you take that from me?"
Solomon leaned over and sniffed a breast and kissed
it. "This scent arouses my ardor."
"My Lord, it is supposed to."
"It comes from Astarte's gardens." He was think-
ing: I am going to have trouble with this woman. He
said: "Persia has no more delightful fragrance."
105
"As long as you like it you will want me."
"I shall always want you," he said ardently, but she
was beginning to weary him.
She put a finger gently to his lips and then kissed.
her finger. "You are such a very great King that you
make me a little afraid of you."
"You may go now," he said. He did not see her eyes
when she arose and turned away. He did not see her

between her teeth. But he did feel a sense of the pres-
ence of death and he wondered how many kings died
young, from poison or the dagger or from strangula-
tion. Whom could he trust besides his mother?
He sent word to her to prepare for him and she had
a table arranged with delicacies, including honeycakes,
spiced dates, pomegranate wine cistern-cooled, and the
unfermented juice of a large red grape that was the
pride of Hebron. Bathsheba seldom touched wine, hav-
ing too many times seen David in his cups behaving with
inane vivacity, dancing before women and exposing him-
self. He had exposed himself before the Ark; and
though custom approved this-nothing was commoner
than for votaries to expose their secrets when worship-
ing their gods-she found it distasteful, possibly be-
cause of her first violent experience with him. But Bath-
sheba had her son's fondness for rich food.
When Solomon entered her tent he saw at once, even
in the dim light, that her appearance was striking. In
her hair she wore clusters of crimson flowers. Her dress.
was a loose robe, reaching to the ground; it was fitted
with long and very wide sleeves. A girdle was draped
many times round her waist, with the ends loosely tied
over the navel and hanging down in front. Her tunic
was of white linen, her girdle of crimson wool. She wore
106
no ornaments but in her hand she carried a small and
exquisitely carved serpent.
She arose when he entered, though as the Queen
Mother it was her privilege to sit; and Solomon looked
her over, observing the way her hair had been massed
and shaped and sniffing the sweet scents in which she
had been bathed. His mother was always as delightful
in her odors as she was forthright in her speech. If he
could build a worldly kingdom, she had the qualities of
a sagacious and stately Queen Mother.
His first words shocked her-but only a little. He
said: "Ahiah has been telling me many things for he
seems to know all things. In Egypt, he says, it is common.
for brothers to mate with sisters-even sometimes sons

the mother he begets himself as his own father and is then
assured of rebirth and of everlasting immortality."
"I'd never heard of that," Bathsheba murmured,

"If I mated with you," said Solomon, looking at her,
"I'd be my own father. Then I'd be a god, and as the
mother of a god you'd be a goddess."
For most people of this time it was a commonplace
notion. But it was strange for the Hebrews, who held
incest to be an abominable sin.
"The Egyptians must be a queer people," she mur-

"Oh no. Most likely we are the queer ones. Ahiah
says they have had a magnificent kingdom for untold.
generations. They were great and renowned even long
before my people began to wander in the deserts."
He did not observe the strange lights in her eyes.
She said: "When are you going?"
107
"Soon. I wonder if it would be all right for me to

"Oh no!" she cried, horrified. "By the holy thunders,

SOLOMON ASSIGNED ONE of his most trusted spies to
the task of watching Naamah and prepared for his jour
ney to Egypt, though he waited until the terrific summer
heat abated. He thought of going by chariot but his few
vehicles, built for war, were very uncomfortable, hav-
ing no seats and nothing to cushion the shocks of con-
tinuous jolting. They were, in fact, little more than a
stoneoak or hornbeam axle fitted to two stout wheels,
and bearing a small platform on which two warriors.
could ride. He had ridden in a chariot over the stony
hills and had had his bowels almost shaken out of him.
He decided to ride the royal ass.
111
And when at last he set out he was a preposterous
figure, even by the standards of his time; and this he
fully realized. With heralds going before him and a
naked slave running on either side, striving to protect
him from sun, wind and insects, he rode at the head of
his entourage. Because Ahiah had told him that Egyp-
tians were fond of music and had many instruments,
from the tinkling sistrum to a huge harp with a dozen
strings, Solomon took with him a score of musicians;
and with the shrill notes of the ugab and the pounding
of the toph in his ears, he looked behind him and saw
first his bodyguard, all mounted, and then his supply
train enveloped by clouds of dust. To protect him from
the heat the two slaves walking at his side held above
him a kind of parasol.
It was early morning when the journey began and
the air was still cool. Solomon sat astride the ass on a
huge cushion, and though he was fairly comfortable,
now and then his feet dragged over thornbush or stones.
He was troubled by a dream which he had had. He
had climbed a tree so tall that he had been able to look
clear across Egypt; and he had at first supposed that
the dream meant that he would ascend to a position of
great power. But he could not remember having de-
scended from the tree. He called to Ahiah and the
scribe came up and rode at his side, with the frantic
slaves now trying to serve both of them. After telling
the dream, with no embellishments at all, Solomon asked
what it meant.
Ahiah said anxiously, "You didn't fall, did you?"
"No. But I don't think I came down. Does it mean
that the gods took me up into the sky?"
"My Lord, I don't know."
Solomon had heard that in Babylon all the kings be-
came gods. There temples were built in their honor and
112
images of them were set in the holy places, where the
people came to offer gifts and to worship. Kings of
Egypt were deified during their lifetime and reverenced
as gods.
"Is the Pharaoh a god, Ahiah?"
"My Lord, so the people believe. The Pharaoh is
the great One, the golden Horus, the son of Ra. He
claims authority over all lands and people, even over
the sun, and the sky and all that is in the sky and the
earth and all that is upon the earth, and every creature
that walks on two legs or four, and all things that fly
or flutter."
"Is that all?" asked Solomon drily. "If he holds
dominion over all things then I must be one of his
subjects."
"From his point of view, my Lord, you are."
Solomon was startled. Then his eyes filled with the
soft light of mirth and he said:
"I'll leave to him dominion over all things that
flutter."
Ahiah's face broke into a smile of joy; he liked noth-
ing better than to find his King in a humorous or a
philosophic mood. "May I humbly suggest that you take
dominion over all things then I must be one of his
things that crawl?"
Solomon snorted. "I'd like to tell him that, the old
goat. Monuments will be erected to me that will chron-
icle my achievements. The skies will open to acclaim
me." And then slyly he added, looking at Ahiah: "So
one boasts who has little."
Ahiah rose to the occasion. "Inscribed on your monu-
ments will be these words: I built a house for the gods
with long cedar beams laid upon broad stone tables;
and there was none like it in all the world, all people did
envy it. I built a great palace for myself and my women
113
and around it were groves and fields of lilies and the
fountains of living waters; and there were none other
so beautiful, no, not one. The doors were of liaru-wood,
of pungent odor, and the floor beams were of stoneoak
and the columns were of white birch-"
"Fine, fine!" cried Solomon. "Ahiah, you will write
my records."
"I built walls and terraces and towers, gates of bronze
and great dwellings, and all kings envied me and came
to bow before me, seeking my wisdom. All kings brought
to me costly gifts. I wore royal linen and purple and
my tables were laden with the choicest viands and wines,
and at nighttime my mistress-" Ahiah was slyly
watching his King "-was like myrrh, and my face on
her breasts was a bee sucking the fruits-"
"Eh!" said Solomon. He was looking at his scribe
with open astonishment.
"How beautiful are your feet in sandals, O Princess
of Egypt-and your navel is like the wine-jar, scented
and open, and your belly is like the harvest under the
booths! O Princess of Egypt, your breasts are like twin
moons-
""
"Here, here!" said Solomon. "Where did you go
that night in Megiddo? You slipped away from me."
"My Lord, the gods did not create me to lie with
women."
"Nonsense, my dear Ahiah. You must have beauti-
ful women-many women." After staring at Ahiah a few
moments Solomon concluded that the man was a poet;
and he beamed upon the gnomelike little fellow, whose
mind seemed to be so astonishingly unlike his body.
Then, looking away toward Egypt, he wondered if a
princess awaited him whose navel was like a scented
wine-jar. These thoughts put him in an amorous mood.
He was on the point of sending back for a concubine
114
when, rounding a turn, he came abruptly to the gate
and well of Bethlehem, and saw Zeruah there. Surely
she must have placed herself in the way of the King;
his heralds had entered the village some time before,
announcing his coming, and there she stood with her

herself pretty. The King was enthralled. He dismounted
and approached her and when she made a move to pros-
trate herself he commanded her to stand. Then he
looked her up and down, noting the loveliness which
her garment did not and was not intended to conceal.

"Zeruah, daughter of Ahban who lives in Bethle-

“I have a baal, my Lord, and his name is Nebat."
Zikla came up and said in a low voice: "He's a stupid

"She's like a green grove in the house of Astarte.
Perhaps Nebat would like a gift."

Solomon was staring at Zikla but in his large strong

When the journey was resumed Solomon sent the

These words, so laden with truth, depressed Solomon,

He was a messenger from the Pharaoh. Flinging him-

Solomon was deeply pleased. For one thing, Yah had

116
it was an auspicious sign to be greeted by a king's emis-

"Arise," he said.
The man arose and backed away, bowing and fawn-

that he might run short of provisions, and so dispatched
Zikla to Hebron to gather food. Solomon liked this valley.
About four thousand feet above the sea and cooler than
Jerusalem it had many acres of rich soil free of stones.
Judea was shrub and thorn with only a few cultivated
patches; in the whole breadth and length of it there was
only this small valley which the gods seemed to favor.
Here were groves of scarlet oak and vineyards and
fields of corn.
The next morning on resuming their journey Solomon
said to the scribe: "Is it true that Abraham, our an-
cient father, was the sun? So Nathan once told me."
"My Lord, I have no knowledge of such things."
"Why did our people come here instead of going
north?"
"I do not know."
The King shifted now and then to ease the strain
on his back or dismounted and walked, with a slave
leading his beast. When he rode or when he walked two
wretched men trudged at his side, trying to shelter him
against heat and pests. For their bleeding limbs and
for their faces, gray with fatigue and famine, Solomon
had no compassion, and Yah certainly had none, for
these were base and wicked Canaanites whom David
had enslaved. They were inferiors, fit only to serve
and die.
Having turned westward from Hebron, Solomon
was now passing up one of the bleak defiles of the hill
117
country, its sides shingled with loose stones and its crest

They offered him soured goatmilk in which had been

The heat and dust and boredom of travel might have
turned any man's mind to thoughts of evil, but Solomon's
temper was tranquil. He was young, he was dreaming
great dreams, and he was looking forward to Egypt
where he expected to learn a great deal. He could have
gone to the coast and thence by ship, so sparing him-
self this tedious and dangerous journey, but he wanted
to have a look at the caravan route, and, above all, at
the country which the Pharaoh would have to pass over
if he came against Israel.
He was dreaming dreams. Never for a moment did
he suspect that on a distant hill he was being watched
and hated by a lean brown man in a goatskin garment.
He was thinking of the wealth he would some day have
and of the ways by which he would get his hands on
it. Because all traffic between countries in the north and
those in the south had to go by sea or traverse land
which Solomon controlled, he was in a position to levy
taxes on some of the trade routes of the surrounding
kingdoms and he intended to levy all the toll he could
squeeze out of them. He also hoped to develop a very
118
large export-import trade. He could export dibs and

and wines, cloth, gems, ivory, furnishings, gold and spi-
ces. He might also export slaves. The Phoenicians were
in the slave-traffic, buying prisoners of war and shipping
them to countries in the far west, where they were sold.
They had bought and shipped all the Hebrews cap-

They spent a day crossing the scalped and feature-
less hills west of Hebron. It was noon of the fourth
day when they reached Gaza. About three miles inland
and standing on a hill that rose a hundred feet above
the surrounding plain, Gaza was an outpost on the edge
of the wilderness. Because it had many wells of pure
water, the nomadic thieves of the trackless wastes east
and south came here to fill their waterskins and to barter
trinkets for food. From here trade routes went east to
Beersheba and thence to the salt sea, and from there
across desert east and south; and north up the coast to
Tyre or across Israel and thence to Damascus and
points east and north; and south to Egypt. Across the
deadly sands from Gaza to Egypt was a march of eight
days, with luck and strong beasts. It was a dangerous
journey in any season, but particularly in the spring
when hot winds from the south came in great blinding
mists of fine sand, hiding the sun and pouring terrors
upon the earth.
"Our Lord the King," said the wily and suspicious
seren of Gaza, "are you going to cross the wilderness?"
"With the help of the gods.'
"Why don't you go by sea?"
"Travel by sea makes me ill," said Solomon, who
had never been on the sea.
The seren was not convinced. He was a crafty rascal
who lived in suspense between two kingdoms, never
knowing when one of them would march over him. He
supposed that Solomon had some sly purpose in mind.
Solomon in fact was looking round at this place to see
if it was worth conquering. If he controlled it he could
build a fort here against the Pharaoh but on reflection
he decided that a gesture so plain and deliberate would
anger Egypt's king.
120
He took an inventory of his provisions, filled the

NINE DAYS LATER the King of Israel, subdued by the
desert's subtle treacheries and dismayed by its evil pow-
ers, stood where the eastern branch of the Nile poured
into the great sea. He had crossed the wastelands of
death where no gods dwelt and now looked westward
to yellow mists blowing across a marsh. Into this bog or
into the quagmires flanking it, whole armies, Ahiah told
him, had vanished. The white sands drifting upon the
marshes gave to them the appearance of solid earth, and
not only men but beasts also walked into them and dis-
appeared. Ahiah said the demons of plagues lived there
and spread pestilence in all directions. Perhaps, Solomon
ventured, these were some of the entrances to the under-
ground world where the phantom-ghosts lived.
They left the desert with its soured wells, the delta with
its bogs and hastened on their way; and when they came

that he saw. In his sumptuous quarters he bathed and
rested and the next morning was standing with Ahiah and

"He's trying to impress me," he said. "He thinks I

"Well, I could do with less wisdom and a good deal

"For a very long time."
"While my ancestors were looking for mudholes!
Ahiah, I want things like these-palaces and gardens

"I will leave records of greater wonders in your king-

Solomon had expected to feel like a country oaf; he
had not foreseen that the pain in him would be so sharp
and deep. Two thousand years or more of social graces,
of art and learning, of magnificent buildings-and what
were his traditions but those of a simple and ignorant
-yes, and unwashed-people! What had been David's
social graces? What were Bathsheba's?
When Solomon presented himself to the Pharaoh he

self bitterly, did King Solomon have, whose father had
been a goatherd blowing on a flute and fighting with
a slingshot!
His humility was so deep and undisguised that he
won the heart of Ahiah. "My Lord, your crown will
be of wisdom. The future will not remember this king

"No, Ahiah, don't flatter me. I begin to see clearly

we can about it."
"My Lord," said Ahiah, "an evil woman is a deep
ditch and a foolish king is a dead tree; but a wise king

Savoring the meaning of the words Solomon looked

The villa assigned to Solomon-it was a palace, really,
and only a little smaller than the one the Pharaoh him-
self occupied-had great hallways and corridors, spacious

The walls and most of the ceilings had friezework of

flushed with pails of water, the water draining like the
bath water into underground pipes which carried it away.
On all sides the verandahs, with open casements, looked

From the first Ahiah had believed that the Sun of Sun's

and powers. It is true that the Pharaoh had assigned to his

While considering these amazing words Solomon look-

This woman, it seemed to him on first glance, was home-

wine and the itch of envy, King Solomon, who was used

thought he saw a tall willowy scarecrow, flat and thin of
chest, with lean shanks faintly visible under a semitrans-

grotesque, and with the peculiar Egyptian face in which
the point of the hair, the point of the nose and the point
129
of the chin lay in the circumference of a circle that was
twice as large as the skull. As a group the Egyptian aris-
tocrats were tall and slender, with oval faces, sloping fore-
heads, long straight noses, receding chins, and large lum-
inous eyes. Their skins were quite white at birth but dark-
ened under the furies of the African sun. Their hair was
dark and sometimes curly. The men shaved their lips and
chins and not infrequently their heads, so that they could
more comfortably wear their enormous wigs. The women
bobbed their hair.
Khate exaggerated all the features typical of the royal
family. Her nose was longer and sharper, her chin was
even more inconspicuous, and her eyes, because of her pale
thin face, looked almost terrifyingly large. Her bobbed
hair had gray in it-or was that a wig? Her voice was
clear, modulated and musical, and she was the superb mis-
tress of it. Solomon was looking at the most cultured
woman he was ever to meet face to face, and possibly it
was a sense of this that made him look awkward.
The Prince excused himself, bowed, and backed away,
with a faint smile on his face as though he had contrived
a piece of mischief; and Solomon was left alone with the
woman. With her thin brows-they had been plucked to
a narrow black line-lifted in gentle wonder, and her head
tilted up and back, so that she seemed to be looking down
at him for she was fully as tall as he was-she waited
for him to speak. She knew, of course, that her father had
given her to this King and would send her away for life
to a place called Jerusalem, somewhere in the northern
deserts. But Khate was a philosopher; she did not resist
those matters over which she had no control.
"What did he say your name is ?" asked Solomon, in
her language.
"Oh, my Lord the King, you speak our language! How
glad I am, for I am too ignorant to speak yours. My
130
name? Well, every Egyptian has two names, as you
know the true name and the good name, the great name
and the little name. The little name is the only one we ever
reveal. My little name is Khate."
"Why do you hide your other name?"
"That turns on an old superstition. Isis-the goddess,
you know is said to have extraordinary power with
words. She became when a mere woman bored with life,
and wished to be transported to the world of the gods.
If she could learn the secret name of the sun-god she
could become a goddess. Well, he was an old god, he
slobbered over himself, he drooled when angry; and so
Isis gathered some of his spittle and made a serpent of it
and laid it in his path. When the serpent stung him he
gave off a great cry. Poison burst from him. Then-but
I am sure I must weary you. Shall we sit?"
She thought she had wearied him because Solomon had
placed a hand to his brow. But he was not weary. The sly
fellow was merely studying her gown. It was embroidered
in religious panels, three below her waist and two above.
While Solomon was wondering what the scenes indicated
his eyes roved to her pubic area; and with a start which
she could hardly have missed he saw with astonishment,
through her almost-transparent gown, that her hair had
been shorn. Hebrew women never polled themselves, save
partially in times of mourning; and under no circumstances
would they have shaved their bodies. Egyptian women.
on the other hand believed that the body's offensive odors
clung to hair, and so sometimes removed all hair, from
head to feet. All offensive things on the body must be shed,
and after the body was thoroughly cleansed it must be per-
fumed and adorned. In their knowledge of cosmetics and
colors, and of the odors of love, Egyptian women were
wise indeed-and wise also in the use of diaphanous gar-
ments, knowing how much to reveal and how much to hide.
131
When Solomon took his hand from his brow and look-
ed at her he saw amusement in her eyes. He followed her
to a luxurious couch and they sat, this man whose people
found human nakedness shameful, this woman who with
unabashed candor would discuss with anyone all matters
pertaining to sex. Girls down here were nubile at ten or
eleven years of age and might, when so young, submit to
sexual embrace in the sanctuaries. In fact, Egyptian
fathers sometimes hired their daughters out; and though
custom did not sanction such avarice it did not disapprove.
As for incest, it was commonplace; the terms brother and
sister had the same emotional content as loved and be-
loved.
Khate was to be amused many times by the sterner
moral code of Solomon's people. "Are you hungry?" she
asked.
"A little," said Solomon, still troubled by her lack of
hair.
"I hope my father noted your preferences in food.
Sometimes he is careless."
"Oh yes," said Solomon, but his thoughts were wander-
ing.
"And in music."
"Oh. Were you speaking to me?"
"My Lord, to whom else would I dare to speak in
your presence?"
That question fetched him back. He looked at her and
their gaze met—and if Khate had looked at his thoughts
in mirrors she could not have read them more clearly.
"Ah well," she said, "all my people talk too much.
Your people, I imagine, are reticent and profound. Mine
are gay and superficial." She put a hand delicately to her
mouth to cover yawn.
Solomon was still looking into her eyes, his gaze so
132
searching that she became ill at ease. The powder on her
pale face became rosy.
"My Lord, are you looking for my secret name?" Del-
icately all her gestures were delicate-she shrugged. She
was a princess of Egypt. She wondered if Israel's King
would ever dare to treat her with cruelty or contempt.
"My Lord, how many wives do you have?"
"Why do you ask?"
"Oh, it's another superstition. I'm curious to know what
my number will be. My people think there is magic in num-
bers. I'd not want to be seven or any multiple of seven
for in that case I would get sick. Have you by any chance
six or thirteen or twenty? If so, I shudder."
What the emotion in Solomon's face was she could not
tell. She thought that possibly she ought to be demure
and silent; but for Khate, whose secret name was Maa,
meaning truth, to be silent in a world which she found in-
exhaustibly fascinating was out of the question. Just the
same, she sat back and waited, studying him meanwhile
under long lashes, her eyes turning almost cold in her
moments of reflection. She waited, but Solomon's atten-
tion was fixed on the musicians and furnishings and the
preparations for the feast. He was observing how things
were done by people for whom great banquets had be-
come second nature. He hoped to be able some day to
give feasts in a setting as brilliant as this one. Looking
over, he saw that Ahiah was busy with his writing-materi-
als and abruptly he rose and crossed the chamber to
speak with him.
"By the gods, she's homely, but I think I may like her."
"Does she talk too much?"
"What woman doesn't?"
Said Ahiah drily: "Like a jewel of gold in a pig's snout
is a woman without discretion. Is she intelligent?"
133
"I think maybe she is but queer. She will teach me
things."
When he sank again to the couch where Khate was re-
clining she looked at him a few moments and said: "My
Lord, is it true that my people once enslaved your peo-
ple?"
That question, asked innocently enough, released some
force in Solomon. He arose so quickly that he seemed to
have been propelled and turned to look down at her.
There were such dreadful things in his eyes that she came
first to a sitting posture and then to her feet, as though
drawn by a force outside her being; and the two of them
looked at one another. He became aware that deep eyes,
with gray and green in them, were looking into his eyes
and that a gentle voice was saying:
"I'm sorry, my Lord, if I was stupid. Your scribe tells
me that you're a very wise king and I know I shall be
honored to be one of your queens. Listen, do you like
music?"
12
SOLOMON AND KHATE occupied one of the large couches
and had propped themselves with pillows so that they half-
reclined, as was the custom, in a position to eat. Solomon
intended to enjoy his dinner, which he had reason to be-
lieve would be sumptuous, but his first interest lay in ob-
serving all the details of arrangement.
The banquet-hall had a great many lamps, cunningly
placed; it was decorated with green branches and vines
and flowers, and flowers were everywhere strewn over
the floor and couches and tables. There were many musi-
134
cians, and so far as he could tell they had no director but
did pretty much as they pleased. Some were singing hymns
to the sun:
Creator of the germ in women
maker of the seed in men
giving life to the son in the body of the mother
nursing him in the womb that he may not weep nor
be afraid....
Some merely caroled, like birds, warbling and gurgling
as though their throats were music-boxes. All people but
Solomon's own thought that music had been invented by
the gods and imbued with magic. For the Egyptians all
music was divine. For Solomon, who had a fair ear, music
was little more than noise, particularly that produced by
wind and percussion instruments.
After most of the guests had arrived-in a vestibule
they were sprinkled with perfumes and crowned with flow-
ers-naked boys and girls, chosen for their beauty, came
with bowls of wine. Egyptian wines Solomon had been
studying since his arrival. From Egypt and elsewhere he
intended to import the choicest-from Mareotis, from
Syena, and the fine dry wines from Chios. He had learned
that Egyptian common folk drank huge quantities of
beer-it was in fact their national drink; but though he
had sampled their beer, cold and hot, sweet, sparkling and
perfumed, he did not care for it. All of it, it seemed to
him, was either sour or bitter or insipid.
A master of ceremonies had been chosen and to him
slaves now came with bowls of water and wine and small
jars containing various essences; and with a great flour-
ish-for he was proud of the honor-he mixed the wines
with water, honey and spices, now and then pausing to
taste and roll his eyes at the guests. Solomon was watch-
ing him when Khate said:
135
"My Lord, do you care for these things?"
Turning, Solomon saw a naked boy standing near with
a plate of delicacies, including salted cheese, onions fried
and spiced, anchovies, tiny spiced sausages, goose livers,
pieces of crab, cuttlefish, nettles that had been steeped in
hot oil, sesame cakes sprinkled with anise, the breasts of
small birds, and mushroom buttons. It was the custom
with Egyptians, as with all people, to eat with their fin-
gers, using neither knife nor fork and but rarely a spoon.
With thumb and finger Solomon now chose among the
dainties, while Khate offered suggestions.
"Our food," she said, "is not so good this evening. I
imagine it is better in your banquet-hall."
In his banquet-hall!
"My Lord, may I suggest this?" She pointed to a name-
less delicacy that tasted of coriander.
Two bands were now playing at the far end of the
hall, each indifferent to and, it seemed to Solomon, con-
temptuous of one another's music. One was of women,
who played on stringed instruments; the other was of men
who blew on reedlike things and smote on drums. There
was no harmony in the music; its only purpose was to pro-
mote excitement and heighten the sensuous delight of the
diners. Solomon looked over at the Pharaoh who, occupy-
ing a couch with one of his wives, was sipping wine from
a golden bowl. He then looked curiously at the vessels on
the tables near him; they seemed to be wrought of solid
gold, though some of them were possibly of electrum,
which had in it less gold than silver. With an unhappy sigh
he compared the wealth and splendors of this king to his
own clay vessels and goathair tents.
"My Lord, are you weary so soon?"
"Do I seem weary?"
"I heard you sigh. The food, I admit, isn't what it
136
should be, and the music tonight is abominable. But this
wine is excellent. May I serve you?"
Waiters, acting a little drunk, now rushed gaily before
those who reclined on couches, setting on the tables the
wine-jars and garnished dishes. Other servants came with
sweet-smelling unguents, shaped in cones, and one of
these, with the base of the cone down, was set in the hair
or wig of each person. Observing the expression on Sol-
omon's face Khate laughed and said:
"It won't bite you."
"What is it?" he asked, for it was up in his hair and
he could not see it. The one on Khate's head had been
thrust down into the great mass of her wig.
"It's a lump of sweetened oil. It'll melt and run down
over your face and garments-oh, good Ammon, you'll
be a mess!-and then a servant will fetch another."
Solomon was smiling. He realized now that there was
a great deal of warmth in this woman. Flushed with
Chian wine he looked at her and said:
"You'll be a jewel in my city."
"How exquisitely you flatter me!"
Female servants came, bearing lovely nosegays of
lotus and poppy; and somewhere a man exploded a loud
oath and said he could drink any man under the couches.
Khate gave a little shudder and said:
"At your banquets, my Lord, does everyone get
drunk?"
At his banquets! "Is it the custom here to get drunk?"
he asked, looking round at the people, a few of whom
were staggering.
"Oh yes," she said. "My people are drunkards." She
touched him with the most delicately intimate gesture
and added in a low voice: "My father will be as drunk
as a god before morning."
"By my beard, no! Will he?"
137
Above the music of lutes and harps a male voice was
singing:
How manifold are all your works, O Ra!
You did create the earth, and you alone
making all that walk upon the earth
and all things that fly and creep
and setting man in his place
and setting woman by his side...
"My Lord, your eyes look tired and inflamed. Did
you cross the deserts? Over here we suffer all the time
from sore eyes because of the winds and sands."
"Is there no cure?" asked Solomon, sipping his wine.
"Oh indeed there is. It is said in ancient writings
that the urine of a faithful wife is an unfailing cure."
Solomon wondered if she was jesting. "If so, why
isn't it used?"
"Oh dear!" she said, chiding him archly. "Are you
so young? My father has searched ten years for a faith-
ful wife-and goes on searching."
Solomon was still smiling. Ahiah had told him that
adultery in Egypt was looked upon with good-natured
tolerance. In a Hebrew wife it was punished with death.
"I'm sure I could find subjects that would interest
you if I weren't so dull. The gods?"
"How many do you have?" he asked, and beckoned
to a servant who had a tray of delicacies.
"A great many. I have only one myself."
"Only one?" asked Solomon, looking at her.
"Isn't one enough? Could I believe in Isis who was
impregnated by a flash of lightning?-or in Attis, the
sacred pine tree whom they seek for three days and
nights at the spring equinox and find that on the third

138
three days? My Lord, is it because man is blessed with
the trinity?"
"I don't know," said Solomon, astonished by her way
of putting things. "Do you really believe there's only

"I do. Akhnaton said there is only one."
"What-was this Akhnaton a man?"
"A king of Egypt who lived long ago."
"Well, then, what is this god's name?"
"I don't think he has a name. He is simply God."
"I'd never heard of him. Is he the sun ?"
"Oh no. Nor the moon, nor any of the stars. He is
a great force for good."
In all his life Solomon had heard nothing so amazing.
"Do your people have many gods?"
"Well, they believe there are many but there is one
who is their god. His name is Yah."
"What is he like?"
"Oh-Well, he's a god of war, he's a very fierce and
vindictive god-"
"God is love," she said.
"What?" He was beginning to feel unhappily that the
woman was mad. "Love?"
"God is love. He is the spirit of compassion, mercy,
forgiveness, tenderness—"
"What god?"
"Just God the Father of all people."
He was staring at her with curious and searching in-
terest. "Really? Where did you pick up such absurd no-
tions? Every city has its god. In my language he is called
the baal. Every kingdom has its god-or many gods-"
"Oh no, my Lord. Forgive me, but those creatures are
myths. They have no existence. Does this god Yah look
like a man?"
"He takes the form of a man and talks to his people."
139
"You don't believe it! God doesn't look like a man.
He is the power in the sun, he is the sun's heat, he is
intelligence, he is goodness and love. He is in all things
for he is the creator of all things."
Solomon made a strange sound that came up from the
depths of the man. Fascinated by this woman, whose verbal
fluency was more astonishing than her appearance, he
looked past her long nose and into the lovely shadows
and lights of her eyes. "He is love?"
"He is our Father who is in heaven."
"Heaven? I don't know that word."
"Heaven is that which makes birds sing and lambs
play. A moment ago I saw heaven in your eyes when you
were looking at the lights in your wine." And while Solo-

"God has no image."
"Oh, no image?"
"He has no form, no shape, being in all things with
all things in him."
Solomon turned away. Her heresies were like rich food;
he could take only so much of them. Out in the room.
girls were clapping their hands or shaking castanets while
mummers rolled and tumbled; and from the far side,
toward the kitchens, servants now came with food. They
came with breast of mutton, roasted in clarified butter
and stuffed with delicacies; with huge dishes of minced
meats, cabbage leaves, chopped onions, rice, peppers, all
saturated with butter; tomatoes dripping with the juice
of boiled meats; many kinds of fish baked in butter and
oils and spiced with many condiments; sweetcakes baked
in layers, with honey spread on one layer, preserved
quince on another, honeyed dates on a third. Egyptians
were gluttons and had the best cooks in the world. The
tables along the couches, including the three assigned to
Solomon and Khate, were now laden with more steaming
140
and odorous dishes than Solomon had ever dreamed of.
Everything that he sampled delighted him. Like his
people, Egyptians ate with the right hand, thrusting it
into dishes and keeping the left hand free and clean to
lift the wine-cup. Solomon poked his hand into every dish
before him and fed the dripping or pouring handfuls
into his mouth. He liked particularly the lamb, roasted
with oil of sesame and powdered pistachio nuts and soaked
with hot aromatics such as cumin and coriander, ginger,
cinnamon, peppers and onion juices. The whole mass of
it, flesh and dressing, had been drenched with lotus-
waters and infused with musk, though now and then in
the dressing, such as stuffed eggs, Solomon bit into the
unmistakable flavor of limes or aloeswood. It was a
strongly spiced and highly seasoned feast, and the rich-
ness of butter and oils was dripping from every feeding
hand. The unguents on heads had melted and run down
over faces; servants now came, bringing fresh cones.
Here and there in the hall was the sick sound of some
glutton who was eating too fast or engorging only the
richest foods; and above all the sounds of eating and
drinking was the music, for two competing bands were
still playing. Fresh dishes were brought in: roasted goose
stuffed with spiced richness, and peacocks—and when
Khate saw the peacock she quietly observed:
"You're a favorite with my father. He serves pea-
cock only to those whom he most highly esteems."
"I am indeed honored," said Solomon gravely.
"Do you have cats in your city?"
"Cats?"
"My people worship cats. I don't but I'm fond of
them. Did you know that we have embalmed thousands
of cats?"
Solomon had a fistful of peacock dressing halfway to
his mouth when her question fell on his ear. Beginning
141
to feel that the whole scene, with its dreadful cacoph-
onies of music and gluttony, was unreal and somehow
sinister, he carried the dressing to his mouth but slowly
as though expecting to bite into a mummy. And as if
the master of ceremonies had sensed his bewilderment,
there now came, with great shouts and beating of drums,
a clown dancing with the mummy of a woman.
"That," said Khate, observing his interest, "is proba-
bly one of the daughters of the god Seb and the goddess
Nut."
"Was it once a woman?"
"Probably the Lady of Beer."
"Who?" he asked hoarsely.
"Isis, the Lady of Beer. Isis is supposed to have in-
vented that terrible drink." She poured from a wine-jar
into Solomon's cup and, leaning archly toward him, said:
"We've also embalmed thousands of dragons."
"Oh?" he said, watching the mummy jigging along
with the clown. "I suppose you've also embalmed jinn
and demons and ghosts."
She perceived that she had offended him. It was a
measure of Khate's character and tact that she now re-
lieved him of his embarrassment by taking the burden
onto herself. The mistress of her face and emotions,
she furtively reached behind her and up and yanked at
her wig, with the result that the half-melted cone came
tumbling down over her face and garments. With a gasp
of horror she looked at it and called to a passing servant;
and in the confusion of putting the greasy mess back
into her wig, Solomon recovered his poise, never sus-
pecting for a moment how unerringly this woman was
reading his thoughts.
"What a fumbling creature I can be!" she cried, look-
ing at the stains around her.
Solomon had eaten too much and was now in distress
142
and he was a little dizzy from the wine. He looked over
at Ahiah and then crossed the hall to whisper in the
scribe's ear.
"Do these people embalm dragons?"
"I don't know."
"You go talk to her a while. I want to move around."
Getting to his feet, Ahiah looked down mournfully at
his materials and then crossed the chamber to bow low

occupy a couch with a princess, and least of all with a
princess of Egypt; but she told him to sit, and dismissed

"I wearied your King. Will he get drunk?"
"My Lady-"
"Most kings get as drunk as hoot owls. What is your
name?"
"Ahiah."
"Does your King like women? I felt that he did not.
Among your people women are not so fortunate as

"Yes, my Lady."
"Are our customs very different from yours?"
"Well, yes-"

Your King?"

"Oh, don't lie about it. It is said bitter almonds will

143
father I'll have some prepared for your King. Now do

"Yes, my Lady."
"Does your city have such buildings as ours and pavil-

Returning to his writing-materials he sat for a little

Wine mocks the thoughts and strong drink rages in
the heart; and whosoever is deceived thereby is not a

EASTWARD FROM AHBAN'S HOUSE toward the salt sea
were two ravines and beyond them a third that fell steeply
downward in the direction of the Jabbok. In this third
ravine until late summer a tiny trickle of water ran and
after that until the rains came there was only a muddy
basin where Ahban's sheep sometimes tried to drink.
144
One evening, about a week after Solomon passed
through Bethlehem, Ahban was following his sheep down
this ravine when with a shudder and a cry of horror, he
stepped on the outflung arm of a dead man. That alone
was enough to paralyze him, for uncleanness, like holi-
ness was contagious. Everything that touched a corpse.
was unclean for seven days. "I'm unclean!" he thought,
despairing; and then his distress was multiplied a hundred-
fold. On looking down he saw the purple bloated face of
his soninlaw.
With a dreadful cry that rang all the way to his house
he flung himself back and raised both hands to ward off
and strike at the multitude of evils. Only by drawing on
all the courage he had was he able at last to advance and
bend forward, his eyes searching the naked body for
wounds. But Ahban knew by the face that Nebat had been
choked to death. He also knew that he had not been dead
for more than a day and a night; in the previous evening
he had trailed his sheep up this ravine.
"Nebat!" he croaked. "Nebat!"
Realizing then that night was almost upon him Ahban
ran away from his sheep, plunging up the ravine like a
man pursued by demons and feeling all over his quivering
flesh the terrors of flight. When he gained the top, Mara
wild-eyed and trembling, was there to meet him. She had
heard his cry.
"Where" he began but his voice failed him. His chin
danced up and down and the muscles of his neck twitched.
His voice was lost. He pointed toward the ravine and
tried again like a tortured stutterer to speak.
"May Yah protect us!" cried Mara.
"Where" Trying to control his shaking body he be-
gan to walk, striding back and forth, his eyes looking
wildly round him for a sign of his daughter. "Where-
Zeruah !"
145
"Oh! Zeruah!"
"Zeruah!"
With her son Jeroboam Zeruah was visiting a neighbor;
when she heard her name called she came running. She
stopped running the moment she could see her father
clearly. In his posture and in the way he held his hands
there was a menace that fetched her down to a walk, a slow
walk, almost pensive. When Ahban shouted to her to
hasten she stopped. Furious, he went forth to meet her,
with Mara quaking at his heels. Looking demure and with
something in her eyes that only her god could have under-
stood, Zeruah waited for her father to come up, one hand
reaching back to comfort her son who was hiding behind
her. Ahban came close to her and looked into her eyes and
never had she seen her father in such a rage. Knowing that
he held over her the absolute power of life or death, that
he might flog her, sell her into slavery, strip her naked and
drive her into the wilderness, or kill her, she looked into
his inflamed eyes and did not shrink.
"Your baal has gone away!" The statement, so far as
he could tell, did not produce in her any response at all.
Did her upper eyelids droop a little, as they sometimes
did, giving to the smoky depths of her eyes a suggestion of
wantonness? Was her lower lip quivering?
"Woman, your baal has gone! Can you hear me?"
"Yes, father."
"He has gone!-gone!-gone!"
"Father, I can hear you."
"But you do not weep!"
"Yes, father, I grieve, I sorrow."
What was in her heart? Was she feeling grief? No, not
that. Had she known that he would be slain? It is possible
that she had sensed it. She had stood by the well with the
eyes of the King on her and had known then that she
would be one of his women...
146
"You do not sorrow, you wicked creature!"
"Yes, father, I sorrow."
Zeruah knew the duties of a widow. The word widow
in her language meant a dumb woman because of the long
period of silence imposed on her; but before the period of
silence there must be the signs and ceremonials of grief.
So abruptly that Ahban was startled, she flung her hands
above her head and brought them down with all her
strength, rending her garment; and when she heard the
cloth tear she began to lament, her voice trilling like a
sweet song in its sorrow. With fingernails she tore at her-
self to bring blood but she did not attack her face and
breasts, as so many widows did. The King would look at
these and with these she might have her way with him.
She had interrupted her grieving to ask herself, "Will
I always be a concubine?" and Ahban now leapt forward
and clutched her shoulders.
"You wicked woman, you're not sorrowing!" If she
did not weep and lament and rend herself Nebat's ghost
would haunt him all the days of his life.
"But, father, I am!" she cried, and she broke away
from him and ran toward the house, shrieking and tearing
her garment.
Ahban dared not leave Nebat all night for the hyenas
and other dragons might find and devour him; but like
most people of his time he lived in terror of darkness.
Glancing wildly round him he saw that the night had
come.
"Mara!"
"Yes, Ahban."
"We must go!"
The night moved in, black and quiet with neither moon
nor stars. Zeruah had gone to her mat in the corner of
the hut and was sitting there, listening, with her son by
147
her. When she heard her parents coming she began to wail
and shriek. Ahban looked in and said:
"We must go get Nebat!"
"Yes, father." She came outside, making sounds of grief
to deceive him.
Mara turned away convulsively and sneezed and at
once Ahban seized the lobes of her ears and yanked them
downward. If a person sneezed during the time of death
and his ears were soundly yanked he could not be blamed
for the misfortune.
"Peace be with him!" Mara cried.
"May Yah keep him and watch over him!"
"Father, we must go."
"See if we have lights."
Zeruah went inside and returned with two fagots that
had been soaked with pitch. She filled the bowl of a lamp
with oil, taking the very last which Ahban had hoarded.
for an emergency. Then she ran to a neighbor's to light
one of the fagots. When she returned, Ahban was wring-
ing his hands and crying:
"Where can I find a red calf?" When persons became
unclean by touching the dead they could purify themselves
by washing their hands above a female red calf or a red
cow, without blemish and without marks of the yoke, that
had been slaughtered by a stream.
"Father, let's not worry about that now."
She touched fire to the other torch and to the wick of
the lamp. She gave the lamp to her mother and one of the
torches to her father and then she brought a mat out of
the hut. She told her son to stay in the house because
night-demons and Lilit especially were the vindictive ene-
mies of children.
"I could help," said Jeroboam, a bold lad who would
grow up to be a bold man.
"No, my son. We won't be long."
148
Zeruah went ahead, holding a torch aloft. A light was
a wonderful thing for it was a part of divinity; it scattered
the darkness and terrors, it made golden rooms in the
night. No demon, not even the most foolhardy, dared to
come within the illumination cast by fire. He dared not
show himself in daylight but hid under stones and in ill-
smelling bogs and in lonely thickets.
For the terrified Ahban this experience was almost more
than he could endure. For an hour they struggled with the
corpse up the ravines until they came at last, exhausted by
labor and fear, to their threshold; and they took Nebat
inside and laid him by a wall, covering him with such
materials as they had. All night Ahban sat on his bed and
shook with grief and fear. Later, they buried the corpse,
observing all the rules which Yah had given to Moses; and
though Zeruah did not pull her hair or lacerate her flesh
her grief seemed so sincere that Ahban was deceived. He
gave two of his sheep for a small female calf whose red
color was that of blood; he slaughtered the beast by the
mudhole where Nebat had been slain; and he and Mara
and Zeruah and Jeroboam washed their hands above
the carcass to cleanse themselves. They would be unclean
for seven days. Their house was defiled and everything in
it. But with the use of pure water and the blood and ashes
of the red calf and with cedar and hyssop they purified
themselves on the third and seventh days; and then Ahban
felt better. He had obeyed all the rules which Yah had
given him.
All the rules but one.
In the desert blood-vengeance had been a tribal law.
There it had been an eye for an eye and a tooth for a
tooth. Ahban knew that he ought to swear vengeance
against the murderer of Nebat; his religion commanded
it; but Mara asked him to wait.
149
"Wait," she said, "till Ahijah comes. He will tell you
what to do."
One morning after the cleansing had been completed
Zeruah was out by the community oven, a pit in the earth
in the shape of a huge round pot, being about three
feet in diameter and the same depth, its sides plastered
with lime. Fire was placed in this pit and when its walls
were hot and brushed clean of smoke and soot, unleavened
dough was spread in thin layers on the walls and baked al-
most instantly. Zeruah was baking bread when her father
came to her.
"I'll tell you without saying it," he began, for he was
afraid of the magic in words and protected himself against
it in all the ways known to him.
Zeruah knew what he had in mind but she said innocent-
ly, "Father, what is it?"
"May Yah never punish your father.”
"May Yah never punish my father."
"May peace and holiness be with me."
"May my father always live in peace.'
19
"He has gone away, yet I do not mean him."
"You mean the sky."
"I mean the sky. It was not demons."
"Peace be with my father."
Having annulled and neutralized as well as he could the
evil in his words, Ahban drew a deep breath and said:
"The King's men did it."
Zeruah looked away toward Jerusalem. "Father, have-
n't we suffered enough? Why do you torment yourself
with this sorrow?"
"They will take you away to the King's city."
"Father, O father!"
"The King's men will come. When they come you must
hide."
She turned on him, her eyes reproaching him. "Isn't it
150
the will of Yah? Isn't the King his Anointed? When Ahi-

"Yes, when Ahijah comes!"
She wanted to say, "Would you be proud if Jeroboam
were King?" But she did not say it, fearing his wrath.
She wanted to say, "You sold me to Nebat for a sheep,
condemning me to live with a dull and stupid man, yet I
did not love him!" Above all else she wanted to say,
"Have you been so foolish as to think that Nebat is Jer-

hand from his left! Yet look at Jeroboam, how bright he
is, how clever, how bold, with the bearing of a king or a
seer!"
But she said only: "Ahijah will surely come soon."
Two days later Zeruah and her son vanished from
Bethlehem.
A week later Ahijah came, swinging on his long free
strides, with only a piece of goatskin to cover his loin and
a rag to bind his great mass of raven hair. Tangled and un-
combed and filled with flying seeds and dust, his mane
hung down his shoulders. What a striking figure he made,
this prophet of Yah, his body baked a golden brown by
the desert sun, his eyes, overhung by heavy brows, flashing
with the holy fires in his being! He was taller than most
of his people. He had a strong face, the nose straight and
long, the chin well-formed and resolute, the mouth made
for castigation and curses. Weathered by storms and self-
denial, hardened by his wrestling with his god, he was
amazingly alive, like one who had just come down from
the high places, as indeed he had. He had been spying on
the King and he knew that the wicked wretch was now in
Egypt, consorting with people even more evil than him-
self. He appeared suddenly one morning and stood be-
fore Ahban, a man born to denounce and terrify and com-
mand.
151
Ahban ran to meet him, clasping his hands in supplica-
tion and crying out: "Ahijah, Yah's holy one, they have
stolen my daughter!"
"Zeruah?"
"Yes, son of Yah!"
"Who has dared to do this?"
"Protect me, Ahijah, while I say it! The King's men
did it!"
"The abominable sons of wickedness! Did they take
Jeroboam ?"
"Yes!"
"The King will suffer for this! He will be cursed seven
times seven until torments consume him and his thigh
rots!"
"Ahijah, they paid me nothing. Look round you—I am
a poor man, poorer than when you saw me last. I gave
two sheep to cleanse my house and my hands-"
"Where is Nebat?"
"By the holy god, haven't you heard?"
"No. Where is he?"
"If you will protect me―"
"I am Yah's prophet. My presence protects you."
"Nebat has gone away."
"Where?"
"Nebat is dead."
"Who killed him?"
""
"Protect me, for I am not a holy man!"
"Yah blesses you and gives you peace. Speak."
"The King's men strangled him."
Ahijah turned and looked toward Jerusalem and he
hated the King. For him, Yah was the god and the guard-
ian of the simple shepherd life, of the poor and the weak
among all the sons of Abraham. The baalim were the gods
of the rich.
"Did they take Zeruah by force?"
152
"I do not know. My daughter seemed strange."
"How long, O Yah," asked Ahijah, addressing the sky,
"will the King turn his face from you? How long before
your anger will be hot against Israel? The King walks in
the places of the wicked and talks with the sons of folly!
O Yah, our father and god who brought us up out of
Egypt, do not turn your face from your people who would

the
eyes of the blind and unstop the ears of the deaf that
your people may see and hear and know you as their god!"
Ahban was staring at the prophet's face and marvel-

"Has she been talking to the wicked?"
"Yes. Ahijah, you must find her and bring her back....
Come, let me wash your feet and set food before you."
While eating unleavened bread and raisins and drink-

hovering worship fully above him, Ahijah said: "The King
is in Egypt."
"Yes, we know it. We saw him go by."
"He's down there," said Ahijah bitterly, "seeking con-
cubines. Yah will cast him down."
"When?" asked Ahban eagerly.
"When it serves Yah's purpose."
Ahban was delighted to be in the presence of one who
controlled the power in words. Ahijah said simply that
the Anointed was wicked and challenged all the evil
forces to contradict him. Solomon was wicked, Ahijah
said, because he was marrying alien women. Had Abra-
ham let Isaac take a Canaanite? No, he had kept him a
bachelor for forty years. Hadn't Isaac and Rebekah ab-
153
horred Esau's foreign wives? Solomon was wicked, for
another thing, because he was working his own people
to death, as well as slaves, to build palaces for himself
and women. He was wicked because he was overtaxing
his people; because he was dedicating walls to Astarte;
because he had lain with Astarte's women; because he was
consorting with the local gods. Where in all the lands of
earth was there a king more wicked than this king?
"He'll be cast down," said Ahijah, "with worms in
his belly."
Ahban was trembling with joy. He wanted Ahijah to
go on to castigate the King with words as sharp as
thorns; but Ahijah now spoke of another matter, dear to
his heart. He told them again that Yah's people were a
peculiar and favored and chosen people. If they obeyed
all the laws Yah gave to Moses they would be prosperous
and they would multiply and fill the earth. Some day they
would rule over the entire earth and all peoples and
kingdoms would bow before Israel's god.
Then he spoke with contempt of Zadok, calling him
a full fellow, fit for a holy place in Moab or Ammon!
The prophet had contempt for the priest, and with rea-
son: Zadok divined with the urim and thummim, with
sheep liver, with rods, arrows, winds and trees. Ahijah
was the lip of Yah, his tongue, his mouthpiece. When he
spoke in full possession of his powers and holiness it
was Yah who spoke.
"I must go," said Ahijah, arising.
"To the King's city?"
"There—and to many places.'
"You must send Zeruah back to us."
"Zeruah," said Ahijah, his lips curling, "is being
watched by eunuchs. Am I allowed to speak to the King's
women?"
That for Ahban was a foolish question. Ahijah, as
154
Yah's captain, had the power to hurl walls down, strike
eunuchs dumb and blind, and convulse the King himself
with sickness and torments.
"Shall I take the vow of vengeance?"
"No, Ahban, leave everything to me."
"Please send my daughter back to us."
"I will see her. I will talk to her. Meanwhile, go on
with your work here, be upright and faithful and pure
in heart, for Yah is in the hearts of those who are pure.
Go to the living waters, for Yah is there. Go to the high
places where he walks. Be at peace in your house, Ahban,
my brother, for I have blessed it and there is no evil
in it. I will come back."
And Ahijah, in whom was the dreadful power of Yah,
went out by the gate and toward the King's city. When
he was no longer in sight Ahban turned to Mara.
"There will be war between Israel's King and Israel's
Prophet."
Mara said simply: "The King will be cast down."

SOLOMON WAS SO FASCINATED by Egypt and so eager to
learn all that he could about the ways and splendors of
a great and ancient kingdom that he spent most of the
winter there, perfecting his knowledge of the language
and spending many hours with Khate, whom he took as
a wife, and coming home by boat to Joppa and thence
overland, arriving home a wiser and fuller man. He was
more than ever determined to have splendors of his
own. He felt more keenly the crudeness and lack of cul-
ture of his people, and of himself also and the whole
155
royal family of Israel. He would accept the challenge.
He would do his best to make Israel great and respected
among the nations.
His quick and eager mind had noted many things on
the Nile which he intended to copy. In the temple of
the sun-god, for instance, he had seen a holy of holies,
the deepest of all the holy places, with no windows and
no light save from the doorway, and in the gloom an
image of the god, about a cubit in height. In the house
for the gods which his workmen were building, there
would be a holy of holies as fine as the one in Egypt. He
had also carefully studied the scenes of divine copulation
on the walls of the temples, and intended to have the arti-
sans in his city chisel and paint similar scenes on the walls
of his godhouse. He also intended to send expeditions
to far places to search for precious metals-there were
no gold mines nearer than Nubia, the tin was in Persia,
the iron was on the south shore of the Black Sea, and the
silver lay beyond the Cilician Gates. He would find
metals closer to home. He wanted floors of blue enameled
tile, walls inlaid with onyx, marble, agate, silver and
gold, and pleasure-gardens all around him filled with
aromatic herbs. Khate had told him that a mausoleum
in her land was more than three hundred cubits in height
and covered an area larger than most cities. Such pro-
digious glories would drive him to greater efforts. The
moment he arrived home he told his foremen to double
the quantity of diorite and basalt for columns and images
and stelae, and of cedar beams for roofs, walls and floors;
and as for his palace, he now decided to have a large
chamber for each wife, a smaller chamber for each con-
cubine, and a small palace for Khate to be built close
by his own.
He had learned many things from his Egyptian wife.
She had told him that health was a matter of food, sleep,
158
purges and emetics. She had scoffed at the notion that
disease and sickness were punishment from the gods.
And one of the wise men there had told Ahiah, who had
told his King, that the root of the white eryngium, whose
natural shape was that of the male parts, had a magical
effect on the waning or overtaxed potencies of men and
was in appearance irresistible to women. Solomon in-
tended to dispatch stewards in search of it. Some men
sought only vice; Solomon was not among them. Some
were enamored of women whom the passions of men
had degraded; Solomon had no interest in these. But he
was a voluptuary with an enormous interest in esoteric
forms of titillation and he intended to explore to the
fullest all the pleasures which the gods had put on
earth for men.
As soon as he had given various orders to his overseers
and looked at what had been accomplished during his
absence he summoned his mother.
"What a kingdom the Pharaoh has!" he cried. “What
a mess I have!"
"You were gone quite a while," said Bathsheba, gently
chiding him.
"But I learned many things. Some day I hope to go
again and learn more things."
"Did you make an alliance?"
"Yes, oh yes!—and what a princess I have- not beauti-
ful, but wise. She is a scented wine-jar!"
"A what?" asked his mother, startled.
"Well no, she isn't. Her breasts are not twin moons
and her mouth is not like crushed grapes; but she is a
wonderful woman. I thank the gods that the Sun of
Suns kept her for me. Beautiful women I can find any-
where, but not women like Khate."
"Khate? Is that her name? When is she coming?"
"When I have a decent house for her. I'd never want
157
her to see my city as it is now. She would be horrified.
How have things gone?"
"Very well, I think. I have some things to tell you
when you have time-and even now I should tell you
that the King of Gezer has sent an insolent message.'
99
"Oh, did he? The dog! I'll take care of him. The
Pharaoh is sending some of his best engineers to help
me."
Later, when he had things well in hand, Solomon told
Benaiah to prepare the host to march against Gezer.
Because all war was sacred and divine, a king did not
simply pick up his weapons and march against an enemy.
The war had to be consecrated, the warriors sanctified.
Benaiah said that Yah would march in the Ark around
the walls of Gezer and the walls would tumble down.
"I don't think so," said Solomon, who was now more
than ever skeptical of such beliefs. But nevertheless he
summoned Zadok and told him to read the oracles; and
later he said: "What do the signs say?"
"The trees and the waters say you will win easily."
"And the stones?"
"The answer is white."
Solomon looked at the priest, knowing that he was
skilled in the art of presages. For a long time the Chal-
deans had read the book of the sky and were able to fore-
tell the future by the position of the stars, which were
divine spirits. One evening when Solomon was in Egypt
the moon and the sun appeared together just above the
horizon and shone with equal brightness; and the wise
men there had said that the gods would bestow great
wealth upon Israel's king. But Khate had said, "It might
be just as well, though, to find some mines for yourself."
"Shall we attack when the moon is old or young?
"When the moon is young."
"In what month?"

"Ziv."
Solomon knew that it was fatal to attack in the month
of Ethanim or Bul. "Yah will want a great feast, won't

"Yes, my Lord."
"Where?"
"In Gibeon."
"Prepare for the feast."
Of the foods most acceptable to the gods there were
the firstfruits of wheat and barley, pomegranates, oil, and
honey, which might be any inspissated fruit juice. Because
the land was owned by the gods and produced only if they
fertilized and watered it, the choicest foods belonged by
divine right to them. Zadok would never have dared to
offer a blind, a lame or a sick beast, or fruits that were
imperfect, or oils that were rancid. Of birds he could
offer the dove or pigeon. Of beasts, the hog and the ass
were unclean because they were holy, having been, in a
long-ago time, the totems of his people. Yah's favorite
meat was the fattened bullock.
Solomon sent men into the fertile northern areas to get
twenty fat young bulls without blemish and he sent heralds
throughout his kingdom to proclaim the feast in Gibeon.
Some of the people, who could afford nothing more, would
bring corn, mixed with oil and salt. Some would bring the
blood of the grape to pour upon the burnt offerings. When
the arrangements were nearly completed Solomon called
Benaiah to his quarters and asked if his warriors were
whetted to a keen edge. It had been a custom with David
to train young men for battle by having them practice
upon and slay prisoners. They had used the men as tar-
gets, destroying them with club and dagger, javelin and
dart and slingshot. But Benaiah said that none had been
trained in David's way because there were no captives to
159
use as targets, all of them having been impressed into the
King's slave-labor army.
"Aren't some of the slaves old or weak or sick?"
"I might find some."
"Take a hundred and use them. Then segregate the
warriors."
Warriors were not only sacred; it was necessary to seg-
regate them and to watch over them, lest an enemy steal
their dung or nail-parings or hair and work destructive
magic against them. It was also necessary to keep them
away from women, for an incontinent warrior was no
better than a child.
One morning Solomon paused to watch the workmen on
his palace and saw with horror that they were sealing in-
fants into the mortar and stone. This was a Canaanite
custom; it was believed that the immuring of babies pro-
tected the buildings from evil spirits. Solomon called the
foreman over.
"Are you burying babies in my building?" he asked.
"Yes, my Lord. It is necessary-"
"No! Take them away and bury them and don't put
any more in my building. Where did you get them?"
"They are slave-babies, my Lord."
Solomon looked round him and was doubly shocked,
for there, not more than twenty feet away, was the proph-
et Ahijah, staring at him.
"Base King!" said Ahijah, his voice quiet and sinister.
"This is not the only abominable custom of the heathen
in your city."
"It's a custom I don't care for," said Solomon. He went
over and faced Ahijah and looked at his bronzed body and
his piece of goatskin. "You believe in many gods," he
said, looking into Ahijah's fierce brooding eyes, "but I was
told in Egypt there is only one god. What do you think of
that, you lunatic from the wilderness?"

"Your people know that you have been consorting with

"All right, you're with Yah. So what does that mean?"

Solomon looked at his scribe for several moments.
