THE GREAT OFFERING to the gods to assure victory Sol-
omon had intended to make at the close of the grain
harvest but too many problems intervened; and then he
postponed it to the Feast of the Booths in the autumn.
This festival, which a part of his people had adopted from
the Canaanites, was a time of thanksgiving and rejoic-
ing. Many of the peasants then set up booths in their
fields instead of returning at night to the villages; and
these they adorned with myrtle and other holy plants,
gathered from the vales.
On the day appointed for the slaughter in Gibeon,
where David had hanged the sons of Saul, slaves started
north at daybreak with the bullocks; and people round-
about came in, bringing their beasts or a basket of food.
Gibeon, a little more than six miles north-by-west of Jeru-
salem, was one of the high places, standing twenty-six
hundred feet above the sea. It had an enormous altar
on which the offerings to the gods could be burned,
censers for the burning of incense, and a shrine which
housed the god-images. There were pillars of stone, each
about ten feet high and two feet thick at the base, in
which the local baal was said to reside; and there were
wooden poles of Asherah, a goddess, crowned with phal-
lic symbols. On the feast-day, the poles and pillars were
decorated with green twigs and flowers and strips of gay
cloth.
Besides the bulls which Solomon was offering there
would be a few beasts fetched by the more prosperous
farmers or by merchants who sought divine favor. Be-
fore noon the environs of Gibeon were a dreadful con-
167
fusion of bawling cattle, of dust, of rejoicing people. The
bulls had been brushed and curried; their horns had been
gilded and hung with strips of red cloth; and some had
pieces of myrtle or pomegranate or quince tied to their
tails. The people themselves were dressed in their gay-
est garments, with red as the dominant color because
red was the color of blood and blood was life. Those
persons too poor to bring a beast had fetched baskets con-
taining barley meal mixed with salt and oil; or wine to
pour on the burning flesh; or a quail to give to the local
baal; or a libation of milk, though only Arabs and
Babylonians customarily gave this food to the gods.
With heralds going before him followed by musicians
Solomon and his party set out early in the morning. On
his left rode his scribe and on his right the priest, and just
behind came Zikla and Benaiah. Ahiah had already made
a record of the day's festivities. He had written down
the number of bulls which Solomon offered as one thou-
sand, and this exaggeration was neither mischief nor
deliberate fraud but only the Oriental's love of em-
bellishment. He wanted posterity to remember Solomon
as the most prodigal and magnificent of all monarchs;
and far from regretting his false records he wondered
if he had not been too modest.
On their way to Gibeon, riding at a leisurely pace in
the warm dry sunshine, Solomon was recalling one of
his many discussions with Khate. She had told him that
the Persians had a god called Mithra, who was born of
a virgin in a cave at the winter solstice, and who had
twelve disciples who seemed to be, she said, the twelve
lunar months. He had two great annual festivals, the
winter solstice, which was his birthday, and the spring
equinox, when he suffered death and resurrection. He
was sometimes known as the savior-god, sometimes as
the lamb. Solomon knew that Osiris and Tammuz and
168
Shamash were sun-gods with the same birthday, that
they also had been born of virgins, together with Attis,
the son of Nana-that they were born in a cave, which
Khate said was the underworld, the night, the winter.
Mithra's birth was announced by a star in the east;
and to the right of this star were three stars in a straight
line, known as the three wise men.
Solomon had been fascinated by such tales and was
now inclined to see in religions the infinite variety of
men's fancies. Khate had said that if all gods were sun-
gods they were all the same god, for there was only one
and Solomon had delighted her with the observation:
"Because there is only one sun, your King Akhnaton de-
cided there is only one god."
Now, with mischief sparkling in his eyes, Solomon
turned to the priest and said: "Zadok, my fine fellow-
you are a fine fellow, though your gloomy face depresses
me-when I was in Egypt I learned that Yah was born
of a virgin. What do you think of that?"
Zadok had learned that his King's questions were in-
tended to tease, to vex and sometimes to outrage him.
He shrugged and said nothing.
Ahiah said: "I learned in Egypt that all gods, and
all great men who are like unto the gods, are born of vir-
gins."
"Then," said Solomon, "I am not a great man. My
mother was no virgin."
"My Lord, possibly she was."
"If so, which god put the seed in her womb?" When
Ahiah did not answer, Solomon turned to the priest.
"Zadok, I was also told that there is only one god and
he is love."
"Love?" said Zadok, lifting his black brows.
"If there is only one god, Zadok, who is he?"
"Yah," said Zadok.
169
"Is Yah love? Is he gentleness and mercy and kind-
ness? Is it Yah when birds sing and lambs play and I
look at the lights in my wine?" Solomon winked at his
scribe. "Suppose," he went on, addressing Zadok, "there
is only one god and he is the father of all people. Sup-
pose there were only one king and one kingdom."
"Some day there will be," said Zadok.
"You mean the king will come from Israel and Israel
will be the only kingdom?"
"Yes."
"You've been listening to Ahijah.”
"Yah," said Zadok stiffly, "makes his intentions known
to me."
"Queen Khate says there is no Yah. What do you think
of that?"
Zadok shrugged again. "Our King must have his jests."
"I'm not jesting. The more I think of what she said
the more wisdom I find in it. She also said there is a
divine mother who is the mother of all things."
"There is much wickedness in Egypt," said Zadok.
"Didn't Yah punish them with ten plagues?"
"I asked them about that. They've never heard of it
and have no record of it. Now if a people had been
punished with ten plagues they wouldn't forget it, would
they?"
"They would lie about it."
Solomon looked up at the sky. If there was only one
god, as Khate believed, then surely the sun was his spirit,
for the sun was the most powerful thing known to man.
Solomon had a deep and abiding sense of the inner unity
and continuity of human beings with the world around
them with the sky-bodies, the animals and plants, even
with water and stones. In his time there were tongues in
stones and songs in running brooks and voices and moods
in all things.
170
Softly Ahiah said: "The spirit of holiness is a lamp,
but the secret place of a woman is darkness."
That piece of impish philosophy made Solomon lean
back and laugh loudly, his eyes turned meanwhile to see
what Zadok made of it. Again teasing the priest he said:
"In my city there is a serpent-god Nehushtan, the Baal
of Anath, the Queen of the Sky."
"There is no Anath," said Zadok.
"Tell me this, Zadok. Does Yah dwell in the Ark or
in my city or on the holy mount?"
"Yah dwells in all those places."
"Queen Khate says there is only God who dwells in
the hearts of men."
"This Egyptian woman," said Zadok contemptuously,
"sounds like a daughter of folly. When is she coming to
your city?"
"When I have a house ready for her. Could I bring the
Pharaoh's daughter over to live in a tent?"
"Where righteousness is, there the house is also."
Solomon looked round him at the stony ground. Ahiah,
returning from Egypt, had compared wisdom to trees,
saying that the foliage was the flowering of the heart. A
strong man, he had said, was a cedar of Lebanon and a
pure woman was a fragrant vineyard. Solomon now re-
membered that in Egypt the lotus was said to reproduce
itself. Growing in water, the plant put forth a flower, in
the center of which was a vessel holding the seeds, shaped
like a bell. The seeds when ripened were unable to fall,
and so produced new shoots without leaving the mother-
plant, until at last, bursting free, they dropped to the water
and were borne to a new home. This plant the Egyptians
revered as a symbol of rebirth and resurrection. Solomon
was now thinking that according to the belief of Ahijah
and Zadok, Israel was a kind of lotus that would be for-
ever independent of all people. He smiled and said:
171
"Well, there's Gibeon. Zadok, have them bring up the
choicest bulls."
Zadok rode on ahead to supervise the zebah or
slaughter, the blood of which would be poured on the
altar-stones, and the minhah, which was offerings of cere-
als and oil. But there was little for him to do. He was
supposed to lay his right hand-never his left, and never
both hands-on the head of each beast, for the head was
the seat of life; and to see that Yah received all those
parts were his due. Already Gibeon looked like a field of
slaughter. There was in the air the smell of blood and
hair and dust. Here a man was struggling with the help
of his neighbor to drag a beast to the altar; there an-
other had cut his animal's throat and was catching the
blood; a third was burning fat in the fire; and others were
dancing and singing before the altar, holding above them
the raw and dripping flesh. But it was an orderly scene
in comparison to what it would be before the sun went
down.
Zadok sent men down the hill to bring the bulls re-
served for the King, and these singly or in pairs were
dragged forth, their nostrils blowing mucus and rage and
their fat bodies heaving and fighting against men who
grasped them by tails and horns and put shoulders against
them to shove. When a bull was dragged up, snorting
and bellowing, Zadok came up with a sharp knife and
thrust the blade deep behind the jaws and slashed down-
ward. The beast was not allowed to fall; a dozen men
supported it, and while it stood, propped on shaking legs,
its eyes bulging and turning glassy, men held pots to
catch the gushing blood. Zadok then poured the blood
upon stones by the fire. The beast was allowed to fall
and men dragged it aside to skin it. People now pushed
in, crying for the raw and quivering flesh, for meat eaten
172
when still warm and half-alive was most infused with the
divine essence.
And so the slaughter continued for hours, until the
area round the altar was a welter of flesh and gore, of
dung, of scattered guts, and of bloody men eating bloody
meat. There were no women present but the kadeshoth,
the holy ones, who were consecrated to the gods; these
waited in the bowers and tents, the qobbah, meaning a
hollow arched structure like the door of life.
The poor men who could not afford to offer beasts
came forward to the altar and sprinkled salt over barley
or wheat and poured a little oil over it or a little wine
and then cast their oblations on the flames. All the men
ate gluttonously. Flesh was prohibited as food save at
the sacred feasts, and so it was that men then ate all
they could eat. It was a time of great rejoicing and thanks-
giving, and for the more emotional ones a time of hyster-
ia; these, overwhelmed by the presence of divinity, lacer-
ated themselves and allowed their blood to mingle with
that at the altar-base.
Nobody, drunk or sober, king or peasant, dared to
drink the blood. It belonged to the gods. The viscera
also belonged to them, particularly the fat of the
omentum and the organs that lay near it. If a man seemed
likely not to strip the fat clean and throw it on the fire,
Zadok was there to rebuke and assist him. If, in an excess
of zeal and awe, a man sprinkled himself with blood or
began to anoint a brother with blood and fat, Zadok
promptly cuffed him and had guards take him away.
Solomon was standing with Ahiah by the table of the
Presence, which was back from the altar and toward the
sanctuary. He observed that Zadok seemed to be hugely
enjoying his duties. He had a large pot filled with blood,
and he was anointing with a drop or two those who
clamored for his attention. He touched them on the point
173
of their nose, their thumbs, their secrets. Upon his own
forehead he made with blood the sign of the sacred union.
Then he poured blood upon the curved stones in the
shape of horns that stood at the four corners of the altar.
Solomon sniffed the air, reeking with blood and burnt
flesh and fat and said to Ahiah: "I'm glad that Queen
Khate isn't looking at this scene. She told me that her
god feels only disgust for such offerings." He looked up
at the sky, seeking the signs of divine favor. The sky
was a clear blue, and that was a good sign, Zadok would
have said, for blue was a symbol of revelation. Some
garlands hanging from poles were purest white, and that
was a good sign, for white was a symbol of the pure
heart. The anointed horns shone blood red, and that
was good, for red was the color of life.
"Zadok would say that Yah is pleased. As for me, I
no longer know what to think."
Ahiah was eating a piece of half-roasted flesh; he hid
his face behind it and smiled. "Perhaps," he said, "he
is angry with all the little foxes who spoil the vines."
Solomon looked at Ahiah and saw him press his face
into the flesh. "You rogue," he said, "you're laughing at
all this."
Ahiah chewed a mouthful and swallowed it and
tongued his teeth. "I laugh when my King laughs. As for
the gods, they change their aspect as each person looks
at them. For the brigands they are brigands. For the
eunuchs they are eunuchs. And I suppose that for kings
they are kings. For one like me, they are malformed and
undersized and have on their tongues the flavor of worm-
wood."
"What a fine fellow you are! Ahiah, you're worth a
thousand women."
And as though the word women reminded him of the
qobbah Solomon turned that way and saw a lovely girl.
174
She was standing in the arched doorway, looking at
him, but at once she dropped her gaze and prostrated her-
self on the earth. Solomon had Ahiah fill his wine-cup,
and then strolled over to the bower. "Arise," he said,
and when the girl stood before him he felt of her
breasts under her garment to learn if they were round
and firm. Then he turned his gaze inward to his emotions.
She was a beautiful thing but he had spent most of the
previous night with a new concubine and he decided that
his ardor was feeble. Turning to Zikla, who was trailing
him, he said: "Have her taken to my city."
In the temple he would have many holy women, all
of them chosen for their beauty. He was having small
chambers built for them where they would be able to
lie with men and fulfill their sacred mission. The earn-
ings of these temple votaries would help to enrich him.
He had heard that in Babylon the sanctuary of the god
Bel was an enormous structure, many times as high as
any in all the land of Israel-higher even than the
temples in Egypt. The tallest tower was furnished with
a huge bed, luxuriously draped and cushioned, with a
table of solid gold beside it. To this chamber came the
loveliest of Babylon's women, chosen for the god's
pleasure; and each night Bel came to this room and slept
in the bed-though Khate said it was the King, the sly
wretch, who slept there for him. Well, that was the divine
right of kings. And at Thebes-he had heard—a woman
slept in the bed of the god Ammon, and was known as
his divine consort; and sometimes, Khate had confided,
the woman was the Queen herself. Egyptian kings were
sired by the god Ammon, who became, for the purpose
of procreation, incarnate in the reigning king, and slept
with the Queen. So it was that every king was a god. On
the walls of the temple he had looked at scenes which
depicted the divine embrace. Would he dare to build a
175
chamber, in the highest tower of the temple, where,
each night, he might sleep with a beautiful virgin and
be the creative agent of the gods? What would Ahijah
think of that?
Solomon called for a roasted fat tail of lamb, of which
he was fond. While sucking the juices from this morsel,
and wondering whether he was the son of a god, his
thoughts turned to the world of food. What kitchens
he would have! What granaries and cellars and spice-
chambers; there all the delicacies known to men would
be prepared for him and his court. There he would have
many a time of drinking, which was the Hebrew term for
feast.
Calling to Zadok he said. "How are the signs?"
"They are all favorable."
"Have the gods feasted well?"
"Yah has feasted."
"How about the Baal of Gibeon? Is he happy?"
"Yah has feasted," said Zadok, and shuddered.
In Gibeon, as in all the high places, there was a beth
elohim, or house of the gods, in which were housed the
local wonders used for divination. Solomon told Zadok
to consult the oracles. This Zadok did with reluctance,
because the Baal of Gibeon was for him a very petty god,
if not indeed an impostor.
Solomon looked at the field of gore and bones and at
the people who were draping the bloody pelts round their
shoulders. Then he sent for Benaiah. When Benaiah
came, looking as though he had crawled through a wet
carcass, Solomon stared at him and asked:
"Benaiah, are you drunk?"
"A little, my Lord." His tongue was thick.
"Have all the preparations been made for our attack
on Gezer?"
"The sons of war are ready."
176
"Have they been lying with women?"
"Oh, good Baal of Gibeon, no!"
"Has the enemy stolen any of their dung or hair?”
Solomon was teasing him.
"My Lord-"
"Have the priests tied up their souls in bundles?"
"My Lord, yes."
"Does the banner of the eagle face the south and
west?"
"Yes, my Lord."
"Does the banner of the lion face the north and
east ?'
"Yes."
"Has Zadok pronounced a curse on the enemy?"
Benaiah might easily have said yes, for he had been
lying in most of his answers; but this question seemed to
confuse him. Before he could quibble or evade or give
any answer at all, Solomon advanced, affecting to be
consumed with rage.
"Are we going to march against an enemy that hasn't
been cursed?"
"My Lord," said Benaiah, full of wine and confusion.
Solomon turned to Zadok. "Go curse the enemy."
Zadok went to higher ground, accompanied by his
King, and faced Gezer, which was invisible behind hills
in the west. Lifting his hands to the sky he called down
upon this people the wrath of Yah. Like one overcome
by loathing and hate, his face distorted, his lips curled
with contempt, his hands clenched and his head nodding
with every word, he cursed these people, calling on Yah
to march with his sons to smite the enemy with lance and
javelin and stone, bursting them open in breast and belly,
spilling their bowels on the earth, and tearing their
stones from their pouches. And then, at Solomon's com-
mand, he called down upon Gezer the anger of all the
177
gods, from Hermon to Beersheba, from Carmel to the
Jabbok, invoking hideous punishments on their stubborn-
ness and presumption, and asking that every one of them,
man, woman and child, be delivered into the hands of
the avengers. When he had finished the curse, sweat
stood on his forehead.
Solomon had listened to every word that was uttered.
He thought it was a good curse. He was sure that he
had never heard a better one. Looking over the altar
smoke and then north to the mountains, he wondered if
there were gods, and if there were, if they had heard
and approved. Whereupon, thinking of Khate and feel-
ing a little foolish, he left in haste for his city, arriving
there after dark.
16
AHIJAH HAD GONE to the holy mount to strengthen his
spiritual powers and receive instructions from Yah and
had then returned to the King's city, resolved to see Zer-
uah; but the weeks passed and he saw her only once and
then she was accompanied by a guard. But one morning
while he was standing in plain view of the huge tent
where she lived with some other concubines she came
out and ran toward him.
"Zeruah!" he cried.
"Ahijah!"
"Come with me! You must return to Bethlehem!"
"No! I came out to tell you that you are being watch-
ed."
"Oh, I know that! A wicked king has his spies. Come
with me-now!"
178
"Ahijah, please! Why be so unreasonable?" She look-
ed at his hair and saw signs of gray in it.
"Do you mean you want to lie with this wicked King?
Are you nothing but a Canaanite? Are you going to
whore your god? Why are you here?"
"Ahijah, listen-and please don't shout, for we are
being watched. Oh, by the holiness of Yah, do you think
this is easy for me?"
"You don't have to stay here, you wicked woman!"
"Oh, but I do. I'm under guard. Ahijah, listen—'
"I don't want to listen to your honeyed words!"
"Please listen-just a moment. Jeroboam is with me.
Ahijah, he might be the King of Israel some day..
Couldn't he?"
Aghast, Ahijah shrank back from her. His eyes looked
inflamed and terrible.
"Ahijah, he's our son. Have you forgotten it? You are
Yah's holy prophet in Israel. If Yah should make it
known to you—'
"No!"
"Ahijah-Oh, why are you so blind? Has Yah never
told you anything about Jeroboam?-your son? Who
will be the next king? Can't you see? Won't you under-
stand? Won't you ask Yah what his purpose is? If I go
back to Bethlehem the King's men would come for me
and I'd be punished. Everything then would be lost."
She had never seen such terrible eyes or so much.
fury, astonishment and pain in a human face. Had he
forgotten? Was he denying his own child?
"Ahijah, I must go back now. Ask Yah to protect me
for I have been seen. But remember-remember what
I said. Remember that I will be doing all I can, but it
is Yah, working through you, his prophet, who must
do it. I'll figure out some way to see you now and then.
I'll bribe the guard. Ahijah, I must go now."
179
"No!"
"Yes, I must. Remember-and goodbye."
She ran back to the tent and Ahijah stood alone, star-
ing after her, and when he moved away he went like a
man whose legs had been numbed.
The next day, the spy reported to Solomon that Zer-
uah had slipped away from her guard and run out to
talk to Ahijah; and the King said, "Hah, did she?" and
forgot the matter. He was too deep in his preparations
for war to think long on a faithless concubine. The cap-
tains of the host had their warriors ready. The shields
and lancepoints had been anointed with holy oil, the war-
riors had been sanctified with ablutions, their souls had
been abstracted by the priests and tied in bundles and
stored away in a safe place. When the war was over the
souls would be loosed from their bundles and set free on
the winds to fill their bodies.
Solomon called Ahiah, and dictated a letter to the
Pharaoh.
"To the King of Egypt, King of Kings and Sun of
Suns, I send you greetings. I am well, and my women
and children and my houses, and I trust everything is well
with you in these things. I am ready to attack Gezer,
where the stubborn and degenerate enemy flaunts his
banners. The engineers whom you sent to my assistance
are the best in the world. May the Sun of Suns, before
whom I prostrate myself seven times seven, rest assured
that this abomination will be destroyed and that I will
protect your northern frontiers."
Ahiah smiled approvingly; he thought his King was
doing very well.
"Now I shall send a message to Queen Khate. Let
me see, she said that this King Akhnaton called his wife
the mistress of his happiness. Very well. To Queen
Khate, Egypt's greatest princess and the mistress of
180
my happiness and joy, I send you greetings. Ten thousand
workmen are building a palace for you and another for
me and a great temple. Everything goes well and ac-
cording to divine plans. I shall make arrangements for
you to come to my city just as soon as I have a house
for you. Meanwhile, be not impatient but rest in peace
and virtue and put your trust in that great one who is
God and Love.
"How was that?" asked Solomon, beaming at his
scribe.
"Very fine, my Lord. No other king can dictate such
felicitous letters."
Then the day came when the battle-horns sounded
from the high hills, proclaiming that Israel was going
to war. It was the common practice for a general to divide
his force into three divisions. These now moved against
Gezer with their banners flying and their horns blowing;
and when, hours later, the King of Gezer saw the armies
advancing, he hung banners from his walls and towers
and had horns sounded at the gates. The Baal of Gezer
and Yah of Israel had gone to war.
Gezer occupied a high place in the center of a grain-
growing plain. Around it was a row of stone pillars,
and a cemetery in which many infants, consecrated to the
Baal, had been buried in clay jars. The King was very
religious; he gave to his god the firstborn sons, who were
a part of the firstfruits. It was Benaiah's thought to
march up with his three armies and lay siege.
But among the engineers from Egypt was a tall, taci-
turn and very homely man named Ahmasou, and a short
fat man named Huya. From a distance they had exam-
ined Gezer's walls and towers and gates and had de-
cided that they were uncommonly strong. In fact, the
thick walls, built of heavy stones and mortar, were set
181
on foundations deep in the earth, and the gates were
the heaviest in Israel.
The three armies drew up before Gezer and coalesced,
forming, as was the Hebrew custom, a circular encamp-
ment, with the supplies in the center. But Ahmasou and
Huya said a siege would be too long and costly. Did
the Hebrews know how to wage war? They doubted it.
Did they know that the most vulnerable spots in the
human frame were the throat just under the ear; the
forehead above the base of the nose; the shoulder where
the collarbone was attached; the soft bowels and the pri-
vate parts, which were exposed and unprotected? And
look at their weapons, said Ahmasou to his colleague!—
stones, clubs, arrows, flint knives. David had introduced
the chariot but had never been successful with it. He had
won his battles by hurling masses of men upon an am-
bushed or surprised enemy. For Ahmasou war was an
art.
Solomon had pitched his headquarters near the supply
depot, and, just after dark of the first night, he sum-
moned Ahmasou and Huya to his tent. With him were
Benaiah and Zikla and Ahiah. Benaiah wanted to assault
the eastern gate in force. Zikla had proposed that a
hundred picked men, wearing helmets and breastplates,
should go under cover of darkness with axes and pikes and
undermine a segment of wall.
"What do you think?" Solomon asked Ahmasou.
"My Lord," said Ahmasou in his slow and prudent
way, "I don't think much of either plan. I imagine that
the walls are too deep in the earth and the gates too
strong."
Lamps were burning in the King's tent but the men
could see one another only dimly; when one spoke, the
others leaned forward to peer at him, as though they
had to see his face to understand his words.
182
"We could dash around in chariots," said Benaiah,
"and hurl torches inside."
Ahmasou stared thoughtfully at Benaiah.
"We have many ladders," said Zikla. "Could we scale
a wall and hold a tower?"
"What do you think?" asked Solomon, looking at Ah-
masou.
"What are these people most afraid of?"
"Of Yah," said Benaiah.
Ahmasou's response was a gentle shrug. He knew, of
course, that the gods were supposed to go with their
people to war. He knew the belief that the opposing
gods tried to frighten one another, and that when one
was successful in this, his people then attacked, because
a frightened god always fled and his army with him. But
he didn't have much faith in gods, either as warriors or
as divine powers.
He sat on the earth floor and smoothed a piece of
ground, and with a stick he made marks to represent the
walls of Gezer, its towers and gates. Ahiah fetched a
lamp and set it on the ground and all the men but Sol-
omon sat on the floor and studied the drawing. He had
observed, Ahmasou said, that a strong wind sometimes
blew from the northwest, and it was his opinion that
the west gate was the weakest. Over here, he went on,
pointing to the plain southeast of the city, an army could
be drawn up in battle array a little before sunset. The
moon would be hidden. This army would make all the
noise it possibly could. It would hurl javelins and stones
and rush forward with battering-rams.
Meanwhile, here and here, he said, indicating the earth
on either side of the attacking army, there would be
fires; and dancing in the wind and in the shadow of the
fires would be effigies, on which the enemy would waste
arrows and javelins. Over here, on the northeast, would
183
be another army, also in full battle gear, and like the
other it would gird itself for assault. At the same moment
both armies would advance against the walls. Mean-
while, on the northwest plain, advancing in the wind
there would be three lines of a hundred men each, bear-
ing young trees; and behind these lines and concealed
by them would come picked assault troops with ladders
for scaling the walls, and other troops with battering-
rams to demolish the west gate, and still other troops
with pitch torches to hurl over the walls. "I propose a
thousand men there," he said. "You will lose most of
them. But you will not lose many elsewhere if these are
successful."
Solomon looked at Benaiah. "What do you think of
the plan?"
Benaiah stubbornly kept his gaze fixed on the draw-
ing, hating its subtleties and complexities. For him war
was simple attack and retreat and attack.
"I accept the plan," said Solomon. "We'll execute it
in the first dark night when there's a wind."
A week was needed to gather the trees and torches
and battering-rams. Then the wind died and for many
days the earth was calm while Zadok stood on high
places exhorting his god. But in the next moon-dark the
winds leapt in from the sea and strong gales blew across
the ramparts of Gezer. A little before sunset the armies
took their positions; fires were kindled, battle trumpets
made a dreadful racket, and all the musicians in the
camp made as much noise as they could. The reserves
in the camp danced round a fire and shrieked and yelled
at the ramparts where, peering over, the enemy was vis-
ible, the thick-lipped and flat-nosed Canaanites, with their
heavy jaws and conical skulls. The intervening distance
was too great to shoot arrows across, but men trod their
bows and some, eager for battle, advanced within arrow
184
range. When darkness came, Gezer's soldiers mounted
the walls and towers and stood boldly in view, hurling
slingshot and arrows and shouting curses. After a while
the crests of the walls were lined with men. They had
with them huge stones and vats of boiling oil to pour
upon Solomon's men if they appeared below.
Darkness like that of a deep cave enveloped the earth.
There was no light but the light of fires. Yah poured
his winds down from the northwest heights and they
swept across Gezer with the force of a mighty anger.
This was Yah's wind, Zadok said. But Yah had sent al-
most too much of his power; when the columns bearing
willows and young oaks advanced toward the western
gate some of the men were blown down, and the torches
behind them loosed sheets of flame that spread and flew
like golden garments.
Ahiah had slipped away to advance with the thousand
men because he intended to record this great battle for
his King and he wanted to see it at firsthand. He saw
dimly ahead of him the massed lines moving as swiftly
as they could, clutching their small trees in the cataracts
of the wind; but in spite of all their efforts their lines
were broken and the flames of the torches shone through.
And so it was that the enemy detected their approach
and sounded alarums on the west towers. United by a
common purpose and propelled by a common emotion
the thousand men rushed in, not a man among them
faltering, though before the vanguard reached the walls
arrows were falling among them and stones and scald-
ing oil were being hurled down. In the confusion nobody
could give orders because no voice could be heard above
the furies of the battle. The first line of trees reached
the wall and parted and fell away, and the second and
third likewise; and then the battering-rams and the
ladders and the torches came in. Men hurled their torches
185
high into the wind and the wind bore them forward and
down into the city. Men stood ladders against the wall
and mounted, taking as weapons axes and stout daggers.
Those with the first battering-ram hurled themselves on
the gate. Forty men, twenty on either side, all of them
chosen for size and strength, supported a long heavy
beam with ropes of leather under it, and bore it forward,
running together, hurling the moving force of it against
the gate's timbers. After three or four desperate efforts
they fell back and another group with another ram took
their place. Those wounded or slain were quickly dragged
away and comrades leapt in to fight where they had
stood. The commander in charge of the thousand was
dead. Dead also after a few minutes were many of his
captains. Ahiah was standing back out of danger. He
could see men on the wall flinging stones down and empty-
ing vats of hot oil.
He did not like war, being a gentle soul who thought
that men ought to live in peace. He did not intend to
die in this wild and bloody and flaming night. He ad-
vanced and stood in the masses of green trees that had
been cast aside, and thus, protected and hidden, he
watched the fight. Within the city fires were raging; there
was a great illumination above the walls, filling this part
of the sky. Standing on the wall he saw a man, limned
against the sky-glow; in one hand he had an axe and in
the other a dagger and he seemed to be looking round
him for a foe. In the next moment an arrow pierced his
temple. Ahiah saw the man sway, saw the axe but not
the dagger fall from his grasp, saw him for a moment
like one preparing to dive, saw what seemed to be a look
of astonishment on his face, saw him plunge and dis-
appear.
Trembling with excitement Ahiah left his shelter and
stumbled over two men. Looking down, he saw that one
186
of the men, lying on his back, had a big ugly wound be-
low his heart, and that the other man was sucking at
the wound and turning away to spit out the blood. Ap-
parently this man believed that the lance had been poi-
soned. Ahiah bent low to look at the face of the wounded
man and saw that he was dead. He looked at the face
of the other man and realized that he was out of his
senses. Then Ahiah went over to a ladder that stood far
down the wall in deep darkness; and as he walked there
came to him dully, like the throbbing of pain in his mind,
the sound of the battering-ram on the gate. Ah, these
were brave men, these sons of David's warriors! He
was proud to be a Hebrew.
Slowly and cautiously he mounted this ladder un-
til his eyes were level with the wall. Peering over, he
could see, as in a great basin of fog and fire, a part of
the city. He could see that a large part of it had been
swept by flame but there was no sign that the eastern
gate had been breached. He backed quickly down the
ladder and went away into the night. Hidden in dark-
ness he looked at the struggle against the draperies of
fire and smoke. He could hear wounded men screaming.
He could hear the muffled blows of the ram. Feeling a
strange feminine warmth for men who could fight with
such courage he looked up at the sky, wondering if Yah—
if there was a Yah-was participating in this massacre.
Was the Baal of Gezer still in his city or had he fled?
Perhaps there was nothing here in the whole deep night
but human beings who were trying to cut one another's
guts out. When he was a student in Egypt he had heard
a philosopher say that if there were gods there was no
justice in them: the evil prospered and the good were
cast down...
So matters stood with this skeptical and civilized man
as he watched the carnage of men and their gods and
187
tried to still the trembling in his limbs. He felt ill. Weary
with the shrieks of the wounded and the gushing of
bowels and blood, he found a shelter and crawled under
it; and when he awoke from fitful sleep, he looked out
and saw that the east was touched with daybreak. Upon
the highest tower the King of Gezer was burning his
sons alive as sacrifices to his god. That at least is what
Ahiah supposed he was doing, for this was a common
practice with kings when they faced defeat; he thought
he could hear screams, he thought he could smell the
odor of flesh and hair. Whether the gate had been
breached he did not know, or whether his own dead had
been taken away for burial. Sick at heart he made a wide
detour to reach the main camp and out in a barley-field
a mile or more from the city walls, he saw the corpse
of one of the Hebrews. Scalded by hot oil, blinded, and
perhaps out of his senses, the poor fellow had crawled
on hands and knees this long distance before falling with
his face in the dust to die.
17
"LOOK AT HIM," said Solomon. "What'll I do with
him?" He was speaking to Ahiah and he was looking at
the King of Gezer, a stricken, gibbering, insane wretch
who was fawning like a beast at Solomon's feet. After
burning his sons as an offering to his god he had surren-
dered, though he had no reason to hope for compassion
in the victor.
"I'd kill him," said Ahiah. "He's half-dead now."
188
The Hebrews were not so brutal in their treatment of
captives as their neighbors. The Egyptians sometimes
cut off the nose, ears, tongue, hands and feet; flogged
with rods; strangled, impaled, beheaded, burned, or em-
balmed alive. Or sometimes they covered prisoners with
a coating of corrosive natron. Assyrians tore the arms out
of position and bound them to cause extreme torture.
They skinned them alive. The Hebrews often cut off a
thumb or big toe, for these were phallic symbols; or they
cut off the prepuce of the uncircumcised.
"He's a fine-looking king!" said Solomon contemp-
tuously and turned away.
He intended to sell all the able-bodied captives, for he
needed funds, and he had given orders that all such men
were to be spared when his soldiers entered the city; but
in their hour of triumph the soldiers forgot orders, and
within Gezer hot passions ran wild. There was looting,
raping and murder, and when Ahiah looked in, he saw
pools of blood. He saw mothers lying everywhere, with
spears driven through them and dead babies clutched to
their breasts.
After a victory it was a custom with most people to
offer a holocaust to their gods, burning prisoners alive
upon pyres; but Solomon told Benaiah to dispatch the
old and wounded and the young and to march the able-
bodied men to his city. He would sell them to the Phoeni-
cians who would ship them westward to the slave-marts.
But Ahijah had watched the siege from a distant hill,
and when Solomon's entourage reached Gibeon the
prophet was there to meet him. Yah had said very clearly
in various commandments to his people that all captives,
men, women and children, were to be slain. Suspecting
that the wicked King intended to sell or himself enslave
the prisoners, he came forward, dressed only in his goat-
skin girdle, and hailed the King.
189
"Well, well, here's Ahijah again," said Solomon, re-
calling that he had held a tryst with Zeruah. "What do
you want now?"
"What are you doing with the captives?"
"It's none of your business."
"It is Yah's business!" Ahijah cried angrily. "These
men belong to him!"
Well, that was true, according to the custom of the
time. Captives were devoted to the god, which meant that
they were his property, to be offered to him on altars.
But Solomon was changing customs.
"Go on back to the holy mount," said Solomon.
Ahijah advanced, his eyes flashing, and all the men
around Solomon but Ahiah fell back. They knew that
this man was a prophet and they were afraid of him.
"Are you not going to obey Yah's commandments?"
"What commandments?" asked Solomon.
"The commandments he gave to Moses."
"I have destroyed Gezer-"
"It was Yah who destroyed Gezer!"
"These prisoners are mine. I will do with them as I
please." To Ahiah Solomon whispered, "Shall I have him
chained?" and Ahiah said, "No."
Ahijah came closer, his black eyes flashing with con-

he is not deceived! You're not a green tree in his house
but Astarte's hireling! Yah commands you to bring all
these men to Gibeon's altars and to burn them upon the
altars!"
Zikla was standing with his hand on his dagger haft,
looking in turn from king to prophet. Benaiah at the
head of his troops was waiting.
Solomon said in a low voice to his scribe: "Well, I
can't stand here all day like a fool. Either I'll put this
man in chains-"
190
"My Lord, let's simply go on and ignore him."
"Very well, I'll do that." He turned to Zikla and said,
"Proceed to my city." He waved to Benaiah. "March!"
he said.
Ahijah fell back, knowing for the moment that he had
lost. If he could have summoned the might of Yah he
would have struck this king where he stood. He would
have shriveled him like a leaf on a hot stone, or made
him spread dung on his face and crawl like a serpent in
the dust. The commandments which Yah had given were
plain and they were changeless. He had said: "Spare
them not, but slay both man and woman, infant and suck-
ling." He had said: "Slay utterly old and young, both
maids and little children." He had said: "And you shall
consume all the people which I shall deliver to you; your
eyes shall have no pity upon them." Moses had been
angered because women and children had been spared
among the Midianites, and he had said: "Kill every
male among the little ones, and kill every woman that
has known man." It was told in the legends: "And we
took all his cities at that time, and utterly destroyed the
men and the women and the little ones of every city, we
left none to remain." It was said: "And we utterly de-
stroyed them as we did unto Sihon king of Heshbon, ut-
terly destroying the men, women and children of every
city." Yah delivered Jericho into their hands and they
utterly destroyed all that was in the city, both man and
woman, young and old. Yah delivered Lachish and smote
with the sword all the souls that were therein. Joshua
passed into Eglon and with the sword he utterly de-
stroyed all the souls that were therein that day. City by
city Yah had delivered them into the hands of his people
and no man, woman or child was left alive.
Ahijah knew these things. He knew the command-

would be to bring down upon Israel his awful wrath,

TIME PASSED, the seasons changed from wet to dry and
from wet to dry three times, and there was peace in Is-
rael. During this period Solomon went again to Egypt to
visit Khate and to learn from her enlightened people.
His foremen drove the workmen and particularly the
slaves to the limit of their strength, building a house for
Khate, a palace for the King and a house for the gods.
When the house for Khate was almost finished, Solomon
sent a message to her, saying: "Mistress of my happiness,
I am ready to receive you.'
99
She came by boat to Joppa and then overland, sending
word ahead that she did not wish to be met with heralds
nor with an armed escort. She paused for a night in Gib-
eon where tents were set up for her and her retinue;
and there she had the hair shaved from her body, there
she bathed and took an emetic and an enema. And on a
warm morning in the time of the new moon, when mothers
exposed their naked children to its light and all women
were privileged to be idle, she came up the hill to the
King's city riding in an electrum chariot that was a gift
from her father. It was drawn by two pure white horses.
Behind her in other chariots of hornbeam came her ladies
and male servants, and behind these was a caravan of
asses laden with furnishings.
Everyone knew that she was coming and the people
could not have been more excited if they had awaited a
visitor from heaven. Solomon's wives were all out of
their tents, and the moment Khate came in sight, stand-
ing up and looking round her, they observed first her
hair, or her wig really, for she had bobbed her own
197
hair close and wore over it an enormous wig as black
as night. In her land wigs indicated rank; the Pharaoh
wore the largest, of course, but when Khate left Egypt
she was privileged to indulge her whims. The top of the
wig looked like a crown and some thought it was spark-
ling with jewels. From it on all sides except over her
face fell heavy strands and braids of hair, all of which
were exactly of the same length, forming a straight edge
across her back and shoulders and a part of her chest.
Her long face and neck were framed like a delicate pic-
ture in the enormous mass of hair. Her ladies-in-waiting,
of whom she had brought two score, had headdresses
much like her own, but smaller; none of them wore the
symbol of Isis, a stepcrown surmounted with horns and a
disc, nor of Hathor, whose symbol was a crescent and
cow's horns, nor of Nephthys, whose headdress was a
house with a door, with a basket above it. Not wishing to
offend the customs of Israel, she had risked nothing more
than a small serpent on her forehead, the mark of the
royal house, and a small ornamental sun-disc pinned to
one side of her coiffure.
They noticed next, in these few first moments, her
dress. It was a huge piece of cloth in gay colors, beauti-
fully draped, for draping was for the Egyptians an art.
It was bound in at the waist with a broad sash. She wore
a collar, fitted close to her throat, and visible only in
front; it was decorated with beads, lotus-flowers, tufts of
papyrus and a sun-disc so small that it was barely show-
ing. On her feet she had papyrus sandals. In her luggage
she had many other pairs, some of leather, some of
plaited palm-leaves.
Like Hebrew women, Egyptian women were fond of
jewelry, and usually wore as much as they could afford;
but Khate the wives observed with astonishment-did
not have rings on her fingers and thumbs, nor bracelets
198
and armlets and anklets, nor carved pectorals, though all
these might have been found in the caravan that followed
her up the hill. She had brought with her jewel-boxes
and jeweled combs, ointment jars of alabaster, bronze
mirrors, musical instruments, including a harp seven feet
tall; elaborately carved and ornamented fans and plumes,
and a huge collection of perfumes and scents. She had
come in style, and every wife sensed this when she step-
ped down from the chariot.
All around her was the multitude, gawking and breath-
less. "What," one wife asked another, "has she put on
her face?" And another whispered: "Look at her
brows!" Her skin after bathing had been softened with
olive, almond and palm oils; green malachite had been
brushed along her nose to emphasize its aquiline fea-
tures; heavy carmine rouge was on her lips; black cir-
cles were under her eyes, made of a mixture of charcoal
and antimony, and these contrasted with the bloodless
white of her complexion and enhanced her abnormally
large eyes. Her brows were narrow black lines. In her
clothes and in the atmosphere around her was such a sat-
uration of sweet scents that persons a hundred feet away
could catch the odor of lotus, nard and musk. Khate
never forgot that a god had come down in an aura of
delicious fragrance to lie with Queen Ahmasi.
Khate descended from the chariot, advanced beyond
the horses, and stood looking at the people, slowly turn-
ing her head this way and that. Solomon stood by her,
wearing his most splendid raiment. The city was still
a chaos of cedar beams, blocks of stone, beggars and
merchants, market-booths, homely walls and towers-
and the stench of ages. But in the immediate foreground
was the King's palace, and Khate's house, close by it.
These were wondrous structures in Israel-but for one
familiar with the hall at Karnak, the great temple at
199
Luxor, the clerestories and architraves and columns and
capitals and tombs, these buildings were small and mean.
She put a delicate kerchief to her nose. "My Lord, I
am very happy to see you!"
"I am happy to see you. I have a feast waiting."
"A feast?" She shrugged for her thinness, though she
did not look a day older than when he had first seen

see how fat they are!"
"Israel's women all grow fat."

"Oh, my Lord!"
She was thinking while looking round her that in this
country women were treated as chattels. Well, it was true
that in her country's art, women were always represented
as being much smaller than the men, the better to indi-
cate their inferior position. It was true that the ordinary
wife spent so much time in the back and least pleasant
part of her house that when put on oath she said, "May
I be sent to the back of the house if I tell a lie."
Smiling at Solomon she said: "If I lie to you, send
me to the back of the house."
"Your house has no back," he said.
"Oh, is it all so perfect?"
"Come and I'll show you."
On either side as they walked was a line of people,

wonderful things. All but the eyes of Solomon's wives.
They were hating this woman, for she had a house of

vants.
200

"Oh yes, I've heard of it. Do you have high winds
here?"
"Sometimes," said Solomon, watching her.
"The sands in my country almost blind us. Well, I
think I'd like a drink of wine."
Solomon was smiling and thinking. They had come to
Khate's house, which smelled of the heat and angers of
quarried stone, of olivewood and cedar, of planks that
had been washed and were pleasantly odorous in the
warm sun. Glancing behind to see if her ladies were com-
ing, Khate then turned and for a few moments looked
across Jerusalem to the mists and greenery of Moab. She
could see no sign of water anywhere. With what did
people irrigate here, with what did they bathe, or did
they bathe at all? She sniffed politely.
Solomon went with her into the house, eager to show
1
201
off its luxuries and to read approval in her face; but
Khate went over and sank to a couch, murmuring that

and he sent for his choicest wine.
She was looking at the ceiling, the floor, the walls.
This was not, it seemed to her, the kind of house a
king might build for a queen. It was—well, too mascu-
line. Spending so much time with women, did he feel
that his structures had to be rugged-for see its huge
beams and broad planks and mighty doors! Nowhere

made beautiful pottery and glass, linen as fine as silk;
designed luxurious furniture inlaid with ivory and ebony
and overlaid with silver and gold; made soft leather

Well, she had brought many things with her: furnish-

She had brought her own dishes, not knowing what to

from delicate vessels . . .
202
Solomon had been observing her. "You don't like it,"

"Oh, my Lord, I do! I like it very much!"
"Are you lying again?"
"Oh no, believe me. I think you build magnificently.
May I bring my ladies in?"
"Oh, by all means."
Her ladies came in, and Solomon was dashed by the
number of persons she had brought with her. Food was

discontent in every tribe north of Judah. All his wives

When the wine came, a sweet light wine from Chios, he

the fairest princess in Egypt and the mistress of my joy."
In Egypt she had learned his language. She now spoke

quisitely you say things! I drink to King Solomon who
will be the greatest and wisest king that Israel will ever

"Thank you," he said.
Then he left her to recover from the journey, and that
evening he sat with his mother, sipping wine.
"She does look like a remarkable woman," said Bath-

like your city?"
"I don't suppose she does. How could she? She is used
to fountains and gardens and many trees and pools, not

trees without more water."
"Did she mention water?"
"Oh yes. What can I have but cisterns?—and cistern

"Is there no stinking water in Egypt?"
"I saw none. These people bathe two or three times

a day. What will she think of us when she smells our

"I guess, my son, you'll have to tell her that one bath

And what were Solomon's women thinking, now that
the Egyptian princess sat in her own house?
Havveh was alone in her small chamber, sitting with
her face toward the door, her elbows on her thighs and
her chin in her palms. When feeling hateful, a mood that
was becoming chronic with her, she usually sat as now,
and the liquid depths of her eyes seemed to be adulterated

evil; but suddenly, as though her mind had filled with
light, her eyes would open wide and the colors of anger
would fade. In one hand she clasped the locket which held
her soul. In her other hand pressed against her chin was a
small bone knife which she had been using to pare her
nails.
Queen Naamah, looking as though she had drunk aloes,
took her eldest son, Rehoboam, a pale slender youth, and
went out to sit on an eastern slope that overlooked the

204
would kill himself with strong drink and gluttony, for

"This wicked woman from Egypt has a palace of her own,

The gods will not permit it!..."
Queen Zeruah was also determined that her son would
be king. Unlike most of the King's women she felt no
bitterness toward Khate and had no wish to scheme against

ought to be Israel's next king.
Jeroboam was sitting with her. He said: "Are you
thinking of the woman from Egypt?"
"Yes."
"Is she a wicked woman?"
"No, darling, I guess not."
"Who is the greatest queen now? Is it this woman,

"It is not your mother."

"Only this, tell him to be very discreet, not to stir up

"But there is trouble," said Jeroboam, recalling what
Ahijah had told him only a few weeks ago.
Queen Etillit, the Syrian, had rushed to a chamber
205
and summoned a maid-servant, a wretched slave who
never knew from one day's end to the next when she
would be beaten to death. Etillit took evil delight in
flogging her servants. It was said of her that before com-
ing here she had slain a girl with an oak cudgel.
Etillit could think of nothing to do but make her toilet
again, though she had spent the early hours under the
hands of her maid. The girl began to brush Etillit's black
hair but fright made her awkward and she fumbled.
In the next moment Etillit was on her feet, her eyes
aflame, her mouth cursing. She smote the slave's face
with the heel of her hand and then looked wildly round
and round, her furious eyes seeking a weapon. She saw a
clay figurine of Astarte and this she seized and broke
into fragments over the woman's head. Clasping her
hands in supplication the slave sank to her knees but
this humble posture was pitch to Etillit's fire. She ran
round and round, hunting for a weapon more deadly. She
took a heavy pitcher and dashed on the woman and
pounded her skull with it. Moaning, the terrified creature
raised her arms above her head, trying to protect her
face.
"You dog!" howled Etillit, having learned that among
the Hebrews dog was an epithet of abuse. "You bitch!
Why did you yank my hair? Answer me, you Philistine
jackal! Answer me!"
Etillit thought next to drag the slave into a hallway
and flog her there. She grasped an ankle and pulled, but
she was a small woman and rage had made her weak.
Flinging the door open she called to a guard.
"Come here!" she cried. The guard came over to the
doorway and looked in. "Take this dog out of my
house!"
The man seized the slave's wrists and pulled her out-
side; and there the woman lay, moaning and shuddering.
206
Taking the guard's whip, Etillit was preparing to lash

"What's the trouble here?" It was one of Khate's
men on his way to the King's quarters.
"How dare you speak to Queen Etillit?"
"Weariness with life. Do you intend to kill her?"
"It's none of your business!"
The man bent to look at the slave's face. "I think she's
dying."
"She's my property!"
"Oh, of course. But why not take her outside? Any-

Etillit rushed back into her room and flung herself
upon her bed. She began to weep and her tears were those
of a woman who hated children because she had none-
who hated the other queens and who hated the King
because he no longer summoned her to his bed. He had
found her barren and had said that she was accursed.
In the paroxysm of her fury and grief she put an open
mouth to her arm and bit into her flesh until she tasted
blood. Then she began to suck at the wound. "May the
god Bel kill them all!" she moaned. "All!-all!"
Outside her door the guard had knelt and was raising
the slave's eyelids to look at her eyes. Convinced that
she was dead he arose and for a few moments looked
round him. There was nobody else in the hallway; other
queens had heard Etillit's wild fury but they had not
opened their doors, being used to her tantrums. The
guard picked the body up and flung it across his shoulder
and bore it outside.

19
THOUGH HIS OFFICIALS LIED to him and flattered him,
telling him what they thought he wanted to hear, Sol-
omon knew that there were rumblings of discontent
throughout his kingdom. He was crushing his people with
taxes; for his court he was taking all the best that Israel
produced. It belonged to him by divine right but his
people were the sons of the desert, with a very strong
sense of individual worth and of human rights. They
were burning with anger against him, and Ahijah was out
there, fanning the flames. As though all that were not
enough, he had a multitude of problems in his court since
Khate's arrival. Etillit, for instance, was convinced that
a god had come to her room and lain with her. Now
Solomon knew that the gods sometimes came to fertilize
a woman but he doubted that any god could be inter-
ested in this barren creature. It was a story full of mis-
chief and wickedness and the whole court was gossiping
about it.
He laid the matter before Bathsheba. "Any king's a
fool," he said, "who takes more than one wife."
"But you've had to," said Bathsheba gently. "The best
way to establish alliances is to marry into the royal
houses."
"If any god came to this creature, it must be Baal-
zebub, the god of flies."
"Most likely."
"Or the god of beetles." And with grim humor he
added: "Or the god of worms and snakes."
"But she's not a serious problem."
"But she is. She's trying to make me look ridiculous.
208
She's telling that I have only the left stone because most
of my children are daughters and that she will have a
child by a god. Why in the name of the underworld do
I have so many daughters and so few sons? Am I weak?
Is my right stone no good?"
"Oh no," said his mother wearily. "You're merely
tormenting yourself. As a matter of fact, it might be
well if Queen Khate has no son. He might be a pretender
to the throne and have powerful backing."
"Khate doesn't want any children."
"No?"
"She knows how to destroy the seed."
For a few moments Bathsheba regarded him with as-
tonishment. "But won't the Pharaoh expect a grandson?"
"He was sick when I was there last. He'll be dead
any day."
"But how does she destroy the seed?"
"I don't know. It's an Egyptian secret and she hasn't
told me."
"Well, well!" said Bathsheba, and she laughed mer-
rily. "We are an ignorant people, aren't we?"
"My trouble," said Solomon sadly, "is riches. I don't
have them. How am I to get them? My factories are pro-
ducing nails, fishhooks, ropes, baskets, pottery-but I
can't trade them for very much. I have a good copper
mine but I have no gold and silver mines. I'll never have
a kingdom like the Pharaoh's. If I only had slaves to
sell! I could trade slaves for a lot of things I need."
"That is true," said Bathsheba, thinking of his prob-
lems. "Kings get slaves by going to war."
"Yes. Well, I could conquer some of these small kings
around me and sell their people but I don't want to.
Should I sell some of my people?"
"No. Do you want all the tribes to rise against you?"
"I have no slaves," said Solomon, shaking his head.
209
"Even the ones I have are getting old and dying. I don't
have enough workmen. I want more stables like the ones
in Megiddo, and forts and temples and warehouses. My
wives want a lot of altars built. But if my slaves all die
and I have nothing to hire workmen with, what will I

days he's going to want payment.'
"I guess you just can't do as much as you thought you
might. But never sell or enslave your own people. Ahijah
would be furious."
"Ahijah. I'd forgotten him. Have you seen him lately?"
"I have a report on him every week. He's still telling
the people that you're a wicked king."
One evening Solomon spoke of Ahijah to Khate. He
often went to her house in the evenings to drink wine
with her, to enjoy her luxurious furnishings, to talk.
"I don't understand prophets," she said. "I mean
lunatics, for that is your word for them. My father's
kingdom is overrun with priests but I had never heard

in the world but yours have these lunatics?"
"I suppose not," said Solomon, feeling more and more
that his people were a queer people. "I've never heard of
any others."
"What is this Ahijah like?"
"A dirty, uncouth, half-naked, ignorant, wild-eyed
lunatic."
Khate laughed, and her laughter came out of the rich
full fountains of her being. "My Lord, is he really like

"Because they're much like him."
"You mean the common people, of course. Can't you

210
told you what Israel is like. A long time ago there was a

"Yes."

"Yes, and a lot more-"

"Well, anyway, other kings are not plagued with

He believes that Yah has taken us not only to be his

"My Lord, so far as I am concerned I am glad you

have! Is Israel to be forever torn between its kings and

"If I could figure out a way-"
"We must. We will."

"I MUST SEE THIS MAN AHIJAH," Khate said the next

When Mara saw him she washed his feet and set food

220
thing like terror in his face. Ahban was breathing heavily

Was it true that the King called Zeruah his lily of the
valley? Was he lying with her night after night in amor-

groaned and hated the King and quickened his stride.
What an evil King he was! He now drove around Is-
rael in Khate's electrum chariot drawn by two white
horses. He had had chariot-races at Megiddo. He was
building altars in Jerusalem for his alien wives. He was
living in the most extravagant luxury and crushing his
people with tithes; but worse than these, he was building
a house for the gods, believing that the proud and mighty
Yah would live in the same building with Shamash and
Melech and Bel.
On the hill above the city he stopped and looked at
the temple and the sight of it filled him with blind furies.
Then he followed a path and was not surprised to learn
that Solomon was encouraging the merchants of all na-
tions to come here and show their wares. The shops stood
with open fronts to the street; the merchants sat on
the earth or on a mat, with their goods piled around
them. Ahijah's shocked eyes saw pottery from western
isles, gay woolens and plaids from Babylon, scented
woods and gums from southern Arabia, embroideries
from Damascus, jewelry, vessels of bronze and electrum,
and furniture from Egypt, and clothes and trinkets and
ornaments from the Phoenician coast cities. Confection-
ers had preserved dates, syrups, pastries smelling of
honey and spices, almonds, pistachio nuts. Goldsmiths
with anvils, pincers, hammers and small forges were set-
221
ting precious stones in gold or silver bracelets, necklaces,
hair ornaments, rings; and all around him, as he went
back and forth along the paths, were the people from
other lands. Solomon, with a gesture of hospitality, had
opened his city to the world, this city that should have
been set apart for Yah and Yah's chosen people. Ahijah
saw Syrians, both men and women, naked to the waist,
dressed in a kind of many-colored drawers, bound at the

shone with jewels. The mountaineers from Lebanon

under his red petasos made of weasel skin. They were
all here, all the wicked of the earth; and he shuddered

to live apart, aloof and unmixed.
The house of the gods was on a kind of plateau above
the old Jebusite fortress, gleaming in the bright sun,
standing in appalling contrast to the huts and shacks
downhill from it. A group of buildings enclosed within a
quadrangle, it copied the style of the Phoenicians (who,
Khate had said to Ahiah, had copied the Egyptians), as
well as of the Tyrians and Babylonians. Ahijah could
see nothing in it that came from his people; and he
stared up at the cherubim-for the two pillars, called
Jachin and Boaz, meaning the sun and the moon, had
been patterned after Melkarth's pillars in Tyre, one of
gold and one of green glass. He looked at the large
222
winged figures with four faces-of man, lion, bull and
eagle-which were intended to symbolize divine attri-
butes. He knew that these also had been copied from
heathen religions. Where in this house was Yah's spirit
manifested? Only in the beautifully veined olivewood of
which the cherubim had been carved; or in the wood of
the doors. Not in the stone columns !-columns like them
stood in the house of Paphos as emblems of the god's
staff; not in a score or a hundred impious and wanton
things to be seen everywhere-not in the white poplar
posts, a favorite symbol of Melkarth, worshiped when
his votaries howled and screeched and lacerated them-
selves, begging the god to return from the underworld
cave. Even the site had been sacred to alien gods.
Later in the day Ahijah tried to enter the inner

He fell into talk with one of the workmen and the

IF KHATE COULD HAVE LOOKED into Ahijah's heart
when he strode angrily out of her house she might have
sent Zeruah after him and have washed her hands of
both of them. Ahijah went to a cave under a hill where,
when in Jerusalem, he usually spent his nights; and the
next day while he was staring at the temple and loathing
it, Jeroboam slipped up to him and spoke. Jeroboam, now
a tall youth, strong and handsome, with a forehead and
smile like his mother's, with the flashing black eyes of
Ahijah, was now serving as a foreman.
231
"Ahijah," he cried, "when did you come?"
"Yesterday," said Ahijah, looking strangely and curi-
ously at the youth before him.
"What do you think of the godhouse?"
"Yah is not in it."
Jeroboam glanced anxiously round him for he knew
that there were spies everywhere. He did not know that
a stooped man in peasant garb who seemed to be busy
examining his feet was Bathsheba's spy, set to watch Ahi-
jah. "Have you seen my mother?"
"Yes."
"Did you talk to her?"
"No. I was not alone with her."
"When she comes out a eunuch guards her."
"I know it," said Ahijah, still staring at Jeroboam as
though to fathom his depths and measure his powers.
"Did you know the King is going to make me a collec-
tor of tithes ?"
"No." Ahijah despised all the collectors of taxes.
"My mother arranged it for me. Would you like to
talk to her?"
"Not yet."
"My mother spends a lot of time with Queen Khate.
I don't know why," said Jeroboam, looking at the
prophet.
"You will know some day."
"When I'm old?" asked Jeroboam mischievously. "The
King is building altars for the alien gods. Have you seen
them ?"
"I will."
"Isn't Yah angry?"
"Yes, he is very angry.'
Only now Jeroboam became conscious of Ahijah's
fixed and searching stare. "Why are you looking at me
that way?" he asked.
232
"You will know some day."
"It seems I have many things to learn," said Jeroboam,
smiling.
"More things than you have ever dreamed of."
Yes, Ahijah had seen the alien altars; there was little
that his sharp eyes missed in the King's city. Living close
to the traditions of his people, of the nomad and the
desert, and remembering from his father and his father's
father all the precious details of the wilderness customs,
he had resolved to preserve them at any cost, for he
had seen with pain and despair and anger how one by
one they were being corrupted by alien habits and beliefs.
In Canaanite communions no sad faces were allowed, no
lepers petitioning divine mercy, no beggars. These wicked.
people danced to music and sang and rejoiced at their
feasts.
Scattered over the land were the high places, each hill-
top crowned with a small grove. Among the trees were
stones that had been sacred to the Canaanites and were
now sacred to many of the Hebrews who lived in the
area. So far into error had some of Ahijah's people gone
that they propitiated local gods, hanging their clothes
on the trees, feasting by the stones, and smearing their
blood on the columns and pillars. Even worse, on some
of the high places was a bower, curtained off, in which
the devotees sexually consummated their worship, after
making offerings of fruit and incense to the god's stone
phallus and kissing it while chanting their praises. Women
were being persuaded by wicked priests that it was a
privilege, indeed, an honor, to be fertilized by those who
acted as the agent of the local baal.
In the southern deserts Ahijah had seen Arab fathers
burn their daughters alive, while wild music drowned their
screams and the lamentations of their mothers. That
was a wicked custom. Among his own people the first-
233
born sons had been offered to Yah. They were not being
offered now, and this contemptuous indifference to Yah's
share of the firstfruits Ahijah was determined to erase.
He had so many things to do, he reflected, while taking
his way to the Mount of Olives. He liked this limestone
range of summits east of Jerusalem and separated from
it only by the Vale of Kidron. There was the olive, the
king of trees, with its gnarled and twisted trunk and
its gray-green foliage. On the southernmost hill Solomon
had built a sanctuary for Milcom, god of the Ammon-
ites, and on another spur a holy place for Shamash, god
of Moab. To both of these Naamah was making offer-
ings, and at the shrine of Milcom Ahijah saw her one
morning a week after he had talked to Jeroboam.
He had spent the night on a summit, waiting for the
new moon, whose appearance marked the beginning of
each month. Trumpets were blown when it appeared,
gifts were offered and incense was burned, and the people
saluted it by kissing their hands. Ahijah had wished to
learn if it was discolored; signs of blood on it would
be an indication of Yah's wrath. When at last it came
up and he looked at it through a lacework of olive twigs
he kissed his right hand to it and said, "Peace be with
Yah's people!"
He had been awakened by sounds in the direction of
Milcom's altar and could now see several persons there.
A few moments later he heard the voice of a bull, and
on going forward a little he perceived that Naamah was
preparing to make an offering. But that was not it at
all. In poor health, she was afraid that she might not
live to see her son enthroned, and she was out here to
prolong her life. The bull had been consecrated by her
priests and was being led to a platform that had been
laid over a pit. Naamah now cast aside her garments
and descended naked into the pit, standing under the
234
bull's neck. After the animal had been maneuvered into
position, several men grasped it and a priest with a sharp
knife cut its throat. Blood gushed down and drenched
Naamah. Ahijah could not see her but he could hear
her ecstatic cries, and he thought that under the deluge
she was waving her arms and shaking her long hair.
Whether her mouth was upturned to drink the blood he
could not tell but he imagined that it was and he shud-
dered. The dying bull was allowed to sink to its knees
but otherwise was held in position until all the life had
run from its throat. Then Naamah came up out of the
pit, red and dripping from head to feet, with blood fall-
ing from her ears and nose and hair. She put her gar-
ments on and set out for the city.
The bull was now skinned and ceremonially burned.
Ahijah had seen these blood baptisms. Sickened by
the wickedness of it he turned north up the hill and pres-
ently came close to a booth on a high place, wherein
the qedeshah or sacred nun waited for men to come and
lie with her.
He went boldly to the arbor and looked in. When the
woman saw him she came forward, crying with delight
and slipping out of her robe as she came. Naked, she
stood before him, and glancing at her sharply Ahijah
thought she was one of his own people. The girl-she
was hardly more than a child, though her body was ma-
tured-reached out, smiling, to touch him. If she had
known that this man was Yah's greatest prophet she
might have fainted, or she might have flung herself upon
him, begging him to get her with child. She saw only a
tall thin man, handsome, and himself half-naked, with
the passion of the summer's sun in his eyes. Showing
off her charms she said:
"Handsome one, you have come to the high place

She was, of course, merely uttering a formula, but

He recoiled from her, but his eyes, wide and fascinated,

way to insult any man was to outrage his beard, and

and anointed with much ceremony. The old fellow al-

"You son of folly!" Ahijah roared. "Go away from
this wicked place!"
The man's thin old voice was shrieking: "You've fouled

"You abomination!" cried Ahijah, yanking.
"May the gods slay you!"
"You abominable son of pollution!"
"May you die tonight! Take your unclean hands off

Sickened by the awful indignity of it, by the evil hands

This night he occupied the small tent of a friend on

But Solomon was not a strong king; he postponed
problems rather than face them. He had already too long
postponed the fight which he had never had the courage
to make, and now he imagined that lightning would
strike, though he did not know when or where. He pre-
ferred peace to strife, his own voluptuous pleasures to
the arduous labor of building a strong and consolidated
kingdom; and he went ahead with preparations for a
great feast. Eager to entertain in the style of other kings,
he was importing the choicest food and wines. His palace,
including the halls, courts, royal chambers, chambers for
his wives and concubines, kitchens and storehouses, was
almost completed and for Israel it was an awesome edi-
fice. But it was neither so large nor so splendid by any
means as the royal palaces in the kingdoms around him.
Even so, it was costing him a great deal to furnish it. He
had needed many beds, couches, lampstands, carpets,
chairs, mirrors, curtains and utensils-all imported and
all made of costly materials and workmanship. Still, he
had not afforded the best of everything. Few of his ves-
sels were made of gold; and such things as brasiers, lamp-
stands, kettles, flagons and pitchers were for the most
part of base metals. Few of his chairs were inlaid with
ivory and covered with cloth of gold. But even so, he had
overreached his means, he was hugely in debt to King
Hiram, and in desperation he looked round him for ways
241
to improve his fortunes and maintain such splendors as
he had.
He had gathered round him a huge body of parasites
who fawned on him, drank his costly wines, consumed
his food and contributed nothing to his resources. Besides
all these, each of his wives had her servants and priests.
He had many children and these required a large staff
of guardians and tutors. A few of his older sons, like
Rehoboam, had taken wives and were demanding luxuries
of their own. They wanted palaces in which to live. Yes
indeed, Solomon had become a goaded and anxious man.
He knew that throughout his kingdom, including Ju-
dea, which had been exempted from taxes because of its
leanness, there were signs of anger and revolt. Needing
more laborers as his slaves died, he had taken by force
some of his people. To be placed under the yoke with
slaves was for free men an indignity so wicked and in-
tolerable that they hated the King with all the hot pas-
sions of their souls; and their hatred became all the more
intense as there came to them month after month news
of what Ahijah was saying.
Ahijah! How Solomon hated the man! He had sent
word that he would like to have him come and discuss
with him affairs in Israel, and Ahijah had sent an in-
solent message. "Tell the wicked King," he had said,
"that I will come when Yah sends me. Until then he can
wear sackcloth."

Dispirited, Solomon went to his chambers to rest. He
was not well. For too many years he had indulged his
242
fondness for rich foods and strong spiced drinks. He

nest of vipers, Solomon was convinced, had never lived
under one roof since the world was created with the

with her servants that they suffered from leakage of the
bowels. All the wives but Zeruah were everlastingly de-

But Naamah was the worst one of the lot. She was

And always she begged him to have someone killed. It
might be Zeruah or Ahiah, the scribe, or the oldest
son of a wife or concubine.

After Rehoboam closed the door behind him, Solomon
thought: I can see now that the fight in Israel will be
between the kings and the prophets. I see it now but I
have seen it too late. He placed a hand over his mouth
and stared at the cedar planks in the floor. Or was it
too late? He arose suddenly but felt a sharp pain under
his right ribs and he sank back to the couch with a hand
clasping his side. Then his thoughts turned to his fine
storehouse of herbs and spices; all the gums, resins, con-
diments and sweet essences had been placed on the earth
246
by the gods for man to use and prolong his life. If a
man only knew which of these to eat he probably would
never die but would be immortal like the gods, or would
renew himself like the moon, like the earth.
Still wondering if it was too late, and if not too late
what he might do about it, he went to the storeroom,
feeling pains down his thighs. A guard hastened over to
unbolt the door. The spice chamber was on a long hall-
way that ran past the kitchens. The moment Solomon
stepped inside he felt better; there was borne upon him
a blend of all the fragrant things that he had been able
to buy in the world's marts. The room had no windows
or vents and so it was only by walking round it and
sniffing at the various jars that he was able to identify
the odors and breathe of those which he thought pro-
moted health. Some of the spices, like mustard, grew
freely in Israel, but others, like cinnamon and pepper,
were so rare and costly and had to be sought in such
far places that they were worth their weight in gold. He
paused at oil of cinnamon bark. He thrust a finger into
a jar of it, reflecting meanwhile that this delightful sub-
stance was found in mysterious places in the southern
deserts by a people who were said to wage war against
monstrous bats. As for pepper, at which he paused next,
it was never tasted by any but kings and others of great
wealth.
After eating a little cinnamon bark and pepper he went
to a chest that was stuffed with savory of the mint fam-
ily, which was highly regarded by his people; and to
thyme, which was a favorite alike of bees and men. The
sweet-smelling coriander seeds, to which he came next,
his nostrils flaring, were an aid to digestion. He ate a
few of the seeds. Then he ate some cardamon seeds,
which a people in the far north called the seeds of para-
dise. He breathed deep of nutmeg, cloves and allspice,
247
and ate a little of each; and it seemd to him that his
pains were leaving him. Then he plugged one nostril
and inhaled the odor of ginger.
Having gone halfway round the room he paused be-
fore a jar and sniffed; and, unable to tell what this sub-
stance was, he sent for his chief cook. The man sniffed
and said:
"My Lord, it is marjoram."
"What does it cure?"
"Toothache, my Lord."
Solomon rubbed some over his gums, for his teeth
had been giving him trouble. Next to the marjoram was
a jar of sage, whose aromatic and slightly camphorous
odor was said to prolong life. He ate a little of it and
began to search for the aphrodisiacs. His godlike crea-
tive powers were waning. Many times in recent months
he had stared at his image in a bronze mirror, holding
his head down and trying to see his skull, for he was
losing his hair. By rubbing with his fingers he could tell
that he had a bald spot on his crown but he was unable
to see it.
"Your hair is all right," Bathsheba had said.
"Don't lie to me. It's falling off, and a bald king is
like a bull who has lost his stones... There, feel."
"You have a lot of hair. It is fine like the threads
