"I can't feel spider threads," he had said, running
finger round and round.
It was a terrible thing for a man to lose his potency,
and for a king above all others. In an effort to restore
his creative powers he had eaten many things besides
strong spices. Some men preferred testicles to nourish
potency and postpone the gray years, particularly those
of tiger and lion; others were partial to the brains of
sparrows, or eringoe-root and valerian-root, or bee-wings
248
and rhinoceros horns; and some drank the blood of bats,
or fennel-juice and oil of champak. Still others favored
eggs and beetle-scales, or the wine of mandrake-root,
which was one of Solomon's daily tonics. He was not
sure that mandrake wine aroused his ardor but he had
no doubt at all of caper-berries, and of these he now ate
generously, thinking meanwhile that at his feast he would
provide jars for the invigoration of his guests. After

and elated; but before leaving the room he mixed in his
palm some sage, oil of thyme, a little saffron and storax,
and some gums and resins, and anointed himself on his
bald spot. He anointed his right large toe, the point of
his staff, the end of his nose. While doing this there
came to him a moment of revelation, as though a god
had entered the chamber. The hidden spirit told him
that he ought to grow a long beard.
Still rubbing his nose into his palm he went to his
chambers and laid aside his garments and looked at him-
self in a mirror. Then he stared down at his private
parts and their reflection, back and forth. The thought
occurred to him that if he could grow dense heavy hair
all over his body from his throat to his toes he might
live to be a very old man. He was still gazing down
when with a start he bent over and looked at himself in-
tently. On his staff was a small red spot about the size
of a pomegranate seed. Apalled, he stared at it for sev
eral moments, and then, feeling weak and old, he went
to a couch and sank.
He was horrified because the genitals were worshiped,
being the creative part of man or woman, and therefore
godlike. The male organ in its upright position was
thought quite fully to manifest the attributes of the
gods; and of its emblems, the favorite with some women
was the serpent, of others, the sacred poles and pillars.
249
the obelisks and columns. With men the favorite sym-
bols of the female part were certain shells, shaped like
the portal of birth, or the mounds and high places with
their sacred groves. The ark itself was an emblem of
the holiest of all holies, the womb, the house of life. For
all these things human emotion was tender and reverent.
A breastless woman, or a woman forever in her courses,
was as abhorrent as a eunuch. Solomon remembered that
he had seen above some Egyptian doors a horseshoe-
like object, representing the portal of birth, placed there
for luck.
He was dismayed and horrified because reverence for
his organs was a part of his religion. Since he was the
god's anointed, his parts were semi-divine. Any disease
or imperfection in this holy center of his being was a
sign that the gods were displeased. And so it was that
he stared with horror and pressed gently round the red
spot. He felt debased. He felt an emotion akin to shame.
He felt alone in a dark unhappiness as wide as the world
and as deep as the loneliness in the eyes of those dying.
Quickly dressing, he sent a servant to tell Khate to

lowing.
"My right stone!" he howled.
"Oh, my Lord, and your hair!"
"Hair all over me! What a king I will be!"
"Your stones and your hair!"
"And my staff!"
Wiping at her eyes with a piece of scented cloth she
looked up at him; and his face was so red and anguished
that she burst again. She got up and walked round and

"If your people would only laugh!"
"By the gods, if they only would!"
"Ahijah-if he would laugh!"
"Ohh!" he cried, tortured. "Ahijah-"
"-has never laughed!"
"Never!"
"My Lord, that's the solution. We'll make him

"If you can make him laugh," said Solomon, sinking
to the couch, "you can have my kingdom."

"I WILL MAKE THAT PROPHET LAUGH," Khate rashly
told herself and sent for Ahiah. The scribe had been
telling his King that an Egyptian soul, wandering on
its way to judgment, protested to Osiris that he had
not oppressed his fellowmen, had slain no one, had not
lied or thieved, had not taken milk from the mouths of
babes, but on the contrary had given food to the hungry,
253
water to the thirsty, and clothes to the naked. The
ghost said further that he had been a father to the or-
phan, a husband to the widow, and a shelter for them
who were cold. Solomon listened gravely and Ahiah con-
cluded: "He who has pity upon the poor gives riches to
God."
"Did Khate tell you these things?"
"Yes, my Lord."
"Some women are very subtle and cunning in their
efforts to influence men. She uses my scribe to make an
Egyptian of me."
"Oh no, she merely tells me these things because I'm
interested."
"And you come and tell me. Don't you think I see
through the conspiracy?"
"My Lord-"
Then a servant looked in to say that Queen Khate
wanted the scribe when he was free; and at the door
Solomon patted the gnomelike fellow and said jestingly:
"She wants to tell you some more about the one who
clothed the naked and sheltered the cold. Then you can
record it as the wisdom of King Solomon."
When Ahiah entered her parlor Khate waved him to
a couch and sent for some of her best wine. "Ahiah, you
darling," she said, for she often called him her darling,
"are you devoted to your King?"
"Yes, my Lady."
"Well, I suppose you know that affairs in Israel are
not going well. Storm, wind, fog, smoke, shadow, fam-
ine and Ahijah. The lightnings of Yah's anger, the thun-
ders of his wrath, pestilence, plagues, diseases—all these,
says the prophet, are coming."
Regarding her closely, he observed again that she was
rather grotesque but fascinating and sometimes beauti-
254
ful. He had learned to love her and his respect for her

"Ahijah-"
"We must do something about him. Your King has

"What can we do?"
"Well, something surely. Are we going to let this

"Don't they?" he said, feeling rebuked.
"Do they? The King was laughing the other evening.
But I've never seen you laugh. Does Ahijah ever laugh?"
"No. Ahijah believes that laughter leads people into
wickedness."
"Really? Oh no! Into wickedness? Ahiah, are you

"Oh no, my Lady. That is what he believes. Yah

"I can believe that. A god of lightning and storm and

"Oh, my Lady, you could never do that."
"I can try. Is he in the city?"
"Yes."
"I want you to find him and persuade him to come
here. Say I have something very important to tell him."
Ahijah at that moment was staring at the cherubim
and observing with horror that they represented the male
and female in intimate union. Ever since his struggle
with the naked girl at the hilltop bower he had been
dreaming dreadful dreams in which sometimes he was
pursued by a number of women, all naked and ardent,
and sometimes by demons in the form of women-by
the rabicu that lay in ambush, the ekimmu that seized
255
and bore away, the gallu that murdered the unwary;
and then again by Lilit who sucked from a man his
maleness, thus destroying him. Ahijah knew that his god
was tempting him and he was proud that he had not
only resisted the wiles of these evil creatures but had
felt more withdrawn and inaccessible to each fresh ap-
proach. And he realized now more deeply than ever be-
fore that men would be godlike but for the blandish-
ments of women.
He was looking at the cherubim and loathing them
when the scribe said softly: "Ahijah."
Ahijah turned and looked at the dwarfed man before
him. "What do you want?"
"Queen Khate asks you to come to her house."
"No!"
"She has something very important to tell you."
"What can an Egyptian woman tell Yah's prophet?"
"She did not confide in me. It is for your ears alone."
"Tell her to go back to Egypt."
"She wants to talk to you for the good of Israel."
And Ahiah added slyly: "She knows what great power
rests in your hands." Ahijah was flattered. Watching
him closely the scribe saw him unbend a little and look
toward Khate's house.
"Is she alone?"
"She said that she would see you alone." Then Ahiah
took his arm and the prophet allowed himself to be led

Khate received him with her most gracious manner,
bowing a little before him and making birdlike gestures
of respect and homage; and Ahijah stalked into her par-
lor and stood, stiff and haughty, staring round him. He
was looking for Zeruah.
Reading his mind Khate asked: "Would you like to
see Zeruah? She's an old friend of yours, I know. I can
256
send for her but I thought perhaps you'd rather be

"Why have you asked me here?"
"To ask your advice," she said, softly, tactfully. She
did not have food and drink on the tables, as at their
former meeting, having perceived that he bitterly re-
sented all signs of luxurious living. "Will you sit?"
Ahijah sat and crossed his hands on his lap. He was
stiff and suspicious and he looked angry. As for Khate,
if she had had a murderous madman before her she could
not have been more tactful in both her gestures and her
speech. And yet, in spite of her delicately intuitive per-
ceptions, she blundered in her next words.
"I wanted to talk with you about what is best for Is-
rael."
"Do you presume to tell me what is best?"
"Oh no! I want you to tell me."
Her humility softened him a little. For a few moments
he seemed to be sniffing the sweet scents around him.
Then he met her eyes. Again she saw in his eyes pas-
sion and loneliness, hate, scorn, contempt, all of these
flashing and burning in the dark depths. In her eyes he

learned that the men of his people did not like to have

"Are you coming to the King's banquet with me?"
"No."
"Oh, but I want you to so much. I have so many
things to learn from you and we could sit together at

"You don't have to go," he said.
"But really, I do. There are some things expected
of me." He gave no answer. She saw that he was waiting
and she thought he seemed to be listening. "Ahijah,
257
through no will of my own I am one of Israel's queens.
You are Israel's great and glorious prophet, with many

"Israel will be the greatest among the kingdoms," he

"Very well, Israel will be the greatest. We both want

The expression on his face was almost a sneer. "You

"Oh, but I do."
"Israel will be great, but not with wicked kings."
"All right, won't you tell me about it? The King

He glanced at her, his eyes luminous with scorn. “Tell

She saw that a dreadful passion was rising in the man.

He arose, with passion boiling up in him and swelling the

Khate arose, feeling a little frightened. There was
258
in this man a capacity for violence that appalled her.

Khate tried to laugh and faltered and then emptied

ably and completely. She suspected now that the forces
making for disaster were too mighty and headstrong to

She sent a servant to ask if the King would see her,
either in his quarters or in her house; and a few minutes

She arose and shrugged, feeling inept and foolish,

as father or brother than as ardent mate, and added:

24
LIKE ALL RULERS OF HIS TIME Solomon held the power
of life or death over every person in his kingdom. He
was the absolute judge; beyond him there was no court
of appeal. Once a week he mounted an ornate gilded
throne-seat, on either side of which stood a bronze lion.
When sitting as judge he wore a great flowing robe
265
that spread over the ivory and goldleaf of his throne
and fell in masses around him. It had many hues because
it was widely believed that gods, like the sun, wore robes
of many colors.
He had no complete code of laws to guide him, such
as a Babylonian king had received from Shamash; but
Ahiah was busy codifying such laws as his people had.
Many matters had come before the King, and progres-
sively, as the years passed and he listened to the gentle
philosophy of Khate, he had become more lenient. He
had formerly said that if an ox gored a slave, the owner
of the slave was to be paid for his loss and the ox was
to be stoned. In more recent judgments he had omitted
the stoning. He had said that if a man's ox killed the
ox of another, the offending beast was to be sold and
the price of it divided equally between the two men.
He had said that if a farmer carelessly caused another's
harvest to burn he was to compensate him from the best
in his own fields. Ahiah thought these judgments remark-
ably wise and for the time they were. But Solomon had
thrown overboard some of the laws which Yah was said
to have given to the prophets, and this Ahijah knew.
One day Ahiah asked: "If a man seduce a virgin who
is not betrothed and lie with her what shall be his punish-
ment?"
Solomon smiled slyly and asked: "What would Ahi-
jah say?"
"I don't know. He'd probably say there was to be no
punishment."
"This is my judgment, he shall take her as his wife
if the father will consent; and if the father will not con-
sent, then he shall pay to the father the price of the
girl."
Another time Ahiah asked: "If a son curse his father
what shall be the punishment?"
266
"You know what Yah says."
"Yes, my Lord."
"But I say I shall first have a look at the father and
the son." And that also Ahiah thought remarkably
lenient, since a curse held enormous and dreadful power.
It was generally believed that a person who had been
cursed would die, and it had been judged fitting that
the one who had laid the curse should die also.
But if Solomon had outraged Ahijah by ignoring some
of Yah's commandments, he filled him with murderous
wrath when the prophet learned that Khate, the abomi-
nable Egyptian, had sat with the judge. In earlier years
Solomon had placed Zadok the priest on his right and
Ahiah on his left but because Zadok agreed with Ahijah
in practically all matters the King for several years now
had been sitting without him.
In the court of judgment there were benches on which
the plaintiffs and defendants could sit, together with their
witnesses; and surrounding them were armed guards.
Gossips, idlers and mischief-mongers were shut out.
The first day Khate was present she sat on a high
chair behind the King, hidden from most of those in
the forefront of the court. The first case was that of
an outraged bridegroom. He said his wife had not been
a virgin, though he had paid a good price for her and
had taken her into his house in good faith. He said that
a midwife, whom he had called in, had slyly produced
a cloth on which was the stain of rabbit-blood; and this
had occurred on the bridal night. The midwife, he said,
had been bribed, for she snickered and peered round
her like a thief.
"9
His face grave but his emotions suffused with humor
Solomon said: "You paid a big price for this woman.'
"Our Lord and King, I did!"
267
"You tell me you gave the firstborn of a cow and five
measures of corn."
"Yes, my Lord."
"That ought to buy a very beautiful virgin, don't you
think?"
"Yes, my Lord."
"And you say this midwife is the daughter of Peleth
of Shiloh, an ill-favored wretch."
"Very ill-favored, my Lord. He consorts with witches."
"And this midwife's name, you tell me, is Dinah."
"Yes, my Lord.”
"Now after you summoned this woman you saw her
and your wife talking together and you knew that they
conspired against you."
There was a faint smile on Ahiah's face. His King
loved to tease people and draw out their sly and evil
motives; putting a person at ease, he would encourage
him to embellish his tale until, betrayed by avarice or
stupidity, he ensnared himself.
"The moment you saw the cloth you knew of course
that you had been tricked. Can you always tell rabbit-
blood from the blood of a virgin?"
"Yes, my Lord."
Solomon turned to Khate and said in a low voice:
"What do you think?"
"I think he's lying with every breath."
"So do I... Well now," said Solomon, addressing the
plaintiff before him, "if I bring several cloths and lay
them before you, some stained with one blood and some
with another, and if you fail to tell me which is rabbit.
and which is human I'll have you stoned. I shall do that,
or you may take this wife and cherish her."
"My Lord!" The poor wretch was beginning to
tremble.
"You have lied to me," said Solomon. "You felt you
268
had made a bad bargain, or more likely you grew weary
of her after you were sated; and so you conspired to get
the return of your beast and the five measures of corn.
I imagine that you bought the girl because you wanted
to lie with her."
The man threw himself prostrate, with his forehead to
the floor.
"Get up," said Solomon, but the man was unable to
rise. Two guards came forward and seized him and
stood him on his shaking legs; and Solomon said: "Ocran,
son of Kohath, your wickedness stinks in my nostrils.
It would be a just punismment to consign you to your
wife's father, to be his slave seven years. You will give
to him the firstborn of two cows and ten measures of
corn for bringing dishonor on his name; and if I ever
learn that you have been unjust to your wife without
cause, your punishment will be much more severe than
that. Do you understand what I have said?"
"Yes!"
"Take him away." Solomon turned to Khate.
"My Lord," she said, "King Akhnaton never passed
judgment with more wisdom."
The next plaintiff accused his neighbor of having stolen
his gods, by which he meant his teraphim or god-images.
These two men were farmers and it was plain that they
were very poor. Their only garment was a ragged cloth
that barely covered their loins. They were dark, bearded,
unclean and surly.
The plaintiff said that he had been toiling in his vine-
yard when he heard a sound as of running feet; where-
upon, hastening to his house, he saw this man, his neigh-
bor, fleeing for dear life, with the idols clasped to his
chest.
"What gods did he steal?" asked Solomon.
"Bel and Ishtar."
269
Solomon turned to Ahiah. In what district did these
men live, and were they Hebrews or Syrians? Ahiah said
they were from Gilead, land of Sihon, petty king of the
Ammonites, and of Og, king of Bashan; but when Sol-
omon questioned the men they said they were from Naph-
tali. Solomon's governor of this district, Ahimaaz, had
married his daughter Basmath. Was it because this
daughter lived there that these men so obviously hated
him?
"Did you steal this man's gods?"
"He owes me two measures of grapes and a measure
of barley."
"Sullen churl, answer your King! Did you steal his
gods?"
"I took them for what he owes me.'
"Why haven't you paid this man his grapes and bar-
ley ?"
The sullen and toilworn fellow drew himself up and
looked into his King's eyes. "I have no grapes and I have
no barley."
"Why haven't you?"
"Because everything I raise is taken for the King's
tables."
For a few moments there was absolute silence in the
chamber. Then guards moved forward, touching their
daggers, because such an indignity offered to the King
deserved instant death. But Solomon waved the guards
back. He turned to Khate and said, "Now listen.
"Do you know Ahijah?" he asked the plaintiff.
"Yes."
"Have you heard him talk?"
"Yes."
"Then why do you have such gods as Bel and Ishtar?
Don't you know there is only one god of Israel and that
his name is Yah?"
270
"Yah is Bel," he said, for like most of his people he
was confused.
"What has Ahijah been telling the people in Naph
tali?...Will you answer your King?"
"He has been telling the people that the King is
wicked."
"Do you think so?"
The man hesitated only a moment. Then he looked
Solomon straight in the eye and said: "Yes."
Solomon turned to Khate. "Shall I sell them to the
Phoenicians?"
"Oh no! Mercy is a better teacher than punishment."
Solomon faced the plaintiff for a moment and then
looked at the defendant. "You will give this man's gods
back to him. Now return to your farm and try to be bet-
ter neighors."
Solomon had been a little angered; he was glad to get
on to the next case. This involved another son of the
soil, a farmer, emaciated, with wild eyes and a loose
open mouth in which only one tooth was visible, brown
with decay. The woman in this case was old and scrawny
and feeble, a wrinkled and halfblind hag who, on hear-
ing a sound, jerked her unkempt head in its direction and
then peered. The man accused her of having laid on
him a death-spell; and a witness whom he had brought
with him said that this woman, whose name was Mikra,
was known by all her neighbors to be an agent of the
demons. In a case of this kind, it was customary to put
the accuser to death if he could not prove his charge;
if he could prove it, the one accused was taken away to
be slain. Solomon looked at the man and the woman and
the witness, trying to read their faces.
"In what ways," he asked the accuser, whose name
was Atarlu, "have you felt the evil?"
The man tried in vain to speak. Moisture gathered
271
over his wild staring eyes and hung in drops from his
lashes. The wrinkles in his faces lay as deep as the bone.
At last with incoherent and babbling speech he said that
he had been working on his threshing-floor when he felt
the presence of this woman and knew that a spell was
being laid on him. He knew this because at once he turned
weak and faint, and there was a pain in him like that
of talons pulling at his bowels, and the earth darkened
with an evil darkness, and overhead he heard the mourn-
ful lamentations of doves. He then saw this woman,
standing away from him toward the sun. By the move-
ments of her lips he knew that she was uttering words.
When he saw that no person was near her he knew that
she was talking to the evil spirits. While he was staring
at her, knowing that she was uttering words against him,
she raised her arms and screamed and denounced him;
and then the earth blackened as though night had en-
gulfed it and he was no longer able to stand. He sank
to the barley chaff and writhed there in torment, know-
ing that a demon had seized him and was struggling
to enter him. He could smell the demon; it had a ter-
rible odor like that from pestilential marshes. In his ears
there was a fearful clamor like that of dragons before
a sheepcote, and he felt his soul being drawn out of him.
He saw his soul leaving him like a light and he called
to it and his soul came back and entered his nostrils,
tickling as it went in. In those periods when the man's
story became unintelligible babble the witness spoke for
him. The witness said he had seen Atarlu's soul and it
was like the new moon. He had seen it go about ten
feet from him and hover and then turn and go back.
This clearly enough was the case of a witch. Indeed,
Solomon reflected, staring at the haggard and evil and
muttering creature, she was the most sinister witch he
had ever seen. He turned to Ahiah.
272
"Is she a witch ?"
"My Lord, ask Queen Khate."
"Is she?" he asked, turning to Khate.
"If there are witches, possibly she is."
"The law says that no witch shall be allowed to live.
We kill them by drowning, stoning or strangling, so there
will be little or no shedding of blood to imperil the
living. Or we bind them to a tree and burn them. Well,
you are sitting as judge today. What is your judgment?"
"I'd say that she's just an old unhappy creature who
has lost her senses. I don't think she could harm any-
one."
"Don't you believe in witches?" asked Solomon, cock-
ing an eye at her.
"No. But in this case I think she would be better off
dead."
"What is to be the method of execution?"
"The quickest and most merciful."
Solomon's judgment was that she was to be struck on
the back of the head with a stone and cast into an alien
river. He summoned Zikla to him and told him what the
form of the punishment was to be and added: "See that
you do it as I have commanded."
The next case was of two men and of a woman who
was the wife of one of them. The two men had been
in a fight. While they were so engaged, the woman, ob-
serving that her husband was getting the worst of it, had
pitched in to assist him and had unwittingly grasped the
enemy by his private parts. That in any case was his
story. That was the charge against her.
Solomon looked at the woman before him. "What is
your name?"
"My Lord, I am Miriam, the daughter of Uri of
Dan."
She was a good-looking woman with intelligence and
273
spirit. "Miriam, is it true that you grasped this man's
secrets ?"
"Oh, my Lord—”
"Don't lie, for Yah is your witness."
"My Lord, I can't say!"
"Why not?"
"I was frightened. Oh, I don't know what I did!"
"What frightened you ?"
"My Lord, my husband was crying. He was hurt. The
enemy stood over him and was kicking him in his face.
Then I saw the enemy reach for a stone and I—well, I
just flew in like a partridge mother who sees her babies
in danger. What I did then I cannot tell."
"Did you try to seize this man's secrets?"
"My Lord, no, oh no!"
"Do you swear it?"
"My Lord, I swear it by Yah, blessed be his holiness!"
"Let me see your fingernails," said Solomon. She held
her hands up and he saw that her nails were not long, as
though worn for gouging, but were almost down to the
pink. He turned to Khate.
"Is she guilty?"
"No wife could ever be guilty in a case of that kind."
"She practically admits her guilt.
99
"She simply doesn't know. She might easily have lied."
"If I don't punish her, this will be more pitch in Ahi-
jah's fire."
"Then it's a wicked fire and Ahijah is a wicked man."
"Miriam-"
"Yes, my Lord."
"After this, if your husband is unjustly attacked, let
him bring the case to me. You keep out of it."
"Yes, my Lord, I will."
The man who had accused her, a very swart heavy-
bearded and stocky fellow, spoke up wrathfully, saying:
274
"Aren't you going to punish this woman for grabbing
on to my holies ?"
Solomon beckoned to Zikla and leaned down to say:
"Put him in chains for a week and bring him back next
time."
The next case involved a father and his son. The father,
who looked more villainous than any other man who had
faced the King this day, had lost an eye, and the other
shone with double intensity. It was fierce and very bright
and as round as a coin. The father said that his son, a
frail sallow lad about ten years old who stood by him,
was rebellious and profane and wicked. Solomon was
sniffing; the man stank horribly as though he had just
crawled out of a sheepcote.
"Go on," Solomon said.
"He refuses to work."
"Do you?" Solomon asked the boy.
The lad stuttered. "I-I-yi-yi-work all I-I can," he
said.
"He is rebellious," said the father, turning his lone
fierce eye on his son.
"I-I-he-he beats me!" the boy said.
Well, a father had a right to flog his son to death if
he wished to. "Do you flog your son?"
"Yah has commanded obedience in sons."
Yah had commanded more than that. Yah had said
that the men of the city should stone the rebellious son
with stones that he die, so that the people should put evil
away, and hear and fear.
"What do you want me to do with your son?"
"Bring him before the men of the city and stone him
until he is dead."
Solomon heard Khate gasp and turned to look at her.
"That is Yah's commandment."
"This poor child is sick from undernourishment."
275
your
"But he is rebellious." To the father he said: "If
son is stoned until he die he will be a complete loss to
you. The King offers you two measures of barley for

The father nodded his head to accept, his shining eye
fixed on the King's face. Solomon looked at a guard and
he came up. "Give him the two measures and tell Zikla
to feed the child."
Other cases came before him. One involved a girl-child
and her token of virginity, which, it was said, had not
been found in the damsel. Solomon said to Ahiah: "Tell
Khate what the law is here."
"Yah has commanded that they shall bring the damsel
to the door of her father's house, and the men of her
city shall beat her with stones that she die."
The next case concerned a young unmarried man and
a married woman who had been found lying together;
and again Solomon said to his scribe: "Tell her the law."
"Yah has said that if a man be found lying with a
woman who is married, then they shall both of them
die, both the man that lay with the woman, and the
woman, so that evil shall be put away from Israel."
Another case was that of a ragged half-insane father
who sought permission to burn his firstborn son on an
altar to Yah because his vineyards had failed him. Sol-
omon cocked an eye at Khate while saying to Ahiah:
"Tell her the law."
"Yah has said that all that belongs to him, both of
man and beast, shall not be redeemed with money or with
other firstfruits but shall surely be put to death."
Feeling weary, and at a loss to know what his judg
ments should be, Solomon postponed these cases; and
when the room was cleared he said to Khate: "Run along
to your house. I'm coming over this evening."
Khate hastened to her house and summoned a maid
276
who used olive and palm oils to soften Khate's skin, stib-
nite to darken her brows and eyes, green malachite along
her nose and a red dye on her lips. Over her wig, which
hung in heavy measured braids to her shoulders, she put
the simple symbolic headdress of a sun-disc with seven
streamers. For her dress she chose a full-length tunic
in pale blue, its fulness at the ankles enhanced by pleated
ruffles. Round her waist she wrapped a linen girdle of
deeper blue, with the knot on the left, the ends hanging
down over her left thigh. On her feet, she wore palm-
leaf sandals, with bronze scarabs over the instep.
She used scents lavishly, and particularly spikenard, of
which the King was fond. This she rubbed on her throat
and breasts. She drenched her girdle with the essence
of lilies, and put a blend of crocus and myrrh on the lobes
of her ears, the nape of her neck, her cheeks. In het
parlor she had various incenses burning, including opo-
balsam, storax, and a cypress resin mixed with saffron.
She had her best wines brought to a table, and told ser-
vants to prepare hot food including fowl.
Solomon came in his royal robes, wearing sandals, but
there was no covering on his thinning hair. He wagged
his short beard imperially and winked. He sat on a couch,
stretched, relaxed, and then leaned forward to sniff at
the wines. "This," he said, indicating the Star of Horus.
Khate sat facing him, and Solomon looked at her
across his golden cup. "Well ?"
"Well!"
"Now you see what some of Yah's commandments
are and why Ahijah is beating his breast so furiously."
"I begin to see the whole thing quite clearly."
Solomon leaned back, looking a little sad and melan-
choly and lonely. It was in such moments that Khate
loved him. She did not speak or even draw a full breath,
for he was staring at her across his cup, with such things
in his eyes as she had never wanted to see there.
277
"You see," he said at last, "what Israel would be like
if I were the kind of king Ahijah wants me to be. And
if I had never gone to Egypt, if I had never heard of
the laws in Babylon, if I had never known you, I might
today be that kind of king."
"My Lord, you honor me much more than I deserve."
"Oh no. But no woman in Israel has had her hand
chopped off in a long time. No son has been stoned at
the city gates. No firstborn son has been burnt to Yah.
No daughter has been burned alive as Jephthah is said to
have burned his daughter. And still Yah has sent no
serpents of fire to bite into his people and no famine to
force them to eat their sons. He has not turned the
rivers to blood."
"Really, did he send serpents of fire?"
"So it is told. Sing the song to me."
In an excellent soprano Khate sang this song:
"It is her love that makes me strong,
she is a book of spells to me!
When I behold my beloved coming my heart is glad,
my arms are spread out to embrace her,
I am as one who carries perfumes!
And when I kiss her her lips are opened
and I make merry without wine
for she is to me as a garden!
She is to me as the wild flowers
and the soft winds across the waters
and oh! she is beautiful when her hand rests in mine!
I put my face to her breast and hear her heart beat,
I feel her lips on my hair
and her breath like lilies of the field is over me!"
Solomon had been studying her as she sang. He said:
"For Ahijah life is fear, for you it is love."
"Why should he fear Yah?"
278
"Because Yah has commanded it. Well, when I am
dead he will move the pigs back into the parlor."
"How well you put it!"
Owlishly he asked: "Did you know one called Joshua
made the sun stand still?"
"No! Did he?"
"Did you know that Yah turned a woman into salt?"
"How dreadful! Where is the poor creature?"
"Standing down there on the salt sea. Of course she
just looks like a short column of salt to me but Ahijah
swears that it is a woman. Well, we can expect such
marvels in Israel for Yah has said, I have made you a
holy people unto me and shall make you the head and
not the tail. But if we do not keep his commandments
he will bring terrors upon us and consumption and the
burning ague that will consume eyes and we shall be pun-
ished with seven times seventy plagues and wild beasts
will come and slay our children and our land shall be
desolate."
She had never before seen him in a mood quite like
this and she was a little afraid of him.
"Ahijah," he said, "will hear of all my judgments
today. He will be very angry. He will go away to the
holy mount and then come back and curse me, and my
hair will fall out and my staff will rot off and I will be
a splendid king. If I continue to listen to you I'll end
up an old woman."
"Women might be more merciful judges, my Lord."
"Which one?-Naamah ?-Etillit?"
"Well, no, I stand rebuked. There are no more vin-
dictive creatures than women."
"But oh, she is beautiful when her hand rests in
mine!" He reached over to clasp a hand and Khate re-
turned the pressure.
"My Lord, in your court today I was thinking that
279
great men will never have more than a few people to
understand and applaud them but the impostors will
always have their multitude."
"Yes," he said and sighed. "Always?"
"I imagine. The priests turned the pigs back into the
parlor after King Akhnaton died. I don't have much
hope for the future."
"I had when I first became king. I have none now."
Then he fetched up one of those profound remarks
that always startled her. "The wise ruler can feel no
mercy because the people will always look upon mercy
as weakness. It is not the mothers that men revere but
the male lion, the tiger, the bull."
He arose, saying that he was weary and would seek
his bed, but at the door he turned and looked back. Khate
hastened over to him. What did he want with her? What
was the grief in him that he would not confess? He as-
tonished her by taking both her hands and pressing
them to his lips.
"Goodnight," he said.
"Goodnight, my Lord. May God bless you."
"What does the word god mean when you say it that
way?"
"A light in the dark places-and mercy for the Mir-
iams whose only sin is love for their husbands."
He pressed her hands and went out into the night and
Khate stared after him until he was lost in shadows.
Then she laid her arms across the door jamb and bowed
her head to her arms. She could hear the beating of her
heart. Behind her closed lids she could feel the pressure
of tears.

25
BECAUSE THE MELONS in Israel were of poor quality
Solomon imported this delicacy from Egypt, where mel-
ons were filled with heavenly sweetness. From Egypt
he also brought fatted ducks, geese and various wildfowl;
luscious concoctions of dates, grapes and honey; and sev-
eral excellent wines. His favorite fruit was the first ripe
figs, and his favorite maxim said that every man should
own a figtree. He was fond of pomegranate juice. But
above all foods he preferred meats, and for his great
banquet he planned to have fatted lambs and bullocks,
gazelle, stag, grouse, duck, goose, and several kinds of
fish. Of fruits there would be figs, dates, cherries, apri-
cots and peaches. Of vegetables many kinds, including
garlic, cucumber, endive, onion, eggplant and pumpkin.
Of wine and beer and strong drink he would have every
kind known in all the lands from Egypt to Persia.
The Canaanites had several annual feastdays, chiefly
in the spring and the times of harvest; and these, many
of the Hebrews, and particularly those who lived where
the earth was more fertile, had adopted. Solomon chose
for his banquet the time of the ingathering in the au-
tumn, when the rejoicing would be great after the last
harvest. Ahijah knew that this was an alien feastday.
But Solomon thought it would be the most appropriate
time, for then, just before the rains, the earth, like a
mother delivered of her child, lay back and was weary,
with her womb emptied and the sadness of approaching
winter upon her.
Solomon hoped that Israel would have a celebration
as magnificent as any in the kingdoms around him. He
281
imported the choicest incense and perfumes, and at
great cost. He gathered the ablest musicians and cooks.
He would have many servants, all of them emanating
sweet odors. He hoped to have several hundred guests.
Though he did not have his father's sense of the dra-
matic, he did perceive that delays whetted appetites and
were like soft winds on the fevers of expectancy.
When will it be? persons asked one another. Would
it be before Astarte again descended to the underworld
and sexual ardor waned?-or not until the coming of
the quail? Would it be when the fig or the almond was
in blossom, when the barley or the grapes were ripe?
When it became known that the feast would fall in
the week of the next ingathering, most of the King's
women became feverishly busy. Havveh was resolved to
be the handsomest woman present and two days before
the banquet she summoned her maid and began her toil-
ette.
Sitting on a stool she looked into a mirror of polished
metal, so placed that the light from a ceiling window
fell on it; and the maid combed her masses of lustrous
hair, using her fingers as a comb. Before the hair was
finally arranged it was gently disheveled and fluffed
open, and into its glowing depths the maid sprinkled
various sweet odors, such as cedar and aloes, spices, lo-
tus and musk. Then the hair was curled in the fashion
of the Assyrians, with bronze curlers heated in a censer;
and presently ringlets lay on her forehead and fell in
soft foliage over her ears and were a richness of fra-
grant curls over her neck and down her shoulders. No
other wife in the court had such glorious hair. Now
the maid drenched all the curls with more perfumes;
and Havveh said, speaking in that resentful tone which
had become habitual with her:
282
"I've heard Queen Khate has the royal perfume of
Persia."
"My Lady, I don't know."
"Of course you don't know." Havveh did not expect
replies. She was talking to herself. "Why should that
homely heathen have scents more costly than mine? Of
what use can they be on her? Think of it! The royal
perfume of Persia has twenty-five odors all blended! It
is sweeter than rose or jasmine. Think of that! Why does
the King never summon me any more? Did I bite him
too hard? But should I say such things to my maid?"
In a toneless voice that evinced no interest at all—
for she had heard these angry outbursts before-the
maid said:
"No, my Lady."
"Do you presume to tell me what to say?"
"No, my Lady."
"You'd watch your tongue if you were waiting on
Queen Etillit."
"Yes, my Lady."
"The King hasn't come to me for years! Think of
that! Ah, maybe I bit him harder than I knew! Many
times I bit him. Why did I?"
"I don't know, my Lady."
"He praised my mound. He kissed my breasts. He
murmured and said that I was his vineyard. But he calls
me no more. Why?"
"I don't know, my Lady."
"I know. It is because I have borne him only daugh-
ters. But they say his right stone is weak. Think of
that!"
"Yes, my Lady."
The maid was old and tired and weary with living.
She had no interest in what Havveh was saying but she
felt it to be her duty to make responses now and then
283
to the mournful monologue.
"What kind of king is it with a weak right stone?
Queen Ezida has only one son. Queen Zeruah's children
by the King are all daughters but her first child was a
son. Still, the King calls me no more. Why?"
"He is busy, my Lady."
"You fool, you would say that! Do you mean he is
busy with virgins? . . . No, I don't want that stuff in
my ears! Is it saffron? I want musk in my ears." And
with that incredible naivete that was as deep in her as
the dark ambivalence of her passions Havveh took a
long breath and said: "I always wanted the King to kiss
in my ears."
"My Lady-"
"I wanted to bite his-No, you clumsy cow, don't
put musk there!"
"The last time, my Lady—"
"No, not that either. You know I always rub that on
my belly."
Around Havveh's neck was the locket, suspended
from a thin golden chain, in which she carried her soul;
and she now drenched the locket with the burning fumes
of oil of champak and mandrake root. In an electrum
bowl she had powdered bull-horn and the dried marrow
from the thighbone of a ram, the two mixed in a paste
with mandrake wine; and while she nibbled at this the
maid finished with her hair. This mixture, which Hav-
veh was eating, was believed to be a love-charm, though
whether it would make her ardent or the one on whom
she fixed her longing she did not know, being quite ig-
norant of magical properties.
"Even if I were ardent," she said, "whom would I
lie with?"
"My Lady, I don't know."
Using a circular palette with a rounded cavity in the
284
middle of the top, surrounded by a broad flat rim, the
maid was preparing a mineral substance. She mixed a
sweet essence with powdered kohl to paint eyebrows and
lashes a heavy black, with powdered malachite or tur-
quoise to paint the lower eyelids green, and with pow-
dered haematite clay or red ochre to deepen the hue of
the lips.
"I'm beautiful," Havveh said, looking at her reflec-
tion in the mirror.
She was beautiful. She had grown very stout but Hav-
veh saw beauty only in a woman's face and hair, paying
no attention to her form; for Havveh the core of her
being was merely a sensation and not a wonder framed
with beauty. She was content to grow fat and shapeless
as long as her face was smooth, her hair glossy, her eyes
clear and bright. Her teeth were still untouched by de-
cay, whereas most of the King's women, she reflected
with pleasure, had decayed and aching teeth; and some,
like Naamah, had a breath that smelled worse than dung.
"Queen Naamah thinks her son will be the next king,"
said Havveh, gazing at her image and tracing a brow
with her forefinger.
"My Lady, I don't know."
"If I had a son!" She traced the other brow and add-
ed: "If I were a king and had a feeble right stone I
would abdicate."
The next day her hair had to be done over, and again
the next morning, the day of the feast. Then her brows
and the margins of her lids were dyed with pigment; and
over her face was smeared a fine mineral powder, mixed
with almond oil, weak vinegar, and odorous gums. When
she closed her eyelid the maid took a probe, dipped it
into a pigment, and drew it gently between the lids,
making black lines which shadowed the eyes and added
to their luster and depth. Her brows were arched and
285
then colored with a gumlike paste that preserved their
form and made them look faintly blue. Her lips were
dyed a blood red, and a little dye was put on the mounds
of her cheeks. She wore earrings of carved horn set
with dark blue jewels; and after studying herself in the
mirror she decided to wear a nosering, a common and
favored ornament. Women who could afford nothing bet-
ter used rings of wood or braided hair; Havveh had a clus-
ter of golden rings which Solomon had given her during
his time of wooing. When she turned her head sharply
these made a tinkling musical sound not unlike that of the
sistrum. She then put on a multiple necklace set with
pearls and arranged it so that the lower end hung mid-
way between her large breasts. Some women wore neck-
laces that hung down across their belly, reaching to their
mound. On her arms she put all the bracelets she had;
these were of ivory, of silver, of gold. She let her right
arm hang and observed that the lower bracelets fell over
her wrists and against her knuckles. Since she would
eat by dipping her right hand into dishes and feeding
with her fingers, she thought it best to put all the brace-
lets on her left arm. Should she wear jingling chains
and bells on her ankles?—or beaded sandals on her feet?
"Put my garments on," she said, for she had been sit-
ting naked, with the folds of her belly spreading over
her mound.
Her underdress was a kind of semitransparent chem-
ise, embroidered across her breasts and hemmed at the
bottom with a wide red band. Over this she put a girdle
to draw in the fat of her belly, and over the girdle an
outer garment which was very full in its folds and drap-
eries, and so had to be confined. No article of clothing
was more highly regarded than the girdle which was
worn on the outside, and on none was employed greater
skill. Havveh's outer girdle was more than twenty feet
.
286
long and about eighteen inches wide; it was of fine linen,
and was dyed a dark red to match the hem of her robe.
The maid wrapped it round and round her mistress and
draped the two ends in a loose knot which was fixed with
a golden clasp just below the bosom. Havveh had no
handbag or purse but used her girdle as one, stuffing into
its ample folds various small articles, including probes,
sachets, and tiny alabaster jars of scent.
When at last she was robed and ready she shook her
head to make the rings in her nose tinkle. Looking at
the maid with disconcerting directness she asked impa-
tiently: "Am I all right?"
"Yes, my Lady."
"You fool, am I beautiful?"
"My Lady is very beautiful."
"But by the gods I'll never be the Queen Mother!
Think of that!" She looked down across her great bos-
om, down to her bare feet, whose nails were painted red.
She kept shaking her head to make the metal in her nose
yield up its music. Stooping, she looked into the mir-
ror and decided in her abrupt way that she needed some-
thing red in her hair, having learned long ago that red
was her color.
"What'll I put in my hair?" Before the weary maid
could answer Havveh cried: "I'll wear a phallus!"
She meant an image of the male organ, another fa-
vorite ornament. It was painted red; that the phallus
could fill with blood was a miracle for people of this
time. These phallic emblems were made of wood, clay,
bone, or of various roots which resembled the organ in
shape. Havveh searched in her chamber until she found
one made of bone. Like most of these images it was ex-
aggerated in size-this one, carved of ramshorn, was
twelve inches long. Havveh thrust it into the depths of
287
her hair, leaving the head exposed. Glancing again in
the mirror she cried:
"My serpents, where are they?"
The serpent was also a phallic symbol. Every royal
woman but Khate had many serpent-images, a few of
gold or silver, many of ivory or wood or base metal.
Now and then a woman-Etillit was one-preferred a
live serpent to the image, and wore one in her hair or
carried it in her bosom.
Havveh chose a slender golden serpent and thrust
this also into her hair, leaving the head exposed and
poised above her forehead. She looked in the mirror and
was pleased.
"Am I all right?"
In the same toneless weary voice the maid said: "My
Lady is very beautiful."
"Why doesn't the King call me?”
"My Lady-"
"Does he still lie with Queen Naamah, that ugly
bitch ?"
"My Lady, I don't know."
Naamah had never been beautiful and was now thick
with middle age and coarsened by the vulgarity and vi-
olence of her passions. She had never tried to be lovely;
if her son became king of Israel she would be satisfied.
She would be present at the King's feast but not to show
off noserings and girdles; she would be there as a fumb-
ling strategist to explore and test the enemy's position.
Zeruah would be there but not to show her hand.
She also was a beautiful woman but with beauty unlike
Havveh's. Havveh's beauty was opaque, or in any case
its depths were not apparent. Zeruah's lay largely in the
subtle and elusive quality of her character. She was also
in middle age, with some gray in her hair, with three
very thin wrinkles on either side of her mouth when
288
she smiled, and with a few bad teeth. The signs of
age were on her throat. But she had not thickened with the
passing of time; there was on her none of that gross-

buttocks of those who were wanton in their self-indul-
gence. She was as trim and firm as she had been that
day by the well when the King first saw her. The girl in
her had matured and ripened; a woman wise in the ways
of the world looked out of the luminous depths of her
eyes. When she was around persons who suspected her
of scheming she affected apathy, an obtuse dulness of
spirit, which had deceived nearly everyone but Khate.
Zeruah now believed that the only obstacle in her
path was the mother of Rehoboam. She knew of course
that Jeroboam, her own son, had no right at all to the
throne. But hidden deep in her mind was the thought
that he had the only right; of all the young men in Is-
rael he alone could be called the son of Yah. That in
any case was the way Zeruah had come to look at the
matter and she was convinced that she could persuade
Ahijah to look at it that way.
The day before the feast she was sitting in Khate's
parlor.
"Will Ahijah be there?"
"Ahijah? I don't suppose he will."
"Why not?"
"Oh, he would never go."
99
"You could persuade him to."
"I? Why should I?"
"Why shouldn't you? Have you persuaded him?"
Suddenly Zeruah realized that the meaning of what
Khate was saying was not what it seemed to be. With a

"Have you seen Ahijah lately?"
289
"No."
"Zeruah, why do you lie when lying will only discredit

Zeruah pretended not to understand the question but

"Jeroboam?"
"I mean your son Jeroboam-the son of-was his

Khate leaned forward and looked into Zeruah's

"Jeroboam's father was named Nebat, wasn't he?"

"Well, well!" cried Khate, and laughed. Her laughter

"If there were no children there would be no people."
"If there were no Zeruahs there would be no need of

"I don't know what you mean.'

Zeruah's marvelous defenses crumbled. Her face

blanched. She gasped and arose to go but Khate spoke

"Sit down! Must you act like a child? Is it news to

Zeruah sank to the couch and recovered a part of her

"Have you read my heart?"
"Oh, in the name of God! Do you want me to lay

"Do you want to see Jeroboam put in chains?"
Zeruah sank back and clasped her hands. She was

"No, oh no! Jeroboam is not even the King's son."
"David was not a king's son."
"David? But there was no king then, was there?"
"Yes."
"Then how did David-"
"He seized the throne."
"Did he slay the king?"
"Yes."
"How strangely things are done in Israel! No wonder

But to come back to the point, what right has your son

Dared she say it? Zeruah considered and decided that

may be many who think they have the right."
291
"Then the whole matter will be reduced to a brawl

"Then what in the world do you mean? Since you talk

"The blindness of mothers! But he has no right to

"Please don't call it that."
"What else is it? . . . So Ahijah himself doesn't

"No. Khate," said Zeruah, laying her cards on the

"The King is afraid of the Pharaoh. If you were to

"No. Zeruah, listen, I've always been fond of you. But
I'm also fond of the King and I approve what he is try-

frankly, I have little respect for the position which you
and Ahijah and all the people like you take. There may

very fine, I think-and some of the laws which your

respected among the nations as long as it tries to live

forever in bondage to the tribal laws of desert patri-

"And so you understand-or do you?-that my posi-

Zeruah's face had turned several shades paler. She now
293
understood that she had said a great deal too much, that
she ought to have stuck to her lie, even though Khate
knew she was lying. She arose, and could not hide the
shaking in her limbs.
"I shall go," she said.
"Very well, but let's understand one another before
you go.
"Will you report me to the King?"
"And if I do?"
"He will have me slain but what will that settle? There
will be war in Israel if he does that."
Khate had risen also and was looking at her. "You

"Yes."
"Well-"
"Report me," said Zeruah, "if you feel that is what
you should do."
"Do you think I'm your enemy now?"
"Aren't you? How can it be otherwise?"

the civil war going? But I will let you know. I promise

"Thanks," said Zeruah, feeling sick and frightened.
She went to the door.

guises for you will be closely watched."

ON THE DAY OF THE GREAT BANQUET Bathsheba was too
ill to leave her bed and Solomon went in to see her about
noon. All morning, in anticipation, he had been drinking
a heavy fragrant liquor and he was feeling in high spirits.
"Mother, how are you?"
"Oh, old and feeble." But her eyes were proud when
she looked at her son. As the son of peasants he had
done, she thought, very well; he did not have such splen-
dors as the great kings had but he had elevated Israel
to a place among the nations. "This is your great day,"
she said.
"This is my great day. I wish you could be there to sit
with me."
"I wish I could. I'll be thinking about you and all your
guests."
He bent over to kiss her forehead and to pat her wast-
ed checks and Bathsheba took his hand and pressed her
lips to it.
"You can tell me all about it tomorrow," she said.
When he entered the banquet-hall the more eager
guests, wearing their finest raiment and jewels, were al-
ready arriving, and in the receiving-chamber Solomon
greeted each one with a kiss. There their feet were
washed and their heads sprinkled with perfumed oils.
In the banquet-hall, heavy with the smoke from burning
censers, the guests were assigned to handsome couches;
and presently there was the babble of voices under the
inharmonious but jubilant music of three orchestras―
the piping of flutes, the jingling of sistrums, and the more
violent sounds of drum, cymbal and bell. Servants came
295
with golden plates heaped with delicacies-pickles and
relishes and sweetmeats, but, above all, various aphro-
disiacs which were believed to promote ardent desires.
These included caper-berries, eringoe-root bathed with oil
of champak, the dried pollen of flowers, aquae amatrices,
small white ants soaked in honey and garlic, the brains
of small birds taken during their mating season, the
hearts of swallows and the testicles of rams. While the
early guests were devouring these things and looking in-
ward to their emotions to measure the effect, servants set
handsome wine-cups before them, filling them from the
spiced wine-jars that stood on tables up and down the
hall. It was a little chilly, for this was midautumn, and
the only heat came from braziers. The only light in the
hall came from wicks which fed from open bowls of
oil. Outside it was a day of overcast and a thin mist of
rain. But the hall was strewn with flowers, including
those which had been thrown on the floor to be trampled.
It was toward evening and some of the guests were
loud with wine and song when Khate arrived. In the
ante-chamber she submitted to the washing of her feet
and the laying aside of her sandals, because Solomon had
decreed that guests should enter the banquet-hall with
bare feet. Looking in, Solomon saw her and advanced to
give her the kiss of welcome.
1?"
"Mistress of my joy, how are you?
"I never felt better, my Lord, but I hate to expose my
feet. They're homelier than my face."
"Beautiful feet," he said gallantly and turned to re-
ceive the next guest.
On entering the main chamber Khate was deafened by
the multitude of sounds; and she could see only dimly,
because the hall was now clouded with the smoke from
censers and lamps and braziers. The ruler of the feast,
smooth and hierophantic, was bowing low before her. It
296
was this man's duty to see that all guests obeyed the
rules, though Solomon's rules were little more than one
commandment, that everyone should eat, drink and be
merry, and give praise to the gods.
"My Lady, your couch is up front."
"No, please. I want one back by the wall."
"His Majesty, the King—”
"No." She reached into her girdle and then thrust a
small bribe into the man's palm. With his eyes lighting
up like fires in his dark oily face he hastened away.
Khate sat back in a far corner where she could be in-
conspicuous, yet could watch the revelry. She looked
round at her neighbors but recognized none of them; the
people in the rear were the most insignificant of all the
guests. On her right sat a cold and haughty woman, with
a star sapphire, opals, moonstones and chrysoberyls shin-
ing in her gray hair. Because all these were regarded as
lucky stones, Khate supposed that some secret terror
lurked behind this woman's frost. To ward off evil she
also had charms hanging from her ears, girdle and fingers.
She was so loaded down with ornamental talismen and
was so fat and shapeless that she looked as though she
might need help to move. On the couch with her was
a short stout man with a vacant face, abnormally full
lips, coarse hair sprouting from his nostrils, and a long
pink dagger scar across his forehead. He was, Khate
imagined, some petty prince from a distant city.
Khate heard a commotion over toward the entrance-
hall and on looking round was amazed to see Ahijah,
and with him, Zeruah. Ahijah had a tuniclike garment
over his goathair girdle, and he looked as if his tangled
mop of hair had been brushed and anointed. Khate arose
and hastened over.
"Well, Ahijah, I didn't know you were coming! Will
you sit with me?"
297
"He's going to sit with me," said Zeruah.
For a moment Khate looked hard into Zeruah's eyes
and then said gaily: "Oh no, he's going to sit with me!"
And she took his arm and he allowed himself to be led
to her couch. After they had sat she asked him:
"Did you see the King as you came in?"
"No. I don't want to see him."
"Then shut your eyes."
It must have been communicated to Solomon that
Khate had gone by the wall, for she had barely settled
herself with Ahijah when the King came searching down
the aisles, a winecup in one hand, spilling golden drops
as he walked, and a plate of tidbits in the other. Though
his face was a bit inflamed and pride was bright in his

Khate and looked down at her and said:
"What are you doing back here?"
"My Lord, I saw you pause back there and kiss a
woman. Is the light so poor that you mistook her for
me?"
Solomon liked a witty jest. He beamed upon her, hav-
ing forgotten that he had come to rebuke her; and then,
dropping heavily to the couch, he put an arm round her
and like a tired lad pressed his cheek to her shoulder.
Only then, looking across her, did he become aware of
Ahijah.
"Who is this fellow?" he asked coldly.
"Don't you recognize him?"
"I've never seen him before."
"Why, my Lord, this is the prophet."
"Whose prophet?"
"This is Ahijah," said Khate, not knowing whether
he was confused or teasing.
"Who let him in?"
"I did."
298
He cuddled against her, as if warmed and silenced

liness. "You do about as you please," he murmured.
"Some day I shall put you in chains." Then out of the
unhappiness in his subconscious mind came the simple
statement: "My mother won't be here tonight. She is
dying."
"My Lord, I'm so sorry!"
"Always polite," murmured Solomon. "Always gra-
cious-always the lady." His arm round her waist gent-

lowered her head to touch his hair, and with a gesture
of pure mischief she put a forefinger to the point of his
nose.
Solomon sat up and looked over at Ahijah, and the
eyes of King and Prophet met. Then Solomon arose, still
looking at him, and said:
"By the living God who is love, you're being neglected.
I see no wine."
"The righteous don't drink wine," said Ahijah stiffly.
"No? You don't drink the blood of the grape?" Then,
acidly, Solomon added: "There was no wine in the desert,
was there? Do you want some goatmilk?"
Solomon turned away, and Khate said to Ahijah, try-
ing to heal the unpleasantness: "The food is excellent."
"Whose food?"
"Oh well, Ahijah, if you won't be reasonable! I think
we should all be proud of the King. See how he has trans-
formed his city."
"I'm thinking of all my people who have died."
"People die all the time, don't they? Tell me, who
brushed your hair? Was it Zeruah?"
Having been born to the purple, having spent her life
amidst luxuries, Khate could hardly understand the tor-
299
ments and furies in Ahijah's breast-for he was a son
of the soil and his friends were the peasants. Having a
skeptical and searching mind she could not understand
his utter devotion to ancient tribal laws.
"This is the Star of Horus," she said, indicating a
wine. "May I serve you?"
"No," he said; and with flashing eyes he looked at
the delicacies on the table before him.
"Ahijah, if you had a son-" She was watching him
closely. She paused there and waited a few moments but
his face told her nothing. "If you had a son," she said,
"would you rather he'd be a king, or a prophet like you?"
"If I had a son, Yah would call him to his work."
My fine fellow, she thought, you're pretty sharp! She
tried again: "If you had a son, would Yah call him to
be a prophet?"
"Yah might call him to be a goatherd."
"Oh? I suppose. But what would you prefer?-if you
had a son."
"That he would do the work that Yah called him to."
"But you'd rather see him on a throne than in a sheep-
cote, wouldn't you?"
"Yah, being all-wise, knows all things."
"Have you never wanted a son?"
"I've done the work Yah called me to do."
"Haven't you ever wanted a wife?"
"The holiness of Yah is my wife."
Yes, indeed, he was a sharp one.
The banquet was now in its second course. Servants
were, bringing light appetizing dishes, such as partridge
livers, salted fish, spiced eggs, pickled olives; and they
were also serving coriander and cardamon seeds to aid
digestion, oil of marjoram for those suffering from tooth-
ache, and bowls of fruit juices to those who felt costive.
Solomon had adopted the Egyptian custom of placing
300
scented unguents in the hair of his guests, and servants
now came with fresh cones for those whose sweet oils
had melted. Khate had a cone in her wig; Ahijah had re-
fused one. Used to the clean air of the outside world
he was fairly sickened by an atmosphere saturated with
so many kinds of incense, all mixed with the odors of
crushed flowers and the perfumes that had been sprinkled
everywhere. The babble and shouting and shrieking had
become a great din far down the hall. Ahijah sat back
in silence, watching Khate nibble at an olive or casting
his hot and reproachful gaze over the multitude.
"This reminds me of Egypt," she said, and was instant-
ly sorry that she had spoken.
"Sodom!" said Ahijah.
Queen Naamah had come to the feast with her heart
set on a daring experiment; and while sitting on a couch
up front she drank deeply of a sweet amber wine from
Syena, nursed her aching teeth, and kept an eye on Prince
Rehoboam, who reclined on a couch close by hers, with
a princess at his side. He was privy to his mother's plan
and was waiting for his cue. Having had no tutor but
his cold and evil mother, he had no emotions that ever
softened to the melting-point, nor thought of anyone's
pleasure but his own. A more supercilious and vacant
prince had never looked contemptuously at a beak-nosed
and gap-toothed princess. To her giddy words he now
and then lent a haughty ear but most of the time he was
wholly absorbed by his own sly thoughts.
Among those present was a sacrificial victim with whom
Naamah hoped to win the favor of her god. It was a
custom with some people to immolate a male human
being annually, usually at the spring equinox; and because
the immolation was looked upon as a great honor, even
as an apotheosis, many men, with little prospect of posi-
tion and power, coveted the honor. Naamah had chosen
301
a man who was fat and round and jolly. He would be
royally treated until the hour of his death. Sometimes,
in other lands, this fantastic scapegoat was represented
as a king; a crown was placed on his head and the robe
of royalty over his shoulders. Naamah's fat little man
was having the time of his life, and well he might, for
he had only a few more months to live, when he would
be hurled into the fiery furnace of the god's belly or
hanged on a tree. As though death walked with him, in-
viting his attention to pleasure, he drank from one wine-
jar after another; he danced wildly; he made love to
every fille de joie who would give him a few moments,
his ardor emanating in heat-waves, his breath gushing
out of him, saturated with alcoholic fumes, his eyes roll-
ing in ludicrous passion.
When the fat man came his way Rehoboam watched
him. His mother had told him that her god would look
with supreme favor on Rehoboam after this plump little
'king' had been devoted. Rehoboam had no doubt of
that. He had the kind of mind that accepted as self-
evident and inevitable anything that would promote his
fortunes and add flavor to his sensual pleasures. He was
staring at the fat man and wondering if he would ex-
plode when thrown into the furnace when Princess Lil-
anna cried in his ear: "Your mother is beckoning!" Re-
hoboam looked at Naamah. She gave a signal and he
arose and went forward to stand by her.
The servants had some little while back brought the
main course-hot and odorous mounds of fatted calves
and lambs and gazelles; partridges and geese and ducks;
and countless bowls of eggs, fruits, vegetables, all of
them prepared by the best cooks in the kingdom. Some
of the guests had left their couches and were staggering
a little. They intercepted the waiters and thrust hands
deep into the meats and juices, into the steaming onions
302
and eggplants, into preserves and jellies, into anything
in fact that came their way; and while some cried out
with pain, their hardier neighbors fed gluttonously, slob-
bering, spilling food on the floor and over their garments,
choking and sneezing and spitting and snorting; or,
smeared with greases, their right hand dripping, they
seized dancing-girls and tried to kiss them. Some would
have thrown them down and covered them but for the
ruler of the feast and his alert assistants.
Naamah chose such a moment. In the far end of the
hall was a throne-chair; Solomon used it sometimes when
receiving his ministers. It was a handsome chair, high-
backed, ornately carved, and mounted on a low dais.
Toward it Naamah now took her way, with Rehoboam at
her side; and when they came to it he mounted and sat,
looking out over the hall and feeling every inch a king.
Naamah stood by him. There was such pandemonium in
the chamber that for a few minutes her daring gesture
passed unobserved.
It was a servant who first saw Rehoboam sitting on the
throne. He whispered to a steward who turned and looked
at Rehoboam, pop-eyed with amazement; and then bade
others look, until one by one or by small groups, all of
those in the lower hall became aware of the usurper
and were staring at him. A few looked upon it as a prank;
the wiser ones saw in it an astounding insolence.
Naamah tried to speak to the multitude but her voice
was not heard, not even by Naamah herself. She wanted
to say that Prince Rehoboam had been chosen as the
King's heir, and was calling attention, with this sym-
bolic act, to his hereditary rights. He was warning all
pretenders to abandon their hopes. These things Naamah
did say, crying shrilly into the din like a woman out of
her mind, but nobody could hear a word she said. Then
there was a deep and fearful silence; the orchestras
303
ceased playing, the clowns and dancing-girls stood still;
and most of the eyes in the hall looked at the prince
and his mother. The enormity of this woman's daring
had silenced even the drunkards. Naamah could have
caused no greater consternation if she had hurled thun-
derbolts into the hall or transformed herself into a
dragon.
When the matter was brought to Solomon's attention
he went part way down the hall and then stared and
rubbed at his eyes and stared; for there Rehoboam sat,
proud and erect, needing only the royal robes and a dia-
dem to complete the impression that he was Israel's king.
Khate and Ahijah were standing on their couch and Khate
was gasping at the audacity. Then Naamah spoke to her
son and he descended and together they came up the
hall. She paused and faced the King, looking at him with
her evil eyes, giving him a triumphant smile with her
toothless mouth; and at this moment the wild emotions
broke their bounds.
Armed guards, who all evening had been standing by
the wall, rushed forward and seized Rehoboam, and
might have slain him then and there if Solomon had not
intervened. There was furious shouting and the overturn-
ing of tables and a dreadful loosening of emotions that
approached panic and riot. With presence of mind Sol-
omon took his son's arm and led him over to Zikla and told
him to guard him. Khate was shouting for attention, but
for all her screaming and gesturing was unable to get it
until, collaring a musician and seizing his instrument,
she rang a bell. The people fell silent and looked at her,
and in sharp clear tones that carried to every ear in the
hall she cried:
"You have misunderstood! The King has arranged for
many acts to entertain us, of which this was one; for
being a great and noble lord whose wisdom is godlike
304
it gives him pleasure to see us rejoice! He wished to show
us in a little tableau that kings ascend to thrones and
kings descend, for they die! For his great sense of wis-
dom and humor let us give thanks! Now we return to
our feasting!"
A silence followed while the people assimilated the
meaning of her words. Then applause shook the hall, and
some of the more exalted ladies, drunk and adoring,
rushed to the King and bussed him on his cheeks. Khate
sank to her couch. She was aware, first, of the awful fury
in Ahijah; and while trying to divine its cause she be-
came conscious next of a tremendous commotion around
the King. She sent a servant to investigate.
Then Ahijah was shouting, drunk not with wine but
with prophetic passion. Still standing he cried to the

"This is another Sodom, you base and wicked and

He was allowed to say no more. Guards standing by

"Where is that lunatic?"
"The guards took him out."
Zikla came running over. "Go find Ahijah," Solomon

Then Solomon felt sick from spiced wines and gluttony

"I don't know but I have an idea. I'll tell you tomor-

IF NAAMAH HAD had the subtle intelligence of a Livia or
of an Agrippina the Elder, both of whom followed her
by a millennium and conspired with equal zeal, she would
have been a dangerous woman, for she was utterly un-
scrupulous and ruthless. But she had a mediocre and un-
imaginative mind. Among all the ministers and other
officials in the court there was none who did not detest
her. She had not perceived the need to ingratiate her-
self and her son into the good will and affection of the
common people. She had never striven to win with flat-
tery what in her obtuse and obstinate way she imagined
might be won with arrogance or force.
She was, after all, a woman of her time and a daughter
of the fierce and unenlightened Ammonites. She believed
implicitly that the gods managed all the affairs of men.
That is what her priests told her, among whom there was
none more civilized than his mistress. Her priests, of
whom she had a half-dozen, took precedence of princes,
307
and for some time had been strutting around Jerusalem
as though they themselves expected to be anointed and
crowned. They were treacherous, mean and sneaking fel-
lows who always told Naamah what they thought she
wanted to hear. Her chief priest, whose name was Ibzan,
had told her, for instance, that Yah was only a local
and powerless baal, and that if she wanted to get her
way in things she should address all her supplications to
Melech.
And this she did.
"O Melech, who flames forth,
the exalted judge of sky and earth,
great warrior, hero, and all-powerful and all-wise god,
grant my son the throne and a long life
and splendors and glories
and triumph over his enemies
and make me the Queen Mother, powerful like
the lionness!"
She would go out at dawn and face the east and pray:
"O Melech, who flames forth,
you have opened the skies,
you have opened the portals of morning!
Hear me, your faithful daughter, and answer
my prayers!
You have raised your head above the earth,
You have covered the world with your light,
spreading your golden heat upon me and my son
that we may have long life and splendors!
Hear me, O Melech!"
In her chamber she prayed several times daily:
"O Melech, greatest of all the gods
in whose right hand is all power,
who loosens the knot of the woman in childbirth
308
and pours your heat into her womb:
Melech, great god, I beseech you,
make my son to reign over Israel forty years
and make his reign begin at once
and destroy King Solomon!..!
When would the King die? she had asked her chief
priest, and distracted by her impatience he had sought the
answer. He had examined a slain lamb for omens: on the
left side of the nape to learn if there was a slit; on the
left side of the bladder for peculiar markings; and the
viscera, particularly the liver. He always gave the same
answer, that Rehoboam would reign forty years and that
his reign would begin soon.
"But when? When?"
Ibzan suggested that she should make offerings to Me-
lech; and he prepared before an altar three tables in a
row, the middle one for the god, the left one for his
consort, the right one for his messenger. Four rams
without blemish were then found, two for the god, and
one each for the messenger and the wife. There were
three censers, over which were strewn cypress and cedar
roots; sesame wine was poured over the tables and altars;
and the priest thereupon prostrated himself, clasping his
staff of cedar, and beseeching the god to answer him.
When would Solomon die? Soon, the oracle said. Would
Rehoboam be wealthy? Very wealthy, said the oracle; all
the kings of the earth would look upon him with envy.
From great distances kings would come to gaze on his
treasure, to learn wisdom from his lips, to win an alliance
with him for their own safety.
All this had taken place just before she made her bold
gesture at the feast. Thereafter some of the persons in
the court began to fawn on Rehoboam, to offer gifts
to him, to address him as the king. And Naamah now re-
309
garded herself as the Queen Mother and began to act

He had just returned from Megiddo, delighted with

In Egypt there was a coveted strain of stallion which
the Syrian princes had been buying for steeds, with Sol-

to keep the very finest studs, hoping eventually to have
the best horses in the world.
He had eaten a great meal and was sitting alone, a

"You drunken or sleepy wretch, why did you let this

"My Lord, she is Queen Naamah!"

"Never, my Lord."
Solomon turned to Naamah and asked angrily: "Now

She might have said, "I came to inquire about your

with women like Khate, and even peasant women like

"I have come to say some things."

AHIAH SENT to Bathsheba's room the most famous physi-
cian in the city. Every king had men skilled in the arts of
magic-the interpreters, sorcerers, wizards, exorcists,
diviners, astrologers-whose duty was to ward off evil,
cultivate the benign spirits and foretell the course of
events. His association with Khate had led Solomon to a
healthy skepticism for such persons, but about noon
Ahiah came into his bedchamber with Labaya, a Hurrian.
Bathsheba had heard that her son was sick and had sent
the physician over.
Labaya presented an astonishing appearance. Sly and
318
furtive with small eyes like black beads, separated only
by the thin wedge of his nose; wrinkled, stooped and
gray, with a low-dished forehead, with cheeks horribly
sunken, for he had lost his teeth; with wide flaring nos-
trils filled with coarse hair, he came in like a fox, soft
and soundless. As striking as the man himself was his as-
sortment of charms and amulets. He was weighted down
with them. They hung from his hair and ears and girdle;
they were fantastic bracelets on his arms and legs; and
besides all these he carried the mummified head of a
man who had lived long ago. Labaya wore only two
colors, red and white, the colors of health and righteous-
ness.
He had become famous by curing a leper. He had
taken two birds, and one he had killed over running
water; the other bird, as well as a piece of cedarwood
shaped as a portal of birth, he had drenched with the
blood of the slain dove. From these sacred things he had
shaken blood upon the leper, and then sprinkled the live
bird seven times, crying out with each sprinkling that the
leper would be healed. Then he had set the bird free.
Thereupon he had told the leper to tarry near the city
for seven days, and to fast; and on the seventh day to
shave the hair off his body and head. On the eighth day
the leper offered an unblemished lamb to the gods, and
with the blood from this lamb Labaya had anointed the
tip of the man's right ear, the thumb of his right hand,
the great toe of his right foot and the point of his staff.
Next, he had taken Syrian manna, the exudation of the
tamarisk, and sprinkled it seven times for the gods in
a holy place, and rubbed it on the leper's ear, thumb, toe
and organ. He poured what was left on the man's polled
head and breathed over him seven incantations. And the
leper was cured.
Such was the story told in the city.

Solomon was staring at the fellow and Labaya was

Labaya had been wondering what to do about that. In

325

KHATE SAID ONE DAY to Zeruah: "Tell Jeroboam to
come and see me. I want to talk to him.”
"Why?"
"To see what sort of person he is. Don't you want
me to?"
"I'd be very glad to have you talk to him."
But because spies and informers were everywhere,
striving with vigilance to improve their position and pay,
it was extremely difficult for Zeruah to see her son; but
one evening she saw him passing and ran out.
"Jeroboam !"
"Mother!"
"Listen, I can be out only a moment. I'm watched.

yet so in talking to her be very careful."
"I will," he said, looking strangely at his mother.
"Jeroboam, listen, and cherish this secret-you will
be the next king of Israel."
"I?" he asked, astounded.

"Has Yah chosen me?"
"Yah will make his will known to Ahijah."
"Does Ahijah say-"
329
"No, not yet. But I'm sure he will when the time

"No," he said. "Of course not." He added: "I'm col-
lector of the tithes in one of the districts. How could
