I
"I must go now. Remember, don't let Khate lead you
into a snare."
Then Zeruah turned and fled back to the King's palace
but at the door she paused and kissed her hand to her
astonished son.
Unwilling and indeed unable to postpone a matter so
rich with hidden and mysterious meanings, Jeroboam that
evening presented himself at Khate's door. It opened,
and he stepped swiftly inside.
"I'm glad you came," she said, and waved him to a
couch. "Have you eaten?"
"Yes, my Lady."
"Will you sit?" Jeroboam sat and she looked at him,
curiously, searchingly. She saw a fairly tall young man,
well-formed, handsome, with a countenance in which
there was as yet no record of slyness or cruelty. He had
his mother's deep and lustrous eyes and her wonderfully
long thick lashes. He had the same mouth, with a rather
full lower lip. He wore a beard, short, a little curly, and
intensely black. His mass of hair fell to his shoulders
and was caught back from his forehead with a kind of
kerchief. If this was Ahijah's son, she thought, there
was nothing in his face to show it, but this opinion she
changed later when she saw his eyes burn and flash with
passion.
"You look much like your mother," she said.
And Jeroboam said politely: "I don't get to see my
mother very often."
"You saw her this evening."
330
He stiffened. "Yes, I did," he said.
"She told you I wanted to see you?"
"Yes."
"Have you been wondering why?"
"Of course."
Khate talked about irrelevant matters, all the while
sizing him up and trying to lead him out; but he was in-
telligent, subtle and shrewd. While talking to him she
thought how foolish it would be for one not born to the
purple to become a king of Israel; and then she remem-
bered that the Hebrews were new to kingship and had
no traditions of royalty. Saul, as nearly as she had been
able to learn, had been a lunatic and a barbarian, who
had preferred the ways and even the food and housing
of peasants. There was no royal family in Israel. There
should be established a ruling dynasty, else there would
always be usurpers, assassinations and strife.
Khate erred in supposing that for a long time Jero-
boam had been privy to his mother's schemes. She erred
again in imagining that he knew, and had known since
childhood, that Nebat was not his father. And she erred
above all in thinking that he spent a great deal of time
with Ahijah.
She poured a cup of wine to loosen his tongue. "How
long since you've seen Ahijah?”
"Oh-" he said, and looked up at the ceiling, trying
to remember. "Several months.'
"Where is he now?"
"I don't know. The last I heard he was in the northern
districts."
"Do you think he's a great prophet?"
"Yes, I do."
"Do you believe in God?"
"In who?" His bewilderment was not feigned.
"In God."
331
"I believe in Yah, who is the greatest of the gods.

"You mean you have a divine Father but no divine

The question caught him wholly unprepared and his

He was remembering his mother's admonition to be

Jeroboam shared the common belief in the soul's ca-

THE SAME EVENING, Zikla, the captain of the King's
guard, was closeted with Naamah, who had asked him
to come to her room. He still looked at what nobody
talking to him expected him to look at. He still dressed
as a Syrian. This evening he wore two large shawls, one
red and one blue, and so draped that the colors alter-
nated; and round his waist he had a rich ornamented
girdle of soft leather. He was bald on the forepart of his
skull but the hair above his ears shone with scented oils.
He wore earrings and bracelets and a necklace of colored
shells threaded on a slender golden chain. He had no
mustache but wore a beard which was about an inch of
thick black hair down his heavy jaws, coming to a point
on his chin. His beard also glistened.
Naamah had summoned him because she was deter
mined to have the King assassinated. When Zikla entered
her chamber he eyed her craftily, trying to divine her
purpose.
"How many men do you command ?" she asked, after
he had lowered his fat body to a small couch.
"Five thousand," he said, grossly exaggerating the
number.
"Five thousand," she said, tapping her thigh with a
forefinger. She wanted to meet his eyes but they were
staring at her feet. Her feet were bare and streaked with
blue veins. "Are they all well-armed?"
340
"Oh yes," he said, gazing at her ugly feet.
On her top jaw in front she had only one tooth, a long
exposed snag, which she had got the habit of sucking
when her mind was busy. To do this she pursed her mouth,
thrusting her lower lip up and bringing her tongue up
behind; and so, with the tooth embraced by tongue and
lip, she sucked at it, pulling her sallow cheeks in until
they seemed almost to meet. She now stared at him and
nursed her tooth.
"Benaiah is dying."
"May the gods forbid!" he cried, though the wily
rascal knew that Benaiah was dying.
"He's very old," said Naamah.
"Yet he seems so young." Zikla was now staring at his
hands, which he had softened with idleness and oils.
"When Benaiah is dead-"
"But he seems so young," said Zikla.
"When he is dead-"
"We must all die," said Zikla, who now seemed to be
counting his fingers.
"Do you realize that the Queen Mother is speaking
to you?"
"Ah!" he cried, and shifted a little. If he had had his
way the night of the feast, Rehoboam would have been
slain and his mother with him. With his right hand he
clasped the thumb and fingers of his left hand. "Your
Majesty, I'm listening."
"Who'll be the new commander of the King's host?"
"I don't know," he said, squeezing his fingers.
"You should be," said Naamah.
With gentle perverseness that infuriated her he said.
blandly: "I'm captain of the King's guard."
"You might be the next commander of the host."
Well, he reflected, she had at last come to the point,

"I may." He raised his head and she thought he was

"Would you like to be commander of the host?"
"It would be a great honor," he said. What did she

"Would you?"
"I've always been so happy in my position that I've

"No," he said, thinking of poison.
Naamah was baffled. "When my son is King-" she

"Your son will be a great king."

"If he favors me."
"Or commander of his host?"
How much longer would she beat round the bushes,
trying to put him in her power?
"You've killed many men," she said.
"As the King has commanded, I have obeyed."

"I was in Egypt," he said blandly. "Nebat fell off a

my son is King I can make it very pleasant or very miser-

"I know that," he said, looking again at his hands.
"My son will have power of death over you and my

Ah well, that threat was plain enough! "You've been

Looking at him, she felt that she could not safely press

and plans. She arose, and she arose as the Quuen Mother,

He went quietly out, backing away and bowing, and
wondering if he could have played his difficult part with
more circumspection.
As for Naamah, she was so frustrated and furious
that the heat of her emotions made her ill. Well, he
would have suggestions, or when her son was king this
man would be burned like a goose on a spit. When baf-
fled she always turned to her god and this she did the
next day.
Solomon had built for her a small godhouse in which
stood a gigantic image of Melech, erected on a great
stone foundation. The god's belly, cast of bronze, was an
open furnace, with passages leading upward so that flame
and smoke would issue from his mouth and nostrils. His
head was that of a bull, with long horns and fierce bulg-
ing eyes. His whole aspect was one of cruel and insatiable
virility.
She sent her priests after a couple of lambs, and when
they reported back she went out with them and a few
musicians and her Ammonite maids. The priests offered
to Melech first some corn and fruit, spices and unguents
343
and oils, all of which were thrown into his belly, in which
a fire was burning fiercely. Pitch fagots were hurled in to
make the fire roar. Then the priests came forth with a
lamb and cut its throat and caught the blood which they
cast on the flames. They then flayed the beast and opened
the carcass; they removed the kidneys and their fat, as
well as other choice portions, and threw these into the
flaming stomach. They would look up at the bullface to
watch the fire shoot from mouth and nostrils, and the
smoke, heavy with savor, issue from all the head's vents.
Before nightfall some of the votaries were beside
themselves with ecstasy. They were feasting too, on the
parts assigned to them, devouring the flesh raw and
anointing one another with blood. They were drinking
deep from jars of wine. Their emotions were inflamed by
the shrill music from flutes and bells and drums. When
the other lamb was brought up and the fire raged with
ferocity steadily more intense and consuming, one of the
priests seized a knife and began to lacerate himself, leap-
ing and whirling and falling, rushing up to thrust hands
into the flames, lapping up his blood and spitting it out
in a thin red mist, and shrieking like one tortured. In a
little while he was bloody from head to feet. Because
his frenzy was contagious, all the persons present, in-
cluding Naamah, began to behave as though demented.
The one who had been dancing crazily gulped so much
wine mixed with blood that he was completely out of his
senses, and shrieking he took a knife and with one wild
stroke unmanned himself and hurled his pouch and
stones into the god's stomach. The glories and furies that
possessed him were rampant in all those present and
each in his own insane way manifested to the god his rev-
erence and joy. Some stripped themselves naked and
hurled their clothes into the furnace. One demented
woman ran howling to the god and tried to climb up to
344
the belly to offer herself; and the priests, observing her
wish, leapt forward to assist her and she was shoved
headfirst into the flaming maw and almost instantly dis-
appeared. Then a frenzied woman with an infant who
had been watching at a little distance, chanting and
shrieking and jumping up and down, ran forward and
hurled her babe, her firstborn son, into the red and
roaring god. Almost nobody was now in possession of
his own will; none of them in a later time would be
able to remember what he had done. The unmanned
priest was bleeding horribly but he was still leaping and
shrieking. Naamah herself looked more like a witch-hag
from the wilderness then a queen from the palace. She
shrilly commanded the people to give more and more!
and they rushed frantically round and round, seeking
things to devote. No child, if children had been here,
would have escaped with its life. Indeed, nobody was
safe from this god's ravenous hunger; and later, after
the ardors of his stomach had cooled, after his holy and
dreadful fires had burned out, of all the bones in his
monstrous torso, none could have told those of child or
woman from those of beast. .
.
Back on a hillside, sitting in a recess under a shelf of
stone, was Ahijah. He was angered by what he saw, not
because of the immolations but because this god was a
wicked god. His people made human offerings, particu-
larly in times of crisis, and would continue to do so for
centuries to come. But Ahijah hated this Naamah woman,
the Ammonite, the alien, the blasphemer and fool. And
while watching the awful scene before him he told him-
self that her son would never be king and that she her-
self would die a hideous death.
After darkness came he still sat, watching the fires,
the one on the altar and the one in the belly; and the
people, dancing wildly round and round the god, most
345
of them now stark naked; and the shadows out in dark-
ness, which were other people, hunting fuel for the fires.
A breeze was blowing his way; Ahijah could smell the
burning spices and gums and resins, mixed with the odor
of flesh. Would Yah choose Jeroboam? Seeking an answer
to this question he had prayed long and fervently, beating
his forehead on the earth.
But Yah had not yet answered him.
Until a late hour he sat here watching the revelers.
All night they would feast and sing and dance. All night
flames would shoot forth like serpent tongues from the
nostrils and mouth of this abominable god. All night
Naamah would be there, naked and bloody and insane,
with only her mantle of gray hair to cover her—all night,
praying for the death of Solomon. Before he left, Ahi-
jah saw the women votaries lying with men at the altar-
base; and when the embrace was done, he saw them rise
and accept payments from the men and toss these into
the stomach, for the earnings of the consecrated women
belonged to the gods or their priests. As Ahijah turned
to go he saw a form crawling on hands and knees into
darkness but he did not know that it was the insane
priest bleeding to death. . .
In this same evening Zikla was talking to his King.
He would have gone to Solomon at once after leaving

next king, he acted with prudence for his own safety.
During the day he busied himself out in the open, where

"My Lord," he said, "there is something which as

"Hah! That infernal hag from the marshes! What

Solomon drained and filled his wine-cup again. If he
had been an able ruler by the standards of his time or
by the standards of later times he would have had both
Naamah and her son slain, and Jeroboam also, after
Khate had told him what Jeroboam said. But it was not
alone a fatal defect of character or a reluctance to des-
troy members of the royal family or those, like Jero-
boam, of whom he had been fond. His position was dif-
ferent from that of other kings and his problems were
more insoluble. Other kings had usurpers, pretenders,
and conspirators to contend against and when they found
them they moved swiftly and ruthlessly to destroy them.
They had the priesthood, as in Egypt, but priesthoods
could be bribed. Solomon had prophets and they could
be neither bribed nor intimidated. He knew his people
well. He had been through northern Israel and he knew
that most of them were ready for revolt. If he were to
349
slay Ahijah, or possibly even Jeroboam, the whole mass
of the common people would rise against him, and
though with his army he could exterminate them he re-
coiled from the thought. If he had moved decisively and
with vigor to make his will supreme the whole history
of Israel might have been different, and in consequence
of that, the whole history of the western world.
There was another matter. He was not a warlike
ruler but he was a shrewd one. He knew that the mo-
ment there was revolt and internal strife in his kingdom,
the Pharaoh, and probably King Hiram also, would
march against him to plunder and pillage. Kings always
did. Khate had told him that when Akhnaton, who de-
tested war, refused to go to the aid of his northern
provinces when they were attacked, the Hittite people
arose and swept away most of his kingdom. Nobody
had marched against David when he was fighting for
his life because he had had no treasure worth stealing.
And so it was that Solomon did little at all. This
night he tried to sleep but arose toward morning to sit

SOLOMON HAD A LARGE roofed stockade in which he con-
fined unruly members of his guard or his armed forces
and he imprisoned Naamah there. Rehoboam somehow
got wind of this and fled to the Ammonites. A week
later Benaiah died and his burial services were hardly
completed when Bathsheba followed him. From these
two blows Solomon never recovered and after Bathshe-
ba's funeral he took to his bed.
"I'm a dead dog," he said to Ahiah. "I can feel my
end in my bones."
He suffered from his dreams, of monstrous serpents,
with power to assume appalling shapes and colors and
dissolve in hideous blood-stains; and of the moon, which
had become erratic and threatened to fall out of the sky,
and of the sun, magnificently lonely in its solitude. And
he suffered from visions when he was wide awake. He
would lie with his eyes closed and would see a phantas-
magoria of innumerable forms and faces and ghostly ap-
352
paritions. The kind of atmosphere they were in-though
this of course he did not know-depended on the nature
of the light that fell on his eyelids. If the light was in-
tense and golden, the haze in which the phantoms moved
was one of glowing yellow, orange and saffron, merging
into darkness whose somber depths were torn by flashes
or streaks of blue light or sudden blotches of red. If
the light in his chamber was dim, then the world of
these beings was dark and fathomless, as though he
were staring into Sheol itself.
It was by no power of will that he summoned these
apparitions. They came unbidden. Wholly independent of
his will they shifted their positions and changed their
shapes. If, on the right margin, he saw a dreadful face,
gross and dark and glowing with anger, and turned his
eyes slowly for a better view of it, at once it vanished;
but then, swimming in out of the darkness, came another
face. This might be the face of a monster half-beast and
half-human or the ugly loathing face of Naamah. Then
it would dissolve in a throng of disembodied spirits
whose tails would entwine in wondrous networks of
spidersilk. More horrifying than any other was a face
that would appear in the center of his vision and look
into his eyes and disintegrate into an evil smile that
would float away in fantastic fragments. Whose face
this was he did not know. In the weird atmosphere he
saw the face of Zeruah, of Zikla, of Ahijah, of nearly
every person he had known but Ahiah and Khate. And
one day he saw the face of David.
He spoke only to Ahiah about these things.
"You say they look at you with hatred?"
"All of them, every abominable son and dog and
daughter."
"Which one is present now?"
Solomon closed his eyes and for a few moments was
353
silent. "One comes. . .It is my mother and her face
is wet with tears. . . but wait, she is going . . . now there
comes the hag Naamah, on my right . . . there . . . but
she's gone. No! Now she's above me, leering." He opened
his eyes. "Yesterday I saw my father's face."
"But he's in Sheol."
"Can it be that I'm looking into Sheol? Ah, my God!"
"What's the matter?" asked Ahiah anxiously.
"Wait," he said and shut his eyes. "Nobody comes
now but I can see long moving tails with a head that is
only an eye
now there comes a face, long, with a

His obsession with these apparitions became so in-
tense and constant and his nights so haunted that Ahiah
feared for his sanity. One day the scribe spoke of the
matter to Khate.
"Is he going mad?" she asked, horrified.
"I don't know."
There had come to the city an Egyptian physician, a
man named Nourhi, who seemed to be, Khate thought
after talking to him, a worthy member of his profession.
He was not among those who for gout prescribed urine,
for quinsy and fever, pellets of dung. On the contrary
he was familiar with some of the sulphates, carbonates
and oxides. He prescribed, for various ailments, such ex-
cellent vegetable remedies as onion, aloe, colchium, saf-
fron and garlic, as well as many bitters. Indeed, his ma-
teria medica was a large one, gathered from thirty years
of practice: he had cures for pus sores, gangrene, burns
and common wounds. He used opium for pain, castor oil
as a cathartic, copper salts for diseases of the eyes.
He had only contempt for doctors who mixed dread-
ful concoctions of dung, urine, saliva, sweat, eel grease,
bee glue, woodlice, omentum of the ram, the secundines
354
of women, burnt hair, vapors, the moss gathered from
old skull bones, the excreta of virgins, toe and fingernails,
woman's milk, the spittle of virgins; who tried to cure
sexually impotent men by anointing their thumb, toe and
nose with the oils of passion; who believed that milk-
stone worn as a charm neutralized the evil eye, that the
moonstone was a theriac, that all animal parts related
to procreation had magical properties; who mixed cala-
mus, gentian, acacia, hyoscyamus, olive oil and salt as a
cure for diarrhea. Nourhi knew the value of emetics and
of balsam sap on wounds. For inflammation of the joints
he prescribed hot sulphur baths and he had some grasp
of the cause of rickets. And above all else he was a man
wise for his time in knowing that some diseases were
housed almost entirely in the patient's mind.
Nourhi was a tall, thin, gray man with kindly eyes. He
did not pretend to be much more than he was and he
never promised to cure.
"I'll be glad to see the King," he told Khate, "and do
what I can."
"Well, let's go over."
When they entered Solomon's chamber Nourhi went
quietly to the bed and asked professionally: "My Lord,
how do you feel?"
"Like a gazelle under the jackals."
"Where are your pains?"
"All through me."
Khate winked at Solomon and said drily: "Nourhi
healed a woman who was pregnant for five years and re-
turned a man to life after he'd been embalmed. He raises
the dead, makes the blind see and restores the old to
youth."
"Restore me," said Solomon.
"My Lord, if I could do that I'd have the secret of
the gods."
355
"Can you interpret dreams and visions?"
"Sometimes."
"Can you explain the weird behavior of the moon?"
"What has the moon been doing?"
"She appears at the wrong time."
Nourhi glanced at Khate. "What else?"
"She threatens to fall out of the sky."
Nourhi was reflecting that the moon meant different
things to different people. For some it was a god, for
some, a goddess, but for him it was only a strange saucer
of light.
"Are the days long by the moon's calculation?"
Ah, now it was coming back to Nourhi: according to
a belief in Babylon, if the days were long by the moon's
reckoning the king's reign would be a long one. If the
moon was full and plump in those times when it should
be, the harvest would be abundant and the king exalted.
Nourhi was not accomplished in moon-lore.
"The King," said Ahiah, explaining, "sees the moon
in his dreams. Sometimes it shrinks to the size of a
melon-seed and usually it is not where it is supposed to
be. When new, its horns are cut off or simply fall off.
When old it is shrunken and misshapen and wabbles in
its journey. Sometimes serpents crawl into the moon as
into a seashell and then they fall out. And sometimes
the moon is bloody."
"You speak of the moon as she, my Lord. Do you
think of the moon as a goddess?"
"The moon is a woman," said Solomon. Khate and
Ahiah glanced at one another.
"Any particular woman?-or just a woman?"
"The moon is Astarte."
"Oh, I see."

"Listen to the fool!" said Khate.
"Yah has spoken to me, his prophet, saying: Evil will
go forth from this city like a whirlwind and a pestilence
and a famine, like a plague and seven plagues; and all of
358
Israel will cry out from the depths, yet my ears will be

you out of the bondage that was Egypt! I am your god

He was tossing his hair back and gesturing, or swing-

"There will be woes in Israel!" Ahijah was shouting,

there was no speaker like him. "All the children will take

they will be cut off and will fall like dung on the fields.
This city of wickedness will be a den for dragons and
a haunt for thieves. There will be no good in it. It walks
with slanders and dwells in the midst of deceit. The eyes
of Yah will be blind above it."
Then his deep voice rose and his passion burst forth:
"A great disaster will come upon this land, even if the
King cuts off his hair, even if he laments on the high
places, even if he crawls in the dust and spreads dung on
his face! The bodies of the wicked will be flesh for the
birds of prey and there will be none to bury their bones!
Bow down, base King, lie down with sorrow, lie down
with the dead! Rub your face in the dung, you evil prince,
for you have whored after the false gods, you have com-
mitted iniquities in Yah's holy places, you have not
thirsted nor come to the waters of righteousness, but
have been a blind shepherd who abandoned your sheep!
Behold..."
Khate was moved in spite of herself. In a low voice
359
she said to Ahiah: "I've wondered how this man could

"Let the wicked forsake their ways and the evil their

Khate took Ahiah's arm and they left the silent throng

"See!" she said to Ahiah. "Look-over there." But

"Don't worry, my Lady. I have my men everywhere."
"Have you found Jeroboam yet?"
"No."
She went to her house, taking the scribe with her, and
they were drinking wine, each sunk in his thoughts, when

360
"Well, physician, do you know the King's trouble?"
"I can only tell you what I think.”
"That is all I want to hear. Dogmas belong to priests,
not to physicians."
"He has some fever but not enough to make him ill.
The torments are in his mind. Are matters progressing

"No. His kingdom is bankrupt and a lunatic is on his

"Yes."

Ahiah was sorely grieved. "Who am I to advise you?"

AHIJAH SPENT ANOTHER MONTH in the city, denouncing
the King or exhorting those who would listen to him.
and no hand was raised to touch him; and as the days
passed the power of his god was upon him and he be-
came more violent in his threats and abuse.
"Yah will come like a lion from the wilderness and
he will roll this wicked city down from the hills and it
will lie scattered like the ruins of abominable altars! He
will come with his wrath and fires and devouring light-
nings and scatter these people who have thrown off the
bridle, the dogs and hypocrites and lechers and gluttons
who foul his holy places! His punishments are as fires
shut up in his bones! His power will sweep over the re-
fuge of liars, the waters of his anger will overflow their
hiding-places!..."
363
If Khate had come out to put a hand on Zeruah, who

like a naked person in a frozen wind. She would have
seen blanched faces in some of those around her. For
Yah was speaking to Ahijah as he was to speak to all his
great prophets:
"The voice of wailing will be heard, and lamentations
and sorrows, and all the sounds of grief, yes, from the
depths to the high places; for Yah made the earth, he
stretched out the tent of the sky, he showed himself in
the green places, he opened the sky and poured down the
rains; for he is the almighty one! Yet his people in this
city have turned their ears away, they have gone whoring
after the false gods of the wicked people! I say unto
you that until swans turn black and crows turn white the
god of Israel will endure and he will be a curse upon the
wicked and a pestilence upon the evil! This whole land
will be a desolation and the King will fall down and
there will be none to lift him up! But for us who are
righteous he will give strength to our horn, holiness to
our wind, mightiness to our bones, and be a blessing upon
us all the days of our life..."
Then suddenly one day he departed, when the hills
were embosomed with fog and cold rain was falling. He
headed for Bethel. On his way he met the King's cara-
vans coming up the hills to the city, some of the beasts
falling to the mud and lying there obstinate and im-
passive, while their drivers stared at them and blew
warmth on their cold hands. Ahead was a vast tent of
fog and cold and people in their caves and huts.
It was about twelve miles to Bethel and the whole dis-
tance was a path of mud. From time to time Ahijah sat
by the way and rubbed his naked feet to keep them from
freezing. He shook with the cold, being almost with-
out clothing, and a mist of waters fell from his long hair
364
and was like frost on his skin. As he neared Bethel he
kept glancing back to see if he had been followed but
there was no sign of a spy anywhere. At the end of his
journey he would find shelter, for he was on his way to
meet Jeroboam.
Bethel stood like Jerusalem in desolate country. There,
according to a legend, one called Jacob had set up a stone
phallus and anointed it. There also were a golden bull
and a brazen serpent, holy emblems of Yah, as well as
a small sanctuary and an altar. Yah had fled Jerusalem
but he was still in Bethel.
Ahijah went beyond Bethel and wandered over the
stones until darkness came, when he slipped swiftly into
the hamlet and went to a small mudbrick structure of two
rooms, without windows, with a floor of earth, and a
roof of rolled clay mixed with barley straw. It had one
door. It was customary to knock on doors to frighten
away evil spirits that might be lurking inside but Ahijah
opened it without knocking and went in.
"Ahijah!"
"Jeroboam, is it you?"
"I've waited long!"
"Yah called me to my labors."
Because there was only one tiny lamp in the gloom
Ahijah at first could see nothing at all; and so he stood
by the door, and Jeroboam knelt and washed his feet.
The room was almost as cold as the world outside. While
Ahijah was peering into the darkness he heard what he
took to be a moan.
"Is someone sick?"
"It's my woman. A child is coming from her.'
Now able to make out her form Ahijah went over and
looked down at Micaah, Jeroboam's devout wife. She was
resting on hands and knees, the customary position in
childbirth, and was whispering holy words to ward off
365
evil. A woman in childbirth was closely guarded, for she
was then vulnerable to many kinds of evil being. Those
in attendance stood guard and prayed for her, or uttered
incantations so powerful that demons vainly assaulted
them, like a weak man leaping against a stone wall. Those
attending her were not allowed to gossip about her and
her past sins or speak any manner of evil against her or
think of evil things. To ease her pains in childbirth she
was allowed to wear some article of her husband's cloth-
ing, usually the girdle. One of Jeroboam's girdles was
drawn tightly round Micaah's waist.
Over and over she was whispering, "Evil things—gɔ
away—go away!"
"Don't worry now," said Jeroboam. "Yah's holy one
is with us and there can be no evil in this house." Then

"No."
"Do they know where I am?"
"It doesn't matter. The King doesn't dare touch you."
Jeroboam wasn't convinced of that. "Did you see my

"I saw her among the people."
"Has Yah chosen the next king?"

Micaah's child was a son. For many days she would
be unclean, which means that birth had consecrated her
to her god; she would not enter a sanctuary or touch any
hallowed thing. On the eighth day the child would be
circumcised and named, and already Jeroboam was think-
ing of names for this, his firstborn, who would some day
be, he hoped, a king in Israel. It was of the gravest im-
portance to choose the right name. Ahijah was on his

366
the naming; and so it was agreed that the prophet would

During the seven days of Ahijah's absence Jeroboam
gazed proudly on his son, faithfully observing all the
ceremonial laws, dwelling on his plans and hopes and
dreams, and thinking of names. The right testicle, which
produced the male child, was Anu or On, and the left,
which produced the female child, was Hoa or Hea. These
were often incorporated into the names of children.
There was, for instance, a story of a Rachel who with
her dying breath had called Jacob's son Benoni, meaning
son of the right stone. Jacob had called him Benjamin,
which meant the same thing. Jeroboam wished to incor-
porate the name of Yah, or Jah, into his son's name; but
what other word should he use? Should he call him
Onijah? He would not incorporate the word baal, as so
many of his people were doing, because Ahijah had said
that the word baal was a stink in Israel. Some parents
named their children for emotional or physical condi-
tions prevailing at birth-as Jerioth for tent curtains,
Azubah for an abandoned desert region (a strange name
indeed), Yischak or Isaac for laughter, Hillel for a tri-
umphant cry, Hedad for a shout of joy. . .
In this time the Hebrews never named a child for a
kinsman.
Jeroboam wanted the names of all his sons to be in-
corporated with Yah. He wanted this because the name
was the essence of the person. If named for a living per-
son the child would be robbed of its soul; but if named
for a god it would be in possession of divine powers. Of
Yah-names, which Jeroboam favored, there were Elijah,
Adonijah, Abijah, Ahijah...
When Ahijah returned Jeroboam looked at him mis-
chievously and said: "I've chosen a name."
"What?"
367
"Ahijah."
"No." Ahijah was the only important Ahijah in Is-
rael.
"I want my son to be like you."
"Your son may be a thief or a drunkard."
His enthusiasm dashed Jeroboam said contritely: "In

"He's very dark," said Ahijah, looking at the babe.

Jeroboam became suspicious. Iram meant only that

889
The name was not made known to the prophet until
many weeks later, when he was wandering in the northern
districts, finding everywhere the bitterness of tyranny
and oppression. Everywhere the people were starved, dis-
pirited and sullen. Of what use to them, they asked, was
a kingdom and its prince of peace if children died of
hunger and their fathers were robbed of the fruits of
their toil? Was this the happier life which Yah had prom-
ised? Better to return to the wilderness, for there,
though poor, the people had lived in justice as neighbors.
One day Ahijah witnessed a scene of violence and pas-
sion. A collector of tithes, one of the King's men, had
appeared in a town and summoned the local shiekh and
his counsellors. He had said that the Anointed, the Lord
of Lords and Sun of Suns, his Majesty, the King of Is-
rael, had learned that the people here were withholding
a part of their taxes. The news spread quickly and an
angry mob gathered and followed the collector from
house to house. He had his men searching the houses and
the holes under the floors which served as storage-bins.
The anger of the people mounted as a storm gathers,
until at last, taking courage from the presence of Ahijah,
a man flung a stone. The stone struck the collector who
at the moment was standing, warmly clothed, peering at
what he took to be a cache of barley. Then other stones
were hurled, and the passion of the mob burst and spread
like flame from a scattered fire. Seizing stones and clubs
the people rushed in, and a few moments later the col-
lector of taxes lay dead in the street, with one eye hang-
ing down his cheek. Naked he lay, and dead, and nobody
covered him.
The murder was for Ahijah merely another sign of
Yah's wrath. He saw other signs in other places and he
was given signs in his dreams. But a greater sign than all
others was written upon the whole land of Israel. It had
370
been a mild open winter, with no snow and only a little
rain. Mount Hermon, usually snowcapped throughout the
year, was streaked with brown. Fountains that usually
gushed in the spring season were stilled, and rivers had
shrunk to half their normal size.
After noting the signs of approaching famine, Ahijah
prayed to his god and out of a great light Yah spoke to
him; and he hastened to the valley of Jericho to find the
one who was known as the son of Nebat. Jeroboam was
not there, nor in Zereda nor in Penuel nor in Bethel;
frightened by Ahijah's long absence he had gone down
the river to the springs of Engedi, where lived a famous
witch. It was this hag, whose name was Isabaal, mean-
ing the oath of Baal, who directed Ahijah to the cave
where Jeroboam and his family were hiding.
That Ahijah had at last received the sign was plain
to Jeroboam the moment he saw the prophet. He rushed
forward, crying, "Welcome, Ahijah, son of Yah! I've
waited a long time for you!"
"Come," said Ahijah, and he led Jeroboam out into

"I knew it!" cried Jeroboam, trembling with eager-

Ahijah was looking at him and he saw the name in Ahi-

Ahijah put his right hand on Jeroboam's hair and said:
"Arise."
Jeroboam stood up, so shaken by emotion that he could

871
a moment later, like the eager and headstrong boy that

"It will not be Jerusalem."
"No!"
"Jeroboam, I say to you, there will be wailing and

The lowly peasant, whom Yah had chosen to be his

33
IN HIS WANDERINGS over Israel in past years Ahijah had
seen his people worshiping in the wicked way of the
Canaanites. He had seen Hebrew women, going in frenzy
from shrine to shrine, seeking to be fertilized by the
agents of alien gods; in the winter season, when the sun
had paled and was dying, he had seen women with bared
breasts and streaming hair bewailing the death of Tam-
muz. Some polled themselves as a sign of grief; and
others, unwilling to sacrifice the glory of their hair, had
lain with strangers, giving their earnings to the abomin-
able priests of Ashtoreth. They had worn the scarlet
anemone in other seasons, believing the flower took its
color from the blood of Tammuz. Sickened and furious
and searching the sky for signs of lightning he had heard
their cries of joy:
A willow that rejoiced not by the watercourse,
herbs that in the garden had drunk no water!
Her lament is for a pool where the fish grow not!
Her chamber is a house that brings forth no child!
Lord, being born again, I die, for I am exalted!
Lord, from life I am born into death, I am freed!
Our god is born again, he is our savior,
for lo, we have found him, we rejoice
for our savior who is risen!
He had seen the mourners go into deep shrines, into
the caves of the earth, and emerge at midnight, crying:
373
The light is waxing, the light comes forth again,
the Virgin has given birth!...
He had seen them carry in procession an infant, with
a halo round its head to symbolize the sun; and the women
and girls, breasts bared, garments torn, running over the
hills, weeping and wailing and crying:
Our savior will deliver us,
he will restore the earth's beauty,
he will fill the wombs!. . .
"Naaman!" they had cried. "Naaman! Naaman!"-
meaning darling, meaning the scarlet flower, symbol of
their god, whose color was his precious blood.
Such were the abominable practices of many Hebrews,
encouraged by their King. The festival of the Passover,
at the full moon nearest the spring equinox, was in vari-
ous forms common to people everywhere; but Ahijah
thought that it belonged only to his people. In earlier
times people at this festival had offered their firstborn
sons in rejoicing over the resurrection of the sun-god.
Ahijah thought that it had been given by Yah to com-
memorate the escape from Egypt, and he was determined
to keep it pure and uncolored and unmixed. He was de-
termined to keep in their original form all the command-
ments which Yah had given to his people. Among these
it was said, You shall not kill-meaning you shall not
kill your tribal brother, lest he seek vengeance, as by
ancient covenants he is commanded to. You shall honor
your parents, lest your waywardness call down upon your
people pestilence or famine. You shall not bear false
witness, for the spoken word was filled with magic and
fulfilled what was spoken. You shall not covet, because
covetousness was an attribute of the Evil Eye. You shall
not speak in vain the name of Yah, for by speaking his
874
name profanely or carelessly you might harm him or
you might destroy the universe. When Ahijah arrived at
Bethel for the holy festival he was close to the inspired
moment of saying, You shall not set images of your god
before him.
After his work of purification was completed, the
prophet looked at the small stone structure, roofed with
a tent covering of black haircloth. This tent of the meet-
ing was Yah's house in Bethel and inside it were the
portable idols. People were coming now, including Jer-
oboam and the local priest; and Ahijah, who loved this
festival, pitched in to help prepare for it and to see that
everything was done in accordance with the divine laws.
Anointing oil had to be mixed. Taking olive oil, Ahijah
scented it with sweet essences and put a few drops on the
altar, on the holy tables, and on the other furnishings.
On the table of the Presence were placed the sacred
loaves; and upon the altar, a group of unhewn stones, the
priest poured a part of the sacred oil. Ahijah's face was
glowing with joy because he was faithfully observing the
commandments of his god, and Jeroboam's face shone to
watch him. Jeroboam carefully observed what Ahijah
did; he was resolved to be a righteous king.
By late afternoon persons began to arrive from the
outlying places, some having traveled most of the day.
They came in bearing branches and flowers or the twigs.
of plants not yet in bloom; because all these things-olive
and palm and acacia, golden broom and honey-suckle,
narcissus and oleander and thyme, mint and quince and
ilex-all these and many more were precious, being a
part of the divine life and beauty which Yah had placed.
on his earth. A few of the people came wearing symbols
of the heathen gods, and these Ahijah tore from them
and threw away, though he did not in all instances know
875
which emblems were wicked. Some of them had these

As the people came in they made music, each accord-

others blowing on whistles, flutes or horns. Those who
had no instruments were singing songs.
Ahijah moved among the people, searching the faces
for spies. Isabaal, the sorceress, had come up from En-

of a lifetime making sly and subtle things of her eyes.
She wore only a simple garment of coarse cloth, held at

horribly emaciated and stooped; he was in mourning and

379

DURING THESE PAST MONTHS Solomon had improved
under the care of Nourhi and in the cheerful presence of
Khate. He had made his peace with King Hiram by ced-
ing to him a score of his most northern cities; he had
faithful spies along the coast between Joppa and Gaza
watching for signs of unfriendliness from a predatory
Pharaoh; he had guards on the Jordan to intercept Re-
381
hoboam if he returned; but he knew that his cause was
hopeless. If there had been no Ahijah it was true never-
theless that his kingdom was bankrupt and most of his
people ready for revolt. And as though all that were
not enough there now came to him report of approach-
ing famine. The barley, he was told, would wither before
the heads filled; the fruit would be small and bitter on
tree and vine. While casting about him for a way out he
remembered that his father, David, when faced with fam-
ine and divine anger, had hanged the seven sons of Saul.
"Should I offer the firstborn sons?" he drily asked
Khate.
"It would do just as much good to stand on your
head."
"Zadok advises me to seek the firstborn son of a
seventh son of a seventh son. What do you say?"
"I think you must be jesting."
"Well, not wholly," he said. "You've made me about
half Egyptian-”
"Oh no. Most of my people are no more civilized
than yours. They have more ridiculous things than
seventh sons of seventh sons."
Ahiah has suggested that I should proclaim my-

"So they imagined, the absurd fellows. All the kings
in Babylon become gods."
"I have reddish hair," said Solomon, "which is the
divine color."

member that some of these man-gods are hanged on a
pine tree and scourged and crucified."
Solomon considered a few moments and then voiced
one of his rarest gems of wisdom. "It's a habit with
people to kill their gods. They're everlastingly trying to
382
drag all things down to their level. If an idiot were to

if Ahijah were king he would make everybody wear goat-

the higher he will rise by comparison."
Then that fateful spring day came when Zikla entered

A long day's journey south of Jericho was the fountain
of Hazezon-tamar or Engedi, the spring of the kid,
which burst in a miracle of foaming waters from the
rock face of the mountain. These were in a sunken area
so parched and poisonous that by contrast they seemed
to be more marvelous than they were. A person ap-
proaching these springs across the wilderness journeyed
for hours without seeing around him any sign of water
or any twig or blade of green; when suddenly, below the
precipice facing the salt sea, he came upon the wonder
of this furious gushing, fringed with its own lovely life,
and fertilizing a small valley of orchards and fields. In
all of Israel there was no greater marvel than these
springs, and because it was so plain that the gods loved
this place Isabaal had made it her home.
Ahiah sent a messenger ahead to tell Isabaal he was
coming and two days later mounted one of the royal
beasts and set off alone, taking a small parcel of food.
Isabaal meanwhile was preparing for his coming. All her
procedures she measured in three parts or in seven: when
in the dark of night she secretly gathered her herbs she
walked three times round the plant, going sunwise; she
murmured three incantations over it; she touched it three
times before plucking it. Before communing with the un-
seen world she drew three circles and stood within them;
no demon had power to pass from a public to a private
place, and least of all to a symbol of the womb. Faith-
385
fully she made her preparations with cleansings, prayer
and meditation, and felt able to summon David when
Ahiah came.
On the afternoon of his arrival she refused to see him
until dark and then led him into the night, going before
him like a shadow. Though he had a clearer grasp of
realities than most people of his time Ahiah felt chilled
and anxious. He cast glances round him and strained his
ears to the night-sounds. Isabaal was crossing an area
of loose stones, where Ahiah stumbled and faltered, but
presently she came to the entrance of a cave. He could
smell the strange other-world odor of its depths and he
I could hear faint sounds in its blackness. Was David's
shade already there? She told him to stand where he was
and suddenly vanished, but whether she entered the cave
he could never have said. In his scalp and over his flesh
were the sensations of terror.
While standing there and trembling he heard a thin
weak voice that seemed to come from far away. He
thought it came up out of the cave; and this indeed is
what he had expected, for some caves led into the under-
world where the ghosts, forlorn and sad and disembod-
ied, wandered in Sheol. At first he could hear the voice
but he could not distinguish words; it seemed to be cry-
ing, to be coming up to him in plaintive grief, like that
of a child lost in the night. But then, with no warning
at all, it came to him clear and distinct, though still thin
and far away and feeble. The voice was asking him who
he was.
"Who are you?"
"I-" said Ahiah, and choked.
"Who are you?"
"I'm Ahiah!" he said.
"Why have you disturbed my rest?"
"Who are you?"
386
"I'm David."
Ahiah's tongue and throat were dry. He moistened his

"Speak up !"
Back in the gloom he thought he saw a figure moving,

King Solomon."
"What does he want?"

The next day Khate and Ahiah were sitting with the

WHEN THE GUARD DELIVERED the King's message to Ahi-
jah the prophet was rather abashed, and thought it well
to wait a few days or weeks to see if his position was
secure; but by midsummer the famine was a desolation
in Israel and insurrection was everywhere in the land.
Then Ahijah came up to the King's city and told the
palace guards that he wished to see him. It was Zikla
who fetched the message.
"Where is he?"
"Outside, waiting."
"Be sure he has no dagger on him and send him in."
Ahijah strode in, wearing only his goatskin, and with-
out making any gesture of respect he looked at Solomon
and said:
"I have come."
392
"I've been expecting you."
Solomon was lying on his bed, propped up with cush-
ions: all around him stood trees and shrubs and images.
of the Mother and Child. As haughty and unbending as
his god, the prophet looked at the trees, the images, the
splendors. This was the first time he had seen the inner-
most retreat of Israel's Lord.
"I hope you like it," said Solomon drily. When Ahijah
did not speak he added: "But you don't, of course. Men
like you can be happy only with deserts and famines. Will
you sit?-or do you intend to pronounce your curse while
standing?"
For Ahijah the King's levity was a measure of his
wickedness. He had departed so far from the way of Yah
and had become so contemptuous of the divine laws that
he was more vile than a Syrian or an Ammonite.
"You say I'm a son of wickedness and that the power
of Yah rests in your right hand. Is that so?"
Ahijah looked at him gravely and said: "That is so."
"Very well. Now before you get your anger up and
start ranting, tell me this: what kind of king would you
have been?"
"A righteous king," said Ahijah.
"Would you have lived in a goathair tent?"
"Look round you!" cried Ahijah, gesturing angrily.
"Have you forgotten what Yah said to Nathan when
David was King?"
"I suppose I have. What did he say?"
"Yah said I have not dwelt in a house since the day
I brought up the children of Israel out of Egypt, even ·
to this day, but have lived in a portable tent."
"He still likes to live in tents, doesn't he?"
"Base King-"
"Wait now. If you start your abominable and infernal
393
shouting I'll have you thrown out. If you want to talk
like a man-”
"You'll have me thrown out?" asked Ahijah, as-
tounded. "I?—the prophet of Yah-"
"I don't care if you're the prophet of all the gods

"I speak for Yah!"
"All right, I speak for God. Now what?"
"You wicked King! Why is Israel consumed by famine?
Because Yah withholds his waters! Why does he with-

"I understand that I'm wicked-"
"You've taken to your bosom the women of aliens; yet
Abraham, did he allow his son to marry a heathen
woman? Did Isaac and Rebekah-"
"They were pretty ignorant people, weren't they?
Mere desert nomads, as I understand the story. Ahijah,
have you ever lain with a woman?"
The question was so insolent that Ahijah choked, but
before he could cry out the King went on blandly:
“As a matter of fact you did—and I know who the
woman is and I know who your son is. Yet you pretend
to be a very righteous fellow, don't you? Wait! I'm talk-
ing. I understand all that you would say. I understand
many things that men like you will never understand. I
asked what kind of king you would have been. I know.
You would try to drag our people back into the wilder-
ness, back to their aimless wanderings, back to the sands
and storms and waterholes-back to the goatcurd and the
locusts. Men like you go crying to the barren mountains
because you hate the soft beauty of the valleys. You
would go naked and lamenting and howling-"
"Your people are naked! They starve while you lie here
394
amidst splendors that you have wrung from their mis-
eries !"
"I suppose you'd have dressed them all in fine linen."
"These are Yah's holy people, his chosen, whom he
promised to make plenteous in goods, in the fruits of
their bodies, for he would open up the skies to give rain,
the land to put forth its abundance! He promised to set
us above, not beneath, if we would walk in his holy way;
but if we depart from the way we'll be cursed, cursed in
the field, cursed in our baskets and our storebins, cursed
in our bodies! He will send upon us vexations and miseries
and woes, as he told the prophets in olden times! He
gave us the laws to live by-”
"Those are only customs and customs change."
"No! His laws are immutable and eternal. They do not
change. It is wicked kings like you who try to change
them."
"If you were king you'd have nothing to do with other
people, would you?"
"Nothing!"
"You'd try to make Israel a holy place for Yah's chil-
dren and for them only; and all other people you'd de-
spise and hate and abjure and curse and pray against.
After a while they'd all hate Israel and combine against
you and march in and hang you on a tree. Do you think
you could keep your people secure in the fruit of their
bodies and their land if you were hurling curses at other
people and calling them the wicked sons of the no-gods?
"This much, Ahijah, I can tell you, that if Israel is
ruled by men like you there will be no Israel. Other na-
tions will kill us or sell us as slaves. They will throw our
walls down and destroy our cities. We will be a stink in
the nostrils of the earth.
"And I tell you this also, that if you arouse the people
to put Jeroboam on the throne you will have war in Is
395
rael. Then, when Israel is weakened by wars within,
kings will march in from the north and the south and
there will be no Israel and the wrong and shame of it
will be on the heads of you and men like you. Is that what
you want?"
"As long as Yah's people are righteous Israel will be
strong, for Yah is mightier than all other gods. No peo-
ple would dare to come against us. If they did, Yah would
smite them for in his hand is all power, now and for-
ever!"
"Oh, you cramp me like an awful bellyache. Yah is
only one god or he may be no god at all—"
"Silence!"
"Don't shout at me, you crazy fellow!"
But the King had uttered blasphemous words and Ahi-
jah's terrible eyes were flashing. He advanced and looked
into Solomon's eyes and summoned his powers. Solomon
stared at the wrath in his face and said:
"Now don't start belching at me "
"You withered root!"
"Come, now!"
"You wretch of sore sickness and sterile seed!"
The passion and hate in Ahijah's face was so dreadful
that Solomon made a move to rise but with a furious
movement the prophet thrust him back.
"In the name of the gods I'll have you chained!"
"You haven't the power! And now be still while Yah
speaks to you!" Hating, avenging, summoning his powers,
Ahijah stood over him, preparing for the curse.
When he saw froth on the man's lips Solomon cried
out: "Oh no you don't! You're not going to come in here
and pronounce any curses on me!"
There was moving up through Ahijah a great and holy
wrath, warming and strengthening him; and when he felt
the full power of his god he burst forth in a tremendous
396
shout: "Curse this wicked King with curses seven times
seven! You will be cursed in the morning and cursed in
the evening, cursed with an evil curse in anger,
until your
name is blotted out and your seed is blotted out in the
whole land of Israel!"
"Oh, in the name of God!" cried Solomon, trying to
rise, but two long arms as tough as hornbeam thrust him
back.
"As long as the sky stands you will be cut off, and
though you hunger you will have no delight in fatness,
though you thirst there will be none to lead you to the
waters! Though you cry out, none will know you! Though
you grope for your watchmen, they will be blind! Though
you lift up your voice and mourn like the doves, Yah
will not hear! Grope, King, like a blind man down the
wall, but he will not see! There will be a new sky and a
new earth, a new sun and a new morning, figs on thistles
and grapes on thorns and fatted lambs in the lair of the
jackal before your cries come to the ears of the god
you have scorned! You have sucked the fatness of your
people..."
Solomon reached behind him and grasped a bell and
rang it twice, and two armed guards ran in. At once they
seized Ahijah by his arms and Solomon said:
"If
your god is so powerful, let him cast them down."
Terrible in his pallor of hate and loathing, Ahijah was
trying to shake off the guards but they held him firmly;
and Solomon left his bed and stood before the prophet.
"You came to curse me and I shove your evil words back
down your throat. In contempt of your ignorance and
bigotry and lunacies I spit on you. These guards will
throw you outside and you will leave my city before night-
fall or I'll put you in chains.
"Take him out."
Still shouting his curse Ahijah was taken out, and Sol-
397
omon lay on his bed with a strange expression on his face,
of anger and disgust and repugnance but mixed with these
was something akin to shame and fear. For a little while
he lay on his back, spent and weary, and stared at the
ceiling, thinking of this wilderness prophet, of his crumb-
ling kingdom, of King Akhnaton and of David, his father,
and of the future. Then he rang a bell and told a servant
to call Khate and Ahiah.
They had been sitting in Khate's parlor, waiting, know-
ing that Ahijah was with the King. When told that Sol-
omon wished to see them, Khate looked at Ahiah.
"Now what, I wonder?"
When they entered Solomon's chamber Khate glanced
at him and thought he looked paler than she had ever
seen him; but his mood seemed to be one of philosophic
calm. He lifted a hand and said quietly:
"Make yourselves comfortable. Ahiah, go bring us
some wine." While Ahiah was out he said to Khate: "Are
you sure you don't want to go back to Egypt? It won't
be long now."
"What won't be long?"
He drew a deep breath and let it out in a long heavy
sigh. "Everything."
"No," she said. "I've thought and thought about it.
I'll stay here."
"They won't be very kind to you. You know, Khate,
I've figured out something else. My people are so severe
with women because they have such need of them. Does
that make sense?"
"Yes. I've had that thought."
"Righteousness is a pretty poor substitute for tender-
ness."
"How well you have said it!"
"Do you think so? In any case that is the way I see it.'
Ahiah came with wine and while he was filling the
898
cups Khate looked round the chamber, thinking of the
King's words. Surrounding the far side of his bed were
many trees of life, chiefly the olive but also the cedar,
palm, oak, willow and vine. At his bedside on a table stood
a crude clay image of the Mother and Child, about three
feet tall, and by them was a small lovely figurine of As-
tarte. His room was redolent of many sweet odors, and
he lay, she reflected, not in a house of death but in a
house of life.
He accepted a cup of wine from Ahiah and said: "We
ought to drink to something." He looked at Khate. "You
laughed at the gods and are well, I built a house for them
and look at me!"
Khate laughed and said: "The wicked prosper, the
good perish. The gods favor those who are most like
them."
"Ahiah, our end draws near; what shall we drink to?"
"You should tell us first what the prophet said."
"The same old thing. He began to curse me and I had
him thrown out-but one thing I forgot to tell him. The
women lament for Tammuz, for Adonis, for Attis-for
all the gods but Yah. No woman ever laments for him."
"Where is Ahijah?"
"I gave him until dark to leave my city. He'll meet the
ingrate called Jeroboam and they'll rouse the people
and come against me."
"How strange it is in Israel!" said Khate." The prophet
curses his king!"
"As long as Israel has kings there will be prophets
to curse them. Tell me : after Akhnaton died who ruled?"
"His son-in-law, a weakling, a mere boy."
"What happened to him?"
"He fell under the assassins." She was thoughtful a
moment and added: "And the magnificent city he built
fell into ruins."
399
"Your people cried to the priests and mine cry to the
prophets and the prophets cry to the mountains. And
they'll go on crying."
"Oh, maybe not. Some day the Nile will understand the
desert and the desert the Nile."
He raised his brows, doubting. "Anyway, I've failed,
and now my father's words come back to me. Does the
old lion roar in the forest? Be patient, my son, he said
to me; you will be king too long, you may think, before
you lie helpless at the feet of your enemies."
"You haven't failed," said Khate impatiently. "When
Akhnaton died they said he had failed, and so he had,
for most of his people. But something remained, and
something will remain of what you have tried to do, some-
where, in the heart of someone. Your people will never
go back to the desert; that is too incredible to think of.
They will mix with other people. The prophets can't hold
them forever to this desert god. The prophets will realize
some day that there is no god of Israel but only one su-
preme being."
Solomon shrugged. "They'll always say that he is Is-
rael's god."
"I doubt that."
"My son, said David, be patient a little longer; the
darkness is closing round me and my feet are lost. Are
the trumpets blown when the people are absent? How
long it took me to understand what he meant! Well, my
dear Khate, this much I can tell you, that my people will
never mix with other people until they have a Mother."
"That may be true. They'll find the Mother when
they learn to love the earth."
"And another very curious thing I've learned. If
women ruled instead of men, God would be a woman."
"Well yes, I suppose so. For some people the gods are
bisexual, being neither man nor woman, but both."
400
"If God is both father and mother, why call God
father?"
"That's what men call him. I think of God as being
a power."
"Well, Ahiah, whom do we drink to? Before long,
Ahijah and Jeroboam will come with their rabble, and
Rehoboam will sneak across the river and there'll be
war. We have a few days or weeks, so let's drink. Then
we'll go down into the underworld—or is there an under-
world?"
"I doubt it," said Khate. "Very probably we just die."
"Shall we drink to the Father, who created man in
his image?-or to the Mother?"
"To neither," said Khate. "Not to Osiris or Yah or
Bel, not to Astarte or Isis or Venus-but to the sun that
warms the seed, and to the valleys that are the womb.'
"Are all those things God?"
"All those and more-warmth and joy and thanks-
giving-"
"And love?" asked Solomon, raising his brows at her.
"Oh yes," she said, smiling. "Love above all."
"Very well." He raised his cup and looked at his scribe.
"It may be only a woman's whim, Ahiah. It may be some
truth that men have missed and women have found. So
let's drink to love, and say that love is both the Father
and the Mother, who together are God."
