AUTHOR'S NOTES
and COMMENTARY
MY FIRST THOUGHT was to append an apparatus of notes,
giving in the briefest possible form and almost entirely within
quotation marks the opinions and conclusions of the scholars
to support my interpretation of the materials in this novel. And
I did prepare the notes in this manner. But they seemed to me
to be pedantic, stiff and unreadable. I then thought that the
reader, if he is interested in what the scholars think, would pre-
for an essay of sorts, in which I would not give all the names of
the authorities, few of which would be familiar to the average
reader, but in a quite informal way would summarize the con-
clusions that have been reached on some of the more important
matters. When now and then I do give the actual words of a
scholar, it is either because he expresses the matter more suc-
cinctly than I could or because the nature of the material seems
to call for his phrasing. There is an advantage of course in let-
ting the authorities speak for themselves; with their own words
one cannot quibble; with my paraphrasing I lay myself open to
question and must therefore ask the reader to believe that this
essay has only one purpose, to present the views as fairly and
briefly as I can.
I do not feel that the historical novelist is exempted from
The reader can judge this for himself. Of the 200 scholars
on whom this novel is based and whose conclusions these notes
summarize these are a few of the names: Driver, Kuenen Wellhau-
sen, Cheyne, Petrie, Oesterley, Robertson Smith, Buchanan Gray, H.
P. Smith, G. A. Smith, T. H. Robinson, H. W. Robinson, Fowler,
Jackson, Frazer, Bewer, Manson, Toy, Baron, Budde, Cornill, Skin-
ner, Pfeiffer, Lake, Jeremias, Winckler, G. F. Moore, Kennedy, Lenor-
mant.
402
those disciplines and standards which apply to scholars and his-
torians. One can write if one chooses in myth, fantasy, legend;
but if one pretends to be a historical novelist one should be
historical. This he will not be if his work does not rest upon the
findings of the best authorities in the field. I have based this
novel upon the highest authorities, departing from them in no
matter on which they have spoken, except in their view of
Solomon himself. These authorities include not only many of
the Higher Critics but also a number in the Egyptian, Assyrian
and Babylonian fields, as well as in medicine, geography and
other related subjects.
TWO HUNDRED YEARS AGO one Dr. Astruc, royal physician at the
court of Louis XV, published some learned studies of the Old
Testament but kept them hidden, being unwilling to disturb
"the ordinary opinions and reverence of society towards the
Bible." For a century and a half a host of scholars in a dozen
countries have been disturbing the reverence and opinions.
Some of them, Professors Jackson and Lake remind us, have
been "the victims of outraged orthodoxy." Nevertheless, "the
corrrectness of the Higher Criticism in its main conclusions was
perforce recognized by all competent scholars throughout the
world. Many cherished beliefs had to be given up, many inter-
pretations useful for moralization abandoned; but... what had
once been regarded as infallible, in ceasing to be so, became
intelligible." It has now been proved, says Prof. Guignebert,
that the religion of the Hebrews "could not be regarded as an in-
digenous growth, but had drawn its constituent elements from
the stock of ideas and beliefs common to the Semitic peoples of
the Orient." He cites as instances the Babylonian story of crea-
tion which "has every appearance" of being the source of the
one in Genesis; and the Code of Hammurabi with its parallels
to "the so-called Law of Moses. These ancient documents prove
conclusively that the alleged isolation of early Judaism was
purely legendary."
403
Until the 18th century, says Prof. Nash, "the Bible held its
head high above reason and history.... The challenge "To the
Sources!' must be accepted. The only alternative was to go back
to the sheltering arms of Tradition." But Tradition, says Prof.
Pfleiderer, “means nothing else than a return to the fundamen-
tal principle that dogma must rule history." Jews as well as
non-Jews have gone to the sources. Sachar, for instance, says
that only a century ago "Hebrew origins were too sacred to
question" and it was difficult "to submit the Bible to ordinary
scientific tests" or even to suggest that "the delightful folklore
of the Holy Writ could not serve as history."
In writing about this subject I found myself in the position
of Prof. Edward Caird: "I have especially had in view that
large and increasing class who have become, partially at least,
alienated from the ordinary dogmatic system of belief, but who,
at the same time, are conscious that they have owed a great
part of their spiritual life to the teachings of the Bible." I have
had before me the words of Salomon Reinach: "I have tried not
to wound any conscience; but I have said what I believe to be
the truth with the emphasis proper to truth." And the words of
Korzybski: "I cannot write just to please the orthodoxy of unin-
formed or misinformed people who do not read, yet know bet-
ter.'" And of Prof. Shotwell: "Where we can understand, it is
a moral crime to cherish the un-understood." Almost 500 years
ago Zwingli wrote of "those unlearned and reckless individuals
who pass sweeping judgments, with the more arrogance the
greater their ignorance." Looking back at the ancient Jews
Harvard's G. F. Moore has put it this way: "They felt much as
a trained Old Testament scholar today feels about a man who,
ignoring all the learning of the past embodied in an exegetical,
historical, and theological tradition that fills hundreds of vol-
umes, and ignorant of the methods of what is called biblical
science, or ignoring its worth and rejecting its authority, un-
dertakes to interpret the Scriptures out of his own head."
There is truth in the witticism that history is a fable that has
been agreed upon. As Toynbee says, history grew out of myth-
404
ology, and in mythology the "distinction between facts and fic-
tions is left undrawn." It may be true that history "has never
succeeded in dispensing with fictitious elements altogether," that
fiction "appears to be an indispensable artifice of thought." It is
true as many besides Toynbee have said that the "effort to re-
suscitate an alien background seldom produces effects that do
not seem either shoddy or labored." Such efforts, says one, are
usually adulterated with "mental anachronism," or, as another
puts it, with the "perpetual play of subjective caprice." Baynes
in his biography of Constantine says that as "students of his-
tory we protest energetically that a man can only be rightly un-
derstood if he be regarded against the background of his world,
that he can only be fairly judged in the light of the standards
and the values of the society in which he lived; and then, hav-
ing formulated the principle, we straightway forget it.... So
hard it is to think ourselves back into a world which is not our
own!"
We must bear in mind that the enormous task of making the
OT intelligible has not been completed. As long ago as 1914,
Prof. Peters, a Higher Critic, wrote that the literature then bear-
ing on the OT was "so vast that it is impossible for any man to
know it or to have read it all." Hundreds of books and thou-
sands of articles have been published since then. Though the
results of all this may be known, at least in a general way, to
literate persons, prejudice against the scholars still exists; and so
it may be well to let Prof. Enslin destroy a misconception:
"There has been much unnecessary prejudice aroused by the
use of the term 'higher criticism.' It is called 'higher' not, as is
often popularly imagined, in arrogant and fancied superiority
to the humble writings under discussion, but in contrast to the
other type of criticism, viz., textual or lower' . . . Higher Crit-
icism may often destroy unfounded traditions and perverse opin-
ions, but its purpose in this is solely to prepare the ground for
constructive building.... Foolish men who try to make the Bi-
It is suggested that the reader keep these words in mind when
we come to what the scholars say about Solomon.
405
ble do what its authors never intended it to do have gone far to-
ward discrediting it.... Higher Criticism enables the Bible to
speak for itself in intelligible language."
WHO WERE THE Hebrews and where did they come from?
Habiru meant the people from beyond the river, but it was
never a proper name of Israel, and the Israelites "never so
called themselves." These people, some time after 1450, came
out of the 'wilderness,' by which they meant the desert, to seize
the lands of Canaan. There is no proof that there were 12 tribes.
Barton thinks the "original Israel probably consisted of 8
tribes," and T. H. Robinson says that it "is generally agreed that
the organization into 12 tribes.... does not accurately repre-
sent the facts of a primitive age." The names of some of the
tribes appear elsewhere as those of gods: Gad "is a well-known
Semitic deity" and Asher may be a modification of Ashur, the
name of another deity.
Was Moses a historic person? Many scholars, including two
of the eminence of Winckler and Cheyne, have dismissed him
as a myth. "I cannot see what right," says Cheyne, "we have to
extract morsels of history from the legendary and late narratives
of Moses." But most scholars accept Moses as a historical per-
son and at least one (Jack) says his historicity has been proved,
though Toynbee has quite recently said that "the characters and
events are mythical." All scholars agree that the story in any
case has been greatly embellished and that it is quite impos-
sible to know what Moses did or taught. Many have pointed
out in the story the myth-elements common to other peoples;
and Oesterley and Robinson say there is "no authority for
bringing the Red Sea into the narrative.... The OT speaks of
the Yam-Suph as the waters in which the Egyptians were over-
whelmed; that means either the 'Reed Sea' or perhaps more
correctly the Sea of Weeds."" The meaning of Moses is un-
known: one thinks it is from mesu, meaning child; another, that
it meant water-child; and a third, that it meant hero. In using
406
ten plagues the chronicler employed the 'completing' number in
antiquity, from the ten fingers or ten toes: ten ages of the
Etruscans, ten commandments, ten spheres of the Pythagor-
eans, etc.
This nomadic people fought in Canaan, which means low-
lands, until triumphant under David. The flora and topography
of the land are amazingly varied: the flora of the southern part
has been compared to parts of our Southwest: the shittim to the
paloverde of the Mojave, the thornbush to mesquite; terebinth
and pistachio shrubs to chaparral, etc. Palestine is a variant of
Philistine; Jerusalem was a Jebusite city. The salem or shalem
in Yerushalem was the name of a Semitic god. The country
roundabout was then and still is, as Klausner says, bare and
terrible. Since 330 A.D. the western hill has been called Zion;
but Macalister and Duncan say it is now established that the
City of David was on the eastern hill.
The language of Israel seems to have become "a mixture of
Accadian, proto-Aramaean ('Amorite'), and Canaanite, with a
number of Egyptian loan-words." It had a smaller vocabulary
than other Semitic languages; only 2 genders; very few adverbs;
and was so deficient in abstract terms that abstractions were ex-
pressed with symbol and metaphor: soul was the breath, being
was the bones, descendents the seed, vigor the horn, compas-
sion the bowels, and so on. It expressed the superlative degree
with doubles: king of kings, song of songs, holy of holies, etc.
The common expression, eating dust, was a refinement of eat-
ing dung. Rock was a common word for deity, and appears of
course in the New Testament. Halleluyah meant literally 'praise
Yah,' Yah (Yahweh) being the name of their Kenite god. The
strongly metaphorical character of their language is shown in
such words as Bethlehem, meaning the house of bread.
They were surrounded by hostile neighbors, most of whom
were small warlike desert tribes. On the north stood the threat
of the growing might of Assyria; on the southwest, Egypt. Soon
after Solomon's death Egypt marched in and plundered the tem-
ple. Whether Solomon sought an alliance with Egypt in mar-
407
riage to an Egyptian princess or whether, as Peet suggests, the
alliance was sought by the Pharaoh, we do not know.
What was the religion of the neighbors? Largely, like that of
all primitive peoples, sun-, moon-, and earth-worship. A rab-
binical passage says: "The Torah [books of Moses] given by
God was made of an integument of white fire, the engraved letters
were in black fire, and it was itself of fire and mixed with fire,
hewn out of fire, and given from the midst of fire." Sun-worship
was "one of the permitted forms of worship in Jerusalem." Sun of
suns was a Hebrew expression, possibly adopted from others. Cer-
tain German scholars have argued that Noah's ark is a sun-myth.
Moon and month in Hebrew are the same word, and the name
Sinai comes from the moon-god Sin.
All their neighbors had a goddess; whether the Israelites ever
had one seems not to be known. If not, they are in this respect
unique, though it is possible that they had one, which was re-
jected by the later prophets and writers. The Divine Mother
was to other Semites a "physical fact" and was worshiped un-
der various names. Briffault has thought that Miriam was pos-
sibly the Hebrew goddess but I have found no support for this
conjecture. Carus says the idea of a goddess-mother was to the
Hebrews "so senseless" that their language avoided the "forma-
tion of a word to express the female form of God." And Cor-
nill thinks Israel never associated "a female deity with the high-
est divine being"; and Baron, a Jew, points to the "lack of a
female deity, a Baalah, the 'great Mother.'" If the Israelites
never had a female deity, this becomes one of the amazing rid-
dles of history.
For other peoples she had many names: Astarte is the Greek
form of the Babylonian Ishtar, whence Ashtoreth, Ashtart, Ash-
era, and other variants. The excavated Astartes show her "na-
ked, with a serpent crawling up her right thigh....in all these
cases the serpent's head is directed toward the vulva of the god-
dess, a fact which proves conclusively that the serpent repre-
sents primarily the fecundizing vis naturae." Though scholars as
great as Cumont have shuddered at the "immorality” in the
408
temples of the goddess, we should look back to the words of
Baynes: it was not immorality for people then. Some think that
if the Kenites, from whom the Hebrews took Yahweh, associ-
ated Ashtart in worship of him, "Moses and the Hebrews would
inevitably worship her too." Cheyne says the Hebrews "fre-
quently combined the name Yahweh with that of the goddess,"
even though the language never developed a feminine form of
kohen, meaning priest. It seems not to be known whether the
Hebrews worshiped a goddess in the wilderness; if they did,
all traces of it were obliterated by the writers of the OT, who
lived centuries afterward.
The Tree of Life, which figures prominently in the OT, was
a common symbol of the 'female principle': "What more clear
than that the conjunction of the Tree and the Serpent is the
fulfillment in nature of that sex-mystery which is so potent in
the life of man and the animals. If the snake had an unmistak-
able resemblance to the male organ in its active state, the foli-
age of the tree or bush is equally remindful of the female." In
putting two trees in the garden Frazer says they confused two
stories; all other people seem to have had only a tree of life.
Some call it that, others, the tree of knowledge, but knowledge
meant "only the ability to use magic arts." Most scholars agree
with Pinches that the tree of life was the vine, though it was
a different plant with different peoples. "The tree of knowledge,
the tree of life, the serpent that tempted Eve.... are so many
figures of speech which the wise understand, but which to the
vulgar are simply trees and snakes." One form of the cross, the
Egyptian ankh, shows the male and female genitals in conjunc-
tion: "That the cross is among other things a phallic emblem
there can be no reasonable doubt." The god himself was cruci-
fied "to the tree which denoted his fructifying power." Others
conjecture that the origin of the cross was the fire-sticks of
primitive man.
The sacred women in the temples of the time are commonly
spoken of as harlots-and again we have to look back to Mr.
Baynes! They were temple nuns, and the "purpose of their exis-
409
tence seems to have been to secure fertility to those men and
women who were barren. The institution was not begotten by
immoral tendencies." The women were 'sacred,' from the Latin
sacer, "the exact equivalent of taboo." The Canaanite sanc-
tuaries were "gigantic brothels" and such "prostitution" made
its way into Israel: "In the precinct of the temple at Jerusalem
were cells with Astarte images in which the Jewish girls prosti-
tuted themselves." Female slaves "were consecrated to the ob-
scene rites....in the name of Yahweh, or some other god."
There was "great sexual license" and the temples of the Semitic
deities "were thronged with sacred prostitutes." Sanger in his
History of Prostitution goes so far as to say that by the time
of the Maccabees, eight centuries later, the temple had become
a "mere brothel."
In this novel Khate's religion is of course that of Akhnaton.
It may seem strangely modern; but as Breasted says, "We must
therefore adjust our minds to the fact that the moral heritage
of modern civilized society originated in a time far earlier than
the Hebrew settlement in Palestine, and has descended to us
from a period when the Hebrew literature now preserved in the
OT did not yet exist." Akhnaton, says another, rose to a height
of theistic and ethical grasp "that has scarcely been surpassed
today"; and Mahaffy declares that there is not a single great
moral or ethical idea in the Jewish or Christian system "which
has not its analogy in the Egyptian faith." Were the Hebrews
influenced by this great monotheistic concept? "Egyptian in-
fluence on Israelite monotheism must be assumed" but the He-
brews were not monotheists in Solomon's time. Many ethical
parallels between the OT and earlier Egyptian writings have
been pointed out: for instance, Psalm 104 is a redaction of a
hymn to Aton.
What was the religion of the Hebrews like? They did not bring
I should like to eschew all editorial comment and opinion in
these notes but I cannot refrain from protesting against the com-
mon habit of calling them brothels and prostitutes. Such scholars
are guilty of applying to them our meanings and judging them by our
standards, a mistake that the anthropologist never makes.
410
out of the wilderness the ten commandments in the OT as we
know them, or anything similar to them. Little or nothing, says
Frazer, of the so-called Mosaic legislation "can be proved to
have emanated from Moses." All reputable scholars accept that;
they all
agree that if Moses had ten commandments we do not
know what they were, but it is believed that in content they
were not moral but were only such taboos as any primitive peo-
ple might have had. If Moses had ten commandments, conjec-
tures on their nature can be found in many authorities. Among
those Budde gives are these: Thou shalt worship no other god;
all the firstborn are mine; six days shalt thou work; the feast of
the unleavened bread shalt thou keep; thou shalt observe the
feast of weeks, etc. But it has been pointed out that since some
of these were agricultural in origin they could not have been
formulated by a desert people. The commandments as we know
them were largely derived from such people as the Babylonians
and Egyptians, because some of them are paralleled in “Egyp-
tian sacred law" and in the Code of Hammurabi, both in exis-
tence long before the time of Moses.
Did they bring the sabbath with them? Though Schauss has
argued that the sabbath was Hebraic in origin, no reputable
scholars accept this view, pointing out that "the sabbath implies
the settled life of agriculture." Possibly it was of Babylonian
origin but a few authorities think that the Hebrews may have
had it before they entered Canaan, though Cheyne believes that
with them it was not pre-exilic and that it was established by
Ezekiel. Budde on the other hand suggests that it was a rest-
day for Kenite smiths. Its origin "presents difficult problems."
For the Babylonians the 15th of the month was called shapattu
or shabbatu; but it was not a day of rest. How it became a day
of rest for the Hebrews "is a mystery." In origin it was un-
doubtedly a lunar festival; and even if the Hebrews had it be-
fore the Exile, for them, says Kohler, a Jewish scholar, "it played
an inferior role to the New Moon." In the prophetic books of
the Hebrews 'sabbath' and 'new moon' are, says Skinner, "as-
sociated in such a way as to suggest that both are lunar festi-
411
vals." This being so, it could not, says Jastrow, "have been orig-
inally celebrated ever 7th day." In Assyria the 1st, 7th, 21st
and 27th days were unlucky days, and the rise of the 7th day
of the week "to paramount importance in the religious life of
the Hebrews belongs to the later period." In making it a rest-
day the Hebrews gave to it a 'historical' origin, as they did with
everything they adopted.
•
When they invaded Canaan they adopted certain agricul-
tural festivals, including the Unleavened Bread, Wheat Harvest
and Ingathering. Some think they brought the Passover with them,
though that it was not historic needs, says Gray, no “elabor-
ate proof." The explanation of the origin and meaning given in
the OT are of a late date. It was observed at the full moon "be-
cause the deity showed himself in his full glory”—that is, the
moon, chief deity of desert nomadic tribes. The victim had to
be consumed between evening and morning, with hide, bones,
entrails, "so that the rising sun may see no vestiges of it re-
maining"-because for desert tribes worshiping the moon the
sun was an enemy. What form it had in the time of Solomon
we do not know. That originally it was a rite of the resurrected
sun-god seems certain; Easter is the modern refinement of that
rite.
Circumcision the Hebrews had of course when they entered
Canaan; but though it was a pre-Yahwistic rite it was not then
"the special mark of a true son of Israel" since "the oldest legal
codes make no mention of it." It was only later when Israel was
in exile among the uncircumcised Babylonians and Assyrians
"that this rite could become a distinctive mark of Yahweh-wor-
ship." When the Hebrews accepted the rite as a special mark
they gave to it, says Baron, "a new and unheard-of meaning,'
that is, as a covenant between them and Yahweh. Whether they
circumcised girls as well as boys is not known; the Arabs did;
and Strabo says that in his time the Jews did. At least four
* See Baron, Social and Religious History of the Jews, I, for an
excellent account of this.
412
theories have been advanced to explain its origin: for hygienic
reasons; as a marriage-rite; as a tribal mark; as an offering to
deity. The weight of opinion is behind the last. Originally it
was "a dedication to the powers of procreation," though later,
Baudissin thinks, "the ritual was addressed to Yahweh with the
words, 'A bridegroom of blood you are to me.'" Cheyne, H. P.
Smith and others say that when the "Hebrew tradition arose it
was already the custom to rub the blood from a young man,
or from a child just circumcised, or to rub the amputated piece
of skin, on the men of the clan." On the practice today Rosenau
is an excellent source.
Apparently they brought the Ark with them. On what it was
opinion varies: one says, a portable throne; another, a military
shrine; a receptacle for an image; a wooden box containing fet-
ishes or inscribed stones, a white and a black, this last being
"the usual view of critics," though some think it contained a
god-image. In any case it was a fetish. "Under the prophetic
teaching Israel gradually outgrew this naive and primitive....
conception” and both the Ark and the Urim and Thummim were
lost. These latter were also stones, "a lot oracle," "two talis-
manic cubes," "sacred lots," "two small stones, either in the
shape of dice or in tablet form, perhaps also of different colors."
These were used "to procure the answer of the deity”: if “both
fell white side upwards the answer was in the affirmative, if black
side upwards then a negative."
They also brought Yah-Yahweh with them, the god whom
they adopted from the Kenites. In the OT "Elohim, the word
translated God, is a plural and may signify either the God of
Israel, heathen gods, angels, or even men." El was the common-
est of their names for deity, and this they used in many words
-Beth-el, Isra-el, etc. Some think Yahweh was originally a vol-
cano-god, others, a storm-god or a refinement of the night-
demon. Blunt says Yahweh is an element in a man's name found
in Babylon 500 years before Moses, and that the Leah tribes
called their god Yah before Moses' time. As for Jehovah, Albert
Schweitzer says of E. von Hartmann, "He has an irritating way
413
of still using the name 'Jehovah.'" Most scholars have. Budde
says, "The word 'Jehovah' was made, early in the 16th century,
by a friar, who was ignorant of the rules of synagogue reading.”
It never was and is not a proper name for Israel's God, though
even some Jewish scholars use it. From David to the Exile the
"common name" for deity was Yah-Jah-Ye-Je-Yeho-Jeho, a tri-
bal god; and it was much later that Yah became (in Paton's
words) "a god with a moral character who transcended the an-
cient limitations of tribal religion."
Undoubtedly they also brought with them the rite of sacri-
fice, which is "the center of the OT." The word did not then
have its present meaning; it meant to make holy, devote, set
apart to deity, because "it would be an unheard of thing for
anyone to partake of the produce of the soil before due pay-
ment." The earth and all things in it belonged to the god, whose
portion was the firstfruits, including the firstborn sons. Because
it was believed that "animals were closely akin to man" in a
later time surrogates were offered for sons, chiefly the bull and
lamb; and still later the surrogate became a payment in money.
The consecrated offering, says Reclus, "be it man, woman, or
virgin, lamb or heifer, cock or dove, represents the deity him-
self....Brahma is the 'imperishable sacrifice'; Indra, Soma,
Hari and the other gods, became incarnate in animals to the
sole end that they might be immolated." The god Siva said, "It
is I that am the actual offering; it is I that you butcher upon my
altars." The thought that a beast could take the place of the
firstborn "is at the root of the sin-offering throughout the whole
of the ancient world." The excavations reveal that the Canaan-
ites buried hundreds of infants, either alive or dead, in stone-
jars, and "well-attested instances are recorded even among the
Hebrews." Indeed, some think that the immolation of infants
in walls was a Hebrew practice until long after Solomon's time.
Sacrifice seems to have had this evolution: first the god himself
was eaten, in the form in which he was represented, whether as
man or beast, to absorb his virtues; and because he was the
Baal or owner of the land he was given the firstfruits, includ-
414
ing firstborn sons. When the rite was refined surrogates were
offered or, finally, a payment in money to redeem the firstborn.
The Jesus story is a refinement of the more ancient immolation
of the god."
In this time there was no belief in a life after death save as
a phantom in Sheol; no punishment in another life for sins com-
mitted here; no resurrection. All these came later from Persian
influence. Sheol was the 'underworld,' a pit under the earth, a
cheerless and terrible place in which the 'shades' wandered
without occupation or joy or hope. For the Babylonians Sheol
was a huge hollow mountain; and our word hell and the Ger-
man Holle originally meant hole and nothing else.
In trying to understand what religion meant then we "have
to deal with a mode of contemplating the world that is quite
foreign to our notions"; we have to learn Hebrew "methods of
thought rather than to argue against certain interpretations from
what we suppose those thoughts to have been," remembering
that "accurate and systematic thinking is no characteristic of the
ancient Semites." The Hebrew religion after the Exile was "very
different from that in Solomon's time." The Hebrews did not pos-
sess a pure monotheism until centuries after Moses but were poly-
theists like all other people. They still worshiped the moon, origin-
ally the supreme deity of all Semitic peoples; and moon-worship
was offered in Jerusalem and the lunar crescent became part of
the headdress of Levite priests. In Talmudic literature the moon
is the emblem of Israel.
Religion was fear; love of God was a "relatively unfamiliar
expression"; and religion was ritual, with little or no moral
basis: "Yahweh was served by ceremony and offering, and little
emphasis was put upon social or private morality." Myth-
ology took the place of dogma. "Of intolerance in the modern
sense of the word ancient society knew nothing; it never per-
secuted a man into particular beliefs for the good of his own
soul." Religion was a "body of ceremonies to be gone through
* Buchanan Gray, Sacrifice in the Old Testament, is the standard
here.
415
with, rather than a body of beliefs to be accepted." Hangings,
such as of the 7 princes, "were looked upon as perfectly nor-
mal occurrences." In practically all matters "ritual offenses were
regarded as more grievous than moral ones.... the idea that
sin against one's neighbor was also a sin against God was un-
known."
After invading Canaan the Hebrews "came strongly under the
influence of the religions there." Most of them fell into alien
practices with the "greatest facility" because agricultural reli-
gions had a "strong fascination" for their sensuous nature. Solo-
mon worshiped foreign gods, though later writers, in an effort
to shield him, threw "the blame upon his wives." They took
over the sacred high places and the altars, and the history of
the high places became "the history of the religion of Israel."
They made "little or no change in their appearance or appoint-
ments."
They adopted, or combined with their own, the phallic wor-
ship of agricultural peoples: "No subject is of greater impor-
tance and significance in the evolution of the human race than
that of sex worship." The "fructifying part of the male organ,
the orchis or orchane (testicles) of the Greeks yields our word
orchard. and-as witnessing or swearing an oath was per-
formed by holding the testicles-an oath in Greek is Orkos. The
testicles on which Abraham swore his servant are called Eduth
or witnesses, and we continue the parallel by calling witnessing
testifying, back to the Latin testes." Many scholars, including
Westropp, Forlong, Jennings, Crawley, hold that there was once
a phallic age which sexed all objects and "made sex the dom-
inant apperceptive organ by which even cosmogony was ex-
plained." In primitive religions the procreative power was wor-
shiped because gods were regarded as its embodiment; virgin-
ity was sacrificed to the gods to secure their favor; and with
some, virginity and continence were enforced. The Code of
Hammurabi was inscribed on a stone in the shape of the male
organ. The belt of Orion was called the staff-and note the ex-
pressions the staff of life and the door of life. The ashera and
416
pillars and groves were all sexual symbols. Mystic communion
with deity was most easily attained through sexual intercourse,
"expressing complete unity with God in terms of the sexual re-
lations of man." OT translators have of course used euphemisms
for most of the sexual terms, such as thigh for the male organ.
The prophets were to struggle against this tendency to adopt
the sensuous practices of agricultural peoples. Wellhausen says
both priest and seer were included in the Arabian kahin, whence
kohen or cohen. In Hebrew prophet and madman were synon-
ymous terms. The meaning of the word prophecy is not clear;
some think it meant to bubble over with speech, others to an-
nounce. The social standing of the prophets "seems to have been
low, and their methods questionable." They were invariably op-
posed to the existing order. Only in later prophecy was con-
science placed in opposition to unworthy impulses. It is prob-
able that I have made Ahijah a far greater man than he was.
The prophet was a holy man but holiness did not have our
meaning, at least not in Solomon's time. "While it is not easy
to fix the exact idea of holiness.... it is quite certain that it has
nothing to do with morality and purity of life." Possibly the
word meant separateness or cutting off. The prophet was also
a godly man. In religions of this time little distinction was made
between the divine and human; the concept of the divine right
of kingship comes from the ancient belief that kings were hardly
distinguishable from gods. The king was the son of god, the
messiah, the savior; and if few traces of this survive in the OT,
that, says Frazer, is because it assumed the form known to us
many centuries later. The prophets strove to elevate deity above
his people and draw a sharp line between the divine and the
human.
We shall now look briefly at a few superstitions and customs,
bearing in mind that practically all customs were religious. The
OT tells us that a virgin was sought for David when he was old
and ill. In ancient times a virgin's potency was universally rec-
ognized. Seduction of a virgin was treated solely as "damage to
property." The notion prevailed that a woman was honored by
417
divine intercourse with a deity or his surrogate, chiefly kings
and priests. Verrall says the "disease, of which more than of
anything else, ancient civilizations perished [was] an imper-
fect ideal of woman." Female chastity was a "slow growth from
the limitations imposed by the masculine sense of ownership
upon the gratification of sexual instincts."
Though scholars speak of the "great movement for the eman-
cipation of women which set in long before" our era, and Have-
lock Ellis presents evidence to show that woman's postion was
high in Babylon and Egypt, by our standards it was not enviable
in Israel. Men loved their daughters, says Sumner, long before
they loved their wives; and Lofthouse adds that "of chivalry
and romantic love in our modern sense the Hebrew knew noth-
ing." There has seldom been any civilized society "in which
a woman may state openly her wishes and desires, and proceed
openly to seek their satisfaction." It was the honor of the man
which was guarded by custodians, seclusion, veils, surgery,
leading "to the conception of the honor and virtue of woman."
The Hebrews did not have marriage as we know it. A married
woman was one "who has become the property of an owner....
The Oriental.... has never been able to rise to the recognition
of the equal rights of man and woman, or even of equal humanity
in them." Nor the Occidental either for that matter. The hus-
band was the baal, meaning possessor; the wife the beulah,
meaning the one possessed. The father had complete power over
his children; he could sell them, give them away, or kill them.
An Egyptian princess occupied a position so much higher than
a Hebrew princess that Solomon's Egyptian marriage must have
been exciting, though of its nature we know nothing.
A woman after childbirth was 'unclean,' meaning sacred or
set apart. "The terms holy or sacred are not to be understood
in the ethical or moral sense." Unclean things were taboo, that
is, temporarily or permanently set aside to the supernatural
powers, such as graves, corpses, swine, etc. It is conjectured
that the pig became 'unclean' because earlier it had been a to-
tem, a god-symbol, and therefore 'holy.' Later, when this was
418
forgotten, another meaning was assigned to it. Why dog be-
came an abusive epithet seems not to be known; the dog was
not a domestic animal in Israel.
All people believed in demons, among whom Lilit was a fa-
vorite. In the Talmud she is the wife of Adam before Eve was
created and became the mother of demons, those creatures
whose haunts were "uncultivated wilds and deserts, bleak sum-
mits of mountains, the pestilential marshes." The essential dif-
ference between a spirit or demon and a god or goddess is
that the latter has individuality. Demons had to be "cajoled, ap-
peased, bribed, and rewarded." If the demonology of the OT is
scanty, this is because later writers obliterated it, since Yahweh
"would tolerate no rivals." Lilit was a nocturnal specter bent on
mischief; she caused men to waste their seed, and weakened
boy babies during their first 8 days-another attempt to explain
circumcision after its origin had been forgotten.
One way to frighten demons off was with magical chants.
Those in this story are based on the Ebers Papyrus and other
sources. There were 30,000 such chants in the royal library of
Assurbanipal, all of them much alike in their repetition and
monotony. Though Baron says that from the outset Israel rejected
all forms of magic the scholars do not support him. "The prac-
tice of magic had always been popular among the Chosen Peo-
ple as far back as the time of Saul"; and Trachtenberg says the
biblical allusions to it "indicate a widespread acquaintance with
its manifold forms at an early time." Sickness was a manifesta-
tion of the wrath of God for transgressions, or was due to the
attack of demons or to the breach of a taboo or to the Evil Eye.
As Breasted says, it is "difficult for the modern mind to un-
derstand how completely the belief in magic penetrated the
whole substance of life...." The Evil Eye seems to have grown
out of notions about envy, and the commandment against it is
of course the one on covetousness. Amongst modern Jews, says
Farbridge, "there is a superstition that in order to nullify the
effects of an evil eye the mother should kiss the child three
times, spitting after each kiss." The witch was an agent of evil:
419
"Thou shalt not suffer a witch to live." Spranger estimated long
ago that this 'divine commandment' led to the death of nine
million people in Europe, many of them by fire. The witches
seem to have deceived their inquirers "by speaking in a weak
thin voice."
Three was a superstitious number; we know that its origin
is the male genitals. We know the origin of ten, twelve, thirteen;
but seven, a great favorite with the Hebrews, is obscure in its
origin. Some have thought its origin the sun, moon and 'five
planets' but others doubt that people knew of five planets when
seven became a holy number. Its origin would seem quite ob-
viously to have been in the four phases of the moon. Philo said
"an infinite number of beauties may be extracted from a care-
ful contemplation of it" and for Augustine 7 was the symbol of
the spirit, and 7 plus 10, or 17, the symbol of holiness. This
number "regulated all the great dates of the Jewish sacred
year"; Hebrews had the sabbatical year, Jubilee year, the year
following 7 times 7; sevenfold sprinkling; sevenbranched can-
dlestick; forgiveness till seventy times seven, etc.; and in the
Babylonian story of creation there were 7 winds, 7 spirits of
storms, 7 divisions of the underworld, 7 zones of the upper
world, and so on. For Aristobulus, a Hellenic Jew, 7 was a sym-
bol of the logos in man, by which man had knowledge of things
human and divine. Six, says Enslin, carried a sinister meaning
because it fell just short of seven and "six upon six upon six repre-
sents the apotheosis of evil.”
Of other customs we may note a few. The Hebrews were the
only ancient people who did not assign to music a supernatur-
al source. Their ideal of music was a "single powerful unisono”;
like other peoples they had no knowledge of harmony. Like
other peoples they sought after aphrodisiacs; and in different
times with different peoples practically all foods but black bread
and milk were invested with the power to arouse carnal pas-
sions. With them as with nearly all ancient peoples "drunk-
eness must have been a common vice." And with them as with
others before the Christian era "the terms 'monogamy,' 'big-
420
amy,' 'polygamy' in the sense in which we use them were un-
known." Polygamy was prohibited for the first time in any part
of the world in the Code of Justinian. "Never until modern times
has there been a law forbidding a man to take more than one
wife."
"War... was essentially a religious duty." Every war "was a
'holy' war. The horrid rites in honor of the gods who fought for
their followers are to be traced in Egypt, Assyria and the OT."
To utterly destroy all people was “an act of service to their god"
and before they went to war the priests or magicians "pronounced
terrible imprecations against the enemy. After victory all prisoners
were to be killed." The custom of fasting before fighting was
"primarily nothing more than a preparation for the sacramental
eating of holy flesh." See Isaiah, xii, 3.
Of the people in this novel who actually existed little is known.
Bathsheba's husband was said to have been a Hittite; she may or
may not have been. The so-called 'Jewish nose' was Hittite, not
Hebrew. In some of the legends about him Solomon had red-
dish hair. Of Naamah nothing is known; possibly her name is the
same as anemone, an epithet in Adonis-Tammuz worship mean-
ing darling. The traditional view of Jeroboam is not supported by
the Higher Critics. The Jewish Pirke Aboth says he "sinned and
caused many to sin" and Dean Milman called him the "politic and
unscrupulous Jeroboam." But H. P. Smith says "The judgment of
posterity on Jeroboam has been curiously influenced by religious
prepossession.” He has long been misunderstood, says Barton:
"He was not a religious innovator, but a religious conservative."
He had "no intention of abandoning Yahweh worship." He
"sought to attain his end by a still more zealous and elaborate
worship of Yahweh." As for the bull-symbol, most scholars think
that Yahweh had been represented by a bull for a long time; and
such as Kuenen and Vatke say that Yahweh was worshiped in the
bull-symbol in all sanctuaries and holy places.
We conclude with Solomon, about whom we know little, the
biblical accounts having been invented or grossly embellished
and exaggerated. Popular historians still give myth and legend
421
as historical fact, as when Will Durant says that it was "probably
from Arabia that the Queen of Sheba came." The Critics dismiss
this as "a legendary addition" that is "fabulously embroidered in
colors of the later South Arabian kingdom"; and Winckler thinks
that if the story had any historic basis the woman was probably
his Egyptian wife. In considering Solomon we must keep in mind
what Driver said of the OT as a whole, that on "the authorship
of the books...the Jews possess no tradition worthy of credence
or regard, but only vague and uncertain reminiscences, inter-
mingled often with idle speculation." The character of Solomon
in the OT is, says Peritz, “in large measure the idealization of the
later editors, who...reflect what Solomon ought to have been
[in their opinion, of course] rather than what he was." History,
says Kennett, "as a modern historian understands it is scarcely to
be found between the covers of the OT." And Cornill: "Embell-
ishments of a mere legendary character . . the Chronicler's
fondness for large numbers...he flings about his hundred thou-
sands and millions wherever the bare attempt to give concrete
representation to the things described is sufficient to demonstrate
their utter impossibility." The account in Chronicles is “com-
pletely unhistorical"; it should be classed, says Pfeiffer (as late
as 1948) "with the books of Jonah, Esther, Tobit, Judith and the
like as historical fiction." It is "generally recognized" that the
book was written at least 600 years after Solomon's death. Of
exaggerations these are typical: "And King Solomon offered a
sacrifice of twenty and two thousand oxen, and an hundred and
twenty thousand sheep... And King Solomon passed all the kings
of the earth in riches and wisdom. And all the kings of the earth
sought the presence of Solomon . . . so there fell down slain of
Israel five hundred thousand chosen men." Prof. Baron in his ex-
cellent The Jewish Community thinks the population probably
never exceeded 80,000. Yet it was from such sources "that huge
sums said to have totaled 666 talents in gold (the equivalent of
some $220,000,000 at the present rate of $35 per ounce) could
have come into Solomon's treasury in a single year." Or $2750
for every man, woman and child! Sachar, another Jewish scholar,
422
points to I Kings, iv, 22-3, which gives an inventory of the daily
necessities of Solomon's court. "There were needed 30,000 kor of
fine flour"-a kor is about 80 gallons, that is, enough flour daily
for ten million pound-loaves!
This is what happened. Hundreds of years after Solomon's
time the Jews, contemplating the glories of peoples around them,
such as the Greeks, became very sensitive to their own past, and
wrote books in an effort to show that their civilization had not
been inferior to any other. Possibly because legends had to some
extent embellished his name they chose Solomon as “the wise man
par excellence of Israel" and ascribed to him "writings composed
long after his time." It was their purpose "to show that the ancient
kings of Israel were patrons of literature like the Ptolemies." The
authorities generally agree that he had nothing to do with Ecclesi-
astes, Proverbs, Psalms, etc. It was only, says Toy, that "The Jew-
ish tradition came to regard him as the ideal of wisdom...and
ascribed to him indiscriminately everything of this sort." As for
the famous judgment of Solomon in the quarrel of the two women
over the babe, this, says Winckler, "does not belong to the Jewish
mind, but had been in circulation in all the countries of the East
long before Solomon's time."
The later Jewish writers not only outrageously exaggerated his
wealth, wisdom and power, but everything that related to him.
No Critic thinks he had 300 wives and 700 concubines; this also is
"grossly exaggerated"; it is conjectured that he possibly had thirty
or forty wives. As for his temple, Maspero says: "Compared with
the magnificent monuments of Egypt and Chaldea, the work of
Solomon was what the Hebrew kingdom appears to us among the
empires of the ancient world-a little temple suited to a little
people." It was, says Olmstead, "impressive only to Israelite
peasants and herdsmen." The Hebrews, says Macalister, "were in-
capable of erecting buildings desired to be 'exceeding magnifi-
cal" but had to send to Phoenicia for what they needed. We
know little about the nature of his temple. Much has been con-
jectured about the cherubim. B. Z. Goldberg says: "And round
about the graven images of lions, palm-trees and cherubim were
428
figures of the lingam and yoni in union. This we know from a pas-
sage in I Kings vii, 36, which has been generall omitted in bibli-
cal translation even as far back as the Peshito. . . Where a trans-
lation is attempted, its sense is vague and almost meaningless. This
phrase, which in Hebrew is K'maar Ish U'lyotha, is rendered by
Rashi, the most authoritative commentator of the 11th century,
on the basis of a passage in the Talmud (Yuma 54a) as: like the
male and female in embrace.'" The teraphim are also obscure.
Many Critics think they were an image of Yahweh because “it
is impossible to adhere to the image prohibition as Mosaic." Others
have suggested that they were originally mummified heads of
ancestors. Or "they may have been statues of ancestors." The
mural which horrified Ahijah in this novel is based on Macalister
and Duncan.
What was Solomon himself like? We do not know. Zeitlin, a
Jew, says that his "moral shortcomings were condemned by the
ancient prophets themselves." On "the flimsiest of pretexts," says
Kennett, he had his brother, rightful heir to the throne, put to
death, “and Joab with even less justification.” “Of statesmanship
in our sense of the word," says H. P. Smith, "he had not the
faintest glimmer." He drafted, says Baron, "tens of thousands of
free subjects to build the temple and the royal palace." We do
not know how long he reigned. "In Hebrew tradition we find that
a period of 40 years is constantly used...40 years being perhaps
regarded as a generation.” His reign “came to be idealized,” says
Emmet, "but his policy could lead only to ruin." The reign of Solo-
mon, says T. H. Robinson, "proved fatal to any chances Israel
may have had of reaching a commanding position among the na-
tions." He built the temple, says Olmstead, to bring religion under
tribute and enrich himself. His reign, says Renan, was "an error in
the general course of the history of Israel." The whole policy of
Solomon, says H. T. Fowler, "meant loss of liberty, economic and
political." Klausner says he "placed too heavy a burden on the
country and thereby hastened the end.” Albright, Blunt, Oesterley,
Robinson and others say that he enslaved his own people, which
was a "glaring violation of the rights of man, as understood by
424
the average Israelite." And the writer of I Kings "tries to shield
the king from the charge of enslaving Israel." Though Sachar
thinks there has been a tendency to over-debunk Solomon he.de-
clares nevertheless that he made "grievous mistakes. He was often
a petty Oriental despot. His genius was never of a moral or spirit-
ual quality” and he left a “tradition of splendor and a bankrupt
treasury to maintain it.”
If Solomon was no more than what the Critics have seen in him
he is not worth writing about. But with all deference to their acute-
ness and their vast erudition I think they have missed the point.
Looking back at Baynes' words on Constantine let us remember
that nearly all kings of his time were despots; that they destroyed
their brothers and all other pretenders to the throne; that they en-
slaved people to promote their own position and power; that they
were cruel and even barbarous by our standards; and, above all,
that having no belief in an after-life and punishment for sins com-
mitted here or rewards for good deeds done here they had few, in
fact almost no, restraints put upon their ambitions, no checks on
their greed and tyranny save those imposed by their enemies.
Cheyne reminds us: "As Mr. G. A. Smith remarks, 'most of the
kings who pray for an entrance among the gods, do so on the plea
that they have been successful tyrants.'" So hard it is, says Baynes,
to think ourselves back into a world which is not our own!
Prof. Goodenough put his finger on what so many have missed:
"The whole history of Israel is a history of the struggle to make
Jews into a people of an exclusive religion." It was a struggle be-
tween the kings on the one hand and the prophets on the other.
Indeed, the struggle from Solomon to Maccabees seems to have
been one between the majority who wanted to mate and mix in
human fellowship, and the prophets and their followers who
strove for the dogma of a chosen and exclusive people and the
sole God who should belong solely to Israel. Toynbee throws some
light on the matter when he says that the Arabian desert “im-
poses upon all human beings in all ages who have the hardihood
to be its inhabitants a rigid and unvarying way of life." The proph-
ets fought to keep the rigid way of the desert traditions and were
425
finally triumphant under the Maccabees. Right or wrong, I see
Solomon as one who accepted the challenge, in Toynbee's sense
of that word. In any case it seems too harsh to say that he com-
pletely failed, for it does appear that he precipitated the struggle
'between prophets and kings' that was to rend Israel for the better
part of a thousand years, and leave its indelible mark on the insti-
tutions, customs and religions of the Western world. There is a
curious prepossession in nearly all people which impels them to
believe that what happened in the past has been in practically all
cases better for progress and humanity than what might have
happened if in any crucial period another set of circumstances had
won. The rigid way triumphed under the Maccabees; and the
Christians in taking over so much of Judaism gave a 'desert psy-
chology' to agricultural peoples. It seems strange that nobody has
remarked on this quite obvious fact, which possibly more than
any other shaped the world we now live in. The great significance
of Solomon in world history, as I see it, can be formulated in a
question: What would the world be like today if Ahijah had lost
and Solomon had won?
V. F.
